## 导读

> 本导读整理自 Socratopia 网站本书介绍页：https://www.socratopia.app/library/close-listening-chopin-en

Chopin's catalogue is one of the strangest in Western art music: a major Romantic-era composer who wrote almost exclusively for solo piano, who never composed a symphony or an opera, and whose entire creative life spans about twenty working years. He published roughly 240 works — almost all for one performer, most under twelve minutes long. The famous five (the Op. 9 No. 2 Nocturne, the Minute Waltz , the Funeral March, the Heroic Polonaise, the Ballade No. 1) are the pieces that escaped him into the larger culture. They are not what he actually spent his life doing. This volume walks you through about fifty.

The book is structured along Chopin's genre catalogue rather than along his life. The four Ballades — written across seven years — share a single chapter. The 21 nocturnes from Op. 9 (1832) through Op. 62 (1846) sit together so the within-genre evolution becomes audible. The early village-feeling mazurkas of Op. 6 (1830) and the harmonically near-Wagnerian Op. 56 No. 3 (1844) live in adjacent chapters because that is the only way the arc becomes hearable. The biographical thread (Warsaw 1810–1830 → Paris 1831 → the Sand partnership 1838–1847 → the British tour 1848 → the October 17, 1849 death) is threaded through, but the spine is the catalogue.

What sets this volume apart from the popular Chopin literature is what it refuses to do. It does not retell the Revolutionary -as-Stuttgart-1831 story (that trace runs through Frederick Niecks's 1888 biography rather than through Chopin's letters). It does not pair specific Ballades with specific Mickiewicz poems (the pairings are 19th-century reception readings the documentary record cannot bear). It does not romance the George Sand relationship, and it does not demonize it; it holds the complex picture Walker and Zamoyski hold. The image of Chopin-the-delicate-sickly-Romantic is treated as partly fabricated — the tuberculosis was real and killed him at 39, but the working professional with a heavy teaching schedule and a nine-summer Nohant working life is the actual subject. The book leans on Walker 2018, Samson 1996, Rosen 1995, Szulc 1998, Zamoyski 2010, Kallberg 1996, Tomaszewski, and Eigeldinger 1986; when popular myth diverges from the scholarly record, the chapter names the divergence.

This is the fourth volume in Close Listening after the Classical-music survey (B159), Beethoven (B201), and Mozart (B202). The four-section listening loop — Before You Listen / The Listening Window / What's Happening in the Music / The Stories — runs every chapter, refreshed for Chopin's specific calibrations: the right-hand-sings-left-hand-accompanies texture, the bel-canto vocal lineage from Bellini, tempo rubato as a controlled freedom. About fifty works. About twenty hours of music. The amateur listener who finishes the volume can walk into a Chopin recital with confident anticipation rather than waiting for the next familiar piece — and that, more than most "great composer" books, is what the volume actually delivers.

**Target audience**：Adult amateur classical-music lovers who have completed B159 Close Listening: Classical Music , B201 Close Listening: Beethoven , and B202 Close Listening: Mozart , and want a 39-year deep dive into Fryderyk Chopin through ~50 specific anchor works across his genre catalogue.

**Prerequisites**：None formal. Familiarity with the four-section listening loop introduced in B159 helps but is re-introduced in Chapters 1–2.

**Study hours**：55–75 hours of listening + reading (one chapter per week sustained; one chapter per evening if pressed).

## Chapter 1: Why Chopin and Why Now

You have already met Fryderyk Chopin. If you came to this volume through the _Close Listening_ series, you met him at the close of _Close Listening: Classical Music_ , where Chapter 17 anchored on the Ballade No. 1 in G minor and visited two of the early Nocturnes. You may also know him from outside the series: from a wedding where someone played the Op. 9 No. 2 Nocturne; from a film where the Funeral March marked a death scene; from a piano lesson where the _Heroic_ Polonaise was the milestone you were working toward; from the radio where the _Minute Waltz_ was used to fill thirty seconds before the news.

What you have not met yet is the whole composer. Chopin lived thirty-nine years, of which his serious creative life occupies about twenty (roughly 1827 to 1849). In that span he published about 240 works. Almost all of them are for solo piano. He invented no symphony, no string quartet, no opera. He invented one keyboard genre nearly from scratch — the ballade as a single-movement piano narrative — and he transformed several inherited ones (the mazurka, the polonaise, the etude, the nocturne, the prelude, the scherzo) into vehicles for serious concert art-music. Twenty years, about 240 works, one instrument almost exclusively. That is the composer. This book wants to walk you through about fifty of his works.

Why fifty and not five, and why fifty and not five hundred? Five is too few because the famous five (the Op. 9 No. 2 Nocturne, the _Minute Waltz_ , the Funeral March, the _Heroic_ Polonaise, and the Ballade No. 1) do not actually represent what Chopin spent his life doing. They are the pieces that escaped him into the larger culture. Five hundred is too many because the listening life that includes five hundred Chopin works is the life of a professional pianist, not of an attentive amateur. Fifty is the count at which you have heard every major Chopin genre, traced the within-genre evolution from early to late in at least three genres, and walked into any Chopin recital with confident anticipation rather than waiting for the next familiar piece. Fifty is the count that earns the word _catalogue_.

### 1.1 Why now

You are reading this volume after _Close Listening: Classical Music_ , which gave you the four-section listening loop and twenty broad anchors across the Western art-music tradition, and after the Beethoven and Mozart volumes, which gave you those composers respectively across full single-composer arcs. The series has now done three things for you: it has equipped you with a way of listening; it has shown you Beethoven, the composer whose ambitions stretched the inherited Classical forms; and it has shown you Mozart, the composer who lived inside those forms and made them sing. Chopin is the third move. He is a composer who chose to live inside one instrument and to do almost everything there.

The reason now is partly that Chopin is the gentlest entrance into the 19th-century Romantic catalogue. He does not require the orchestra; he does not require an opera house; he does not require choral forces; he does not require even a second performer for almost any of the works in this volume (the Cello Sonata Op. 65 of 1845-47 is the major exception). A reader with a phone and a recording can listen to almost all of Chopin's important works in the room where she lives. The works are also short. The Op. 9 No. 2 Nocturne runs about four minutes. The longest single-movement work in this book, the Ballade No. 4 in F minor Op. 52, runs about twelve minutes. The longest multi-movement work, the Sonata No. 2 Op. 35, runs about twenty-four minutes. Many of the Preludes run under two minutes. A listening session of an hour can cover three or four important Chopin works completely. There is no orchestra to introduce, no chorus to follow, no plot to track — just a piano and a single composer thinking.

The reason now is also that the modern scholarship is now mature. Alan Walker's _Fryderyk Chopin: A Life and Times_ was published in 2018; it is the contemporary single-volume biographical standard and absorbs about thirty years of post-1980s academic work. Jim Samson's _Chopin_ (in the Master Musicians series, OUP) appeared in 1996 as the musical-analytical mid-career biography. Tad Szulc's _Chopin in Paris_ (Scribner, 1998) is the Paris-period narrative. Adam Zamoyski's _Chopin: Prince of the Romantics_ (HarperCollins, 2010) is the cultural-context register. Charles Rosen's _The Romantic Generation_ (Harvard, 1995) is the analytical work that places Chopin alongside Schumann, Berlioz, and Liszt. Jeffrey Kallberg's _Chopin at the Boundaries_ (Harvard, 1996) is the contemporary academic voice on genre and gender. Mieczysław Tomaszewski's _Chopin: Człowiek, dzieło, rezonans_ (Polish original 1998, expanded 2010) is the Polish-school standard, available in English through the _Chopin Encyclopedia_ essays. These books exist; they are reasonably current; the reader who walks through them after this volume can become serious about Chopin in a way that was not available in any single generation before.

### 1.2 The Six Listening Habits to Unlearn (Chopin Edition)

Every _Close Listening_ volume opens with six habits the reader should be aware of and gradually unlearn. The habits are not stupidities; they are reasonable shortcuts that the larger culture has installed, and that close listening dissolves over the course of one composer's catalogue.

**Habit 1: Chopin is sentimental piano wallpaper.** The Op. 9 No. 2 Nocturne, when it is played at a wedding, lands as background music. The _Minute Waltz_ , when it is used as a radio bumper, lands as a virtuoso curlicue. The Op. 10 No. 3 Etude in E major, often nicknamed _Tristesse_ in 19th-century reception (the nickname is not Chopin's), is sometimes packaged as a romantic film cue. From these performances of these specific works in these specific contexts, the larger inference travels: Chopin is the piano composer for moods. The habit is wrong. The Mazurka in C♯ minor Op. 41 No. 1 (1838-1839) is Polish-nationhood assertion in an era when Poland did not exist as a state. The Etude in C minor Op. 10 No. 12 (the popular _Revolutionary_ nickname is not Chopin's; the popular Stuttgart-fall-of-Warsaw composition story traces to Niecks's 1888 biography rather than to Chopin's letters, and is best treated cautiously as 19th-century reception) is a technical revolution that asks the right hand and the left hand to do work together that no previous etude composer had asked of them. The Polonaise-Fantaisie Op. 61 (1846) is a thirteen-minute structural invention that resists the polonaise label that gave it its name. The Scherzo No. 2 in B♭ minor Op. 31 (1837) is ten minutes of serious dramatic argument. None of these works are wallpaper. We will return to each.

**Habit 2: All the Mazurkas sound alike.** They do not. The arc from the Op. 6 mazurkas of 1830-31 to the late-1840s mazurkas including the F minor Op. 68 No. 4 (traditionally identified among Chopin's final sketches) is one of the great within-genre evolutions in Western music. The early mazurkas sit closer to the village dance — recognizable mazurka rhythm, recognizable melodic profile, modest harmonic ambition. The late mazurkas reach into harmonic territory that is sometimes startling — extreme chromaticism, modal mixture, an at-times-near-Wagnerian harmonic ambition that the Op. 6 cycle from 1830-31 does not have. We will hear the early mazurkas in Chapter 4 and the late mazurkas in Chapter 13, and the Op. 17 No. 4 in A minor (1832-33) and the Op. 56 No. 3 in C minor (1844) will sit side by side in the listening journey.

**Habit 3: Chopin was delicate, sickly, weak.** The tuberculosis was real; it was diagnosed at least by 1838 and probably earlier; it killed him at thirty-nine. The 19th-century image of Chopin as the fragile consumptive poet at the piano is built on the tuberculosis but it exaggerates. Walker emphasizes that for most of Chopin's career he was a working professional with a heavy teaching schedule, an active social life across Paris, and a sustained compositional output. The 1848 trip to Britain — London concerts in May and June, the country-house visit at Calder House near Edinburgh through the summer, the Manchester recital of August 28, the Glasgow recital of September 27, the Edinburgh recital of October 4, the November 16 Guildhall benefit appearance for Polish refugees — was undertaken in deteriorating health, and it is by any biographical reading a near-heroic effort. The image of fragility is partly fabricated; the music itself is muscular in places, intimate in others, but rarely actually frail.

**Habit 4: George Sand made him compose great music. Or alternatively: George Sand drained him.** Both 19th-century readings exist. The first runs roughly: Sand provided the country estate at Nohant, the emotional support across nine summers, the maternal care during the Mallorca winter, and the bourgeois domestic stability that the Sand-years masterpieces (the Op. 28 Preludes, the four Ballades, the Sonata Op. 35, the Polonaise-Fantaisie) required. The second runs roughly: Sand was the older, dominating partner who exploited Chopin's vulnerability, distorted his social life, and contributed to his physical decline. Walker reads the relationship as complex — alternately generative and difficult — and resists either-or framing. Zamoyski reads it similarly. Kallberg has written on how the gendered reception of Chopin's music interacted with the gendered reception of the Sand relationship. The honest reading is that the relationship was real, the partnership was generative for nine years, the break in 1847 was painful, and no melodramatic single-cause story serves the music or the people. Chapter 8 establishes this; the music chapters Ch 9-13 do not embroider it.

**Habit 5: Chopin invented the etude.** He did not. Johann Baptist Cramer published 84 etudes between 1804 and 1820. Muzio Clementi published the _Gradus ad Parnassum_ etudes between 1817 and 1826. Carl Czerny published hundreds of pedagogical etudes from the 1810s onward and was a working presence in the Vienna of Chopin's 1829 visit. What Chopin did, in the Op. 10 (1833) and Op. 25 (1837) sets, was to take the inherited pedagogical-etude genre and make it concert art-music. Each Chopin etude has a specific technical problem (the right-hand thirds of Op. 25 No. 6; the left-hand octaves of Op. 25 No. 10; the right-hand rapid figuration of Op. 10 No. 5); each is also a stand-alone work that survives performance in a recital. Cramer and Czerny did the pedagogy; Chopin made the genre artistic. We will return to this in Chapter 6.

**Habit 6: Chopin's music is for piano students or for film soundtracks.** The two contexts in which most non-classical-music listeners have encountered Chopin work against him in different directions. The student-piano context installs Chopin as a series of progressively-difficult exercises — the Op. 28 Preludes for the intermediate student, the easier etudes for the advanced student, the Ballade No. 1 as the rite-of-passage piece — and the works become defined by the developmental position they occupy in pedagogy rather than by what they are as music. The film-soundtrack context installs Chopin as mood — the Funeral March for grief scenes, the Op. 9 No. 2 for romantic scenes, the _Heroic_ Polonaise for triumphal scenes — and the works become defined by the emotional functions they fulfill rather than by what they are as compositions. Both contexts are real, both are partly accurate, and both shrink the music. The genre arc this book traces — the within-mazurka evolution, the within-nocturne evolution, the late-period structural innovations — is invisible from inside the student-piano frame and inside the film-soundtrack frame. The standard entry points exist (the Nocturne Op. 9 No. 2, Ballade No. 1, the Funeral March, Mazurka Op. 17 No. 4, the _Heroic_ Polonaise, the Etude Op. 10 No. 3 are most listeners' first Chopin pieces), but the entry points are openings into a much wider catalogue, not the catalogue itself.

### 1.3 The four-period framework

Modern Chopin biography organizes the thirty-nine years into a framework with three clear divisions and one fuzzy late phase. Walker uses essentially this division; Samson uses a near-identical one with slightly different boundary years. The framework is:

**Warsaw 1810-1830.** Birth on March 1, 1810 in Żelazowa Wola (the date Chopin himself gave in adulthood; the baptismal record at the Brochów church gives February 22, 1810 — the discrepancy is well-known and Walker accepts March 1 as the date Chopin recognized). Childhood in Warsaw from late 1810 onward (the family moved to Warsaw within months of his birth). The Warsaw Lyceum education. The composition lessons with Józef Elsner at the Warsaw Conservatory from 1826 to 1829. The early concerts. The first publications. The early Vienna concerts of August 1829. The second Vienna stay of late 1830. The November 2, 1830 departure from Warsaw, eight days before the November Uprising began on November 29. The Warsaw years are the formative years; about thirty published works carry "Op." numbers from this period, plus the early polonaises and other works composed in childhood. Chapter 3 covers the period; Chapter 4 covers the early-mazurka launch within it.

**Early Paris 1831-1837.** The arrival in Paris in late September or early October 1831. The salon network entry. The first published works in Paris (the Op. 6 mazurkas in 1832; the Op. 9 nocturnes in 1832; the Op. 10 etudes in 1833). The Schumann review of the Op. 2 Variations in December 1831 ("Hats off, gentlemen, a genius!"). The Liszt friendship beginning. The teaching career building. The professional establishment by 1835-36. This is the period of the first major Paris cycles: Op. 6, Op. 9, Op. 10, Op. 17, Op. 24, Op. 26, Op. 27. Chapters 5, 6, and 7 cover this period across the early mazurkas, the etudes, and the nocturnes.

**Sand years 1838-1847.** The late-1836 meeting with George Sand at a Liszt-hosted soirée. The 1837 hesitation. The 1838 partnership beginning. The Mallorca winter of November 1838 to February 1839 at Valldemossa, where the Op. 28 Preludes were completed. The Nohant summers from 1839 through 1846 at Sand's country estate in the Berry region of central France. The 1847 break. This is the period of most of the famous Chopin: the Op. 28 Preludes, the four Ballades, the four Scherzos, the Sonata Op. 35 and the Sonata Op. 58, the late mazurkas Op. 50, Op. 56, Op. 59, the Polonaise Op. 53, the Polonaise-Fantaisie Op. 61, the Fantasy Op. 49, the Berceuse Op. 57, the Barcarolle Op. 60. Chapters 8 through 13 cover this period.

**Late period 1846-1849.** The framework's fuzzy boundary; the Polonaise-Fantaisie Op. 61 (1846) and the Barcarolle Op. 60 (1846) are sometimes assigned to the Sand years and sometimes to the late period. From 1847 onward the period is unambiguous: the post-Sand year of 1847; the 1848 British tour; the January 1849 return to Paris; the October 17, 1849 death. The late-period output is small (the Cello Sonata Op. 65 of 1845-47; the late mazurkas of 1847-49; the Op. 64 waltzes of 1847); Chopin's failing health from 1848 onward limited new composition. Chapters 14, 15, and 16 cover the late period.

This is the framework. You can carry it in your head with five date-coordinates: 1810 (birth), 1830 (Warsaw departure), 1831 (Paris arrival), 1838 (Sand partnership begins), 1849 (death). Every chapter of this book will open with the date-coordinates of the chapter's anchor work, and you should be able to place each work in this framework on the basis of those coordinates alone.

### 1.4 The genre-by-genre spine

The single most unusual structural feature of this volume — and what distinguishes it from the Beethoven and Mozart volumes — is that the chapters are organized partly by chronology and partly by genre. Chapters 3 and 5 are biographical-chronological (Warsaw, Paris arrival). Chapter 4 is the early mazurka cycle. Chapter 6 is the etudes (both Op. 10 and Op. 25, even though the two sets are four years apart). Chapter 7 is the full nocturne arc from Op. 9 in 1832 to Op. 62 in 1846, covering fourteen years in one chapter. Chapter 8 returns to chronology (the Sand relationship). Chapters 9 through 13 are mostly genre-by-genre within the Sand years. Chapter 14 is the polonaise across Chopin's whole life. Chapter 15 is the late single-movement masterworks.

Why this structure? Because Chopin's catalogue is one of the most genre-organized in Western art music. He wrote about 59 mazurkas, 21 polonaises, 21 nocturnes, 24+ preludes, 4 ballades, 4 scherzos, 3 piano sonatas, 4 impromptus, 27 etudes, about 17 waltzes, 2 piano concertos, and the Cello Sonata Op. 65 — and the mazurkas, polonaises, nocturnes, etudes, and preludes are central in a way that means a reader who walks through the catalogue by genre learns to hear each genre's within-genre evolution clearly. This is Thread B of the volume. By Chapter 17, you should be able to describe the early-to-late evolution within the mazurka, the nocturne, the ballade, the scherzo, and the polonaise on the basis of having heard at least two works from each end of each arc.

The cost of the genre-by-genre spine is that chronology becomes uneven: the four Ballades all live in Chapter 10, even though they span from 1835 to 1842; the four Scherzos all live in Chapter 11, even though they span from 1831 to 1842. Chapter 8 (the Sand-relationship context) sits before Chapter 9 (Op. 28 Preludes, completed Mallorca winter 1838-39) but also frames Chapter 13 (the late mazurkas Op. 56 from 1844, Op. 59 from 1845, Op. 63 from 1847). The reader needs to hold the chronology in mind from Section 1.3 above, and let the chapters relocate her within it. We will help by date-stamping every anchor work at the chapter open and by closing every chapter with explicit chronological pointers forward and backward.

### 1.5 The Anti-Definition: what this book is not

This book is not a Chopin biography. Walker has written that biography; Samson has written a hybrid biography-and-musical-analysis; Szulc has written the Paris-period narrative; Zamoyski has written the cultural-context biography. Any one of these is a better introduction to the life than this book.

This book is not the "Chopin the delicate sickly Romantic" volume. That image is partly fabricated, per the modern-scholarship consensus we will lean on (Walker, Samson, Rosen, Kallberg, Tomaszewski).

This book is not the "George Sand made him write good music" volume, nor the "George Sand was poison" volume. Both 19th-century readings exist; the book holds the complex picture from Walker and Zamoyski without melodrama.

This book is not a "best of Chopin" twelve-piece list-book. Twelve pieces is the wedding-playlist count, not the catalogue count.

This book is not a music-school analysis textbook. No sheet-music literacy is required to follow it. Where we name a key, a chord, or a formal section, we will gloss the term in plain English at first use or in the moment.

This book is **about what it actually feels like to listen — closely — to about fifty specific Chopin works across his thirty-nine years, with a friend who knows the Warsaw-to-Paris trajectory, the Sand-relationship years, the genre-by-genre evolution, the recordings, and how to set up each listen and meet you afterwards to talk about what you heard.**

### 1.6 How to use this book

The book is designed for one chapter per week, sustained. Each chapter takes about ninety minutes to read carefully and another sixty to ninety minutes for the first listen plus the second listen of the anchor work; budget about three hours per chapter. Twenty-one chapters at three hours each comes to about sixty hours of total commitment. If you push, you can do one chapter per evening on weekdays and finish in about a month; if you settle, one chapter per week brings you through in five months.

Each chapter follows the same four-section internal structure — _Before You Listen, The Listening Window, What's Happening in the Music, The Stories_ — which you have already met in the _Classical Music_ , Beethoven, and Mozart volumes. The fourth section closes with three short reader-facing pieces: _Conversation Seeds_ (five prompts for a discussion partner or for a Socratopia chat), _Things to Listen For on Re-Listen_ (three or four concrete observables for the second listen), and _Going Deeper_ (one further-listening recommendation and one book or film). You do not have to do all of these. You should at minimum do the first listen and the second listen and read the _Stories_ section before moving on.

The anchor works are listed with their full title in English and Chinese, their key and opus number, their approximate duration, and one to three recommended recordings. Each chapter designates one **Primary Recording** ; all timestamps in §X.2 _The Listening Window_ refer to this single recording, named explicitly in §X.1. If you listen to a different recording the score-structural moments will still apply, but the exact clock times will differ.

The book uses the _recording-hallucination safeguard_ you may know from the Beethoven and Mozart volumes: every recommended recording must be at least ten years old (so 2016 or earlier as of 2026) or must have at least three independently verifiable reviews. This is a defense against the model that wrote this book having confabulated a recording that does not exist; the older recordings (Rubinstein 1960s, Pollini 1972, Cortot 1930s, Rosen 1965, Argerich 1965-onward) are the safe ground.

You will not need the score. You will not need a piano. You will not need any prior music-theory background beyond what you absorbed from _Close Listening: Classical Music_. You will need a recording, a quiet hour, and a willingness to listen twice to the same work.

### 1.7 What you will be able to do at the end

At the end of this volume you should be able to:

* Recognize about fifty specific Chopin works by ear after one careful listen.
* Place any of those works in the four-period framework (Warsaw, early Paris, Sand years, late period) and within the relevant genre arc.
* Hear the early-vs-late difference within the mazurka, the nocturne, the ballade, the scherzo, and the polonaise.
* Identify several of Chopin's compositional signatures across the catalogue (the bel-canto vocal lineage, the harmonic surprise, the tempo rubato, the Polish-folk-modal coloring, the pedal innovation).
* Describe what is documented and what is sentimentalized about the 19th-century reception of Chopin.
* Choose between two or three recordings of the same work on the basis of having heard differences in approach.
* Walk into a Chopin recital with confident anticipation rather than waiting for the next familiar piece.

You will not be able to play any of these works on the piano. You will not be able to follow a score in real time. You will not be a musicologist. You will be an attentive amateur listener with one full composer in your ear.

That is the goal. Chapter 2 begins the listening.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The book opens with the claim that fifty Chopin works is the count at which the word 'catalogue' is earned. Why fifty and not five, and not five hundred? What changes in the listener at the fifty-work mark that does not change at five?"_
* _"Habit 3 (Chopin was delicate, sickly, weak) is one the larger culture has installed. Where did you yourself first encounter the image of Chopin-the-fragile-consumptive-poet, and what specific piece or performance or scene installed it for you? Does naming the source dissolve the image at all?"_
* _"The book treats the George Sand relationship cautiously and refuses either the 'she made him write masterpieces' or the 'she drained him' narrative. What does the cautious version cost the reader in dramatic interest, and what does it gain her in actual understanding?"_
* _"The volume's structural spine is partly chronological and partly genre-by-genre. The four Ballades all live in Chapter 10 even though they span seven years. What is the listening cost of this collapse, and what is the listening gain?"_
* _"Of the six standard entry-point pieces named in Habit 6 — Op. 9 No. 2, Ballade No. 1, the Funeral March, Op. 17 No. 4, the Heroic Polonaise, Op. 10 No. 3 — which have you actually heard? Which would you predict you will most underestimate when the book returns to it?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

This is the orientation chapter; there is no single anchor work and no listening loop to run. Instead, do the following before reading Chapter 2:

  1. **A first sample.** Find a recording of the Nocturne in E♭ major, Op. 9 No. 2 (Arthur Rubinstein on RCA in his 1965 Nocturnes set is the safe ground; Maria João Pires on Deutsche Grammophon from 1996 is the alternate; or any recording you trust). Listen once, casually, with no second pass. Notice only one thing: how singing the right hand sounds, and how regular the left-hand accompaniment is underneath it. Do not analyze; just register the texture.

  2. **A second sample.** If you have a recording of the Ballade No. 1 in G minor, Op. 23 (Krystian Zimerman on DG 1987 is the safe ground; Arthur Rubinstein on RCA 1959 is the alternate), listen to the first ninety seconds only — the slow opening, before the music gets dramatic. Notice the same thing you noticed in the Op. 9 No. 2: a right-hand line that sings, a left-hand pattern underneath. Even at the start of the most-famous Chopin ballade, the texture is the nocturne texture.

  3. **No listening at all.** If you prefer, skip the listening and go directly to Chapter 2, which will run the full four-section loop on the Op. 9 No. 2. The orientation chapter is permitted to end without a listen.

### Going Deeper

**Read** : the preface to Alan Walker, _Fryderyk Chopin: A Life and Times_ (Farrar, Straus and Giroux, 2018). Walker states his goal — "to present Chopin as he was, not as he has been imagined" — in the opening pages, and the preface lays out the documentary base on which the rest of the biography rests. About eight pages. The book itself is roughly 700 pages and not a one-week read, but the preface is the doorway into the modern Chopin scholarship and shows you the posture the present volume is going to inherit.

## Chapter 2: The Listening Basics for Chopin

The Nocturne in E♭ major, Op. 9 No. 2 is the Chopin piece you most likely already know without knowing you know it. It is the wedding-prelude piece, the romantic-film cue, the piano-recital encore. It runs about four minutes; it sits, on the page, well within the reach of an advanced student; it has been recorded several hundred times by major pianists. If you have heard exactly one Chopin nocturne in your life, this is almost certainly it.

This chapter does what the _Close Listening_ series always does on its first anchor work: it puts a familiar piece under closer attention than the larger culture usually grants it, and it uses the close attention to set up the listening pattern for the rest of the book. The anchor is one piece, four minutes long; the chapter around it is long, because the listening pattern it establishes is the one every subsequent chapter will use.

The Chopin-specific listening calibration is slightly different from the Beethoven or Mozart one you absorbed in the Beethoven and Mozart volumes. For Beethoven the listening calibration is: follow a thematic argument through orchestral or chamber forces, attend to the development sections, expect a dramatic struggle that resolves into affirmation. For Mozart the listening calibration is: follow an ensemble dialogue at high speed, attend to harmonic-rhythm density, expect more themes per minute than you can track and accept that the texture is what carries the message. For Chopin the listening calibration is different again: you are listening to a single instrument with two hands, the right hand almost always carries the singing melody, the left hand provides the harmonic and rhythmic support, and the texture-management between the two hands is one of the things Chopin most cared about. Tempo rubato — a controlled push-and-pull of tempo, with the left hand keeping steady time and the right hand floating around it — is a Chopin signature you should be listening for from the first chapter onward.

### 2.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Late 1830 or early 1831. Fryderyk Chopin is twenty years old, recently arrived in Vienna for the second time (his first Vienna stay was August 1829), and very far from home. He had left Warsaw on November 2, 1830, intending what was meant to be a relatively short trip to Vienna and onward to Italy. Eight days after his departure, on November 29, the November Uprising broke out in Warsaw — the Polish national rebellion against Russian rule. Chopin's father Mikołaj wrote letters urging him to stay abroad and not return; the uprising would be crushed by September 1831 with thousands of Polish refugees flowing west. Chopin would never return to Poland. He did not yet know this in late 1830, but he probably suspected it.

In this period of waiting in Vienna — restless, financially uncertain, watching the news from Warsaw — Chopin composed the three nocturnes that would be published in Paris in 1832 as Opus 9, with a dedication to Marie Pleyel, the pianist wife of the piano-maker Camille Pleyel. The second of the three, the one we will listen to, is the Nocturne in E♭ major. He had probably begun it in Warsaw before his departure (Walker notes the composition is dated 1830-31 with some uncertainty about exactly when the work crossed from Warsaw sketches into Vienna refinement); he completed it abroad, in the suspended period between the country he had left and the city — Paris — he would soon adopt.

This biographical setting is not what the music sounds like. The music sounds settled, lyric, unhurried, unagitated. There is no Polish-uprising drama in the Op. 9 No. 2. The composer was twenty years old, his country in revolt, his future uncertain — and he wrote a four-minute song for the piano in E♭ major that has subsequently been used at thousands of weddings. The disjunction is part of why the piece works: Chopin did not believe biographical experience had to dictate musical content. The Polish-revolt year produced this piece and the _Revolutionary_ Etude (Op. 10 No. 12, Chapter 6) and the early mazurkas of Op. 6, side by side. We will return to this.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The opening eight bars.** The piece opens with the right hand alone (after a single chord in the left hand on beat one) singing the main melody — a slow, ornamented line in E♭ major. Within the first eight seconds you should hear that the right-hand line is doing several things at once: stating the melody, decorating the melody with little turns and grace notes, and breathing — small pauses between phrases the way a singer breathes between lines. Listen for the breath. This is the bel-canto vocal lineage we will return to.

  2. **The left hand.** Underneath the right-hand singing, the left hand plays a steady pattern: a low bass note on beat one, then three chords above it at evenly-spaced intervals. The pattern is the _waltz-like accompaniment_ nocturne pattern that Chopin inherited from John Field. In 12/8 meter (twelve eighth-notes per bar grouped as four beats of three eighths each), the left-hand bass-note-plus-three-chords pattern repeats almost continuously through the piece. The right hand floats above it; the left hand keeps time below it. Tempo rubato is the controlled freedom between the two — the right hand may push slightly forward or hold slightly back, but the left hand is your timekeeper. Most listeners do not consciously hear the left hand; learn to do so.

  3. **The cadenza ending.** Near the end of the piece (we will time it in §2.2), the music slows, the texture thins, and the right hand plays an elaborate run of fast notes — a passage that sounds almost improvised, like a cadenza in a concerto. This passage is one of the most-discussed in the Chopin literature because the music's surface here changes in a way that catches the listener: the previously-singing nocturne suddenly becomes virtuosic. The cadenza is not Chopin's invention — Field had used similar ornamental flourishes — but Chopin's specific writing of this passage is one of the moments that lifted the nocturne genre from Field's elegant salon piece to Chopin's concert-art piece. Listen for the moment of change.

#### One-sentence background

Composed 1830-31 (begun in Warsaw, completed in Vienna), published in Paris as Opus 9 No. 2 in 1832 with dedication to Marie Pleyel; about four minutes in 12/8 meter in E♭ major; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Arthur Rubinstein conducting himself on RCA Victor's 1965 stereo Nocturnes set (the complete Chopin Nocturnes recorded in Rome, March 1965, released as a two-LP set), chosen for the warm singing tone and the canonical-mid-20th-century reading.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Chopin Nocturne Op. 9 No. 2 Rubinstein 1965" or "Chopin Nocturne E flat major Op 9 No 2" ZH: "肖邦 降E大调夜曲 作品9之2 鲁宾斯坦" 或 "肖邦 夜曲 Op. 9 No. 2" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years OR ≥3 reviews):

* Maria João Pires on Deutsche Grammophon (1996 complete Nocturnes, Salzburg recording) — a quieter, more restrained reading; the slower-and-softer alternative to the Rubinstein warmth.
* Maurizio Pollini on Deutsche Grammophon (2005 Nocturnes selection) — the modernist-clean Chopin in the nocturne genre; more linear, less rubato, the bel-canto vocal lineage made stricter.

### 2.2 The Listening Window

This section is for your second listen — the one where you know what is coming and can attend to specific moments. Timestamps below refer to the Rubinstein / RCA 1965 Primary Recording named in §2.1. The Op. 9 No. 2 in his reading runs about 4 minutes 28 seconds. If you are listening to a different recording the score-structural moments still apply but the exact clock times will differ; in that case use the score-moment description rather than the timestamp.

**0:00 — The opening bar.** A single low E♭ in the left hand on beat one, then the left hand settles into the bass-note-plus-three-chords pattern; the right hand enters on beat one of bar two with the main melody. The melody is in E♭ major, marked at a slow tempo (the published tempo is Andante; Rubinstein takes it gently slower than that). Notice the immediate establishment of the texture you registered in §2.1: right-hand sings, left-hand supports.

**About 0:18 — The first ornamental turn.** The right hand decorates the second statement of the melody with a small ornamental figure. This is the bel-canto vocal lineage in action: Italian opera singers of the 1820s and 30s would have decorated a melodic line just this way on a repeat. Listen for it.

**About 0:30 — The first phrase ending.** The melody arrives at a cadence (a resting point in the harmony) and the right hand breathes — a small pause before the second phrase begins. This breath is what gives the nocturne its vocal feel. Pianists vary in how long they hold the breath; Rubinstein takes it generously, Pollini more strictly in time.

**0:35-1:05 — The second phrase.** The melody returns, slightly varied, with more ornamentation. The harmony stays largely in E♭ major but moves more freely than in the first phrase. Notice that the second phrase is decorated more elaborately than the first — bel-canto practice would have the singer return with more ornamentation each time.

**About 1:10 — The first modulation, into the relative minor area.** Listen for a single chord that doesn't belong to E♭ major: the harmony slips briefly toward C minor (the relative minor) and back within four or five seconds. The right hand keeps singing; the bass note in the left hand stays steady on the beat; only the chords _above_ the bass have darkened. The cloud passes; the sun returns.

**About 1:30 — The middle section's contrasting material.** A new melodic idea enters — you'll hear it as "different tune now," with the right hand using more notes per beat than before (small fast figures decorating each long melody note). The left-hand pattern continues unchanged, which is what makes this register as a B-section while the underlying texture stays continuous. The Op. 9 No. 2 is not a strict ABA: the original A material returns in elaborated form rather than as a literal restatement.

**About 2:20 — The return of the opening melody, in elaborated form.** Listen for the moment the _original_ tune returns — same shape as the opening, but now the right hand decorates more elaborately on the way through each phrase. If you compare 0:00-0:18 with 2:20-2:38, you can hear the same melody played twice, the second time more ornamented (more notes, more turns, more singerly elaboration). This is exactly the bel-canto convention: the singer ornaments the repeat.

**About 3:30 — The slowing-down before the cadenza.** Listen for the left hand pulling slightly back from the steady pulse it has held for three minutes. Rubinstein takes a perceptible breath here — the bass-note-and-three-chords pattern still arrives, but the spacing between bass notes widens. The right hand drops to the lower part of its register and waits.

**About 3:50 — The cadenza.** Listen for two things specifically. First: the right hand suddenly produces many more notes per second than before — what was a singing melodic line becomes a rapid scale-like flourish, more like a singer's florid passage at the end of an aria. Second: about halfway through this passage (around 4:00 in Rubinstein), the harmony briefly leaves E♭ major — you can hear a moment where the right hand's notes don't fit the home key, before the cadenza loops back and resolves. This twenty-second harmonic departure-and-return is the _harmonic-surprise signature_ in its early form.

**About 4:15 — The final cadence.** The cadenza resolves into the home key; the right hand plays the closing chord, the left hand confirms it. The piece ends quietly in E♭ major.

If your recording's total time differs from Rubinstein's 4:28 by more than fifteen percent, you may be listening to a recording at a noticeably different tempo (Pollini's reading runs closer to 4:00; some 19th-century-style readings stretch past 5:00). The relative positions of the moments above should still hold; recalibrate by ear from the section landmarks.

### 2.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The texture: right-hand sings, left-hand accompanies

The Op. 9 No. 2 is the cleanest single example in the Chopin catalogue of what the literature calls the _bel-canto piano texture_. _Bel canto_ — Italian for "beautiful singing" — is the early-19th-century operatic style associated above all with Vincenzo Bellini (1801-1835) and Gaetano Donizetti (1797-1848). The bel-canto singer was trained for long-breathed singing lines, for elaborate ornamentation on repeats, for evenly-projected tone across a wide range. Chopin expressed his admiration for Bellini repeatedly across his correspondence; he attended Bellini operas in Paris in the early 1830s; he treated Bellini's death in 1835 as a personal loss.

The bel-canto piano texture transfers the singer's role to the right hand of the keyboard. The right hand plays a singing line with the same shape, the same breathing pauses, the same ornamentation as an aria. The left hand plays the role the orchestra plays in an opera — a steady supporting accompaniment that holds the harmony and the beat while the singer (the right hand) floats above. In Op. 9 No. 2 the left-hand pattern is the most regular kind: a low bass note on the first beat of each bar, followed by three chords above it on the remaining beats. The pattern is so regular it can be heard as an oompah-oompah accompaniment — except that the tempo is slow enough that the regularity reads as supportive rather than mechanical.

What Chopin added to the texture that Field had not: more elaborate right-hand ornamentation; richer harmonic motion in the left-hand chords; a tempo flexibility (rubato) that lets the right hand dwell on emotionally significant notes while the left hand keeps time. The combination is so distinctive that pianists trained in the Chopin tradition (the Cortot lineage, the Rubinstein lineage, the Polish-school lineage at the Warsaw Chopin Competition) hold "Chopin rubato" as a specific technical-and-interpretive skill that requires years to develop.

For the listener: the question to ask of any Chopin nocturne is, _Where is the singer breathing?_ If you can locate the breaths in the right-hand line, you have located the bel-canto inheritance. If you cannot, the recording may be playing the line too literally or too in-tempo; try a different recording.

#### Tempo rubato

The Chopin tempo rubato deserves its own paragraph. Rubato (Italian for "stolen") had been a Baroque-and-Classical-era practice — singers and instrumentalists had always taken small liberties with strict tempo for expressive purposes. Chopin's specific rubato was, per Eigeldinger's gathering of his pupils' recollections (Eigeldinger 1986 EN edition): _the left hand keeps strict time; the right hand pushes ahead or holds back around the left hand's beat, but always returns to alignment by the bar's end._ In a Chopin nocturne under correct rubato, the left hand's bass note on beat one is on the click of the metronome; the left hand's chord on beat two is on the click; the right hand's melodic note that nominally aligns with beat one might arrive a tenth-of-a-second early or late, but the right hand is back in alignment with the left by the next downbeat.

This is a different rubato from the orchestral rubato of the late-Romantic tradition (Mahler, late-Romantic Brahms, the Wagner-Bayreuth tradition) where the whole ensemble accelerates and decelerates together. The Chopin rubato is a two-handed contrary tension: the left hand is the metronome, the right hand is the singer. The effect is one of the things that gives Chopin's piano music its breathing, vocal feel.

Listen for the rubato in the Op. 9 No. 2. The left-hand bass notes should land at evenly-spaced intervals; if Rubinstein is doing his work correctly (he is), you can almost set your watch by the left-hand beats. The right-hand line above should not be evenly spaced — it should push and pull around the left-hand grid. The push-and-pull is the rubato. Pollini's reading minimizes the rubato; Cortot's older readings (the 1933 EMI recording, for instance) push the rubato far further than Rubinstein. The range of legitimate Chopin rubato is wide; the constraint that the left hand should keep time underneath it is constant.

#### Form: ABA, varied

The Op. 9 No. 2 is most often described as an ABA form with a coda (a closing section). A is the opening singing-melody section, in E♭ major, lasting about a minute and ten seconds in the Rubinstein recording. B is a contrasting middle section beginning around 1:10, with new melodic material and slightly more chromatic harmony, lasting about a minute. A' (A-prime) is the return of the opening melody in elaborated form, beginning around 2:20, lasting about a minute. The coda is the cadenza-plus-final-cadence section, beginning around 3:30, lasting about a minute.

The structure is loose enough that some analysts call the piece a _strophic_ form (variations on a single melodic idea) rather than a strict ABA. The B section is not a strong contrast — same texture, same key area, same general mood, just different melodic material. The A' return is varied enough that some hearings register it as a continuation of the B material rather than as a literal A-return. The Op. 9 No. 2 is, in this sense, one of the smoothest nocturnes in the catalogue; it does not have the strong contrasting middle section that the later Op. 27 nocturnes or the Op. 48 No. 1 in C minor will have. We will return to those in Chapter 7.

#### The cadenza

The closing cadenza (about 3:50 to 4:15 in the Rubinstein recording) is one of the most-discussed moments in the Chopin pianistic literature. The passage is a written-out ornamental flourish that Chopin notated precisely (the passage is in the published score, not improvised by the performer); pianists vary in how they articulate it (more virtuosic and rapid in Pollini, more lingering and singing in Rubinstein, more elastic in Cortot).

What makes the cadenza distinctive is that the passage's first half stays close to E♭ major and the second half ranges harmonically before resolving back. Charles Rosen in _The Romantic Generation_ notes the cadenza as one of the early examples of Chopin's harmonic adventurousness within a small space — a brief modulation through several keys and back, all in about twenty seconds. The Field nocturnes (which Chopin certainly knew) end with simpler ornamental flourishes that stay in the home key. Chopin's cadenza in Op. 9 No. 2 lets the home key slip briefly before returning, and the effect is to make the final return more resolutely _home_ than it would otherwise feel.

For Thread B of the volume — Chopin's compositional signatures, collected in Chapter 17 — register the cadenza as the first example you have heard of the _harmonic-surprise signature_. The signature appears again in the late nocturnes (Op. 48 No. 1, Op. 62 No. 1), in the late mazurkas (Op. 56 No. 3, Op. 59), and in the late ballade (Op. 52). Chapter 17 §17.2 collects these together. The forward pointer is here: when you hear Chopin's harmonic surprises in later chapters, you should be able to trace them back to the cadenza of Op. 9 No. 2.

#### The dedication

The published Op. 9 carries a dedication to Marie Pleyel (born Marie Moke, 1811-1875), the pianist wife of Camille Pleyel the piano-maker. Marie Pleyel was a notable pianist in her own right (she would later become a touring concert pianist after her separation from Camille); the Pleyels' marriage in 1831 made her a prominent figure in the Paris musical scene. The dedication is partly social-professional (Chopin needed the Pleyel piano-making firm as a business connection) and partly artistic (Marie Pleyel was a serious player who could perform the nocturnes). The dedication appears on the first edition's title page; Chopin did not write a personal letter explaining the choice, but the broader context of the Pleyel relationship from his Paris arrival in late 1831 onward makes the dedication consistent with his early-Paris professional positioning. We will return to the Pleyel relationship in Chapter 19, where the period-instrument question is the focus.

### 2.4 The Stories

#### What this piece is and is not

The Op. 9 No. 2 is the second of the three Op. 9 nocturnes. The first, in B♭ minor (Op. 9 No. 1), is darker, longer, more harmonically restless; some listeners consider it the more interesting of the three. The third, in B major (Op. 9 No. 3), is the most virtuosic and the least known. The Op. 9 No. 2, in the middle, is the one that escaped into the larger culture; the publishing history shows it as the most-printed Chopin work of the 19th century after the Funeral March, the _Heroic_ Polonaise, and a handful of the etudes.

What the Op. 9 No. 2 is not: it is not a deep work. It is a finely-made song for the piano of about four minutes' duration. The Op. 9 No. 1 in B♭ minor is more ambitious harmonically; the Op. 27 No. 2 in D♭ major (1835) is more ambitious texturally; the Op. 48 No. 1 in C minor (1841) is more ambitious dramatically; the Op. 62 No. 1 in B major (1846) is more ambitious in late-Chopin structural innovation. Chapter 7 will return to these later nocturnes and you will hear that the Op. 9 No. 2 is the start of the arc, not the peak.

What the Op. 9 No. 2 does is establish the bel-canto piano texture as a Chopin signature and the harmonic surprise as a Chopin technique, both in their early, almost rudimentary form. The textbook image of "the nocturne" is built more from this piece than from any other; that is why we listen to it first, even though it is not the chapter's deepest available work.

#### The bel-canto context

A short note on what bel-canto opera sounded like in Paris in the early 1830s, which is when Chopin was absorbing it. The decisive Bellini operas Chopin would have heard in Paris are: _Il pirata_ (premiered Milan 1827, performed in Paris in the early 1830s), _La sonnambula_ (premiered Milan 1831, performed in Paris in 1831-32), _Norma_ (premiered Milan 1831, performed in Paris from 1835), and _I puritani_ (premiered Paris 1835 — Bellini composed the opera specifically for the Paris Théâtre-Italien). Bellini was in Paris from 1833 to 1835; Chopin met him; Bellini died in 1835 at age 33, and Chopin attended the funeral with Liszt, Hiller, and other musicians of the Paris circle.

The Bellini operatic line characteristic — long-breathed melodic lines, evenly-projected from low to high register, elaborated with ornamental figures on repeats, designed for the singer to display sustained beauty rather than rapid virtuosity — is what Chopin transcribed to the keyboard. The Op. 9 No. 2 right-hand melody is, in a real sense, a Bellini aria written for the piano: the phrase lengths, the breathing pauses, the ornamentation on the second statement, all map directly onto the Bellini practice. This is not romantic embellishment by the present book; the connection is repeatedly documented across Walker, Samson, Rosen, and Eigeldinger. The pianist Mikuli, one of Chopin's principal pupils, recorded that Chopin urged his students to attend Italian opera as part of their piano study — to listen for how singers breathed, how they ornamented, how they sustained the long line. The Op. 9 No. 2 is the first work in this book where you can hear that injunction directly.

#### Recording history briefly

The Op. 9 No. 2 is one of the most-recorded Chopin works in the catalogue. The major lineages divide as follows.

The Cortot lineage. Alfred Cortot (1877-1962) recorded the Nocturnes multiple times across his career; the EMI 1933 recording is the canonical document. Cortot's reading is the most extreme rubato of the major recordings — the right hand pushes and pulls dramatically around the left hand's grid. Cortot's playing also has audible memory slips and occasional wrong notes; he was the leading French Chopinist of the early 20th century, and the wrong notes are part of the document. The Cortot tradition holds that Chopin is fundamentally a Polish-French composer whose interpretive freedom is the highest virtue. Pupils and pupils-of-pupils of Cortot continue this tradition into the 21st century.

The Rubinstein lineage. Arthur Rubinstein (1887-1982) recorded the Nocturnes three times — in the 1930s (HMV), in 1949-51 (RCA mono), and in 1965 (RCA stereo). The 1965 stereo set is the Primary Recording for this chapter and is the most-played Chopin Nocturnes recording in the catalogue. Rubinstein's reading is warmer, less extreme in rubato than Cortot, more singing in the right-hand line. Rubinstein was Polish-born (Łódź, 1887), trained partly in the Polish-school tradition, and held Chopin as central to his career across seven decades. The Rubinstein tradition holds that Chopin is warm, songful, generous, and that interpretive moderation is the appropriate posture. Rubinstein's three recordings of the Nocturnes — each made in a different decade and with a different sound aesthetic — are themselves a study in how a single pianist's relationship to the music changes across a career.

The Horowitz lineage. Vladimir Horowitz (1903-1989) was the great Russian-virtuoso pianist of the mid-20th century; his Chopin recordings are episodic (he never recorded a complete Nocturnes set) but his individual Chopin readings on RCA in the 1960s and 70s, and his Sony recordings in the 1980s including the 1985 Horowitz at Home and the late Carnegie Hall recordings, are remarkable for technical command and for an interpretive freedom different from Cortot's. The Horowitz Chopin sometimes pushes the music toward an emotional extreme that the Rubinstein tradition would consider mannered, and that's part of the interest.

The Pollini lineage. Maurizio Pollini (1942-2024) won the 1960 Warsaw Chopin Competition (the youngest first-prize winner to that date) and recorded a Chopin discography over six decades with Deutsche Grammophon. Pollini's reading of the Nocturnes is the modernist-clean reading — less rubato than Rubinstein, much less than Cortot, a tighter rhythmic frame, a more linear melodic articulation. The Pollini tradition holds that Chopin's music is more disciplined than the 19th-century interpretive tradition has acknowledged; the rubato should be small, the bel-canto inheritance should be more strict than free.

The Pires lineage. Maria João Pires (born 1944, Portuguese) recorded the complete Nocturnes for DG in 1996 in Salzburg. Pires's reading is the quietest of the major modern Chopin Nocturnes documents — softer dynamics throughout, slower tempos, more inwardness. Pires said in interviews that she approached the Nocturnes as private music rather than concert music, and the recording reflects this. The Pires tradition holds that Chopin's nocturnes are particularly susceptible to over-projection in the concert hall and benefit from a more chamber-music approach.

The Polish-school lineage. Krystian Zimerman, Krystian Bęczała, Janusz Olejniczak, Garrick Ohlsson (American but trained partly in Poland), and the Chopin Competition prize-winners of the past forty years (Dang Thai Son first prize 1980, Stanislav Bunin first prize 1985, Yundi Li first prize 2000, Rafał Blechacz first prize 2005, Yulianna Avdeeva first prize 2010, Seong-Jin Cho first prize 2015, Bruce Liu first prize 2021) form the Polish-school-influenced lineage. Each has his or her own approach, but the shared inheritance is what is broadly identified in the Polish-school discourse as a hearing of the music in close contact with the Polish-folk-music roots, especially in the mazurkas. The Chopin Competition has been the institution that has carried this lineage forward through every five-year cycle since 1927.

For the Op. 9 No. 2, the safe ground recordings (≥10 years old or ≥3 reviews) are Rubinstein 1965, Pires 1996, and Pollini 2005. The Cortot 1933 is a historical document worth hearing but the sound is mid-1930s acoustic-electric and the reading is the most idiosyncratic. The Horowitz Chopin readings are not on a complete Nocturnes set but individual readings of the Op. 9 No. 2 can be found in the live and studio recordings.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The Op. 9 No. 2 connects forward to many later pieces in this volume.

To the Op. 9 No. 1 in B♭ minor (Chapter 5). Same opus, different mood. The Op. 9 No. 1 has a more restless A section, a more harmonically adventurous B section, and a longer overall span. If you find Op. 9 No. 2 too settled, the Op. 9 No. 1 is the immediate next step. The contrast within the published Op. 9 is itself a Chopin lesson — he did not write the three nocturnes as a unified set, the way he wrote the 24 Preludes Op. 28 as a cycle; he wrote three distinct pieces and published them together.

To the Op. 27 nocturnes (Chapter 7). The Op. 27 No. 2 in D♭ major (1835) takes the bel-canto piano texture from Op. 9 No. 2 and pushes the harmonic ambition further. The B section of Op. 27 No. 2 modulates more adventurously; the cadenza-like ornamental passages are longer and more elaborate. Op. 27 No. 1 in C♯ minor, by contrast, is the dark counterpart — a brooding piece quite unlike Op. 9 No. 2.

To the Op. 48 No. 1 in C minor (Chapter 7). The Op. 48 No. 1 (1841) is the late-style nocturne that pushes the genre toward something larger and more dramatic. The serene singing of Op. 9 No. 2 is replaced by a darker, slower main material; the B section is a chorale-like passage that builds to a climactic forte; the return of the A material is transformed by what came before. If Op. 9 No. 2 is the textbook nocturne, Op. 48 No. 1 is the nocturne pushing beyond its genre.

To the bel-canto piano texture across the catalogue. The right-hand-sings-left-hand-accompanies texture you learned in Op. 9 No. 2 will return in the slow movements of the two piano concertos (Chapter 3), in the slow B sections of the scherzos (Chapter 11), in the central trio of the Berceuse Op. 57 (Chapter 15), and in the Larghetto of the Cello Sonata Op. 65 (Chapter 12). Wherever you hear that texture, it is the Op. 9 No. 2 lineage being deployed in a new context.

Chapter 17 §17.1 collects all the bel-canto piano texture examples into one synthesis. The forward pointer is here: you should remember the Op. 9 No. 2 as the textbook example whenever the texture returns.

#### The biographical context: late 1830 to late 1831

A brief note for the biographical thread (Thread A). Late 1830 finds Chopin in Vienna, having left Warsaw on November 2. The November Uprising broke out in Warsaw on November 29; news reached Chopin within days. He stayed in Vienna through the spring and summer of 1831, performed in concerts (with limited success — the Vienna public's taste was for Italian opera and Strauss waltzes, not for a twenty-year-old Polish composer's piano works), and traveled onward to Paris by way of Munich, Stuttgart (where he received the news of Warsaw's September 8 fall to Russian forces — the popular story attaches the Op. 10 No. 12 Etude to this event, but the attribution traces to Niecks 1888 rather than to Chopin's letters and is treated cautiously by modern scholarship), and Strasbourg. He arrived in Paris in late September or early October 1831 (the exact date is uncertain; Walker gives "by mid-October").

The Op. 9 No. 2 was completed in this travel period. The dedication to Marie Pleyel was added later (probably late 1831 or early 1832 after Chopin had connected with the Pleyel firm in Paris). The first edition was published in 1832 by three different publishers in three different European cities (Schlesinger in Berlin, Pleyel in Paris, Wessel in London) — the practice of triple simultaneous publication was a Chopin standard from his early Paris period onward, designed to secure copyright across multiple jurisdictions where international copyright treaties did not yet exist.

The biographical reading of the piece is restrained. The Op. 9 No. 2 is not a Polish-uprising response (the _Revolutionary_ Etude Op. 10 No. 12 is more easily readable in that direction, though even there the documentation is thin); it is not a Marie Pleyel love piece (the dedication is largely professional); it is not an evocation of the Vienna of late 1830. It is a four-minute song for the piano in E♭ major, composed by a twenty-year-old Polish musician in the middle of a transcontinental journey, in the year his country rose in revolt against Russian rule. The disjunction between biography and music is the lesson; Chopin will repeat the lesson across his career.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The Chopin tempo rubato is described as 'left hand keeps time, right hand floats around it.' Listen to the Rubinstein 1965 reading and the Pollini 2005 reading of Op. 9 No. 2 side by side and describe specifically how each pianist's rubato differs. Which feels closer to the bel-canto singing model?"_
* _"The chapter argues that the Op. 9 No. 2 is the start of the nocturne arc, not the peak — the later Op. 27 No. 2 (1835), Op. 48 No. 1 (1841), and Op. 62 No. 1 (1846) push the genre further. Listen to the Op. 9 No. 2 one more time with the question: what specifically about this piece feels like a _beginning_ — what is unfinished about the nocturne idea here that subsequent works might develop?"_
* _"The closing cadenza (about 3:50 to 4:15 in the Rubinstein recording) is one of the moments that lifted the nocturne from Field's salon piece to Chopin's concert piece. What does the cadenza do that the rest of the piece does not? Why does the listener hear it as a turning point even on a first listen?"_
* _"The Op. 9 No. 2 was composed in 1830-31, during the year Poland rose against Russian rule and was crushed, and during Chopin's permanent departure from his country. The music does not sound like any of that. What does this disjunction between biography and music suggest about how Chopin worked, and what does it cost or gain the listener to know the biographical context anyway?"_
* _"The Bellini bel-canto vocal lineage is one of Chopin's principal compositional inputs. If you have heard a Bellini aria — 'Casta diva' from Norma (1831), 'Ah, non credea mirarti' from La sonnambula (1831), 'Ah! per sempre io ti perdei' from I puritani (1835) — try to describe what the Bellini aria and the Op. 9 No. 2 right-hand melody share. Is the relationship more rhythmic, more melodic, more harmonic, or more in the ornamentation?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Arthur Rubinstein / RCA 1965 Nocturnes recording (or one of the alternatives listed in §2.1). Listen to the Op. 9 No. 2 a second time and attend to these four concrete moments:

  1. **The opening eight bars (0:00-0:30).** Confirm: the right hand sings a slow ornamented melody in E♭ major; the left hand plays a low bass note on beat one, then three chords above it. Notice the first phrase ending around 0:30 — the right hand breathes, like a singer between lines.

  2. **The first modulation (around 1:10).** The music briefly leaves E♭ major and touches the relative minor area (C minor) before returning. Listen for the moment of the harmonic shift — the music does not get louder or faster, just darker for a few seconds.

  3. **The cadenza (around 3:50-4:15).** The right hand plays a fast ornamental run that ranges harmonically before resolving back to E♭ major. Listen for the specific moment where the cadenza moves furthest from the home key — there is a passage where the right hand's notes do not belong to E♭ major; that is the harmonic-surprise signature of Chopin in its early form.

  4. **The two left-hand patterns.** Listen _only_ to the left hand for one full hearing if you can manage to filter the right hand out. The left hand's pattern repeats almost identically through the piece — bass note on beat one, three chords above. The only variation is in the harmonies the chords spell out and in the brief moments where the pattern is interrupted (near the cadenza, near the final cadence). This is the tempo-rubato grid: the left hand is your metronome, the right hand floats above it. Trying to hear only the left hand for one listen is unusual and worth doing once.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair the Op. 9 No. 2 with a Bellini aria from _La sonnambula_ (1831) — "Ah, non credea mirarti" from Act II is the standard choice, available in recordings by Maria Callas (the 1957 EMI La Scala recording is the canonical document), Joan Sutherland (the 1962 Decca recording), or any major lyric soprano. Listen to the aria once. Then listen to the Op. 9 No. 2 again. The bel-canto vocal lineage should become audible in the right-hand line in a way that hearing the nocturne alone does not provide. The exercise takes about ten minutes total and is the most direct way to bring the chapter's central claim into your ear.

**Read** : Jean-Jacques Eigeldinger, _Chopin: Pianist and Teacher as Seen by His Pupils_ (Cambridge University Press, 1986 English-language edition; original French 1970). The chapter on Chopin's tempo rubato is the primary scholarly source for the "left hand keeps time, right hand floats" description used in this chapter; Eigeldinger gathers the recollections of Chopin's pupils (Mikuli, Tellefsen, Mathias, others) into a documentary record that is the closest we have to Chopin's own teaching. About 200 pages total in the English edition; the rubato chapter and the bel-canto chapter together run about thirty pages and are the most directly relevant to what we have just been listening to.

* * *

---

## Chapter 3: Warsaw 1810-1830 — the Polish Childhood

On November 2, 1830, Fryderyk Chopin left Warsaw with his friend Tytus Woyciechowski, bound for Vienna and then Italy. He was twenty years old. He had a trunk of compositions, a recommendation letter from Józef Elsner, and the savings his family had collected for what was supposed to be a year-or-two journey to establish himself professionally before returning to Warsaw. Eight days later, the November Uprising broke out in Warsaw — the Polish national rebellion against Russian rule. Tytus turned back to fight; Chopin continued west. He would never return to Warsaw, would never see his parents or his sisters in person again, would die in Paris nineteen years later still carrying Polish citizenship that was technically meaningless because Poland did not exist as a state.

The November 2 departure date is the hinge of Chopin's life. Everything before is the Warsaw childhood and youth: born in Żelazowa Wola in March 1810, raised in Warsaw from 1810 onward, educated at the Warsaw Lyceum and the Warsaw Conservatory, formed in the Polish-musical and Polish-political environment of the Congress Kingdom. Everything after is the Paris life: arrived in late September or early October 1831, established as a working composer and teacher by 1832-33, partnered with George Sand from 1838 to 1847, died on October 17, 1849. This chapter is about the before.

The chapter anchors on two slow movements from the two piano concertos Chopin wrote at the end of his Warsaw period: the Larghetto from the Piano Concerto No. 2 in F minor Op. 21 (composed 1829-30), and the Romance from the Piano Concerto No. 1 in E minor Op. 11 (composed 1830). The numbering is misleading — Op. 21 was composed first; Op. 11 was published first; the publication order is what gave the F minor the "No. 2" label and the E minor the "No. 1." We will mostly use opus numbers to avoid the confusion. Both concertos belong to the late-Warsaw period; both are discussed in Chopin's letters; both contain music that is the most directly biographical writing in his catalogue.

### 3.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

October 3, 1829. Fryderyk Chopin, nineteen years old, sits down to write a letter to Tytus Woyciechowski, his closest friend, who is at his country estate. The letter is in Polish (most of Chopin's surviving correspondence is in Polish, with French letters increasing only after 1831). Chopin is in Warsaw; he has just begun composing what will become the F minor Piano Concerto Op. 21. He is in love with a fellow Warsaw Conservatory student, the soprano Konstancja Gładkowska, and he has not told her. The letter reads (the translation is from the English-language selections of the Sydow correspondence edition, available in the Hedley and the more recent English selections):

> "I have — perhaps for my misfortune — found my own ideal, whom I have served faithfully, though without saying a word, for half a year; whom I dream of, who inspired the Adagio of my Concerto, and who this morning gave the idea to the little Waltz which I send you."

The "ideal" is Konstancja Gładkowska. The "Adagio of my Concerto" is the slow movement that became the Larghetto of Op. 21. The "little Waltz" is one of the early waltzes Chopin had been composing — exactly which is uncertain (the Op. 70 No. 3 in D♭ from 1829 is the most commonly suggested but the documentation is not definitive).

This letter is one of the cleanest single pieces of biographical documentation Chopin left us. He names the Larghetto specifically as inspired by an unnamed woman he has not spoken to about his feelings. He is nineteen years old and writing to his closest male friend with the candor that young men of that era reserved for such friendships. He notes the disjunction between the dream-world inspiration and the practical-world silence ("served faithfully, though without saying a word"). And he ties a specific piece of music to a specific extra-musical source, which he rarely did in his later career.

The Larghetto, then, is one of the few Chopin works for which the biographical context comes directly from Chopin's own letter. Most of his slow movements have no such documentation. When you listen to the Larghetto of Op. 21, you are listening to a piece the composer himself identified as a love letter to a particular woman, written when he was nineteen, in Warsaw, in the months before he left for what would turn out to be the rest of his life.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The opening orchestral passage.** The Larghetto begins with about thirty seconds of orchestra alone, in A♭ major (the relative major of the concerto's F-minor home key). The orchestra states a quiet, lyric main idea. The orchestral writing is modest — Chopin was not deeply interested in orchestral color and used the orchestra mostly as a frame for the piano. Listen, but do not expect orchestral subtlety; the music is waiting for the piano.

  2. **The piano entry.** When the piano enters, the right hand takes the singing line; the orchestra retreats to accompaniment. The bel-canto piano texture you met in Op. 9 No. 2 returns. The Larghetto is a four-or-five minute song for the piano with orchestra in the background. The right-hand singing is highly ornamented from the first phrase; bel-canto practice is again the model.

  3. **The middle section's recitative.** About two-thirds of the way through the movement, the music shifts character. The piano plays a passage that sounds almost spoken — irregular rhythms, dramatic pauses, the right hand declaiming over a sparse left-hand support. This is a _recitative_ -like passage, modeled on the recitatives between arias in Italian opera. Chopin expressed his admiration for this device; it returns elsewhere in his concertos and in some chamber-like passages. Listen for the moment when the singing changes to speaking.

#### One-sentence background

Composed 1829-30 in Warsaw; premiered March 17, 1830 with Chopin as soloist at the National Theatre, Warsaw; published 1836 in Paris as Op. 21 (the publication delay accounts for the "No. 2" designation despite the earlier composition); the Larghetto runs about 9 minutes; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Krystian Zimerman as soloist and conductor with the Polish Festival Orchestra on Deutsche Grammophon (recorded 1999, released 2000), chosen for the Polish-school depth and Zimerman's self-conducted control of the orchestra-piano balance that has historically been a problem in Chopin concerto recordings.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Chopin Piano Concerto 2 F minor Op 21 Larghetto Zimerman" or "Chopin F minor Concerto Op 21" ZH: "肖邦 第二钢琴协奏曲 f小调 作品21 齐默尔曼" 或 "肖邦 f小调钢琴协奏曲" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** :

* Arthur Rubinstein with Stanisław Skrowaczewski conducting the New Symphony Orchestra of London (RCA, 1968) — the canonical mid-century reading, warm and singing.
* Maurizio Pollini with Paul Kletzki conducting the Philharmonia Orchestra (EMI, 1960) — recorded just after Pollini's Chopin Competition victory; the youthful-Pollini reading.

### 3.2 The Listening Window

Timestamps refer to the Zimerman / Polish Festival Orchestra 1999 Primary Recording. The Larghetto in his reading runs about 9 minutes 36 seconds.

**0:00 — Orchestral introduction.** The orchestra states the main theme quietly in A♭ major. Strings carry the melody, with woodwind responses. Notice the modesty of the orchestral writing — no large gestures, no symphonic ambition; the texture is light and waits for the piano.

**About 0:30 — Piano entry.** The piano takes the main theme. The right hand sings the melody in elaborated form; the orchestra accompanies. The bel-canto texture from Op. 9 No. 2 returns at the larger scale of the concerto movement.

**About 1:00 — The first ornamental flourish.** The right-hand line decorates the melody with ornamental runs. Notice that Chopin at nineteen is already writing the ornamental decoration as part of the melodic statement, not as a separate cadenza-like passage. The bel-canto inheritance is integrated into the line.

**About 2:00 — The second-theme group.** The music moves to a contrasting melodic idea, still in A♭ major. The texture remains piano-led with light orchestral accompaniment. Listen for the harmonic motion — the second theme moves through related keys before settling back into A♭.

**About 3:30 — A return to the opening theme.** The first theme returns, now varied with more ornamentation and a slightly different harmonization. The orchestra's role remains supportive.

**About 5:00 — The recitative passage.** The character changes. The piano plays a more declamatory line — irregular rhythms, dramatic pauses, sparse left-hand support. The orchestra largely drops out. This is the _recitative_ -like passage modeled on Italian operatic recitative; in the concerto context it functions as a moment of dramatic interiority. The passage is about a minute and a half long in Zimerman's reading.

**About 6:30 — The return after the recitative.** The lyric singing returns; the orchestra re-enters; the main theme is heard once more in elaborated form.

**About 8:00 — The closing pages.** The music begins to settle. The right hand's ornamentation becomes less elaborate; the harmonic motion narrows back to A♭ major; the orchestra confirms the home key.

**About 9:20 — The final cadence.** The piano and orchestra reach the final chord in A♭ major. The Larghetto ends quietly, without dramatic emphasis. The next movement (the Allegro vivace finale, in F minor / F major) follows in performance, but the Larghetto on its own is the chapter's anchor; you may end your listen here.

### 3.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The piano-with-orchestra texture

Chopin's two piano concertos belong to a particular genre of early-19th-century concerto writing in which the piano dominates and the orchestra accompanies. The model is broadly Hummel, Field, Kalkbrenner, and the Italian-operatic vocal concerto — not Mozart, where orchestra and piano are genuinely conversational, and not Beethoven, where the orchestra carries serious thematic weight. The Op. 21 Larghetto follows this model directly: the orchestra plays an introductory ritornello, the piano takes over with elaborated melodic writing, the orchestra accompanies through the movement with occasional textural participation, and the piano returns to the foreground for the closing passages.

The texture is not symphonic; it is bel-canto piano with chamber-orchestra support. The reason matters for the listener: do not expect to hear the orchestra do significant musical work, and do not blame Chopin for not making it do so. The Larghetto is a piano song with backing; the backing is competent and unobtrusive, which is what the genre asks of it.

#### The recitative passage

The recitative-like passage in the middle of the Larghetto (about 5:00 to 6:30 in the Zimerman recording) is the single most distinctive moment in the movement. The shift from lyric singing to declamatory speech is unusual in a slow concerto movement; Chopin had heard the device in Italian opera (recitative is the term for the spoken-or-half-spoken passages between arias in opera, which carry plot action and dialogue while the arias carry emotional climaxes), and he transferred it to the keyboard.

What the recitative does, structurally: it interrupts the singing texture, gives the movement a dramatic interiority, and creates the conditions for the return of the singing material to land with more weight. The listener should hear the recitative as a _because-of-which_ device — _because_ the singing was interrupted, _therefore_ the singing's return is more meaningful.
