Recitative-like passages return elsewhere in Chopin: in the Polonaise-Fantaisie Op. 61 (Chapter 14), in some of the late ballades (Chapter 10), in moments of the Sonata Op. 35 (Chapter 12), and in the Op. 28 Preludes. The Larghetto of Op. 21 is the early example; later examples build on it.

#### Why F minor

The Op. 21 concerto is in F minor — the home key. This is the only Chopin concerto in a minor home key. The Op. 11 (E minor) is the other piano concerto, also in a minor home key (E minor). The choice of minor home key for both concertos is part of the early-Chopin pattern of associating minor keys with serious or interior emotional material. By his Paris period the major-key works become more numerous and the minor-key choices are reserved for specific dramatic statements.

The Larghetto, however, is in A♭ major (the relative major of F minor) — a brighter key by relation. Chopin's slow-movement-in-relative-major choice is conventional for a minor-key concerto of the 1820s. The contrast between the F-minor outer movements and the A♭-major slow movement gives the work its emotional architecture: dramatic and tense in the outer movements, lyric and consoling in the middle.

The "love letter" association Chopin made in the October 3, 1829 letter to Tytus is with the slow movement specifically, not with the concerto as a whole. The Larghetto is the consoling middle of the work; the F-minor outer movements carry other affects. The biographical reading is therefore quite specific: the slow movement is the Konstancja Gładkowska piece; the rest of the concerto is not.

#### Konstancja Gładkowska

A short note. Konstancja Gładkowska (1810-1889) was a fellow Warsaw Conservatory student of Chopin's, a soprano. They were the same age. Chopin's infatuation lasted from at least early 1829 (when the letter quoted in §3.1 was written) through 1830 and possibly into 1831. He never declared his feelings; she may not have known of them; she married Józef Grabowski in 1832 and lived an ordinary life through the 19th century. After Chopin's death she was interviewed for biographical purposes by Niecks and others, and she confirmed that Chopin had been a fellow student and that she had been aware of his presence but did not say more about romantic content.

The Larghetto stands as a recorded testimonial of a one-sided teenage romantic feeling. Modern scholarship (Walker, especially) treats it with appropriate restraint: the music does not require the biographical context to work, and the biographical context does not justify melodramatic reading of the music. The Larghetto is a beautiful slow movement that the composer himself happened to name as connected to a particular feeling; later listeners can take the connection or leave it.

#### The Op. 11 Romance (a brief companion)

The second movement of the Op. 11 concerto (E minor concerto, composed 1830), titled _Romance: Larghetto_ , is a companion piece to the Op. 21 Larghetto. It is in E major (the parallel major of the home E minor — a Chopin-unusual choice, since the relative major G major is the conventional move). It runs about ten or eleven minutes. The texture is the same bel-canto piano with orchestra. The mood is more elegiac than the Op. 21 Larghetto — slower, more inward, more sustained.

Chopin's October 15, 1830 letter to Tytus (Sydow correspondence, Hedley English selections) describes the Romance:

> "It is not meant to make a strong impression. It is rather a romance, quiet, melancholy, giving the impression of someone looking gently towards a spot which calls up in his mind a thousand happy memories. It is a kind of reverie in the moonlight on a beautiful spring evening."

This is the closest Chopin came to a programmatic description of one of his slow movements. The "thousand happy memories" reading is consistent with the late-Warsaw period: Chopin was about to leave the city of his childhood; the music looks back at it with composure. The Op. 11 Romance and the Op. 21 Larghetto together form the late-Warsaw slow-movement pair.

The chapter's primary anchor is the Op. 21 Larghetto; if you have additional listening time, the Op. 11 Romance is the natural companion. Both are usually included in the same Chopin concertos recordings, so the practical step is straightforward.

### 3.4 The Stories

#### The Warsaw of 1810-1830

A short setting. The Warsaw of Chopin's childhood was the capital of the Congress Kingdom of Poland — a political entity created at the Congress of Vienna in 1815 as a quasi-state under the Russian tsar (who held the title "King of Poland"). The Congress Kingdom had its own constitution, currency, and language officially, but in practice Russian authority dominated. The 1830 November Uprising was the inevitable consequence of accumulated grievances; it was crushed by September 1831 and the constitutional autonomy was abolished. From 1831 until 1918, Warsaw was effectively Russian-administered.

In the period of Chopin's growing up (1815-1830), the city had about 130,000 inhabitants, a functioning theater, a conservatory, a university, several newspapers, and a robust music life. The Mozart works in the 1810s and 20s were in active performance; the Beethoven symphonies were beginning to be known; the Italian opera tradition was the dominant operatic culture. Chopin's musical environment was therefore broadly comparable to the cultural environments of Vienna and Berlin, with a distinct Polish overlay and a heavier Italian-opera presence than further west.

#### The Chopin family

Mikołaj Chopin (1771-1844), the father, was French-born — from Marainville, Lorraine. He emigrated to Poland in 1787 as a young man and built his life there. He was a tutor in aristocratic households (the Skarbek family at Żelazowa Wola, where Fryderyk was born), then a teacher of French at the Warsaw Lyceum and other Warsaw schools. He spoke French as his native language and Polish as his adopted language. His correspondence with his son after 1830 is in French.

Justyna Krzyżanowska (1782-1861), the mother, was Polish-born. She married Mikołaj in 1806. She was a pianist and singer at the Skarbek household where she met Mikołaj. The Chopin children — three sisters and Fryderyk, the only son — grew up in a bilingual French-Polish household, with their mother contributing the Polish-musical environment and their father contributing the French-cultural-and-intellectual environment.

The three sisters: Ludwika (1807-1855), the eldest, was the closest to Fryderyk and would become the principal correspondent across his Paris years; she would also visit him in Paris in his final months in 1849 and would bring his heart back to Warsaw after his death. Izabela (1811-1881) and Emilia (1812-1827) were the younger sisters; Emilia died of tuberculosis at fourteen, and the family knowledge of tuberculosis as a household disease begins from her death.

The household was middle-class by Warsaw standards: educated, multilingual, musically literate, modest in income, central to the cultural life of the city through Mikołaj's teaching connections and Justyna's musical activities. Chopin grew up surrounded by adults who took music seriously and by sisters who shared in his musical education.

#### The Elsner lessons

Józef Elsner (1769-1854) was the director of the Warsaw Conservatory from its founding in 1821 to its closure in 1831 (the conservatory was suspended after the November Uprising). He had been a composer and teacher in the German tradition — trained in Vienna, an admirer of Mozart and Haydn, with a strong sense of formal architecture. He took Chopin as a private pupil from about 1822 (when Chopin was twelve) and formalized the conservatory enrollment in 1826 (when Chopin was sixteen). Elsner supervised Chopin's composition lessons for three years until Chopin's graduation in 1829.

Elsner's role in Chopin's formation was central but not directive. He recognized Chopin's gifts early; the surviving teaching evaluations describe Chopin as a student of "extraordinary capabilities" and "musical genius." Elsner did not push Chopin into the conventional symphonic-and-operatic compositional path; he allowed Chopin to develop the piano-centered direction that became his life. The famous Elsner phrase from Chopin's 1829 graduation evaluation — "Chopin, Frédéric, three years of theory of composition, third year — musical genius" — is the kind of teacher's testimony that has survived because it predicted what subsequently happened.

After Chopin left Warsaw, Elsner continued teaching in Warsaw through 1854. The two corresponded but did not meet again. Elsner's role in the Chopin biography is therefore confined to the Warsaw years; he is the teacher who recognized the student and let him develop.

#### The early publications and the late-Warsaw output

By the time of his November 2, 1830 departure, Chopin had completed roughly the following works:

* Two piano concertos (Op. 11 in E minor, Op. 21 in F minor)
* The Variations on Mozart's "Là ci darem la mano," Op. 2 (1827)
* The Polonaise in G♭ major, Op. 71 No. 2 (composed about 1828, published posthumously)
* Several early waltzes (now grouped as Op. 70 and others)
* The Rondo à la mazur, Op. 5 (1826)
* The Krakowiak concerto rondo, Op. 14 (1828)
* About a dozen mazurkas (some published as Op. 6 and Op. 7 after he reached Paris; some held back)
* About a dozen songs (Polish-language; mostly published posthumously)
* A handful of trio and chamber-music sketches

The output is substantial for a twenty-year-old — particularly impressive given that almost everything important is for solo piano or piano-and-orchestra. Chopin had decided by 1828 or so that his career would be the piano; the chamber and orchestral sketches that he attempted in the late Warsaw years are not central. His mature genre concentration was already established.

The early Vienna concerts of August 1829 — two public concerts at the Kärntnertortheater — were moderately successful. Chopin played the _Là ci darem la mano_ Variations and the Krakowiak Rondo. The Vienna critics treated him as a curiosity (a Polish pianist) rather than as a major figure. He returned to Warsaw in September 1829 and continued composing. The plan for the longer Western journey took shape through 1830.

#### The November 2, 1830 departure

The contemporary account: Chopin and Tytus Woyciechowski left Warsaw on November 2, 1830, by stagecoach, traveling first to Kalisz and then to the Austrian border. At the border, Polish friends — including Elsner — met them with a silver cup containing Polish earth, which was presented to Chopin as a keepsake. The cup remained in Chopin's possession through his life; it is now displayed in Polish museums. (Some 19th-century biographies romanticize this scene; the established core — a meeting at the border, a presentation of Polish earth, an emotional farewell — is reasonably secure.)

From Kalisz they continued to Vienna, arriving in late November. The November Uprising began in Warsaw on November 29, 1830. Chopin received news of the uprising in early December. He did not return. The reasoning that emerges from his letters to his parents in 1830-31 is complex: he believed his role was to continue his musical career abroad rather than to take up arms (he had no military training and was not in good health), and his parents — particularly Mikołaj, the practical Frenchman — supported this view in their letters back. Tytus, however, did return to Warsaw to fight; he survived the uprising and remained in Polish lands. The Chopin-Tytus correspondence continued through Chopin's life.

Chopin remained in Vienna through 1830-31, performed in occasional concerts (with limited success), and traveled onward to Paris in late summer 1831, arriving in late September or early October. The Stuttgart stop on the route (where he received news of Warsaw's September 8, 1831 fall to Russian forces — Walker dates the news arrival to early-to-mid September 1831, by which time Chopin was likely in Stuttgart or Strasbourg) is the traditional setting for the popular _Revolutionary_ Etude story attached to Op. 10 No. 12; the attribution traces to Niecks 1888 rather than to Chopin's own letters, and is best treated cautiously.

#### Reading the two slow movements

The two slow movements anchored in this chapter — the Op. 21 Larghetto in A♭ major and the Op. 11 Romance in E major — are the late-Warsaw lyric voice. Both are bel-canto piano with orchestral accompaniment. Both are oriented toward looking gently at something that is being left behind. The Op. 21 was explicitly named by Chopin as a piece for an unspoken love (Konstancja Gładkowska); the Op. 11 Romance was described as "a kind of reverie in the moonlight on a beautiful spring evening."

The biographical reading is that Chopin in the late Warsaw period — eighteen, nineteen, twenty years old, in love silently with a fellow student, about to leave his city, his family, and his country — wrote the slow movements that the larger culture has subsequently treated as some of his most direct emotional statements. The reading is reasonable and the documentation is secure. What we should not do is project the same biographical reading backward onto every Chopin slow movement; most are not directly attested in this way, and most do not need to be.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The Op. 21 Larghetto and the Op. 11 Romance connect forward in several directions.

To the bel-canto piano texture across the Paris years. The right-hand-sings-left-hand-accompanies-with-orchestra texture you hear here returns in the slow B sections of the scherzos (Chapter 11), in the slow middle of the Berceuse Op. 57 (Chapter 15), and in the Larghetto of the Cello Sonata Op. 65 (Chapter 12). The Op. 9 No. 2 Nocturne from Chapter 2 is the smaller-scale solo-piano version of the same texture.

To the recitative passages across the catalogue. The recitative-like middle of the Op. 21 Larghetto (around 5:00-6:30) returns in the Polonaise-Fantaisie Op. 61 (Chapter 14), in moments of the Ballade No. 4 Op. 52 (Chapter 10), and in the Sonata Op. 35 (Chapter 12). Wherever you hear the singing-line interrupted by a more declamatory passage, the Op. 21 Larghetto is the early model.

To Konstancja Gładkowska herself. The biographical figure does not return — she is not the subject of any other Chopin work, although some 19th-century biographies tried to make her so. The Op. 21 Larghetto stands as her single memorial in Chopin's catalogue.

#### Recording history briefly

The Chopin concertos pose a specific recording problem: the orchestral writing is modest and easy to under-project or over-project, and the balance between piano and orchestra is difficult to manage on disc. The recording history has many candidates and few uncontroversial choices.

The Rubinstein / Skrowaczewski 1968 RCA recording is the canonical mid-20th-century reading. Skrowaczewski (Polish-born conductor, 1923-2017) supports Rubinstein's warm singing with a sensitive orchestral frame; the recording's main weakness is some orchestral thinness in the louder passages but the slow movements work well.

The Pollini / Kletzki 1960 EMI recording is the youthful-Pollini document — recorded shortly after Pollini's Chopin Competition first prize in 1960. Pollini's reading is cleaner and more linear than Rubinstein's; the orchestra is the older Philharmonia under Kletzki, with a slightly dated sound.

The Zimerman / Polish Festival Orchestra 1999 DG recording (the Primary Recording for this chapter) is Zimerman as both soloist and conductor, with an orchestra he assembled and rehearsed specifically for the recording project. The orchestra-piano balance is exceptional; Zimerman's authority over the rehearsal process eliminates the conventional concertos-recording problem of an under-rehearsed orchestra. The reading is slower than Rubinstein's and more weighted; some listeners prefer the Rubinstein for its warmth and find Zimerman's gravity excessive. Both readings are legitimate.

The Argerich Op. 21 readings exist in multiple live recordings; her concert performances of the F minor have an electricity that the studio recordings of others do not match, but the documentation is uneven. Her 1968 commercial recording on EMI with Charles Dutoit conducting the Montreal Symphony is one document; live recordings with various orchestras are available. The Argerich Chopin concertos are an acquired taste — extreme dynamic range, extreme rubato — but they are real listening experiences.

#### The biographical context: Warsaw 1829-1830

Place this in the 39-year arc. The two slow movements anchored in this chapter were composed in Chopin's last full year in Warsaw, October 1829 to roughly mid-1830. He was nineteen and turning twenty. He had completed his Conservatory education in July 1829. The two concertos are the major late-Warsaw works; the rest of the period contains the early mazurkas (Op. 6, the published Op. 7 and Op. 17 cycles drafted partly in Warsaw, the Op. 24 cycle drafted partly in Paris), the early polonaises (Op. 26 was begun in Warsaw), the early waltzes, and the early nocturnes (Op. 9 began in Warsaw and was completed in Vienna).

The departure of November 2, 1830 was the end of this period. The Vienna months of late 1830 through summer 1831 were a transition; the Paris arrival in late September or early October 1831 was the beginning of the next period. Chapter 4 covers the mazurka launch — the genre Chopin would compose in across his whole life — and Chapter 5 covers the Paris arrival.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"Chopin's October 3, 1829 letter to Tytus is one of the cleanest direct connections between a specific feeling (love for Konstancja Gładkowska) and a specific piece (the Larghetto of Op. 21). What does the documentation add to your listening of the Larghetto, and what would the music lose or gain if you did not know about Konstancja at all?"_
* _"The recitative passage in the middle of the Larghetto (around 5:00-6:30 in the Zimerman recording) is described as modeled on Italian operatic recitative. What does the recitative interrupt in the Larghetto, and what does the singing material's return after the recitative gain or lose by having been interrupted?"_
* _"Chopin chose to leave Warsaw on November 2, 1830, eight days before the November Uprising began. His friend Tytus turned back to fight; Chopin continued west. He never returned. What does this choice tell you about how the young Chopin understood his role, and how would the Chopin we listen to be different if he had stayed and fought?"_
* _"The Op. 21 was composed before the Op. 11 but published later, giving the F minor the 'No. 2' designation and the E minor the 'No. 1.' How much does the numbering matter, and what does it say about how 19th-century publication shaped subsequent listening?"_
* _"The orchestral writing in Chopin's concertos is modest — even thin — and modern critics have sometimes faulted Chopin for not having developed the orchestral side. Listening to the Op. 21 Larghetto, does the orchestral modesty bother you, or does it feel right for what the music is trying to do?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Zimerman / Polish Festival Orchestra 1999 DG recording (or one of the alternatives). Listen to the Op. 21 Larghetto a second time and attend to these four moments:

  1. **The piano entry (around 0:30).** Confirm: the orchestra has stated the theme; the piano enters with the same theme elaborated. Notice the bel-canto texture — right hand sings, orchestra accompanies the way the operatic orchestra accompanies an aria. The texture is the same as Op. 9 No. 2 from Chapter 2, now at larger scale.

  2. **The shift into the recitative (around 5:00).** Listen for the moment the singing texture changes to a more declamatory texture. The right hand's rhythms become irregular; the orchestra largely drops out; the music sounds like speech rather than song. The shift is one of the clearest examples of operatic device transferred to the keyboard.

  3. **The return after the recitative (around 6:30).** Listen for the moment the singing texture returns. The orchestra re-enters; the right hand takes up the lyric line again. Notice whether the return feels like _the same_ as the opening singing, or feels weighted by what just happened. (It should feel weighted.)

  4. **The Op. 11 Romance for comparison.** If you have time, listen to the Romance from Op. 11 (the slow movement of the E minor concerto), about 10 minutes. Same bel-canto piano-with-orchestra texture. Slightly more elegiac mood. The Op. 11 Romance was described by Chopin in his October 15, 1830 letter as "a kind of reverie in the moonlight on a beautiful spring evening." The two slow movements together are the late-Warsaw lyric voice.

### Going Deeper

**Optional listening** (if you have time — the Field nocturne comparison in Chapter 7 covers similar ground): pair the Op. 21 Larghetto with the slow movement of John Field's Piano Concerto No. 4 in E♭ major (composed around 1814). Field was the Irish-Russian pianist-composer whose nocturnes Chopin admired and partly inherited from; his concertos use the same bel-canto piano-with-orchestra texture that Chopin would adopt. Recordings of the Field concertos by Míceál O'Rourke with the London Mozart Players (Chandos, 1992-95) or by John O'Conor (Telarc, 1999) provide the comparison. Listening to a Field slow movement and then to the Op. 21 Larghetto will let you hear what Chopin inherited and what he added.

**Read** : Alan Walker, _Fryderyk Chopin: A Life and Times_ (FSG, 2018), the first 150 pages — the Warsaw childhood, the Conservatory years, the early travels, the November 1830 departure. The Walker chapters on the Warsaw period are the contemporary biographical standard; they document the family, the Elsner relationship, the Konstancja Gładkowska context, and the departure with the documentary rigor that 19th-century biographies (Niecks, Karasowski) lack. Walker leans on Polish-school scholarship (Tomaszewski, Kobylańska) for the Warsaw documentation that earlier English-language biographies missed.

* * *

---

## Chapter 4: The Polish Mazurka — Early Years

Chopin wrote about fifty-nine mazurkas across his life. This is the highest count of any single Chopin genre. He composed mazurkas in his teens, in his late Warsaw years, in his early Paris years, through the Sand-period middle period, and in his final months on his deathbed. The mazurkas span thirty years and span Chopin's whole compositional development; the early ones from 1830-31 are recognizably the same genre as the late ones from 1849, but the late ones reach harmonic territory the early ones do not approach.

This chapter is the launch of the mazurka arc. We will listen to the early mazurka cycles (Op. 6 of 1830-31, Op. 7 of 1830-31, Op. 17 of 1832-33, Op. 24 of 1834-35) and anchor on the Mazurka in A minor Op. 17 No. 4 as the chapter's primary listening anchor. The late mazurkas (Op. 50, Op. 56, Op. 59, Op. 63, and the late-1840s sketches published posthumously by Fontana) will be the subject of Chapter 13. Together Chapter 4 and Chapter 13 cover the mazurka arc; Chapter 17 §17.6 collects what changed across it.

The mazurka is the only Chopin genre with a direct Polish folk-music base. The polonaise too has Polish origins, but the polonaise had been a court-and-ballroom dance for two centuries by Chopin's time and was familiar in salons across Europe. The mazurka — the village dance of the Mazovia region around Warsaw — was less internationally known when Chopin began composing in the genre, and Chopin's mazurkas were among the works that made the genre an international vocabulary item. Polish émigrés in Paris recognized Chopin's mazurkas as a homeland voice; Polish historians from Niecks 1888 onward have treated the mazurkas as Polish-national-identity assertion in an era when Poland did not exist as a state. Modern Polish-school scholarship (Tomaszewski, Kobylańska, more recently the editors of the Polish National Edition) treats the mazurkas as the most identifiably Polish part of the Chopin catalogue.

### 4.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Sometime in the late autumn of 1831, in Paris. Fryderyk Chopin, recently arrived from Vienna by way of Munich, Stuttgart, and Strasbourg, is twenty-one years old and looking for income. He has letters of introduction; he has the Op. 2 Variations on Mozart's _Là ci darem la mano_ which Schumann had recently praised in the _Allgemeine musikalische Zeitung_ ; he has the unpublished Op. 6 mazurka cycle in his trunk along with the Op. 9 nocturnes and the Op. 10 etudes-in-progress.

He visits Maurice Schlesinger, the Paris music publisher who would become one of his principal publishers. He plays for Schlesinger from the manuscripts. Schlesinger agrees to publish. The Op. 6 mazurkas — five mazurkas in F♯ minor, C♯ minor, E major, E♭ minor, and C major — appear in print in Paris in 1832 (Schlesinger in Berlin published the same set; Wessel in London the same year; the triple-simultaneous publication was Chopin's standard from his early Paris days).

For French readers in 1832, the Op. 6 mazurkas were the first published Chopin mazurka cycle. They were also, for most French readers, the first mazurkas they had encountered as serious art-music — the Polish dance now circulating in Paris in the salons of the Polish émigré community after the November Uprising's defeat in September 1831. Chopin's mazurkas arrived in print exactly when Paris was thinking about Poland; the political resonance was inevitable. Schumann in his review of the Op. 6 mazurkas (his second major Chopin review, after the Op. 2 Variations review of December 1831) wrote that the mazurkas were "guns concealed in flowers" — a phrase that would be quoted in every subsequent biography. Schumann recognized that the mazurkas were not just dances; they carried something political that the dance form did not name explicitly.

Whether Schumann's reading was Chopin's intention is unclear. Chopin in his letters did not write about the mazurkas as political works. He wrote about them as Polish — connected to the home he had left — but not specifically as protest. The political reading is the reception's interpretation more than the composer's stated purpose; modern scholarship (Walker, Samson, Kallberg) treats the political dimension as real but as one of several dimensions, not as the single key to the music. We will return to this.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The triple meter and the dance pulse.** The mazurka is in 3/4 (or sometimes 3/8) — three beats per bar, like a waltz, but with the accent typically falling on beat two or beat three rather than on beat one. The displacement of the accent away from the downbeat is the most identifiable mazurka rhythmic feature. Listen for the offbeat accent; it should feel slightly uneven in a way that a waltz does not.

  2. **The folk-modal coloring.** Chopin's mazurkas often use harmonies that do not belong to standard major-or-minor scales. The most distinctive is the _raised fourth_ — in A minor, for example, raising the fourth scale degree from D to D♯, which creates a brightness on what should be a darker tone. This is the _Lydian_ coloring, familiar from Polish folk music in the highland regions. The Op. 17 No. 4 in A minor uses this device repeatedly. Other distinctive harmonies include the _augmented-second interval_ (a wide melodic leap that sounds Eastern-European or vaguely Middle-Eastern), and the _bagpipe-drone_ left hand (a sustained low note in the bass while the upper voices move above it).

  3. **The melancholy posture.** The Op. 17 No. 4 in A minor is one of the most-known melancholy-mazurkas. The minor key, the slow tempo (marked Lento ma non troppo), the descending melodic line, the avoidance of forte dynamics — all suggest grief rather than dance. Yet the work is still recognizably a mazurka by its rhythm. The genre's range from the dance-energy mazurkas (Op. 6 No. 1, Op. 7 No. 1, the _Grande Polonaise Brillante_ -adjacent showpieces) to the deeply melancholic mazurkas (Op. 17 No. 4, Op. 24 No. 4) is a Chopin invention. Folk-music mazurkas were largely dance-energetic; Chopin extended the genre to grief.

#### One-sentence background

The early-Paris mazurka cycles were composed and published from 1830 to 1835: Op. 6 (five mazurkas, 1830-31), Op. 7 (five mazurkas, 1830-31), Op. 17 (four mazurkas, 1832-33), Op. 24 (four mazurkas, 1834-35). The Mazurka in A minor Op. 17 No. 4 (composed 1832-33, published 1834) is the chapter anchor; it runs about 4 minutes 30 seconds. The **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Arthur Rubinstein on RCA Victor's 1965-66 complete Mazurkas (recorded in Rome, March 1965 and February 1966; released as a three-LP set), chosen for the Polish-school depth that Rubinstein brought to the mazurkas across his career and for the recording's status as the canonical complete document.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Chopin Mazurka A minor Op 17 No 4 Rubinstein" or "Chopin Mazurkas Rubinstein 1966" ZH: "肖邦 a小调玛祖卡 作品17之4 鲁宾斯坦" 或 "肖邦 玛祖卡集" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** :

* Vladimir Ashkenazy on Decca (complete Mazurkas recorded 1976-85) — the Russian-school reading; less folk-rhythmic, more pianistic, with the rubato pulled toward the larger gesture.
* Idil Biret on Naxos (complete Mazurkas, 1990s) — a budget-tier reading, but with a more transparent rhythmic articulation than Rubinstein's that some listeners prefer for the folk-music dimension.

### 4.2 The Listening Window

Timestamps refer to the Rubinstein 1965-66 Primary Recording. The Op. 17 No. 4 in his reading runs about 4 minutes 35 seconds.

**0:00 — The opening.** A quiet harmonic gesture in the left hand: a sustained A minor chord. The right hand enters around half a second later with the main melodic line — descending, slow, in A minor. Notice the immediate establishment of melancholy: minor key, slow tempo, descending melodic shape.

**0:08 — The mazurka rhythm.** After the opening introduction, the texture settles into the mazurka pattern: three-beats-per-bar, with the accent often on beat three rather than on beat one. The left hand plays a bass note on beat one and chord-and-chord on beats two and three. Listen for the rhythm; it is gentler than the dance-mazurka pulse but still recognizably triple.

**About 0:30 — The first phrase ending.** The melody descends to a cadence on the dominant of A minor (E). The right hand breathes; the harmonic motion pauses; the mazurka pulse continues underneath. Notice that even in melancholy, the dance pulse holds.

**0:30-1:00 — The second phrase.** The melody returns, slightly varied. The harmonic motion moves more freely. Listen for moments where the harmony reaches outside standard A minor — there is at least one chord with the raised fourth (D♯ rather than D), which gives the texture its slightly distant, Lydian color.

**About 1:10 — The middle section, in A major.** The music shifts to A major (the parallel major). The mood lifts briefly — sun appearing through grief. The melodic line in the right hand becomes more ornamental; the texture brightens. The mazurka rhythm remains. The shift is brief.

**About 2:00 — Return to A minor.** The mazurka returns to the home key and to the melancholy main material. The descending melodic line, the slow tempo, the gentle dance pulse.

**About 2:30 — The middle-of-piece harmonic stretching.** Listen for a passage where the harmony moves outside the conventional A-minor area more freely. The chromaticism is gentle but it is present; the mazurka does not stay strictly diatonic.

**About 3:30 — The closing pages.** The music begins to settle. The melodic line repeats; the harmonic motion narrows.

**About 4:25 — The final cadence.** The mazurka ends quietly, on the home A-minor chord. Listen specifically to the last few bars: the music does not resolve into A major (the _picardy third_ convention of ending a minor-key piece in major), and the lack of resolution into brightness is the work's signature. The Op. 17 No. 4 ends in melancholy.

### 4.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The three source dances

Polish folk-music tradition distinguishes three closely related dances that share the mazurka rhythm: the _mazur_ , the _oberek_ , and the _kujawiak_. The differences are real but subtle.

The _mazur_ (sometimes called _mazurek_) is the medium-tempo dance — moderate energy, accent often on beat three, suitable for a village dance floor with paired couples. This is the rhythm most commonly associated with the word _mazurka_ in non-Polish vocabulary; when someone says "a mazurka," the _mazur_ pulse is what they usually mean.

The _oberek_ is the fast dance — sometimes very fast, with a spinning energy (the name relates to the Polish verb _obracać_ , "to turn"). The oberek is the wild-village-dance mazurka. Some of Chopin's faster mazurkas pull toward this end of the rhythmic spectrum.

The _kujawiak_ is the slow dance — slower, often melancholy, sometimes lyric, suitable for the more inward expressive moments. The Op. 17 No. 4 in A minor is closer to the kujawiak end of the spectrum than to the mazur or oberek. Polish-school scholarship (Tomaszewski especially) traces each Chopin mazurka to the specific source-dance that dominates its rhythmic posture; many Chopin mazurkas combine elements of two or all three.

Chopin himself did not label the mazurkas with the source-dance names; he called them all "Mazurka" in the published titles. The Polish-school differentiation is the listener's tool, not the composer's tag.

#### The accent on beat two or three

The single most identifiable mazurka rhythmic feature is the accent's displacement from beat one. In a waltz, the accent falls on beat one (the _strong_ beat); in a mazurka, the accent often falls on beat two or beat three. The displacement creates a slightly unsteady feel — like a dancer who arrives a little late at the strong beat, or who emphasizes the wrong beat.

In the Op. 17 No. 4, the accent's placement varies across the piece. Listen for moments where the melodic emphasis or the dynamic stress falls on the third beat of a bar — the descending melodic line often emphasizes the third beat in this way. Pianists vary in how much they project this displacement. Rubinstein keeps the displacement understated in the 1965 reading; Russian-school pianists like Ashkenazy tend to project it less; some Polish-school pianists project it more strongly. The full mazurka tradition holds that the accent's displacement is one of the things that gets lost when the music is played too cleanly.

#### The Lydian raised fourth

The harmonic device most associated with the Chopin mazurka is the raised fourth scale degree. In A major or A minor, the fourth scale degree is D; raising it to D♯ creates the _Lydian mode_ (an old church mode characterized by the raised fourth). The raised fourth is a Polish-folk-music coloring; the Tatra mountain folk music of southern Poland uses the device prominently, and the highland folk tradition (the _góralski_ music) is identified as a source Chopin's mazurkas reach back to (Tomaszewski, in particular, develops this thread).

In the Op. 17 No. 4 the raised fourth appears in moments where the harmony briefly reaches outside the standard A minor. The effect is a brightness on what should be a darker tone — a distant, slightly exotic color. Listen for the device; once you can hear it, you will hear it in many of the Chopin mazurkas.

#### The bagpipe drone

The other distinctive Chopin-mazurka device is the _bagpipe drone_ : a sustained low note in the left hand while the upper voices move above it. The device imitates the Polish village bagpipe (the _koza_ , _gajdy_ , or _dudy_ depending on region), which would have played the dance rhythm at village weddings and harvests. Chopin had heard bagpipe-playing during summer vacations at the Skarbek estate at Żelazowa Wola in his childhood, per family recollections gathered by Walker and by Polish-school scholarship; the bagpipe-drone device appears in many mazurkas as a direct folk-music quotation.

In the Op. 17 No. 4, the bagpipe drone is less prominent than in some other mazurkas (the Op. 6 No. 1 in F♯ minor has it more clearly; the Op. 7 No. 1 in B♭ major has it as the opening texture). Listen for the device in those mazurkas if you have time; it is the most direct evidence of folk-music citation in the Chopin mazurka catalogue.

#### The genre's evolution from village to art-music

Chopin did not transcribe Polish folk mazurkas. He composed mazurkas in the genre, using its rhythmic profile and several of its folk-music harmonic devices, but the works are his own compositions. This is documented across Walker, Samson, and the Polish-school scholarship: Chopin's mazurkas are _stylized_ , not _ethnographic_. The village mazurka would have had simpler melodic lines, simpler harmonies, more strictly regular rhythm; Chopin's mazurkas elaborate all of these.

The genre's transformation is therefore not "Chopin transcribed Polish folk tunes." It is "Chopin took the rhythmic profile and some of the harmonic vocabulary of a village dance, and made art-music compositions in the genre." The political dimension follows from this: in 1832 Paris, a published mazurka was a recognizably Polish form, and a serious art-music mazurka was a claim that Polish musical materials could carry serious artistic weight. This claim was political in the partitioned-Poland context without being explicitly political in any specific assertion.

#### The Schumann "guns concealed in flowers" reading

The Robert Schumann phrase from his Chopin reviews — "guns concealed in flowers" — applies to the political reception of the mazurkas. Schumann was responding to the Op. 6 mazurkas in 1832-33; he heard in them not just the new genre but also the political subtext of a Polish composer publishing Polish-folk-rooted music in the year after the November Uprising. The phrase has been quoted in nearly every subsequent Chopin biography because it is short and good.

What Schumann meant: the surface of the mazurkas (the dance-like character, the modest scale, the salon-suitable length) conceals something more serious (the assertion of Polish musical identity, the claim of a partitioned nation to a musical voice). The "flowers" are the dance-like surface; the "guns" are the political content beneath.

Chopin did not adopt Schumann's framing in his own letters. His correspondence about the mazurkas is restrained — he wrote about them as Polish, as connected to home, as music he cared about. He did not write about them as concealed weapons. The Schumann reading is a reading that the music allows but that Chopin did not invite.

Modern scholarship (Kallberg especially) treats the political dimension as real but as one dimension among several. Kallberg's work on the gender-and-genre reception of Chopin notes that the mazurkas were variously coded "Polish-national" in 19th-century Polish reception and "feminine" or "exotic" in 19th-century Western European reception, and that both codings tell us more about reception than about the music itself. The honest reading: the mazurkas carry Polish-national resonance for those who hear them that way, and they are also serious art-music compositions that can be heard without the political frame.

#### The early-cycle structure

The Op. 6, Op. 7, Op. 17, and Op. 24 cycles are each grouped sets of three to five mazurkas, published together. The pattern was an early-Chopin marketing convenience — a salon pianist could buy the set as a single piece of sheet music and have several mazurkas to choose among — rather than an intended unified cycle (the way the Op. 28 Preludes are an intended cycle).

The pattern in the early Paris cycles is roughly: a major-key mazurka with bagpipe-drone or dance-energy character, then one or two contrasting mazurkas in minor keys with more melancholic or harmonically experimental character, then a closing mazurka that returns to the energy of the opening. The Op. 17 cycle (four mazurkas: B♭ major, E minor, A♭ major, A minor) follows this pattern: the first two are lighter, the third is more lyric, the fourth (the Op. 17 No. 4 anchor of this chapter) is the most melancholic and emotionally weighted.

The cycle structure is not strict and Chopin did not enforce it. Listeners can play any single mazurka without the cycle; the cycles are a publication convenience. The within-cycle relationships are sometimes audible but rarely central.

### 4.4 The Stories

#### What this anchor is and is not

The Op. 17 No. 4 in A minor is not the famous Chopin mazurka. The Op. 7 No. 1 in B♭ major and the Op. 33 No. 4 in B minor are more frequently played in popular contexts. The Op. 17 No. 4 is, however, the melancholy-mazurka archetype — the piece that 20th-century pianists and critics have most often cited as the "deep mazurka," and the work that established the Chopin mazurka as capable of grief.

What the Op. 17 No. 4 is: a slow A-minor mazurka in three-part form, with a brief A-major middle section and a return to A-minor melancholy, ending without resolution into major. The harmonic vocabulary includes the raised fourth, brief chromatic stretches, and a generally diatonic A-minor harmonic motion. The texture is the bel-canto-piano texture you know from Op. 9 No. 2, transposed into the mazurka idiom.

What the Op. 17 No. 4 is not: it is not the deepest Chopin mazurka. The Op. 24 No. 4 in B♭ minor, the Op. 30 No. 4 in C♯ minor, the Op. 56 No. 3 in C minor (Chapter 13), the Op. 59 No. 1 in A minor (Chapter 13), and the Op. 63 No. 2 in F minor (Chapter 13) push further. The Op. 17 No. 4 is the gateway; the late mazurkas are the destination.

#### The genre's Polish-political resonance

A short note on what the mazurka meant in the Polish émigré community in Paris from 1831 onward. The November Uprising's defeat in September 1831 produced a wave of Polish refugees in Paris — about 5,000 to 8,000 Polish émigrés by the mid-1830s, including aristocrats (Prince Adam Czartoryski, the head of the émigré political community, lived at the Hôtel Lambert in Paris), poets (Adam Mickiewicz, the poet whose ballades influenced Chopin's keyboard ballades, lived in Paris from 1832), and musicians.

For this community, Chopin's mazurkas were the homeland's music. A Polish émigré attending a Chopin salon concert in 1833 or 1834 was attending a performance of music from the country that no longer existed as a state. The political resonance was inevitable; it did not require Chopin to mark the music as political for the community to receive it that way.

For Polish national identity through the 19th century — partition until 1918, independence 1918-1939, Nazi occupation, Soviet domination 1945-1989, post-1989 democratic Poland — the Chopin mazurkas have remained one of the principal carriers of Polish identity. The annual Chopin Competition in Warsaw, held since 1927 (founded by Jerzy Żurawlew during the Second Polish Republic), uses the mazurkas as one of the competition's central evaluation pieces; the "mazurka prize" awarded for best mazurka performance is one of the competition's most-prestigious sub-prizes (Dang Thai Son won it in 1980; Bunin in 1985; Blechacz in 2005; Cho in 2015).

#### The Konstancja Gładkowska resonance (briefly)

A small note. The Op. 17 No. 4 was composed in Paris in 1832-33. By this time Konstancja Gładkowska — the Warsaw-period unspoken-love figure from the Op. 21 Larghetto (Chapter 3) — had married Józef Grabowski in early 1832. Chopin would have learned of the marriage through correspondence with his Warsaw family.

The Op. 17 No. 4's date of composition and the timing of the Gładkowska marriage are coincidental enough that some early biographies linked them. Modern scholarship (Walker) does not assert a direct connection; Chopin did not write about the Op. 17 No. 4 as a Konstancja piece. The mazurka stands as A-minor melancholy without biographical attribution. If you find the resonance helpful, the timing permits the reading; if you find it imposed, you can let it go.

#### Recording history briefly

The complete Chopin Mazurkas have been recorded many times. The major lineages divide as follows.

The Rubinstein lineage. Arthur Rubinstein recorded the complete Mazurkas multiple times; the 1965-66 RCA stereo set is the canonical document and the Primary Recording for this chapter. Rubinstein's mazurkas are the Polish-school inheritance brought into the warm-singing American-recording context. His rubato is moderate; his Polish-folk-music inflections are present but not extreme; his rhythmic articulation is steady. The reading is the safe starting point for any listener building a Chopin mazurka library.

The Ashkenazy lineage. Vladimir Ashkenazy recorded the complete Mazurkas across 1976-85 for Decca. His reading is more Russian-school in approach — less folk-rhythmic, more pianistic, with the rubato pulled toward the larger gesture. The reading is broadly admired but is occasionally criticized for losing some of the folk-music dimension.

The Polish-school lineage. Several Polish-school pianists have recorded complete or partial Mazurkas with stronger folk-music emphasis. Henryk Sztompka (1901-1964) recorded a famous set in the early 1960s on Muza (the Polish state label); his reading is more rhythmically idiomatic than Rubinstein's. Janusz Olejniczak has recorded various selections. Garrick Ohlsson (American but with strong Polish-school connections) recorded the complete Mazurkas for Hyperion in 2008-09. The Polish-school readings tend to project the rhythmic displacement (the accent on beat two or three) more strongly than the Rubinstein-Ashkenazy mainstream.

The Biret lineage. Idil Biret (born 1941, Turkish, with European training) recorded the complete Mazurkas for Naxos in the 1990s. Her reading is more transparent in rhythmic articulation than Rubinstein's; some listeners prefer it for the folk-music dimension despite the budget-label production.

The Argerich question. Martha Argerich has played and recorded several individual mazurkas across her career but has never recorded a complete cycle. Her live-concert mazurka readings are powerful documents when they exist on disc; the documentation is scattered.

For the Op. 17 No. 4 specifically, the Rubinstein 1965 is the safe ground. The Sztompka 1960s and the Ohlsson 2008-09 are the alternative recommendations within the recording-hallucination safeguard.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The Op. 17 No. 4 connects forward to many later mazurkas.

To the Op. 24 No. 4 in B♭ minor (composed 1834-35). Similar melancholy posture, more harmonically adventurous. The Op. 24 No. 4 is the "darker sister" of the Op. 17 No. 4 in some 19th-century critical writing.

To the Op. 30 No. 4 in C♯ minor (1837). The dark-mazurka tradition continues; the late Paris middle-period mazurkas. The Op. 30 set as a whole is one of the most-discussed cycles.

To the Op. 41 cycle (1838-39). Four mazurkas: E minor, B major, A♭ major, C♯ minor. The C♯ minor of Op. 41 No. 1 is one of the most-discussed Chopin mazurkas and is cited in Chapter 1 §1.2 Habit 1 as a counter-example to the "Chopin is sentimental piano wallpaper" reading.

To the late mazurkas (Op. 50, Op. 56, Op. 59, Op. 63 of the 1840s) covered in Chapter 13. The within-genre evolution from Op. 6/17/24 to Op. 56/59/63 is one of the great within-genre evolutions in Western music. Chapter 17 §17.6 collects the arc.

To the Polish-folk-modal coloring across the catalogue. The Lydian raised fourth and the augmented-second interval and the bagpipe-drone left hand all return in the late mazurkas, in some of the polonaises (Chapter 14), and in occasional moments of other genres (the trio sections of some scherzos, the central episode of the Polonaise-Fantaisie). Chapter 17 §17.4 collects the Polish-folk-modal-coloring examples.

#### Cross-piece links to the rest of the catalogue

The mazurka's Polish-political resonance connects to the polonaises (Chapter 14), where the same Polish-national assertion is the explicit content. Chopin's two Polish-folk-rooted genres — the mazurka (village folk dance) and the polonaise (court-and-ballroom dance with national-ceremonial associations) — together make up the most identifiably Polish part of his catalogue. The Op. 53 _Heroic_ Polonaise (1842, Chapter 14) and the late mazurkas (Chapter 13) are the demonstration pieces.

#### The biographical context: 1832-1835 in Paris

Where in the 39 years: the Op. 17 No. 4 was composed in 1832-33 in Paris, during the period of Chopin's professional establishment in the city. The salon network had opened to him; the Pleyel piano-maker connection was established; the publishing relationships with Schlesinger in Berlin, Pleyel in Paris, and Wessel in London were in place. The Schumann reviews of Op. 2 (December 1831) and Op. 6 (early 1833) had announced Chopin to the German-language music press. The teaching career had begun; by 1833 Chopin was supporting himself partly through private piano lessons in addition to publication royalties and occasional concert performances.

The Polish émigré community in Paris was active and Chopin was connected to it through Prince Adam Czartoryski's circle and through the Polish poets (Mickiewicz) who shared the city. The political community provided social context and audience for the mazurkas; the broader Paris music public provided audience for the nocturnes, the etudes, and the concertos.

Chapter 5 covers the Paris arrival and the early Paris establishment in more biographical detail; the present chapter has been about the mazurka launch within that biographical setting.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"Schumann's phrase 'guns concealed in flowers' has been quoted in nearly every Chopin biography. Listening to the Op. 17 No. 4 in A minor, do you hear flowers concealing guns, or do you hear melancholy that is its own subject? What does the Schumann framing add or take away?"_
* _"The raised fourth (Lydian) coloring is one of the Polish-folk-music devices Chopin uses. Listen for it in Op. 17 No. 4 — the moments where the harmony briefly reaches outside standard A minor toward something brighter or stranger. Can you locate at least one such moment by ear?"_
* _"Chopin's mazurkas are stylized rather than ethnographic — he did not transcribe folk tunes. Yet they are recognized as carrying Polish identity. What does this tell you about the relationship between musical materials and national identity? Is the same true of any other composer-and-nation pair you know?"_
* _"The Op. 17 No. 4 ends without resolution into major (no picardy third). For a 19th-century minor-key piece this is unusual. What does the choice of ending tell you about the work's intended affect, and what would an ending-in-major have cost?"_
* _"If you had to recommend one Chopin mazurka to someone who has never heard Chopin's mazurkas, would you start with the Op. 17 No. 4 (this chapter's anchor) or with a livelier mazurka like the Op. 7 No. 1 or the Op. 33 No. 4? What does the first impression set up for the second?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Rubinstein 1965-66 complete Mazurkas (or one of the alternatives). Listen to the Op. 17 No. 4 a second time and attend to these four moments:

  1. **The opening eight bars (0:00-0:30).** Confirm: a quiet A-minor harmonic gesture in the left hand, then the descending main melody in the right hand. Notice the immediate establishment of melancholy and the simultaneous establishment of the mazurka rhythm — the music is melancholic _and_ danced, simultaneously.

  2. **The A-major middle section (around 1:10-2:00).** Listen for the moment when the music shifts to A major; the brightening is gentle but unmistakable. Notice that the mazurka rhythm continues unchanged through the modulation — only the harmony brightens.

  3. **The accent on beat two or three.** Listen carefully to the rhythmic stress within bars. Where is the dynamic or melodic emphasis falling? In a strict waltz, beat one would be heaviest. In the mazurka, listen for moments where beat two or beat three carries more weight than beat one. The displacement is the mazurka rhythmic signature.

  4. **The final cadence (around 4:25).** Confirm: the mazurka ends in A minor, not in A major. There is no picardy-third resolution into brightness. Listen to the last few bars; the melancholy holds to the end.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair the Op. 17 No. 4 with a Polish folk mazurka recording. Several anthologies of Polish village music exist; the _Music of Poland_ compilations on the Nonesuch label (1970s) and the more recent _Mazurkas: Folk Music of Mazovia_ (Polish state label Muza, various editions) provide unbroken folk-tradition source material. Listening to a village mazurka and then to Chopin's Op. 17 No. 4 will let you hear what Chopin stylized — the rhythmic frame and some of the harmonic vocabulary survive in his composition; the simpler melodic lines and stricter regularity of the village dance do not. The exercise takes about fifteen minutes and is the most direct way to hear Chopin's relationship to the folk material.

**Read** : the chapter on the mazurkas in Jim Samson, _Chopin_ (Oxford Master Musicians series, 1996). Samson is the musical-analytical voice on the mazurkas; his treatment of the within-genre evolution from Op. 6 to Op. 68 is the standard English-language analytical reading. About thirty pages; it covers the dance origins, the harmonic vocabulary, the rhythmic profile, and the political dimension. The chapter is a useful companion to the present chapter and prepares you for the late mazurkas in Chapter 13.
