## Chapter 5: The 1831 Arrival in Paris

In late September or early October 1831, Fryderyk Chopin arrived in Paris. He was twenty-one. He had spent the previous eleven months traveling from Warsaw to Vienna to Munich to Stuttgart to Strasbourg, and finally to Paris. His country, Poland, had risen in revolt against Russian rule in November 1830, eight days after his departure; the revolt had been crushed in September 1831, while he was still en route. The city he arrived in was the music capital of Europe, full of Polish émigrés fleeing the failed uprising and full of opportunity for a Polish musician with an Op. 2 Variations the Schumann review of December 1831 would soon praise.

This chapter is biographical-and-musical. It traces the Paris arrival, the entry into the salon network, the early professional establishment, the publication of the first major Paris-period works, and the friendships with Robert Schumann (through reviews), Franz Liszt (in person), Camille and Marie Pleyel (the piano-maker connection), and the Polish émigré community around Prince Adam Czartoryski at the Hôtel Lambert. The anchor works are the Variations on Mozart's "Là ci darem la mano" Op. 2 (composed 1827, the subject of Schumann's December 1831 review) and the Nocturnes Op. 9 (published 1832, with the chapter visiting Op. 9 No. 1 in B♭ minor that we did not anchor in Chapter 2 alongside a deeper return to Op. 9 No. 2). The chapter is one of the lighter-density chapters of the volume; it is anchor-shared rather than anchor-heavy.

### 5.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

December 7, 1831. Robert Schumann, twenty-one years old, in Leipzig, sits down to write a review for the _Allgemeine musikalische Zeitung_ — the German-language music journal of record. He has been sent for review a recently-published piece by an unknown Polish composer: Variations on Mozart's "Là ci darem la mano" Op. 2 by Fryderyk Chopin. Schumann is himself an aspiring composer and a precocious critic; he would launch his own _Neue Zeitschrift für Musik_ three years later, in 1834, but for now reviews for the _AmZ_ and other established journals. He has been reading the Chopin Variations carefully and listening to them at the piano with friends.

The review appears in the December 7, 1831 issue. The famous phrase is from the opening: "Hut ab, ihr Herren, ein Genie!" — "Hats off, gentlemen, a genius!" Schumann had invented a fictional reviewer persona, Eusebius, through whose voice this acclamation is made; Eusebius is described as bursting into the review-writing scene with the news that there is a new composer to attend to. The review then proceeds with detailed analysis of the variations — what Chopin had done with Mozart's "Là ci darem la mano" theme, why the harmonization was unusual, how the work demonstrated compositional sophistication beyond what twenty-one-year-old composers usually displayed.

The review reached Paris within weeks. Chopin had been in Paris only since late September or early October; he was in the early phase of professional networking; he had not yet met Schumann (the two would correspond occasionally but never met in person). For a twenty-one-year-old Polish composer recently arrived in a foreign city, having his work declared the appearance of a _Genie_ in the Leipzig music press was a transformative event for his Paris career. Schlesinger in Berlin and Wessel in London were now more eager to publish him; Paris salon doors that might have been harder to open were now easier; Liszt and other established Paris musicians would seek him out.

Chopin's own response to the Schumann review was modest and bemused. In a letter to his family in Warsaw he mentioned the review with mild humor — Schumann's enthusiasm exceeded what Chopin himself thought the Op. 2 Variations warranted. The Op. 2 was a 1827 youthful work; Chopin in late 1831 was already past it, working on the Op. 6 mazurkas, the Op. 9 nocturnes, the Op. 10 etudes. But the review was the cultural event that fixed Chopin in the German-language musical imagination as a major composer and that helped his Paris career launch.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

For this chapter, the listening anchor splits between two relatively brief encounters:

  1. **The Op. 2 opening (the Variations on "Là ci darem la mano").** The work opens with a long introduction — about three minutes of slow, exploratory writing before the Mozart theme is stated. Listen for the moment of theme statement (it comes after the introduction); the simple, recognizable Mozart melody from _Don Giovanni_ should be immediately familiar. Then listen for how Chopin transforms it through five variations. The work is showy and youthful; do not expect the depth of the later Chopin. It is a virtuoso variation-set for piano with orchestral accompaniment, in the manner of Hummel and Kalkbrenner.

  2. **The Op. 9 No. 1 opening (the B♭-minor Nocturne).** The first of the three Op. 9 nocturnes is darker and more harmonically restless than the No. 2 that we anchored in Chapter 2. The opening melody descends, the harmonic motion is more uncertain than in Op. 9 No. 2, and the middle section is dramatically more contrasting. Listen for the difference between Op. 9 No. 1 and Op. 9 No. 2; the same opus number, the same publication, but very different pieces.

  3. **The Op. 9 No. 3 opening (the B-major Nocturne).** The third of the Op. 9 nocturnes is the most virtuosic — fast ornamental figuration, more elaborate technical demands, a brighter overall mood. Listen for the contrast with both Op. 9 No. 1 and No. 2. The three Op. 9 nocturnes together demonstrate the range Chopin saw in the genre from the very start.

#### One-sentence background

Op. 2: composed 1827 in Warsaw; premiered Vienna 1829; published Vienna 1830 (Haslinger), then Paris 1833 (Schlesinger); about 12-14 minutes for piano with orchestra; subject of Schumann's December 1831 review. Op. 9: composed 1830-32 (begun Warsaw, completed Vienna and Paris); published Paris 1832; three nocturnes in B♭ minor, E♭ major (Chapter 2 anchor), and B major; about 18 minutes total. The **Primary Recording** for Op. 2 is Krystian Zimerman with the Polish Festival Orchestra (DG, 1999, paired with the concertos); for Op. 9 No. 1 it is Arthur Rubinstein on the 1965 RCA Nocturnes; for Op. 9 No. 3 the same Rubinstein recording.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Chopin La ci darem la mano Variations Op 2 Zimerman" / "Chopin Nocturne Op 9 No 1 Rubinstein" ZH: "肖邦 唐璜变奏曲 作品2" / "肖邦 降b小调夜曲 作品9之1" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

### 5.2 The Listening Window

Because this chapter splits the listening across three works of moderate scale, the listening window is briefer than in the anchor-heavy chapters. Timestamps below refer to the Primary Recordings named in §5.1.

**Op. 2 Variations** :

* **0:00 Orchestral introduction.** A short orchestral passage in B♭ major sets the texture.
* **About 0:40 Piano entry.** The piano begins the work's long introduction with slow, exploratory writing.
* **About 3:00 The Mozart theme.** Chopin states the theme from Mozart's "Là ci darem la mano" (the duet from Act I of _Don Giovanni_ , where Don Giovanni invites Zerlina to give him her hand). The melody is the recognizable Mozart line.
* **About 3:45 Variation 1.** The theme is decorated with rapid right-hand figuration.
* **About 4:45 Variation 2.** A more lyric treatment.
* **About 5:45 Variation 3.** The piano displays virtuosic ornament.
* **About 7:00 Variation 4 (Adagio).** A slow, lyric treatment in a minor mode briefly.
* **About 9:00 Variation 5 (Alla Polacca).** The work closes with a polacca (a polonaise-rhythmic finale): the Polish-coloring closing.
* **About 12:00 Final cadence.**

**Op. 9 No. 1 in B♭ minor** (Rubinstein 1965, about 6 minutes):

* **0:00 Opening.** Descending melodic line in B♭ minor; left hand in the bel-canto pattern.
* **About 1:30 Middle section.** A contrasting D♭ major passage, more harmonically restless than the Op. 9 No. 2 middle section. The mood is more dramatic.
* **About 4:00 Return.** The B♭-minor material returns, varied.
* **About 5:30 Final cadence.** A more troubled close than Op. 9 No. 2.

**Op. 9 No. 3 in B major** (Rubinstein 1965, about 7 minutes):

* **0:00 Opening.** Faster than the first two of the set; right-hand figuration is more elaborate.
* **About 2:00 Middle section.** A dramatic contrast (a faster, more virtuosic central episode).
* **About 5:00 Return.** The opening material returns.
* **About 6:30 Final cadence.** A brighter close than Op. 9 No. 1.

### 5.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The Op. 2 as Chopin's first international success

The Op. 2 Variations were composed in Warsaw in 1827, when Chopin was seventeen. They are not deep music; they are youthful display pieces in the variation-set genre that was a Hummel-Kalkbrenner-Field standard for early-19th-century piano-with-orchestra showpieces. What makes them historically important is that they were the work that brought Chopin to international attention. The Vienna premiere in 1829 was the first European-capital exposure; the December 1831 Schumann review fixed Chopin in the German-language music imagination.

An honest assessment: the Op. 2 is worth hearing once. It is not worth re-hearing as often as the mature works. Its role in Chopin's biography is the launch role; its role in his musical legacy is minor.

#### The Op. 9 set as Paris-arrival statement

The Op. 9 nocturnes were the work Chopin chose to publish as his first nocturne set in Paris. The choice was strategic: the nocturne was a familiar genre to Paris salon audiences (Field's nocturnes were known), and a Chopin nocturne set positioned him as a composer in a familiar genre with a recognizable identity. The dedication to Marie Pleyel established the Pleyel piano-maker connection.

The three nocturnes were not composed together; they were drafted at different points from 1830 through 1832 (Walker's chronology puts Op. 9 No. 2 as the earliest, begun in Warsaw; Op. 9 No. 1 and No. 3 as completed in Paris). The set was assembled for publication rather than as a unified cycle. The three pieces nevertheless work as a sequence: Op. 9 No. 1 the dark drama, Op. 9 No. 2 the singing tranquility, Op. 9 No. 3 the virtuosic brilliance. Read this way, the set is a small showcase of the nocturne's expressive range.

#### The Schumann review framework

A short note on Schumann's review as a critical document. Schumann was twenty-one in December 1831, the same age as Chopin. His review of Op. 2 was published in the _Allgemeine musikalische Zeitung_ and not in his own _Neue Zeitschrift für Musik_ (which had not yet launched). The Eusebius/Florestan personas through which Schumann reviewed (Eusebius the dreamer-enthusiast, Florestan the energetic-critic) were already part of his critical practice.

The review's content beyond the famous opening phrase is detailed musical analysis. Schumann discusses the harmonic adventurousness of Chopin's variation treatments, the originality of the introduction, the variety of textures, the closing alla polacca's national flavor. The review treats Chopin as a serious composer to be analyzed, not as a curiosity. This treatment was new — the German-language music press of the late 1820s and 1830s tended to dismiss Polish musicians or to treat them as regional curiosities — and the review's framing as "a genius from Poland" partly invented the critical category Chopin would occupy through the rest of his life.

Modern scholarship (Walker, Samson) treats the Schumann review as the launch event of Chopin's Paris career. It was not the only event — the Pleyel connection, the Czartoryski circle, the early salon performances all contributed — but the review was the public event that mattered most for the German-language and English-language reception.

### 5.4 The Stories

#### The Paris of 1831

A short setting. The Paris of October 1831 was the music capital of Europe. The Paris Opéra had recently moved into the Salle Le Peletier; the Théâtre-Italien performed Italian opera with Bellini, Donizetti, Rossini, and others; the salon culture (private musical evenings at aristocratic and bourgeois homes) was the venue for most serious chamber and solo-piano music. Rossini lived in Paris (he had retired from opera composition in 1829 and lived in Paris from then onward); Cherubini was the director of the Conservatoire; Berlioz was the rising French composer; Liszt was an established Paris virtuoso; Kalkbrenner, Hiller, and Mendelssohn (visiting from Berlin) were active. The Polish émigré community after September 1831 added several thousand Polish refugees including aristocrats, intellectuals, and musicians.

For a young foreign musician arriving in October 1831, the Paris music scene was both opportunity and intimidation. The opportunity: the salon network was extensive, the piano-publishing industry was strong, the audience for new piano music was sophisticated and large. The intimidation: the competition was severe, the gatekeeping was real (the salons were socially exclusive), the financial uncertainty was constant.

#### The salon network

Chopin's entry into the Paris salon network was facilitated by several factors. The letter of introduction from Prince Antoni Radziwiłł (composed in Berlin or Vienna in 1829-30; Radziwiłł was Chopin's earliest aristocratic patron, dedicatee of the Piano Trio in G minor Op. 8) opened the Czartoryski-circle Polish-émigré salons. The Pleyel piano-maker connection (Camille Pleyel ran the firm; Pleyel pianos were one of the two leading Paris piano makers along with Erard) gave Chopin access to the salons that used Pleyel instruments. The Liszt friendship (begun shortly after Chopin's arrival; the two played together at salon evenings) extended Chopin's network through Liszt's Paris connections.

By early 1832 Chopin was performing in salons regularly. His first major public concert was on February 26, 1832 at the Salle Pleyel — a Paris debut. The concert featured the Op. 2 Variations (with orchestral accompaniment), the F-minor Concerto (with orchestral accompaniment), and shorter works. Reviews were positive; the audience was the right Paris salon audience (which would carry word into the wider salon network); the financial result was modest. The concert was the public confirmation of what the salons had been hearing privately.

Through the early 1830s Chopin's public concerts were few — about thirty total across his Paris career, of which perhaps ten were full solo recitals and twenty were appearances on shared programs or in salon contexts. The salon was Chopin's primary performing environment. By 1834-35 his teaching practice was also established; he was charging the highest piano-lesson rates in Paris (about 20 francs per lesson, when the bourgeois standard was 10 francs); his students included aristocratic and wealthy-bourgeois women primarily, with a few advanced male students.

#### The Polish émigré community

The Polish émigré community after September 1831 was concentrated around two centers in Paris. The Hôtel Lambert on Île Saint-Louis was the home of Prince Adam Jerzy Czartoryski (1770-1861), the aristocratic head of the Polish émigré political community; the Czartoryski salons brought together Polish aristocrats, intellectuals, and visiting Polish artists. The Bibliothèque Polonaise on Quai d'Orléans was the cultural-institutional center, holding Polish books and archives and hosting Polish cultural events.

Adam Mickiewicz (1798-1855), the major Polish poet, arrived in Paris in 1832 and lived there for most of the 1830s. The Mickiewicz-Chopin friendship is attested in correspondence and in the contemporary recollections; Mickiewicz's ballades (the _Romantyczność_ poems and others) are sometimes cited as inspiration for Chopin's keyboard ballades (Chapter 10), though Chopin himself did not name specific Mickiewicz poems as sources.

For Chopin the Polish community was both supportive (audience, friendship, cultural belonging) and constraining (the political-exile context shaped expectations of his music). He moved between the Polish community and the broader Paris musical community across his career; he kept friendships in both. He did not become a political activist in the Polish exile cause beyond modest financial contributions (he played in benefit concerts for Polish refugees several times); his political contribution was the music — particularly the mazurkas (Chapter 4 and Chapter 13) and the polonaises (Chapter 14).

#### The Liszt friendship

Franz Liszt (1811-1886) and Chopin met in late 1831 or early 1832 in Paris. They were close in age (Liszt about a year younger than Chopin), close in profession (both keyboard virtuoso-composers in Paris), and close in artistic sensibility (both extending the inherited Classical-into-Romantic style). The friendship lasted through Chopin's life, with visible strain around 1837-1838 when Liszt began the partnership with Marie d'Agoult that paralleled the Chopin-Sand partnership beginning around the same time.

Liszt's contribution to the Chopin biography includes the introduction to Sand (Liszt hosted the soirée at which Chopin and Sand first met in late 1836), the dedications of the Op. 10 etudes to Liszt (1833), and the early biographical essay (the 1852 Liszt _Frédéric Chopin_ , published shortly after Chopin's death; the work is partly authoritative as personal recollection and partly contains Liszt-perspective embroidery — modern scholarship treats it cautiously per the single-source-anecdote discipline).

The two pianists' approaches differed. Liszt was the more theatrical performer, the more orchestral-scale composer, the more public-spectacle musician. Chopin was the more intimate performer, the more piano-centered composer, the more salon-scale musician. The contrast was friendly and productive; each respected the other; each represented a different model for the keyboard virtuoso-composer in mid-19th-century Paris.

#### The Pleyel relationship

Camille Pleyel (1788-1855) ran the Pleyel piano-making firm in Paris (founded by his father Ignaz Pleyel in 1807). The firm made some of the finest Paris pianos of the 1830s and 40s. Chopin expressed his preference for Pleyel pianos repeatedly across his career; the Mikuli-recorded Chopin quote — that when he was well he played on a Pleyel, and when he was unwell he played on an Erard — is the standard formulation. (Eigeldinger gathers the documentation in _Chopin: Pianist and Teacher as Seen by His Pupils_.)

The Pleyel firm provided Chopin with pianos for performance and for home use. The Salle Pleyel was the concert hall associated with the firm; Chopin's Paris recitals were often given there. The Mallorca winter of 1838-39 (Chapter 9) involved a famously delayed Pleyel piano shipment that Chopin awaited at Valldemossa. The Pleyel relationship continued through Chopin's life. Marie Pleyel (1811-1875), Camille's wife, was the dedicatee of the Op. 9 nocturnes; her own pianistic career was prominent in the 1830s.

The Pleyel-vs-Erard question — period instrument preferences, the sound difference between the two firms, the implications for modern performance — is the focus of Chapter 19. Here we note that the Pleyel connection was established in Chopin's early Paris years and remained constant.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The Op. 9 set connects forward to the full nocturne arc (Chapter 7). The Op. 27 No. 2 in D♭ major (1835) takes the bel-canto texture of Op. 9 No. 2 forward; the Op. 48 No. 1 in C minor (1841) takes the dramatic intensity of Op. 9 No. 1 forward into the late style; the Op. 62 No. 1 in B major (1846) returns to the brighter mood and pushes the late-style harmonic experimentation.

The Op. 2 Variations connect forward to the _Fantasy on Polish Airs_ Op. 13 (1828, related youthful piano-and-orchestra work, not anchored in the volume) and forward (in genre-terms) to nothing major in the mature Chopin catalogue. Chopin abandoned the variation-set as a serious genre after 1830; his later works in variation-like form (the Berceuse Op. 57, Chapter 15) are not standard variation sets.

The Schumann reception arc continues throughout the catalogue — Schumann's later Chopin reviews (the Mazurkas Op. 6, the Etudes Op. 10, the Op. 35 Sonata in 1841) shape the German-language critical reception across Chopin's life. Schumann and Chopin never met in person but maintained a friendly correspondence (occasional letters across the 1830s and 1840s).

#### Recording history briefly

The Op. 2 Variations are not a standard repertoire piece. The Zimerman 1999 DG recording is the modern reference (paired with the two concertos); the older Rubinstein recordings include the Op. 2 in some Chopin-orchestral compilations from the 1960s. Beyond these, Op. 2 is performed less frequently than the other piano-with-orchestra works.

The Op. 9 set has many recordings; we discussed this in Chapter 2. For Op. 9 No. 1 specifically the Rubinstein 1965 is the standard. The Pollini, Pires, Cortot, and other readings discussed in Chapter 2 apply across the Op. 9 set.

#### The biographical context: 1831-1834 in Paris

Biographical coordinates: Chopin's first three years in Paris (late 1831 through 1834) were the period of establishment. The Schumann review (December 1831) launched the German-language reputation. The February 1832 Paris debut concert established the Paris audience. The publication of Op. 6, Op. 7, Op. 9, and Op. 10 from 1832 to 1833 established the Paris publishing relationships. The teaching practice grew; the salon network deepened; the Liszt friendship formed; the Pleyel connection consolidated.

By 1834-35 Chopin was a professionally established composer-pianist in Paris. The financial precariousness of late 1831 had been replaced by a comfortable bourgeois income. He had moved through several Paris apartments (the Rue de la Chaussée d'Antin from 1833 onward was a stable address); he had built a circle of friends including Liszt, Hiller, Berlioz (occasionally), the Czartoryski circle, the Pleyel circle, and the Polish émigré community.

Chapter 6 covers the Etudes, which span 1829-1837 and were Chopin's genre-transformation project across this same period. Chapter 7 covers the full Nocturne arc. Chapter 8 introduces the George Sand relationship (late 1836 meeting, 1838 partnership begins).

### Conversation Seeds

* _"Schumann's December 1831 'Hats off, gentlemen, a genius!' review launched Chopin's Paris career in the German-language music press. What does it mean that a major composer was 'launched' by a review of what Chopin himself considered a youthful work (Op. 2)? Is this typical or unusual in 19th-century musical careers?"_
* _"The Op. 9 set has three nocturnes in three very different moods (B♭ minor's drama, E♭ major's tranquility, B major's brilliance). Yet they were published together as a single opus. What does the publication of a varied set tell you about how Chopin wanted the genre to be heard?"_
* _"Chopin's salon-based performance practice (about thirty public concerts in his whole life) was unusual even for the 1830s. Liszt by contrast gave hundreds of public concerts. What does Chopin's choice tell you about his sense of his music, and what does the salon environment allow that a concert hall does not?"_
* _"The Polish émigré community in Paris from 1831 provided Chopin with audience, friendship, and cultural belonging — but also constrained him by expecting him to be 'the Polish composer.' What evidence in his career suggests he balanced this tension, and what does it cost an artist to be associated with a national identity?"_
* _"Chopin and Liszt were close in age, profession, and Paris circle, but their pianism and composition went in very different directions — Liszt toward orchestral-scale display, Chopin toward salon-scale intimacy. What do you imagine they admired in each other, given how different their choices were, and is admiration across opposite aesthetics easier or harder than admiration within shared aesthetics?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

For this chapter, three brief listening tasks:

  1. **The Op. 2 Variations introduction (0:00-3:00 in the Zimerman recording).** Listen to the introduction before the Mozart theme appears. The slow, exploratory writing is unusual for an early-19th-century variation set, which would typically state the theme immediately. Why did Chopin write the long introduction? What does it accomplish before the Mozart theme arrives?

  2. **The Op. 9 No. 1 in B♭ minor (the full piece, about 6 minutes in the Rubinstein 1965).** Listen to it as a counterpart to Op. 9 No. 2 from Chapter 2. The same bel-canto piano texture, the same Marie Pleyel dedication, the same publication year (1832), but a different mood — darker, more harmonically restless, more dramatically shaped. Notice how the middle section of Op. 9 No. 1 contrasts more strongly with the outer sections than the Op. 9 No. 2 middle section did.

  3. **The Op. 9 No. 3 in B major (the full piece, about 7 minutes).** Listen for the third Op. 9 nocturne's virtuosic dimension. The opening figuration is faster than in No. 1 or No. 2; the middle section is more dramatic. Notice that the three Op. 9 nocturnes together demonstrate the range of the genre as Chopin understood it from the start.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair the Op. 2 Variations with the Mozart duet "Là ci darem la mano" from Act I of _Don Giovanni_ (premiered Prague 1787). Recordings of _Don Giovanni_ with Karl Böhm (DG, 1977) or with Daniel Barenboim (EMI, 1973) include the duet at high quality; the duet runs about 4 minutes and is sung by the Don Giovanni baritone and the Zerlina soprano. Listen to the duet once; then listen to the Op. 2 Variations again. The theme should be immediately recognizable in Chopin's variation treatments. The exercise demonstrates the cross-genre conversation that the variation set was — Chopin in 1827 transforming a Mozart operatic melody from 1787 into a Polish-virtuoso piano piece.

**Read** : Tad Szulc, _Chopin in Paris_ (Scribner, 1998), the chapters on the 1831-1834 establishment period. Szulc is the journalist-biographer voice and provides the most readable English-language account of the Paris arrival and the salon network. The book is roughly 450 pages; the early-Paris chapters cover about 80 pages and can be read in two sittings.

## Chapter 6: The Etudes — Op. 10 (1833) + Op. 25 (1837)

The Chopin etudes are the heart of modern piano pedagogy and the heart of mid-19th-century piano art-music simultaneously. The Op. 10 set of twelve etudes (composed 1829-1832, published 1833 with dedication to Franz Liszt) and the Op. 25 set of twelve etudes (composed 1832-1837, published 1837 with dedication to Marie d'Agoult) together comprise twenty-four works that any serious pianist must engage with — for technical development as a student and for repertoire as a performer. The _Trois nouvelles études_ of 1839 (composed for the _Méthode des méthodes de piano_ of Fétis and Moscheles) add three shorter pieces to the genre but are not in the main Op. 10 / Op. 25 sequence.

This chapter argues that the Chopin etudes are simultaneously _pedagogy_ (each work built around a specific technical problem) and _concert art-music_ (each work survives performance in a recital, in some cases as a single piece). The two functions are not separable: the same work that teaches you to play right-hand thirds at speed (Op. 25 No. 6 in G♯ minor) is also a serious art-music statement of about three minutes' duration that can be heard, not just practiced. The transformation of the etude from pure pedagogical exercise (Cramer, Czerny, Clementi) into art-music (Chopin) is the genre-revolution that the Op. 10 and Op. 25 sets accomplished.

The chapter anchors on four etudes across the two sets, chosen to demonstrate the genre's range: **Op. 10 No. 12 in C minor** (the "Revolutionary," composed 1831, ~3 min); **Op. 10 No. 3 in E major** (the "Tristesse," composed 1832, ~4 min); **Op. 25 No. 11 in A minor** (the "Winter Wind," composed 1834, ~4 min); and **Op. 25 No. 7 in C♯ minor** (the "Cello," composed 1836, ~5 min). All four nicknames are 19th-century reception inventions, not Chopin's; the Stuttgart-fall-of-Warsaw composition story attached to Op. 10 No. 12 traces to Niecks 1888 rather than to Chopin's own letters and is best treated cautiously. We will discuss each provenance in the relevant section.

### 6.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

September 8, 1831. Fryderyk Chopin, twenty-one years old, is in Stuttgart, partway through the journey from Vienna to Paris that will take him from his country to his life. He has been on the road since the early summer of 1831; he passed through Munich, played a brief concert there in August, and continued via Stuttgart toward Strasbourg and Paris. The Stuttgart stop is partly logistical (a stagecoach connection) and partly health-related (he is unwell, the documentation suggests; a stop in a comfortable hotel is welcome).

In Stuttgart, sometime in early-to-mid September 1831, Chopin receives news that Warsaw has fallen to Russian forces on September 8. The November Uprising is collapsing; the Russian Tsar's army has retaken the city; Polish refugees are streaming west. Chopin's family is in Warsaw and presumably safe but the political-and-personal world he had known is now formally destroyed.

The popular story — repeated in nearly every 19th-century Chopin biography from Niecks 1888 onward, and into most 20th-century biographies — has Chopin in his Stuttgart hotel room writing, in a single passionate session, the Etude in C minor that would become Op. 10 No. 12, nicknamed the "Revolutionary" Etude. The story is dramatic, the etude is appropriate to the dramatic setting (a stormy C minor with left-hand cascades and a defiant right-hand melodic line), and the popular reception has been delighted with the narrative.

The documentation does not support the story in this specific form. Chopin himself did not write about the Op. 10 No. 12 as connected to the Stuttgart news in any surviving letter. The earliest source for the Stuttgart-and-Op-10-No-12 connection is Frederick Niecks's 1888 biography _Frederick Chopin as a Man and Musician_ , which assembled the story from late-19th-century Polish-émigré reminiscences. Modern scholarship (Walker, Samson) treats the story as 19th-century reception embroidery — possibly true in some looser form (Chopin was certainly distressed by the news, and the Op. 10 No. 12 was being composed in this approximate period), but not securely attested in the specific Stuttgart-hotel-room form.

The composition date of Op. 10 No. 12 is more carefully dated by modern scholarship to 1831, with the etude probably worked on across the second half of that year — possibly in Stuttgart but also in Paris after the late-September arrival. The "Revolutionary" nickname is 19th-century reception (it became standard in the 1840s and 50s through critical writing and popular piano-pedagogy materials); Chopin did not use it. What is securely established is that Op. 10 No. 12 was composed in late 1831, that it is in C minor, that it has the cascading-left-hand and defiant-right-hand character that suited the Polish-uprising-context reading, and that the music has carried the political-romantic resonance through all of its subsequent reception.

For our listening, the relevant point: the Op. 10 No. 12 is a serious art-music etude in C minor that was composed in 1831, the year of the November Uprising's defeat, by a Polish composer in transit between Warsaw and Paris. The specific Stuttgart-hotel-room composition scene is decorative; the broader context is real.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

For each of the four anchor etudes:

  1. **Op. 10 No. 12 (the "Revolutionary").** Listen for the _opening cascade_ — the left hand begins immediately with a torrent of fast descending notes, while the right hand enters within the first second with a defiant chord-melody. The technical problem the etude addresses is the left-hand-cascade-with-right-hand-melody coordination: the pianist must keep the left hand moving at high speed across the keyboard while the right hand projects a heroic melodic line above it. Listen for the sustained energy across the work's three minutes; the music rarely settles.

  2. **Op. 10 No. 3 (the "Tristesse").** Listen for the _singing right-hand melody_ over the chordal left-hand accompaniment. The technical problem the etude addresses is the projection of a singing legato (smooth, connected) line in the right hand while the left hand maintains a more chordal texture. The opening melody is one of the most-known Chopin melodies in the wider culture; many listeners will recognize it. The middle section is more turbulent, with a brief virtuosic outburst; the return of the opening melody after the storm is the work's expressive heart.

  3. **Op. 25 No. 11 (the "Winter Wind").** Listen for the _right-hand chromatic figuration_ — a rapid succession of notes that climb and descend across the keyboard, sometimes outlining harmonies and sometimes acting as melodic decoration. The left hand plays a sustained, march-like main melodic line. The combination is technically extreme; the etude is one of the most demanding in the set. The opening introduction is quieter and sets up the storm that arrives at the main material.

  4. **Op. 25 No. 7 (the "Cello").** Listen for the _singing bass line in the left hand_ — the cellistic melodic line that the left hand carries while the right hand provides chord accompaniment. The technical problem is the reversal of the usual right-hand-sings-left-hand-accompanies texture; here the left hand carries the song, and the right hand provides the supporting figuration. The middle section briefly brings the right hand forward; the return of the cellistic left-hand melody is the work's emotional center.

#### One-sentence background

Op. 10 (twelve etudes; composed 1829-1832; published 1833 with dedication to Liszt; about 30-35 min total). Op. 25 (twelve etudes; composed 1832-1837; published 1837 with dedication to Marie d'Agoult; about 30-35 min total). The **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Maurizio Pollini on Deutsche Grammophon, recorded 1972 (the complete Op. 10 and Op. 25 etudes in a single double-LP release, often cited as the modernist-standard reading); chosen for the technical precision and the modernist-clean approach that defines the genre in 20th- and 21st-century reception.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Chopin Etudes Op 10 Pollini 1972" or "Chopin Etudes Op 25 Pollini" ZH: "肖邦 练习曲 作品10 波利尼" 或 "肖邦 练习曲 作品25" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** :

* Alfred Cortot on EMI (the recordings of 1933-1934, monophonic; the canonical pre-war Chopin Etudes set) — Cortot's reading is the Chopin-rubato extreme with occasional wrong notes; the historical document.
* Murray Perahia on Sony (recorded 2002, complete Etudes) — the modern lyric-and-singing reading; an alternative to Pollini's modernist clarity.
* Vladimir Ashkenazy on Decca (recorded 1976) — the Russian-school reading, between Pollini's clarity and Cortot's rubato.

### 6.2 The Listening Window

Timestamps refer to the Pollini 1972 Primary Recording.

**Op. 10 No. 12 "Revolutionary" in C minor** (about 2:35):

* **0:00 Opening cascade.** Left hand enters with a torrent of descending notes; right hand enters within seconds with a defiant chord-melody in C minor. The texture is immediately full and energetic.
* **About 0:30 First major harmonic shift.** The music moves through a related minor key area before returning to C minor.
* **About 1:00 The middle section.** The cascade continues; the right-hand melody moves into a more lyric register briefly, then re-intensifies.
* **About 1:45 Return.** The main material returns with full energy.
* **About 2:20 The climax.** The right hand reaches the highest register; the left hand sustains the cascade beneath.
* **About 2:35 Final cadence.** The work ends with a fortissimo C-minor chord.

**Op. 10 No. 3 "Tristesse" in E major** (about 4:00):

* **0:00 Opening melody.** Right hand states a slow, singing melody in E major; left hand provides a chordal accompaniment in the bel-canto-piano texture you know.
* **About 0:45 Phrase development.** The melody is repeated and elaborated.
* **About 1:30 Middle section storm.** A more turbulent passage interrupts the singing; the right hand plays fast figuration while the harmony moves more freely.
* **About 2:30 Return of the opening melody.** The singing returns; the storm has passed.
* **About 3:30 Closing pages.** The melody settles toward the cadence.
* **About 4:00 Final cadence.** The work ends quietly in E major.

**Op. 25 No. 11 "Winter Wind" in A minor** (about 3:45):

* **0:00 Quiet introduction.** A few bars of quiet, exploratory writing in A minor; the storm has not yet arrived.
* **About 0:15 The storm begins.** Right hand enters with the chromatic figuration; left hand begins the march-like main melodic line. The texture is immediately full and demanding.
* **About 1:30 Middle section.** The figuration continues; the harmonic motion explores related keys.
* **About 2:45 Return.** The main material returns with full intensity.
* **About 3:30 Final cadence.** The work ends with a fortissimo close.

**Op. 25 No. 7 "Cello" in C♯ minor** (about 5:00):

* **0:00 Opening.** Left hand enters with the cellistic main melodic line in C♯ minor; right hand provides chord accompaniment.
* **About 1:00 Phrase development.** The left-hand melody is repeated and elaborated; the right hand adds more figuration.
* **About 2:30 Middle section.** The right hand briefly takes a more melodic role; the left hand continues with the cellistic line beneath.
* **About 3:30 Return.** The opening cellistic melody returns in elaborated form.
* **About 5:00 Final cadence.** The work ends quietly in C♯ minor.

### 6.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The etude as genre before Chopin

The etude as a genre was well-established by the time Chopin began composing in it. Johann Baptist Cramer (1771-1858) published 84 etudes between 1804 and 1820, designed as systematic piano pedagogy and circulated through Europe in pedagogical use. Muzio Clementi (1752-1832) published the _Gradus ad Parnassum_ etudes between 1817 and 1826, a hundred etudes presenting progressive technical challenges. Carl Czerny (1791-1857) published hundreds of pedagogical etudes from the 1810s onward; the Czerny etudes became the standard piano-student vocabulary across the 19th century and into the 20th.

What distinguished these pre-Chopin etudes: each was built around a specific technical problem (right-hand thirds; left-hand octaves; rapid scale patterns; chord voicings), and each was designed primarily for the student's practice rather than for concert performance. They were not literature; they were exercises. A few — particularly some Clementi _Gradus ad Parnassum_ etudes — could survive performance, but the genre as a whole was understood as pedagogy.

The pre-Chopin etude could be very effective pedagogy. A young pianist who worked through the Cramer etudes had developed a substantial technical vocabulary; a student who completed the _Gradus ad Parnassum_ had reached a high pianistic standard. But the etudes were not music to be listened to; they were music to be played.

#### Chopin's transformation

What Chopin did in the Op. 10 and Op. 25 etudes was retain the pedagogical foundation (each etude is built around a specific technical problem) and add the art-music dimension (each etude is also a complete, performable work of musical substance). The Op. 10 No. 12 has the technical problem of left-hand cascade with right-hand melodic projection; it also has a dramatic, three-minute musical argument in C minor that is heard as concert music. The Op. 10 No. 3 has the technical problem of right-hand legato singing line; it also has one of the most famous melodies in the Chopin catalogue. The Op. 25 No. 11 has the technical problem of right-hand chromatic figuration with a sustained left-hand melodic line; it also has the dramatic intensity that earned it the "Winter Wind" nickname in 19th-century reception.

The transformation is not that Chopin removed the pedagogical dimension; the etudes are still pedagogical, and every serious pianist works through them as part of technical development. The transformation is that Chopin added the musical-substance dimension; the etudes survive performance in a recital context that the Cramer-Czerny etudes do not.

The historical importance of this transformation: it changed what an etude could be. After Chopin, the etude became a major genre for art-music composition. Liszt wrote his _Transcendental Etudes_ (1837, revised 1851) and _Paganini Etudes_ (1851) partly in response to Chopin's Op. 10 and Op. 25; Schumann wrote his Études Symphoniques Op. 13 (1834-37) in a different vein but in the same elevated-etude tradition; Debussy wrote his late Etudes (1915) explicitly in dialogue with Chopin; Ligeti wrote his Etudes (1985-2001) explicitly in dialogue with both Chopin and Debussy. The genre's elevation from pedagogy to art-music traces directly to the Op. 10 and Op. 25 sets.

#### The technical problems

A short catalogue of the technical problems addressed across the Op. 10 and Op. 25 sets. The pianist who works through both sets has encountered most of the major early-19th-century pianistic challenges.

**Op. 10** :

* No. 1 (C major): right-hand arpeggios across wide intervals
* No. 2 (A minor): right-hand chromatic scales played by the third, fourth, and fifth fingers
* No. 3 (E major): right-hand legato singing line over chordal accompaniment (the "Tristesse")
* No. 4 (C♯ minor): rapid alternation of right-hand and left-hand figures (the "Torrent")
* No. 5 (G♭ major): right-hand black-key scales (the "Black Key")
* No. 6 (E♭ minor): right-hand chromatic legato in slow tempo
* No. 7 (C major): right-hand double-note figures
* No. 8 (F major): right-hand brilliant figuration with left-hand melodic line
* No. 9 (F minor): left-hand wide arpeggios
* No. 10 (A♭ major): right-hand parallel-sixths legato
* No. 11 (E♭ major): right-hand wide-interval arpeggios in slow tempo (sometimes called "Arpeggio")
* No. 12 (C minor): left-hand cascade with right-hand chord-melody (the "Revolutionary")

**Op. 25** :

* No. 1 (A♭ major): right-hand wide arpeggios with embedded melodic line (the "Aeolian Harp" or "Shepherd Boy")
* No. 2 (F minor): right-hand triplets against left-hand duplets (rhythmic-independence problem)
* No. 3 (F major): right-hand brilliant figuration
* No. 4 (A minor): right-hand and left-hand syncopated chord patterns
* No. 5 (E minor): mid-tempo, left-hand mazurka-like accompaniment (sometimes called "Wrong Note")
* No. 6 (G♯ minor): right-hand legato thirds at speed
* No. 7 (C♯ minor): left-hand cellistic singing line with right-hand chord accompaniment (the "Cello")
* No. 8 (D♭ major): right-hand parallel-sixths legato at speed
* No. 9 (G♭ major): right-hand figuration with left-hand jumping bass (the "Butterfly")
* No. 10 (B minor): left-hand octaves at high speed (the "Octave")
* No. 11 (A minor): right-hand chromatic figuration with left-hand sustained melody (the "Winter Wind")
* No. 12 (C minor): right-hand and left-hand parallel arpeggios at high speed (the "Ocean")

Many of these works carry the "nickname" labels that 19th-century reception attached; none of the nicknames are Chopin's. Many of the nicknames are evocative (the "Aeolian Harp" for Op. 25 No. 1; the "Butterfly" for Op. 25 No. 9; the "Octave" for Op. 25 No. 10) and reasonably descriptive; some are less appropriate (the "Tristesse" for Op. 10 No. 3 carries a melancholy reading the music does not require; the "Cello" for Op. 25 No. 7 is descriptive of the technical-musical idea). The reader should treat nicknames as 19th-century-reception conveniences rather than as Chopin's intent.

#### The dedications

Op. 10 is dedicated to Franz Liszt — "à son ami Franz Liszt." The dedication is dated 1833, when the two were in the early years of their Paris friendship. Liszt was the leading Paris keyboard virtuoso, and the dedication was both friendly and strategic — Liszt would presumably perform the etudes in his concerts (he did, occasionally), and his public association with the set would help its circulation.

Op. 25 is dedicated to Marie d'Agoult — Liszt's partner from 1835, an aristocratic woman who had left her husband (the Comte d'Agoult) for Liszt. The dedication is dated 1837. The choice of dedicatee was a Chopin gesture toward the Liszt-d'Agoult partnership in its early phase; it parallels the contemporary Chopin-Sand partnership (which would begin in 1838, after Op. 25 was published). The dedications of the etude sets are therefore framed by the romantic relationships of Chopin's Paris circle in the 1830s.

#### The Trois nouvelles études

A short note on the three additional etudes Chopin composed in 1839 for the _Méthode des méthodes de piano_ — a pedagogical anthology edited by François-Joseph Fétis and Ignaz Moscheles. The three are short pieces, each about two minutes long: in F minor (right-hand triplet figures against duple bass), A♭ major (right-hand legato), and D♭ major (right-hand legato sixths). They are not as ambitious as the Op. 10 or Op. 25 etudes; they were composed quickly to fulfill a publishing commission. They are sometimes included in complete-etude recordings (Pollini, Ashkenazy, Perahia, Cortot — most major sets include them).

#### The "Revolutionary" reading

A brief return to the Op. 10 No. 12 "Revolutionary" question. The Stuttgart-fall-of-Warsaw composition story traces to Niecks 1888. The popular reception's reading is that the etude is Chopin's musical response to the September 1831 destruction of Warsaw — the left-hand cascade as the chaos of the Russian assault, the right-hand defiant melody as Polish resistance, the C-minor tonality as grief-mixed-with-defiance.

The reading is romantic and possibly overreaching. What is securely known: the etude was composed in 1831 in the same approximate months as the news from Warsaw; the music has a C-minor stormy character that the political-romantic reading suits; Chopin did not deny the reading in his lifetime but also did not affirm it. The earliest secure source for the connection is Niecks 1888, which gathered it from late-19th-century Polish reminiscences.

Modern scholarship (Walker, Samson) treats the connection as 19th-century reception embroidery — likely with a kernel of truth (Chopin in late 1831 was distressed by the Polish news, and the music of late 1831 was shaped by it broadly), but not securely attested in the specific Stuttgart-hotel-room form. The reader should be aware of the reading without taking it as established fact; the music works either way.

### 6.4 The Stories

#### Why this chapter combines Op. 10 and Op. 25

A short note on the chapter's structural choice. Op. 10 was published in 1833; Op. 25 was published in 1837. Four years separate them, and Chopin's compositional development across those four years was substantial — he moved from early-Paris establishment into the early-Sand-period middle phase. Yet the chapter handles both sets together, on the argument that the etude-as-art-music project is single across both sets, and that the within-set evolution from Op. 10 to Op. 25 is one of the principal within-genre evolutions in the catalogue.

What changes from Op. 10 to Op. 25: the Op. 10 etudes are more extroverted, more virtuosic-display-oriented, more energetic; the Op. 25 etudes are more inward, more harmonically experimental, more various in mood. The Op. 25 No. 7 "Cello" — a slow, lyric, cellistic-left-hand work — has no Op. 10 equivalent; the Op. 10 etudes do not include a slow-and-lyric example in this register. The Op. 25 No. 11 "Winter Wind" is more harmonically restless than the Op. 10 No. 12 "Revolutionary," even as both are stormy minor-key etudes. The Op. 25 No. 1 "Aeolian Harp" presents an introspective slow work in a way that no Op. 10 etude does.

The within-set evolution is therefore the etude-genre's deepening across the four years from Op. 10 to Op. 25 — more inwardness, more harmonic experimentation, more variety of mood. Chapter 17 §17.6 collects the genre's evolution into the synthesis discussion.

#### The Charles Rosen analysis

A short note on Charles Rosen's analysis of the etudes as art-music in _The Romantic Generation_ (1995). Rosen argues that the Chopin etudes accomplished what no previous etude had accomplished: they made the technical-problem-as-musical-substance equivalence work. That is, in a Chopin etude the technical challenge is not separate from the musical content; the technical challenge _is_ the musical content. The Op. 25 No. 6 in G♯ minor — right-hand legato thirds at speed — is not a piece about a technical problem; it is a piece whose technical problem is the musical thing it has to say.

Rosen's argument has consequences for how we listen. A Chopin etude is not "music with a difficulty added." A Chopin etude is "music whose difficulty is the music." The pianist's technical execution and the listener's musical experience are unified.

This is why a Chopin etude played slowly and carefully (a student practicing) sounds genuinely different from a Chopin etude played at tempo with full technical command (a concert performance) — not just faster, but more musically substantial. The difficulty has to be present in the performance for the music to fully exist. This is also why the Chopin etudes resist transcription to easier forms; a simplified version of Op. 10 No. 12 is not "Op. 10 No. 12 with the difficulty removed"; it is a different piece.

#### Recording history briefly

The Chopin Etudes have been recorded many times. The major lineages divide as follows.

The Cortot lineage. Alfred Cortot recorded the complete Etudes multiple times across his career; the 1933-1934 EMI recordings are the canonical pre-war document. Cortot's reading is the Chopin-rubato extreme — every etude carries interpretive freedom that subsequent generations have found excessive or revealing depending on taste. The Cortot recordings also include audible wrong notes (memory slips, technical lapses); these are part of the document and were not retouched. The Cortot Etudes are historical recordings; they should be heard but not used as a standard of technical performance.

The Pollini lineage. Maurizio Pollini recorded the complete Etudes for DG in 1972 (the Op. 10 and Op. 25 on a single double-LP, released 1973). The recording was made about twelve years after Pollini's 1960 Chopin Competition first prize. The Pollini reading is the modernist-clean standard — extreme technical precision, minimal rubato, linear melodic projection. The recording defined the second-half-of-20th-century interpretation of the Etudes and is the Primary Recording for this chapter.

The Perahia lineage. Murray Perahia recorded the complete Etudes for Sony in 2002. The Perahia reading is the singing-and-lyric alternative to Pollini's clarity — more rubato, more singing legato, more inwardness. Some listeners find Perahia warmer; others find Pollini more authoritative.

The Ashkenazy lineage. Vladimir Ashkenazy recorded the complete Etudes for Decca in 1976. The Ashkenazy reading sits between Cortot's rubato and Pollini's clarity; it is a mid-century Russian-school approach to the Etudes that many listeners regard as a balanced introduction.

The Arrau lineage. Claudio Arrau recorded the Etudes for Philips in the late 1950s and early 1960s. Arrau's reading is more weighted than Pollini's, less rubato than Cortot's; it represents the Latin American-and-European hybrid tradition that Arrau brought to the Chopin repertoire.

For the four anchor etudes (Op. 10 No. 3, Op. 10 No. 12, Op. 25 No. 7, Op. 25 No. 11), the safe ground is the Pollini 1972, the Perahia 2002, the Ashkenazy 1976, or the Cortot 1933 (as historical document). All are within the recording-hallucination safeguard.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The etudes connect to many later works.

To the Op. 28 Preludes (Chapter 9). The Preludes Op. 28 (1839) are sometimes described as the etudes' shorter siblings — twenty-four miniatures, each focused on a single mood or texture, modeled partly on Bach's WTC. The relationship is real but not direct; the Preludes are not technical-problem pieces in the way the Etudes are.

To the Ballades (Chapter 10). The Ballades draw on the etude's virtuosic vocabulary — the cascading-figuration passages in Ballade No. 1 (~7-8 minutes in), the right-hand brilliant figuration in the codas of Ballade Nos. 1 and 4. The etude's technical demands inform the Ballade's virtuosic moments.

To the Polonaise-Fantaisie Op. 61 (Chapter 14). The late-period structural work uses etude-derived figuration in its middle and closing pages.

To the Scherzos (Chapter 11). The Scherzo No. 2 (1837) was composed in the same approximate years as the Op. 25 etudes and shares some of their technical vocabulary, particularly in the fast outer sections.

To the Sonatas (Chapter 12). The Op. 35 Sonata (1839) and Op. 58 Sonata (1844) deploy etude-derived virtuosic figuration in their faster movements.

The etudes are the technical core of the Chopin pianistic vocabulary; almost every later work draws on them in some form. Chapter 17 §17.6 collects the etude-derived technical vocabulary across the catalogue.

#### The biographical context: 1829-1837

Where this sits in the 39-year arc. The Op. 10 etudes were composed between 1829 and 1832 — the last year in Warsaw, the journey to Paris (1830-31), and the early Paris period. The Op. 25 etudes were composed between 1832 and 1837 — the established Paris period, the years of professional consolidation. Together the sets span the period from late Warsaw to the early Sand period.

The dedications (Liszt for Op. 10, Marie d'Agoult for Op. 25) anchor the etudes in the Paris circle of the mid-1830s. The publication years (1833 for Op. 10, 1837 for Op. 25) mark the work's entry into the Paris-and-international concert vocabulary. The 1837 publication date for Op. 25 is significant — it places the set's release just before the Sand partnership began in 1838, on the threshold of Chopin's middle period.

Chapter 7 covers the full Nocturne arc, which spans 1832 (Op. 9) through 1846 (Op. 62) and is the genre that connects the early-Paris period to the late period across fourteen years. Chapter 8 introduces the Sand relationship that will shape Chapters 9 through 13.

Pick the Op. 25 No. 11 'Winter Wind' and think about whether the difficulty of the right-hand chromatic figuration is _what the music is doing_ , or whether the figuration is a vehicle for some other musical idea."_
* _"The Op. 10 etudes (published 1833) are dedicated to Liszt; the Op. 25 etudes (published 1837) are dedicated to Marie d'Agoult, Liszt's partner. What does the choice of dedications tell you about Chopin's relationship to the Paris musical-romantic circle of the mid-1830s?"_
* _"The 'Tristesse' nickname for Op. 10 No. 3 — applied in 19th-century reception, not by Chopin — has shaped how the work is heard for two centuries. Listening to the etude, do you hear the melancholy the nickname suggests, or do you hear something else that the nickname has obscured?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Pollini 1972 recording (or one of the alternatives). Listen to the four anchor etudes a second time and attend to these moments:

  1. **Op. 10 No. 12 "Revolutionary": the left-hand cascade (entire piece).** Listen _only_ to the left hand if you can; ignore the right-hand melody for one hearing. The left-hand cascade is the work's technical core. Notice the descending patterns, the sweeping range across the keyboard, the sustained energy across nearly three minutes.

  2. **Op. 10 No. 3 "Tristesse": the right-hand legato (0:00-1:30).** Listen for the singing quality of the right-hand line. Bel-canto piano texture again. Notice the breathing pauses between phrases; notice the ornamentation on repeated phrases.

  3. **Op. 25 No. 11 "Winter Wind": the introduction-into-storm transition (about 0:15).** Listen for the moment the storm arrives. The opening introduction is quiet, exploratory; then the right-hand chromatic figuration enters and the texture is immediately full. The transition is one of the most-discussed moments in the Chopin etudes.

  4. **Op. 25 No. 7 "Cello": the left-hand cellistic melody (0:00-1:00).** Listen for the singing quality of the _left hand_. The reversal of the usual bel-canto-piano texture is the etude's signature. Notice that the cellistic line in the left hand has the same kind of singing and breathing as a right-hand nocturne melody.

### Going Deeper

**Optional listening** (if you want the etude-extension comparison; otherwise skip and prioritize the Bellini pairing from Chapter 2): pair the Op. 10 No. 12 with Liszt's _Transcendental Etude_ No. 4 "Mazeppa" (composed 1837, revised 1851) — a virtuoso etude in C minor (later revised key) with similar dramatic-storm character. The Boris Berezovsky recording (Teldec 1999) or the György Cziffra recording (EMI various dates 1960s) provides good documentation. Listening to Liszt's "Mazeppa" after Chopin's "Revolutionary" demonstrates what Liszt extended in the etude-as-art-music tradition: longer span, more orchestral texture, more theatrical gesture. Chopin's etude is shorter, tighter, more concentrated; Liszt's is larger and more spectacular. Both are legitimate; the contrast is illuminating.

**Read** : the chapter on the Chopin Etudes in Charles Rosen, _The Romantic Generation_ (Harvard, 1995). Rosen's analysis of the etudes as art-music (and his argument that the technical-problem-is-musical-content equivalence is what makes the genre work) is the standard contemporary scholarly reading. About thirty pages; it covers the historical context, the technical problems, the dedications, and the within-genre evolution from Op. 10 to Op. 25.
