## Chapter 7: The Nocturnes — the Full Genre Arc

This chapter is a single genre across fourteen years. Chopin published twenty-one nocturnes (eighteen during his lifetime in nine opus numbers; three published posthumously). The genre runs from Op. 9 in 1832 through Op. 62 in 1846 — the full arc of his Paris-and-Sand-years productive life. The early nocturnes are bel-canto piano with light textures; the late nocturnes use Chopin's mature harmonic vocabulary, sustained-pedal innovation, and structural deepening. To hear the genre's evolution from early to late is to hear Chopin's compositional growth in one of its cleanest arcs.

The chapter anchors on four nocturnes across the arc: Op. 9 No. 2 in E♭ major (1830-31, deepened from Chapter 2); Op. 27 No. 2 in D♭ major (1835); Op. 48 No. 1 in C minor (1841); and Op. 62 No. 1 in B major (1846). The four span fourteen years and show the genre's transformation from elegant inheritor of Field through middle-period elaboration into late-period structural ambition.

The chapter is anchor-heavy. We will move through each of the four works with a listening section, a structural-and-harmonic discussion, and a cross-piece-link section. The chapter is long for the same reason Chapter 6 was long: the within-genre evolution is the chapter's whole subject, and the four anchor points are the four moments at which the evolution can be heard.

### 7.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Paris, late 1832. John Field, Irish-born and Russian-resident for thirty years, passes through Paris on his way back to Moscow. He is fifty years old, in poor health (he would die of cancer four years later, January 1837), and seeing once more the European capitals he had largely left behind. Chopin, twenty-two, recently arrived in Paris and already publishing his own Op. 9 Nocturnes (1832, dedicated to Marie Pleyel), goes to meet him.

The two pianists' meeting is on record but the encounter was brief and, by most contemporary accounts, not particularly warm. Field is reported to have been cool about Chopin's elaboration of the genre he had invented — the Op. 9 nocturnes by 1832 had reached a degree of ornamental and harmonic elaboration that the Field nocturnes did not approach, and Field, who was no longer composing nocturnes himself by this point, evidently did not entirely approve. The contemporary anecdotes about Field's specific reactions survive mostly at second hand through later 19th-century writers (Wilhelm von Lenz and others) and modern scholarship treats them cautiously.

What is securely on the record: Field invented the solo-piano nocturne in St Petersburg from around 1812 onward — about eighteen nocturnes published in sets across his career, each a relatively short single-movement work with a singing right-hand melody over a broken-chord left-hand accompaniment. Chopin certainly knew the Field nocturnes and recommended them to his pupils as the genre's forebears. The meeting in 1832 was the only one between the inventor of the genre and the composer who would transform it.

What Chopin added to the Field nocturne: more elaborate right-hand ornamentation drawn from bel-canto opera (Bellini particularly); richer harmonic motion in the left-hand patterns; a wider range of mood from the calm-contemplative center toward both more turbulent middle sections and more harmonically adventurous textures; and, in the late nocturnes, structural deepening that takes the genre toward through-composed forms beyond the standard ABA Field model. The genre's elevation from elegant salon piece to serious concert-art piece is the Chopin transformation — the transformation that Field, by some accounts, did not entirely approve.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

For each of the four anchor nocturnes:

  1. **Op. 9 No. 2 (the return).** Re-listen to the work from Chapter 2 briefly; the bel-canto texture, the ornamental ABA, the closing cadenza. This is the start of the arc.

  2. **Op. 27 No. 2 in D♭ major.** Listen for the _singing duet texture_ — the right hand often divides into two voices, one in a higher register and one in a lower, both singing simultaneously like two soprano voices in an operatic duet. This is one of Chopin's signature texture innovations in the nocturne. The Op. 27 No. 2 is also more harmonically restless than Op. 9 No. 2; the modulations are wider, the chromaticism more present.

  3. **Op. 48 No. 1 in C minor.** Listen for the _three-section dramatic shape_ — a slow, weighted opening in C minor; a chorale-like middle section in C major that builds to a climactic forte; a return of the opening material in a transformed form. The work is the most-dramatic Chopin nocturne; the climactic forte in the middle section is unusual for the genre. This is the late-style nocturne pushing toward something larger.

  4. **Op. 62 No. 1 in B major.** Listen for the _late-style harmonic experimentation_ — modulations that move further from the home key than the earlier nocturnes risked, and a structural openness that resists the ABA framework. The work is one of Chopin's late masterpieces in any genre; it is often cited as one of the most-discussed Chopin nocturnes in the analytical literature (Samson, Rosen, Kallberg).

#### One-sentence background

Op. 9 No. 2 (composed 1830-31; published 1832; ~4 minutes). Op. 27 No. 2 (composed 1835; published 1836; ~6 minutes). Op. 48 No. 1 (composed 1841; published 1842; ~6 minutes). Op. 62 No. 1 (composed 1846; published 1846; ~7 minutes). The **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Arthur Rubinstein on RCA Victor's 1965 complete Nocturnes (the same set anchored in Chapter 2), chosen for the canonical mid-20th-century reading of the full arc.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Chopin Nocturnes Rubinstein 1965" or "Chopin Nocturne Op 27 No 2 D flat" / "Chopin Nocturne Op 48 No 1 C minor" / "Chopin Nocturne Op 62 No 1 B major" ZH: "肖邦 夜曲全集 鲁宾斯坦" 或 "肖邦 降D大调夜曲 作品27之2" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** :

* Maria João Pires on DG (1996 complete Nocturnes, Salzburg recording): the restrained, inward reading; particularly fine in the late nocturnes.
* Maurizio Pollini on DG (2005 Nocturnes selection): modernist-clean readings of the late nocturnes.
* Claudio Arrau on Philips (complete Nocturnes, 1977-79): the weighted reading, slower than Rubinstein, with a particularly deep treatment of Op. 48 No. 1.

### 7.2 The Listening Window

Timestamps refer to the Rubinstein 1965 Primary Recording.

**Op. 27 No. 2 in D♭ major** (about 6:00):

* **0:00 Opening.** Left hand enters with the bel-canto pattern in D♭ major; right hand enters with the singing main melody.
* **About 0:30 The two-voice texture.** The right hand begins to project two simultaneous lines (a higher voice and a lower voice, both singing). This is the duet texture.
* **About 1:30 The first harmonic excursion.** The music moves away from D♭ major into more harmonically restless territory; the modulations are wider than in Op. 9 No. 2.
* **About 2:30 The middle section.** A contrasting middle passage, more ornamental, with more chromatic motion.
* **About 4:00 Return.** The opening duet texture returns in elaborated form.
* **About 5:30 Closing pages.** The music settles toward the cadence; the two-voice texture continues.
* **About 6:00 Final cadence.** The nocturne ends quietly in D♭ major.

**Op. 48 No. 1 in C minor** (about 6:30):

* **0:00 Opening.** Slow, weighted, in C minor. The texture is heavier than the earlier nocturnes; the chordal writing is fuller.
* **About 0:30 First phrase development.** The opening material is developed; the harmonic motion is wider.
* **About 1:30 Transition to the middle section.** The texture thins; the harmony moves to C major.
* **About 2:00 The chorale-like middle section.** A new texture: four-voice chordal writing in C major, in a slow, processional mood. The music builds gradually.
* **About 3:30 The climactic forte.** The chorale reaches a full-dynamic climax (unusual for a nocturne). The chordal texture is fortissimo; the right hand and left hand are both engaged at high dynamic.
* **About 4:00 Return.** The opening C-minor material returns, now transformed by what came before. The texture is more agitated; the rhythm is doubled in places, creating a sense of urgency.
* **About 6:00 Closing pages.** The music settles toward the cadence.
* **About 6:30 Final cadence.** The nocturne ends in C minor.

**Op. 62 No. 1 in B major** (about 7:00):

* **0:00 Opening.** Right hand enters with the singing main melody in B major; left hand provides the bel-canto pattern. The texture is rich; the ornamentation is more elaborate than in the earlier nocturnes.
* **About 1:00 First harmonic experimentation.** The music begins to modulate more freely; the harmonies reach into territories the earlier nocturnes did not approach.
* **About 2:00 The middle section.** A contrasting passage in a related key area, with more sustained pedal effects (the Eigeldinger documentation of Chopin's pedal markings in the late nocturnes is most relevant here).
* **About 3:30 Return.** The opening singing melody returns in elaborated form. The ornamental decoration is now more elaborate than in any of the earlier nocturnes; long ornamental cadenza-like passages embedded in the main melodic statement.
* **About 5:30 Closing pages.** The music explores related harmonies one more time before settling.
* **About 7:00 Final cadence.** The nocturne ends quietly in B major.

### 7.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The genre's evolution from Op. 9 to Op. 62

A short summary. The Op. 9 nocturnes (1830-32) are the elegant inheritor of Field — singing right-hand melody, broken-chord left-hand accompaniment, ABA-like structure with closing cadenza, modest harmonic ambition, moderate dynamic range. The Op. 27 nocturnes (1835) deepen the genre — more harmonically adventurous middle sections, the introduction of the two-voice duet texture in the right hand, more elaborate ornamentation, wider modulations. The Op. 32, Op. 37, Op. 48, and Op. 55 nocturnes (1837-1843) continue the deepening; the Op. 48 No. 1 in C minor is the dramatic peak of the mid-period nocturne and pushes the genre toward symphonic-scale ambition with its chorale-like middle section and climactic forte. The Op. 62 nocturnes (1846) — Chopin's last published nocturnes — represent the late-style culmination, with structural openness, harmonic experimentation, and sustained-pedal effects that the earlier nocturnes did not approach.

The arc is therefore: Field-inheritor (Op. 9) → harmonic deepening (Op. 27) → dramatic intensification (Op. 48) → late-style structural-and-harmonic experimentation (Op. 62). Each step is clear in the catalogue; each is heard in the four anchor works of this chapter.

#### The two-voice duet texture (Op. 27 No. 2)

The Op. 27 No. 2 in D♭ major is the classic example of Chopin's two-voice duet texture in the nocturne. The right hand often plays two simultaneous melodic lines — one in a higher register, one in a lower — both singing simultaneously like two operatic voices in a duet. The technique transfers the operatic vocal duet (a Bellini specialty; the "Mira, o Norma" duet from _Norma_ is the textbook example) onto the keyboard.

For the listener: when the texture works, you can hear two singers at once. The higher voice carries one melodic line; the lower voice carries another; the harmonies between them create the work's central beauty. This is a Chopin innovation in the nocturne genre — Field had not used the duet texture; Chopin introduced it and other 19th-century nocturne composers adopted it.

#### The chorale middle section (Op. 48 No. 1)

The Op. 48 No. 1 in C minor is the dramatic peak of the mid-period nocturnes. The chorale-like middle section (about 2:00-3:30 in the Rubinstein 1965 reading) is the work's most-discussed passage. The chorale is in C major; the texture is four-voice chordal writing (a four-part choral harmony adapted to the piano); the dynamic builds gradually from quiet to fortissimo; the rhythm is solemn and processional.

The chorale's appearance in the middle of a C-minor nocturne is a structural surprise. The Field-derived ABA nocturne would have a contrasting middle section but not typically a chorale; the chorale brings into the nocturne a texture more associated with sacred music (Bach chorales; choral hymns) than with the operatic-vocal nocturne tradition. Chopin's choice to combine the operatic-vocal opening with the chorale middle is the work's structural innovation.

The climactic forte at the end of the chorale (around 3:30) is the loudest moment in any Chopin nocturne. It transforms the genre by allowing the nocturne to reach symphonic-scale dynamic intensity. The return of the C-minor material after the climax is also transformed — the texture is more agitated, the rhythm is doubled in places (creating a sense of urgency that the opening did not have), and the work's closing pages carry the weight of what came before.

The Op. 48 No. 1 is the nocturne that pushes the genre beyond itself — toward something that wants the resources of a larger form. Samson and Rosen both treat it as the moment where Chopin asked the nocturne to do more than the genre had been designed to do.

#### The late-style structural openness (Op. 62 No. 1)

The Op. 62 No. 1 in B major (1846) is one of the most-analyzed Chopin nocturnes in the late-style literature. What distinguishes it: the structural openness. The piece resists the ABA framework. The opening A material returns, but in a form so elaborated that it does not feel like a literal return; the middle section is less clearly contrasted; the closing pages explore related harmonies before settling.

Charles Rosen analyzes the Op. 62 No. 1 as a late-style example of through-composed form within a nominally ABA structure — the music flows continuously without the strong section divisions of the earlier nocturnes. The result is that the listener does not hear "now we are in the A section; now we are in the B section; now we are back in A" — instead, the music feels continuous, with the section-changes less marked.

The harmonic experimentation is also late-style. The Op. 62 No. 1 modulates more freely than the earlier nocturnes risked; the harmonic surprises are gentler than in Op. 48 No. 1 but more pervasive — the music is harmonically restless throughout, with the home key of B major never quite secure until the final cadence. This is the late-Chopin harmonic vocabulary in concentrated form.

The Op. 62 No. 1 is therefore the late-style culmination of the nocturne arc. By 1846 Chopin had developed the genre as far as he would; the Op. 62 No. 2 in E major (the companion piece) is the genre's last published Chopin work; he composed no further nocturnes between 1846 and his death in 1849. The arc closes at Op. 62.

#### The pedal innovation

A short note on Chopin's pedal innovation, which is most audible in the late nocturnes. The piano's sustaining pedal (the right pedal) holds the strings open when the keys are released, allowing notes to resonate and harmonies to blend. Eigeldinger documents that Chopin was the first composer to specify pedal markings systematically in his published scores; the earlier composers (Beethoven, Schubert) used pedal markings occasionally but not as a regular notation element.

In the Op. 62 No. 1 (and in the late mazurkas and the Op. 28 Preludes), Chopin's pedal markings create sustained-resonance effects that blur the harmonic motion in ways the earlier nocturnes do not. The middle section of Op. 62 No. 1 (around 2:00-3:30) is sustained-pedal territory — the harmonies blur, the texture becomes wash-like, the singing line floats above a resonant background. This is a late-Chopin signature; you will hear it again in the late mazurkas (Chapter 13) and in some passages of the Polonaise-Fantaisie (Chapter 14).

For the listener: when you hear blurred harmonic motion in a Chopin late work, the pedal is doing the work. The composer's specific pedal markings are part of the work's notated content.

### 7.4 The Stories

#### The genre's range

The Chopin nocturnes are sometimes treated as a single mood — the gentle-romantic-piano mood that the Op. 9 No. 2 has installed in the larger culture. The genre is wider than this. The Op. 9 No. 1 in B♭ minor (Chapter 5) is darker; the Op. 27 No. 1 in C♯ minor is brooding and harmonically restless; the Op. 32 No. 2 in A♭ major is calm and lyric; the Op. 37 No. 2 in G major is bright and dance-like; the Op. 48 No. 1 in C minor (this chapter) is the dramatic peak; the Op. 55 No. 1 in F minor is darker and elegiac; the Op. 62 No. 1 in B major (this chapter) is the late-style culmination.

A complete listening to all twenty-one nocturnes — which takes about two and a half hours total — demonstrates the genre's range. The reader who has only heard Op. 9 No. 2 has heard the entrance; the reader who has heard four or five across the arc has heard the genre; the reader who has heard the full set has heard one composer's lifelong engagement with a single inherited form.

#### The Field connection

A brief return to John Field. Field's nocturnes are not widely recorded; the best documentation is the John O'Conor complete Field Nocturnes on Telarc (1989). Listening to a Field nocturne is illuminating for what Chopin inherited and what he added: the singing-right-hand-over-broken-chord-left-hand texture is the Field invention; the elaborate ornamentation, the harmonic adventurousness, the structural deepening are Chopin's additions. The Field nocturnes are also genuinely lovely works on their own; the genre would have survived as a Field invention even if Chopin had not adopted it. But Chopin's adoption transformed it.

#### The salon and the late nocturne

A short note on the social context. The Chopin nocturnes were salon music — composed for performance at private musical evenings in Parisian salons. The Op. 9 nocturnes were dedicated to Marie Pleyel; the Op. 27 nocturnes were dedicated to Countess d'Appony (the wife of the Austrian ambassador); the Op. 48 nocturnes to Mademoiselle Laure Duperré; the Op. 62 nocturnes to Mademoiselle R. de Könneritz. The dedications anchor the works in the Paris aristocratic-salon culture of the 1830s and 1840s.

The salon context shaped the genre. A nocturne would typically be heard by an audience of perhaps thirty people in a domestic setting; the dynamic range needed to fit a room rather than a concert hall; the duration needed to fit a salon program (typically four to seven minutes); the emotional posture needed to be intimate rather than theatrical. The genre's intimacy is partly a function of its venue.

The late nocturnes (Op. 48, Op. 62) push against the salon's intimate scale. The Op. 48 No. 1's climactic forte is unusual for salon music; the Op. 62 No. 1's structural openness is unusual for a salon-length form. Chopin in the 1840s was extending the genre toward something that would have suited a concert hall better — though most of his late performances were still salon-based.

#### Recording history briefly

The Rubinstein 1965 set is the canonical complete Nocturnes recording. We have discussed it in Chapter 2. The major alternative complete sets are the Pires 1996 DG (restrained, inward, particularly fine in the late nocturnes); the Arrau 1977-79 Philips (weighted, slower, with deep treatment of Op. 48 No. 1); the Pollini 2005 selection (modernist-clean, focused on the major nocturnes rather than complete); the Barenboim 1981-82 DG (warmer and more rubato-rich than Pollini, more contained than Arrau); the Cortot 1949 EMI (the late Cortot reading, after the famous 1949 set; rubato extreme and full of historical character).

For Op. 27 No. 2 specifically, the Rubinstein 1965, Pires 1996, and Arrau 1977-79 readings differ instructively. Rubinstein's reading projects the two-voice duet texture clearly; Pires's reading is quieter and more inward; Arrau's reading is weighted and emphasizes the harmonic restlessness.

For Op. 48 No. 1, the Arrau 1977-79 reading is often cited as the deepest single recording — Arrau's weighted approach suits the work's dramatic ambition. Rubinstein 1965 is the canonical-warm reading. Pollini 2005 in his selection includes Op. 48 No. 1 and brings modernist clarity to it.

For Op. 62 No. 1, the late-style demands particularly suit Pires's restrained approach; her 1996 reading is one of the deepest documents of the late nocturne style.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The nocturne arc connects to many works.

To the slow movements of the concertos (Chapter 3). The bel-canto piano-with-orchestra texture of the Op. 21 Larghetto and the Op. 11 Romance is the same texture as the nocturnes, scaled up to include the orchestra.

To the slow B sections of the scherzos (Chapter 11). The middle sections of the scherzos use nocturne-like singing textures; the Op. 31 in B♭ minor middle section is a clear example.

To the slow movements of the sonatas (Chapter 12). The Larghetto of Op. 35 (the slow movement that is not the famous Funeral March) and the Largo of Op. 58 use nocturne-like textures.

To the Berceuse Op. 57 (Chapter 15). The Berceuse is in a way a single elaborated nocturne — singing right-hand variations over a left-hand ostinato.

To the late mazurkas (Chapter 13). The harmonic experimentation of the late nocturnes parallels the harmonic experimentation of the late mazurkas; both are late-Chopin signatures.

Chapter 17 §17.1 collects the bel-canto-piano-texture examples and §17.6 collects the late-style harmonic experimentation across the catalogue. The nocturne arc anchors both threads.

#### The biographical context: 1832-1846

The nocturne arc in the 39-year frame: it spans fourteen years from Op. 9 (1832) to Op. 62 (1846). Across that span Chopin moved from the early-Paris establishment period through the early Sand years through the mature Sand-period middle phase. The Op. 27 nocturnes (1835) were composed in the established-Paris years (late-Schumann-reception period, Liszt friendship, Pleyel relationship). The Op. 48 nocturnes (1841) were composed in the early Nohant-summer years (the first three summers at Sand's estate in central France: 1839, 1840, 1841). The Op. 62 nocturnes (1846) were composed in the final Sand-summer year, just before the 1847 break.

The nocturne genre therefore tracks across Chopin's whole productive life from his Paris arrival to nearly his death. Only the mazurka and the polonaise have similar arc-coverage; the other genres are concentrated in shorter periods (the etudes 1829-37; the ballades 1835-42; the scherzos 1831-42; the sonatas 1839 and 1844; the preludes 1838-39).

Chapter 8 introduces the Sand relationship that frames the late nocturnes through the late mazurkas. Chapter 9 covers the Op. 28 Preludes (1839, Mallorca winter).

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The Op. 9 No. 2 (1830-31) and the Op. 62 No. 1 (1846) are both nocturnes in the same genre — but listening to them side by side, the differences are wider than the genre name suggests. Pick three specific differences you can hear, and describe what they tell you about Chopin's fifteen-year development."_
* _"The Op. 48 No. 1 in C minor has a chorale-like middle section that builds to a fortissimo climax — unusual for a nocturne. What does the chorale do to the nocturne's intimate-salon character, and why might Chopin have wanted to break that character?"_
* _"The two-voice duet texture in the Op. 27 No. 2 transfers the operatic vocal duet (Bellini's 'Mira, o Norma' from _Norma_ , for example) onto the keyboard. Listen for moments where you can hear two singers at once. Is the technique convincing as 'two singers,' or does it remain piano-music about singers?"_
* _"The Op. 62 No. 1 in B major resists the ABA structural framework — the section divisions are blurred, the harmonic motion is restless. What does the structural openness do to your listening experience compared to the clearer-structured Op. 9 No. 2?"_
* _"Field invented the nocturne; Chopin transformed it. What would a Chopin nocturne sound like if Chopin had not had the Field model — and is there any later 19th-century nocturne composer (Fauré, Debussy) who developed the genre further past Chopin?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Rubinstein 1965 complete Nocturnes (or one of the alternatives). Listen across the four anchor nocturnes a second time and attend to these moments:

  1. **Op. 9 No. 2: a brief return.** Listen to the first minute and a half. Confirm the bel-canto texture, the ornamental ABA-like form, the elegant simplicity. This is the start of the arc.

  2. **Op. 27 No. 2: the two-voice duet (around 0:30).** Listen for the moment the right hand begins to project two simultaneous singing lines — a higher voice and a lower voice. The duet texture should become audible if you attend to it.

  3. **Op. 48 No. 1: the chorale climax (around 3:30).** Listen for the moment the chorale builds to fortissimo. The dynamic peak is the loudest moment in any Chopin nocturne. Notice what the return of the C-minor material after the climax sounds like — it should feel transformed, not the same as the opening.

  4. **Op. 62 No. 1: the late-style harmonic restlessness (across the whole piece).** Listen across the whole work for moments where the music seems harmonically uncertain — where the home key of B major is not quite secure. Notice how the structural sections are blurred; you may not be able to identify where A ends and B begins.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair the Op. 27 No. 2 with John Field's Nocturne No. 5 in B♭ major (composed around 1817). The John O'Conor recording of the complete Field Nocturnes on Telarc (1989) is the standard documentation. Listen to one Field nocturne; then listen to the Op. 27 No. 2 again. The Field invention (singing right hand, broken-chord left hand) is clearly the model; the Chopin elaboration (two-voice texture, ornamentation, harmonic adventurousness) is clearly the development. The exercise takes about twelve minutes total.

**Read** : the chapter on the Nocturnes in Jim Samson, _Chopin_ (Oxford Master Musicians series, 1996). Samson's analysis covers the Field inheritance, the within-genre evolution from Op. 9 to Op. 62, the two-voice texture, and the late-style structural openness. About twenty-five pages; it is the most accessible English-language analytical treatment of the genre.

## Chapter 8: The George Sand Relationship (1836 meeting, 1838-1847 partnership)

This chapter is biographical context. It does not anchor on a Chopin work; the music chapters Ch 9 through Ch 13 are the works of the Sand-years middle period, and this chapter establishes the relationship context against which those chapters will be heard. The argument the chapter holds throughout: the relationship was real, the partnership was generative for nine years, the break in 1847 was painful, and no melodramatic single-cause story serves the music or the people.

The chapter follows a chronological narrative through three sections: the late-1836 meeting and 1837 hesitation; the 1838 partnership and the Mallorca winter; the Nohant summers and the 1847 break.

### 8.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Paris, autumn 1846. George Sand's new novel _Lucrezia Floriani_ has been appearing serially in the Parisian press through the summer and is about to be published as a complete volume. The novel tells the story of an aging Italian actress (Lucrezia) who falls in love with a younger Polish prince (Karol). Karol is jealous, possessive, physically delicate, emotionally consuming; Lucrezia carries his weight and is exhausted by it. The book is being read across Paris salons, including the salon where Chopin spends most of his evenings. Acquaintances ask each other quietly whether the Karol figure is a portrait of the Polish composer who has been Sand's partner for eight years.

Chopin has read the novel, or has had it read to him; the surviving evidence suggests both at different times. His public statement is neutrality — the resemblance, if there is one, is coincidental. Sand denies a deliberate portrait. Some 1846 Paris-salon observers conclude that Sand was working through her feelings about Chopin in fictional form; others conclude the resemblance is genuine but not intentional, the kind of unconscious portraiture a novelist falls into when writing about a household she shares with the subject. The conversations across Paris-salon evenings in autumn 1846 are uneasy.

What is securely on the record: within ten months of the novel's serial appearance, the Sand-Chopin partnership would collapse — proximately over Solange's marriage to Clésinger (July 1847), but with the _Lucrezia Floriani_ episode as a prefiguration that everyone in the Paris salons subsequently remembered. Whether or not Sand had intended a portrait of Chopin, the partnership did not survive 1847.

This chapter is read rather than listened to. The listening pattern of the book pauses here; the works of the Sand years are the subject of Chapters 9 through 13, and this chapter sets up the context — including the 1846 _Lucrezia Floriani_ moment — against which those listenings will be heard.

If you wish to anchor your reading in music, the following Sand-period works are most worth keeping in mind as you read: the Op. 28 Preludes (Chapter 9, completed Valldemossa winter 1838-39); the Ballade No. 2 in F major Op. 38 (Chapter 10, composed Mallorca/Nohant 1839); the Sonata No. 2 in B♭ minor Op. 35 "Funeral March" (Chapter 12, Nohant 1839); the Mazurkas Op. 50 (Chapter 13, 1842), Op. 56 (1844), Op. 59 (1845), Op. 63 (1847); the Ballade No. 4 in F minor Op. 52 (Chapter 10, Nohant 1842); the Polonaise Op. 53 "Heroic" (Chapter 14, Nohant 1842); the Polonaise-Fantaisie Op. 61 (Chapter 14, Nohant 1845-46); the Berceuse Op. 57 (Chapter 15, Nohant 1844); the Barcarolle Op. 60 (Chapter 15, Nohant 1845-46).

### 8.2 The Meeting and the 1837 Hesitation

#### Late 1836

The first meeting between Chopin and George Sand took place in late 1836, at a soirée hosted by Franz Liszt and Marie d'Agoult at the Hôtel de France on Paris's rue Laffitte. The exact date is uncertain — different sources give October, November, or December — but the late-1836 framing is secure. Liszt had been Chopin's friend since the early Paris years; Marie d'Agoult had been Liszt's partner since 1835. Sand was a Paris friend of the d'Agoults; she was thirty-two years old, a major French novelist with several published novels (_Indiana_ 1832, _Lélia_ 1833, _Mauprat_ 1837 in progress) and a high public profile.

The first impressions are recorded as negative on Chopin's side. He reportedly told a friend after the soirée that Sand had repelled him — her appearance (she dressed in masculine clothing, smoked cigars, used the masculine pen name George Sand), her manners (direct, public, intellectual), her reputation (the Aurore Dudevant separation from her husband, the affair with Jules Sandeau, the affair with Alfred de Musset). Chopin was a discrete bourgeois Parisian; Sand was a public figure of the kind he was not naturally drawn to.

Sand's first impression of Chopin is recorded in her later correspondence with Liszt: she had been told Chopin was the great pianist-composer she ought to meet, and the meeting had not made a particular impression — she found him reserved and physically delicate but had not formed a strong opinion.

#### 1837: hesitation

Through 1837 the acquaintance did not develop into anything. Chopin met Sand occasionally at Liszt-d'Agoult evenings; they exchanged civilities; the relationship did not progress. Chopin's own preoccupations through 1837 were professional (the Op. 25 Etudes were being completed and published) and emotional (he had been corresponding with Maria Wodzińska, a Polish aristocratic acquaintance, with marriage in view; the engagement collapsed in mid-1837 when the Wodzińska family withdrew, citing Chopin's poor health and uncertain professional prospects). The Wodzińska affair was the unsuccessful Polish-aristocratic marriage attempt; Chopin's emotional energy through 1837 was committed there.

Sand through 1837 had her own preoccupations: the conclusion of the Musset affair, her writing (_Mauprat_ and other novels), her two children (Maurice born 1823, Solange born 1828; both lived primarily with Sand at the Nohant estate her grandmother had left her). Sand was also exploring the Polish émigré community through other connections (Adam Mickiewicz, the Polish poet, was a friend).

The relationship that would become the partnership did not begin in 1837. The turning point is in spring 1838.

#### The Tytus Woyciechowski question

A note on a contested contemporary topic the reader will encounter in modern Chopin scholarship.

Tytus Woyciechowski (1808-1879) was Chopin's closest friend from his Warsaw years onward. The two had been schoolmates; Tytus traveled with Chopin from Warsaw to Vienna in November 1830 (Chapter 3) before turning back to fight in the November Uprising; the correspondence between them across the 1830s is one of the principal documentary sources for the young Chopin's inner life.

The letters use language that, by the conventions of early-19th-century male friendship, expressed deep affection. Chopin's surviving letters to Tytus contain passages of intense warmth — declarations of love and longing, references to physical closeness, language that modern readers find startlingly intimate. The 19th-century and most of the 20th-century scholarship treated this language as the conventional rhetoric of male friendship — affectionate but not romantic, in the way 19th-century men routinely wrote to close male friends.

Recent scholarship (Adam Zamoyski's biography among others; the Swiss researcher Moritz Weber's 2020 work has been particularly direct) has reopened the question of whether Chopin's emotional attachments included romantic same-sex feeling for Tytus and possibly others. The documentary evidence is suggestive but not conclusive — 19th-century epistolary conventions make a definitive reading difficult; Chopin's Wodzińska engagement and his Sand partnership are documented in different registers; the cultural-historical context (Catholic Polish bourgeois, mid-19th-century Paris) was not one in which explicit same-sex attachment would have appeared in surviving letters.

For the present volume the question is observational rather than determinative. The contemporary Chopin reception includes this conversation; the reader will encounter it in the recent scholarship. The honest contemporary reading: the documentary record is open, modern scholars disagree, and the question of Chopin's emotional life is more complex than the standard Sand-relationship narrative alone suggests. This book does not adjudicate; it acknowledges the conversation exists.

### 8.3 1838: the Partnership Begins

#### Spring 1838

By spring 1838 the Wodzińska engagement was definitively over. Chopin was emotionally available; Sand had become more interested. The evidence is in their letters: Sand wrote to a mutual friend in late spring 1838 with a long, intense letter expressing her interest in Chopin and asking for advice. The letter (the "Confession" letter) was passed eventually to Chopin (with or without Sand's full knowledge, depending on interpretation); his response was that he had been overcautious before but was now ready to consider the relationship.

Through the summer of 1838 the partnership formed. Chopin and Sand began meeting privately. By late summer the relationship was an open Paris-circle understanding. The decision to spend the winter together — in a warm climate, partly for Chopin's lung health (the tuberculosis was now diagnosed and worsening), partly for Sand's son Maurice (who was also unwell), partly for the practical advantages of being away from Paris's bourgeois scrutiny — was made in late summer 1838.

The destination they chose was Mallorca. Sand had read favorable accounts of the Spanish Balearic islands; the climate was supposed to be Mediterranean and gentle; the cost of living was supposed to be low. The plan was to spend October 1838 through spring 1839 there.

#### The Mallorca winter

The Mallorca winter of November 1838 to February 1839 is one of the most-documented episodes in Chopin's biography. Sand's own _Un hiver à Majorque_ (1841) is the principal source; Chopin's letters from Mallorca to his friends (Wojciech Grzymała, Julian Fontana, others) provide the parallel documentation; Walker, Szulc, Zamoyski synthesize the sources.

The events: Chopin and Sand with her two children (Maurice fifteen, Solange ten) traveled from Paris through Perpignan and Barcelona, arriving in Palma de Mallorca in early November 1838. They moved into the Valldemossa monastery (the Real Cartuja de Valldemossa, a former Carthusian monastery in the mountains north of Palma; the monastery had been secularized in 1835 and the cells were available for rent). The setting was beautiful — mountain landscape, Mediterranean views, the monastery's stone architecture — but the practical conditions were difficult: the cells were unheated, the local food was unfamiliar and difficult, the local population's attitude toward the unmarried foreign woman and the visibly ill Polish man traveling together was hostile (a Catholic island in the 1830s did not welcome the moral irregularity of the situation).

Chopin's health deteriorated through the winter. The tuberculosis intensified; he coughed blood; he became visibly weaker. The Pleyel piano that had been shipped from Paris for his composing did not arrive until late December (the shipment was held up by Mallorcan customs); for the first weeks Chopin worked on a small upright that was inadequate for his composing. The combination of cold, illness, poor food, and isolation made the winter the worst period of his life to that date.

What he composed in the winter: most of the Op. 28 Preludes were completed at Valldemossa, building on drafts begun in Paris before the trip (Samson's analysis is the standard reference for the composition history). The Ballade No. 2 in F major Op. 38 (Chapter 10) was begun at Valldemossa and completed at Nohant the following summer. The Scherzo No. 3 in C♯ minor Op. 39 (Chapter 11) was also composed partly at Valldemossa. The Polonaise in A major Op. 40 No. 1 (the "Military") was composed there. The compositional output was substantial despite the conditions; Chopin's commitment to work continued through the illness.

The Mallorca winter ended in February 1839. The household returned to Marseille, where Chopin recovered slowly through the spring. By summer 1839 he was at Nohant, Sand's country estate in central France, beginning the first of the Nohant summers.

### 8.4 The Nohant Summers, 1839-1846

#### The estate and the rhythm

Nohant was Sand's estate in the Berry region of central France, about 300 kilometers south of Paris, inherited from her grandmother Marie-Aurore de Saxe (the natural granddaughter of King Augustus II of Poland — a Polish-historical detail Sand herself sometimes invoked). The estate was substantial: a large country house, gardens, surrounding farmland, and a position in the local Berry community as the household of the most prominent landowner.

The pattern through 1839-1846: Sand and Chopin would spend the warmer months (typically late spring through autumn) at Nohant, often hosting visitors (Delacroix the painter, Mickiewicz the Polish poet, Maurice's tutors, Solange's tutors and later companions, occasional Paris musicians including Pauline Viardot the singer, occasional Polish friends). The winter months they would spend in Paris (Sand had an apartment, Chopin had his own apartment from 1842 onward at the Square d'Orléans, an artists' colony). The cycle was Paris-winters/Nohant-summers across eight years.

Nohant was productive for Chopin's composition. The country environment was quieter than Paris; the schedule was less demanding (fewer salons, less teaching, more solitary work time); the household provided practical support. Most of the major Sand-period works were composed at Nohant: the Op. 28 Preludes were completed in the months after Mallorca (some at Nohant in summer 1839); the Op. 35 Sonata was composed at Nohant in 1839; the Op. 38 Ballade No. 2 was completed at Nohant in 1839; the Op. 47 Ballade No. 3 was composed at Nohant in 1841; the Op. 52 Ballade No. 4 at Nohant in 1842; the Op. 53 Polonaise "Heroic" at Nohant in 1842; the Op. 49 Fantasy at Nohant in 1841; the Op. 57 Berceuse at Nohant in 1844; the Op. 58 Sonata No. 3 at Nohant in 1844; the Op. 60 Barcarolle at Nohant in 1845-46; the Op. 61 Polonaise-Fantaisie at Nohant in 1845-46. The Nohant summers are the productive heart of Chopin's career.

#### The household dynamics

The Nohant household dynamics through the eight summers were complex. Sand was the central figure — the estate owner, the host, the writer, the mother of Maurice and Solange. Chopin was the partner — not the husband (the marriage that some Paris observers expected never happened), not the formal householder, but the long-term resident-partner of the head of household. Maurice and Solange were the children-and-then-young-adults of the household — Maurice growing up across the eight years from sixteen to twenty-three; Solange from eleven to eighteen and then nineteen.

Chopin's relationship to the children was paternal-but-not-paternal. He took an interest in their education; he played for them; he traveled with them on summer outings. The Maurice-Chopin relationship was warm in the early years but cooled through the 1840s — Maurice came to resent the household's accommodation of Chopin's needs and to side with his mother in any tension. The Solange-Chopin relationship was warmer and stayed so; Solange was the daughter who continued to be friendly with Chopin even during the 1847 break, and her marriage to the sculptor Auguste Clésinger in 1847 became the proximate trigger for the Sand-Chopin break (see below).

The household tensions through the 1840s grew gradually. Chopin's health was declining; Sand's writing demanded long hours and the household had to accommodate them; the children were becoming young adults with their own interests and emotional volatility. By 1846 the household was no longer the comfortable Paris-winters/Nohant-summers rhythm it had been in 1840-43.

#### The works as biographical context, not as biographical narrative

A key point for the chapters that follow. The works of the Sand period are not Sand-relationship documentaries. The Op. 28 Preludes do not narrate the Mallorca winter; the Op. 35 Sonata does not narrate Sand's emotional weather; the Op. 47 Ballade No. 3 does not narrate the Nohant summer of 1841. The works were composed in the Sand-relationship context, and the relationship provided the practical conditions for their composition, but the works themselves are art-music rather than biographical document.

This book holds the Sand-period works as Chopin's middle-period output, with the Sand relationship as part of the practical context. We will not write of the Op. 28 No. 15 "Raindrop" as "Chopin's response to the Mallorca rain at Valldemossa" (the popular reception's reading; the documentation is loose, and the music does not need the reading) or of the Op. 35 Funeral March as "Chopin's response to his deteriorating health" (the popular reading; the documentation again loose). The chapters that follow describe the composition setting and let the music speak.

### 8.5 The Lucrezia Floriani Controversy

A short note on Sand's 1846 novel _Lucrezia Floriani_. The novel tells the story of an aging Italian actress (Lucrezia Floriani) who falls in love with a younger Polish prince (Karol). Karol is described as physically delicate, emotionally fragile, jealous, oppressive in his love, and ultimately destructive. Lucrezia carries his weight and is worn down by it.

The novel was widely read in Paris in 1846. Chopin certainly read it. Sand later claimed she had not modeled Karol on Chopin specifically — that the character was a literary type, not a portrait. Modern scholarship divides on this question: some biographers (Niecks 1888, others) take the Karol-as-Chopin reading as obvious; others (Walker, Zamoyski) treat Sand's denial as plausible and the resemblance as partly coincidental.

What is on the record: the Karol character has substantial overlap with Chopin's known temperament; Sand did write the novel during the period when the Chopin partnership was becoming strained; the publication of _Lucrezia Floriani_ coincided with a worsening of the Sand-Chopin relationship in late 1846. Whether Sand intended the portrait or whether the portrait was inadvertent, the publication of the novel was followed by deterioration in the partnership; Sand may have been working through her feelings about Chopin in fictional form, or she may have been writing a separate story that her partner happened to recognize himself in.

The controversy matters for the biographical reading because it tells the reader something about how Sand was thinking about Chopin in late 1846 — whether or not the novel was a deliberate portrait, the resemblance is real and the timing is suggestive. The break in 1847 was already prefigured in this 1846 fiction.

### 8.6 The 1847 Break

#### The Solange marriage

The proximate trigger for the Sand-Chopin break in 1847 was the marriage of Solange (Sand's daughter) to the sculptor Auguste Clésinger. Clésinger was a financially-uncertain, emotionally-difficult man (he had a known history of debt and erratic behavior); Sand opposed the marriage from the start. Solange, however, was determined; the marriage went ahead in May 1847 over Sand's continued objection.

The conflict that followed was over family loyalty. Solange sided with her husband against her mother; Maurice sided with his mother; Sand wanted Chopin to side with her. Chopin, characteristically, refused to take a strong side — he was sympathetic to Solange (his warmer relationship across the eight Nohant years), critical of Clésinger (whom he had limited respect for), and unwilling to abandon Solange even when Sand demanded that he do so. Chopin's refusal to choose Sand over Solange precipitated the break.

The actual events of July 1847: a violent argument between Sand and Solange-and-Clésinger at Nohant; Sand expelling Solange and Clésinger from the estate; Solange writing to Chopin in Paris asking him to provide his carriage to bring her and Clésinger back to Paris; Chopin sending the carriage. The sending of the carriage was the act that, from Sand's perspective, demonstrated that Chopin had chosen Solange over her.

The Sand-Chopin correspondence in late 1847 documents the break. Sand's letter of July 28, 1847 is the principal document: she wrote to Chopin in characteristically direct terms, asserting that the partnership was over, accusing Chopin of betrayal, framing the carriage-incident as the proof. Chopin's response (lost, but the substance is reported in his letters to others) was that he had not chosen Solange over Sand but had simply provided practical help to a young woman in distress. Sand did not accept this; the partnership was finished.

#### After the break

Chopin and Sand did not reconcile. They saw each other once more, briefly and accidentally, on a Paris staircase in March 1848 (the "passing on the staircase" encounter recorded in the contemporary letters; Sand told Chopin about Maurice's marriage prospects; the exchange lasted minutes); they had no further contact. Chopin's tuberculosis worsened across 1848 and 1849; Sand was not present at his death on October 17, 1849; she was at Nohant when she received the news.

Sand wrote about Chopin in her memoir _Histoire de ma vie_ (1854-55), published five years after his death. The memoir's account is sympathetic to Chopin in places and critical in others; Sand attempted retrospectively to frame the partnership as one in which she had been the carer and Chopin had been the difficult-but-beloved patient. The memoir's account is partly authoritative (Sand was there for nine years) and partly self-shaping (written after the break and after Chopin's death, with all the retrospective shaping that allowed). Modern scholarship treats _Histoire de ma vie_ as a primary source that must be read against Chopin's own letters and against the other independent recollections; no single source is determinative.

Sand survived Chopin by twenty-six years; she died in 1876 at age 72. Her literary career continued through the 1850s and 60s and into the 1870s; she remained one of the major French novelists of the 19th century until her death. Chopin's death at thirty-nine cut short what Sand's longer life lets us see — what Chopin might have written if he had lived another decade or two.

### 8.7 Reading the Sand Relationship Today

A short summary. The honest contemporary reading of the Sand-Chopin partnership emphasizes:

* The relationship was real, was generative for nine years, and was important to both partners.
* The relationship was also complex, sometimes difficult, and was not a stable bourgeois marriage but a long-term unmarried partnership with significant tensions.
* The Sand-as-generative-Muse reading and the Sand-as-destructive-Lover reading are both 19th-century melodramas; neither serves the music or the people.
* The Nohant summers provided the practical conditions for most of the major Sand-period works; the works themselves are not biographical documents but art-music compositions.
* The 1847 break was proximately about the Solange marriage but had deeper roots in the accumulated tensions of the previous years.
* The works of the Sand period (Chapters 9 through 13) should be heard as Chopin's mature middle-period art-music, with the Sand context as background rather than as interpretive key.

The chapters that follow will treat the Sand context as background and will not embroider the biographical dimension into the musical analysis. Chapter 9 covers the Op. 28 Preludes (composed across 1838-39, completed at Valldemossa); Chapter 10 the four Ballades; Chapter 11 the four Scherzos; Chapter 12 the Sonatas and the Cello Sonata; Chapter 13 the late Mazurkas and the Waltzes.

Pick whichever 19th-century melodrama is more familiar to you and describe specifically what the complex reading removes from it, and what it puts in its place."_

What does this tell you about the relationship between composing conditions and compositional output, and is there a generalizable lesson?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

There is no listening for this chapter. If you wish to begin orienting your ear toward the works that will be discussed in the chapters that follow, listen briefly to:

  1. **The Op. 28 No. 15 in D♭ major (the "Raindrop")** — about 5 minutes. This is the Op. 28 Prelude most-associated in 19th-century reception with the Mallorca winter (the "Raindrop" nickname is attributed in some sources to Sand). Chapter 9 will return to it; you might hear it once now as a partial anchor for the Mallorca-winter setting.

  2. **The Op. 35 third movement Funeral March** — about 9 minutes. Composed at Nohant in 1839 (the funeral march itself was composed separately in 1837 and incorporated into the 1839 sonata). Chapter 12 will return to it. The first Nohant-summer work; the most-famous Chopin slow-movement after the Op. 21 Larghetto.

  3. **The Op. 53 Polonaise "Heroic"** — about 7 minutes. Composed at Nohant in 1842. Chapter 14 will return to it. The most-celebrated of the Nohant-summer works in 19th-century reception.

### Going Deeper

**Read** : the chapters on the Sand relationship in Alan Walker, _Fryderyk Chopin: A Life and Times_ (FSG, 2018). Walker's treatment is the contemporary biographical standard. He covers the 1836 meeting, the 1837 hesitation, the 1838 partnership, the Mallorca winter, the Nohant summers, and the 1847 break with documentary rigor and without either the Sand-as-Muse or Sand-as-destroyer melodrama. About 150 pages across the central biographical chapters; the relevant chapters can be read across one or two weeks alongside the present chapter.

## Chapter 9: The Preludes Op. 28 (1839)

The twenty-four Preludes Op. 28 are the work that most directly carries the Mallorca winter into the Chopin catalogue. Most were drafted in Paris before the November 1838 departure; most were completed or revised during the difficult Valldemossa stay (November 1838 - February 1839); the set was published in September 1839 by Catelin in Paris (the French publisher), with the German publication by Breitkopf & Härtel in Leipzig and the English publication by Wessel in London. The set is one of the central documents of Chopin's middle period.

The set is organized as twenty-four preludes, one in each major and minor key. The model is Bach's _Well-Tempered Clavier_ — but Bach's WTC pairs each prelude with a fugue (Bach: 24 preludes-and-fugues; Chopin: 24 preludes alone). Chopin's other departure from the Bach model is the ordering. Bach's WTC ascends chromatically (C major, C minor, C♯ major, C♯ minor, D major, D minor, and so on); Chopin's Op. 28 follows the circle of fifths (C major / A minor; G major / E minor; D major / B minor; A major / F♯ minor; E major / C♯ minor; B major / G♯ minor; F♯ major / E♭ minor; D♭ major / B♭ minor; A♭ major / F minor; E♭ major / C minor; B♭ major / G minor; F major / D minor). The Chopin ordering pairs each major-key prelude with its relative minor (the minor key sharing the same key signature) and arranges the pairs in the circle-of-fifths sequence.

The result is a coherent ~35-minute cycle. The preludes range in length from well under a minute (No. 7 in A major is about 45 seconds) to over four minutes (No. 15 in D♭ major and No. 24 in D minor are the longest, around 5 minutes each). The moods range across the full Chopin expressive vocabulary — calm-lyric (No. 7, No. 11), stormy (No. 16, No. 22, No. 24), melancholic (No. 4, No. 6, No. 20), brilliant (No. 12, No. 16, No. 24), restless (No. 1, No. 2), serene (No. 23). The set is a survey of the Chopin expressive register in concentrated miniature form.

The chapter anchors on four specific preludes: **No. 4 in E minor** (~2 minutes), **No. 15 in D♭ major (the "Raindrop")** (~5 minutes), **No. 20 in C minor** (~2 minutes), and **No. 24 in D minor** (~3 minutes). The chapter also discusses the cycle as a whole — the question of whether the twenty-four preludes work as a unified work or as a collection of twenty-four independent miniatures.

### 9.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

January 28, 1839. The Pleyel piano that Chopin had been waiting for since November had finally arrived at Valldemossa. The instrument had been delayed by Mallorcan customs (a duty dispute over the piano's classification took weeks to resolve) and by the difficulty of transporting a Pleyel from Palma up the mountain road to Valldemossa. By the time it arrived in late January Chopin had been at Valldemossa for almost three months working on a small inadequate upright; the Pleyel's arrival was the moment he could properly complete the Preludes that had been in draft since Paris.

The household at Valldemossa through January 1839: Chopin, Sand, Maurice (now sixteen), Solange (eleven), and the local servant they had hired. The conditions had been difficult — the cells of the former monastery were unheated, the local food was unfamiliar, the local population's attitudes were hostile, Chopin's health was deteriorating, the work had been disrupted. But the work had continued. Sand wrote in _Un hiver à Majorque_ about Chopin composing through coughing fits, working on what would become the Preludes through the worst of the Mallorca winter.

The Op. 28 Preludes were largely complete by mid-February 1839, when the household began the journey back to Marseille. The final revisions and the manuscript preparation continued through the spring at Marseille; the autograph fair copy was sent to the publisher Catelin in Paris in summer 1839 and the set was published in September.

For the listener: the Op. 28 Preludes carry the difficulty of their composition without making it the music's content. The set is not "the Mallorca winter set"; the popular nickname of No. 15 as the "Raindrop" — attributed in some sources to Sand and to the Valldemossa storms — does not constitute evidence that the work depicts rain or storms specifically. The set is a survey of the Chopin expressive register; the Mallorca context is the practical setting of its completion, not the musical content.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

The Op. 28 Preludes are best heard as a complete cycle on a first listen, with the individual preludes received as parts of a sequence rather than as isolated pieces. The complete cycle runs about 35 minutes; many recordings include it complete on a single disc.

For each of the four anchor preludes:

  1. **No. 4 in E minor (~2 minutes).** Listen for the _descending bass line and the static-ostinato-chord right-hand pattern_. The piece's main structure is the slow descent of the bass through chromatic motion while the right hand plays repeated chords in a static rhythmic pattern. The melodic interest is concentrated in the right hand's chord-voicing changes and in the bass's descending line; the overall mood is one of suspended grief.

  2. **No. 15 in D♭ major (the "Raindrop", ~5 minutes).** Listen for the _repeated A♭ in the inner voice_ — through almost the whole piece, an inner voice (in the middle of the texture) pulses the same note A♭ (or G♯ in the C♯-minor middle section, which is the enharmonic equivalent). This pulsing inner voice is what the popular "Raindrop" nickname refers to. The outer sections are in D♭ major and are calm-lyric; the middle section moves to C♯ minor and becomes darker, more chordal, more intense. The return to D♭ major after the C♯-minor middle section is the work's emotional center.

  3. **No. 20 in C minor (~2 minutes).** Listen for the _chordal, march-like, slow texture_ in C minor. The piece is short, slow, and dignified — almost a funeral march or a chorale. Many funerals through 19th- and 20th-century reception have used this piece. The contemporary record indicates it was played at Chopin's own funeral at the Madeleine on October 30, 1849 (per the order of service, though the documentation of the specific Preludes used at the funeral is not as detailed as for other pieces like the Mozart Requiem).

  4. **No. 24 in D minor (~3 minutes).** Listen for the _cascading-left-hand virtuosity and right-hand defiant melodic line_ — similar to the Op. 10 No. 12 "Revolutionary" texture but in D minor and with a more sustained dramatic build. The piece closes the cycle with three forte D-minor low-bass octaves — the most dramatic ending in the Chopin catalogue.

#### One-sentence background

Composed mostly 1838-39 (some preludes drafted earlier, possibly back to 1835); the bulk of completion and revision at Valldemossa winter 1838-39; published September 1839 by Catelin in Paris; dedicated to Camille Pleyel (the publisher in some editions; the French Catelin edition dedicated to Pleyel) and Joseph Kessler (the German Breitkopf & Härtel edition dedicated to Kessler). The complete cycle runs about 35 minutes. The **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Martha Argerich on Deutsche Grammophon (recorded 1977, the complete Op. 28 cycle on a single LP), chosen for the electric energy across the cycle and the canonical Argerich-Chopin reading.
