* Maurizio Pollini on DG (1974 Preludes) — the modernist-clean reading; cooler than Argerich, very precise.
* Sviatoslav Richter, various live recordings of selected preludes (1950s-70s) — the Russian-school reading; particularly strong in the slower, more inward preludes.
* Krystian Zimerman on DG (1999 Ballades + Preludes) — though Zimerman has not recorded the complete Op. 28, his selected-prelude recordings provide the Polish-school perspective.

### 9.2 The Listening Window

Timestamps refer to the Argerich 1977 Primary Recording.

**No. 4 in E minor** (about 2:00):

* **0:00 Opening.** Right hand enters with sustained chord; left hand begins the descending bass line.
* **About 0:30 Descending bass continues.** The chromatic descent of the bass is the piece's structural spine. The right-hand chords change in voicing but the rhythmic pattern is static.
* **About 1:00 A brief harmonic excursion.** The descending bass arrives at a moment of harmonic tension; the music holds.
* **About 1:30 Final descent.** The bass continues its descent to the lowest point.
* **About 2:00 Final cadence.** The piece ends with a quiet, unresolved E-minor close.

**No. 15 in D♭ major "Raindrop"** (about 5:00):

* **0:00 Opening.** Listen for the _inner voice_ from the second or third bar — a single repeating A♭ in the middle of the texture, sitting between the right hand's lyric melody (above) and the left hand's broken-chord accompaniment (below). The pulse is steady, eighth-note speed, almost mechanically regular. Once you hear it, it does not stop.
* **About 1:30 The middle section in C♯ minor.** Listen for the moment the harmony slides from D♭ major into C♯ minor (the parallel minor, written enharmonically — the _same key signature_ on paper, the same pitches at the keyboard, but the chordal vocabulary darkens). Listen specifically: the pulsing A♭ from the opening is _still there_ , now spelled as G♯ on the page but the same pitch in your ear. The continuity of the inner voice across the modulation is what makes the piece a single argument rather than two contrasting sections.
* **About 3:00 The middle section's climax.** Listen for the C♯-minor chords thickening — four-voice chordal writing in the right hand, fuller accompaniment in the left hand, the pulsing inner voice now embedded in dense chord-stacks rather than sitting transparently between melody and bass. The dynamic reaches forte and stays there.
* **About 4:00 Return to D♭ major.** Listen for the moment the chords _brighten_ — the same pulsing inner voice, but now sitting again between a lyric melody above and a gentle accompaniment below. The return is a relief, not a triumph.
* **About 5:00 Final cadence.** The piece ends quietly in D♭ major. Listen for the inner voice's final pulses — they fade with the piece rather than stopping abruptly.

**No. 20 in C minor** (about 1:30):

* **0:00 Opening.** Slow, chordal, march-like, in C minor.
* **About 0:30 Middle phrase.** The chordal writing continues; the dynamic builds slightly.
* **About 1:00 Closing phrase.** The music descends toward the cadence.
* **About 1:30 Final cadence.** The piece ends quietly in C minor.

**No. 24 in D minor** (about 2:30):

* **0:00 Opening.** Listen for the left hand entering alone with a _torrent_ of fast notes — cascading downward and back up across nearly three octaves. The right hand enters within two seconds with a chord-melody in D minor that has the _quarter-eighth-eighth_ dotted rhythmic profile of a defiant march. Compare the texture to the Op. 10 No. 12 "Revolutionary" (Chapter 6): same cascade-and-chord-melody combination, now compressed into a prelude.
* **About 0:30 First harmonic shift.** The cascade continues; the harmonic motion moves through related keys. Listen for the right hand's melodic figure being _transposed_ into the new keys while the left-hand cascade keeps its shape.
* **About 1:30 Climactic build.** Listen for the dynamic climbing toward fortissimo and the right-hand melody reaching its highest register. The left hand's cascade now spans an even wider keyboard range.
* **About 2:15 The three final octaves.** Listen specifically for the _three_ fortissimo D-minor low-bass octaves at the very end — struck cleanly, evenly spaced, no flourish between them. The cycle does not fade into silence (the way the Funeral March will, four years later in 1839); it closes with three blows. This is the most dramatic ending in the Chopin catalogue.

### 9.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The cycle as a whole

The Op. 28 Preludes raise the question of whether the twenty-four preludes constitute a single cycle (one work of about 35 minutes' duration) or a collection of twenty-four independent miniatures (each work performable on its own). Chopin's contemporaries disagreed; modern performance practice divides; scholarly opinion divides.

The arguments for the cycle reading: the twenty-four preludes were composed and published as a set; the circle-of-fifths ordering creates a sense of progression through all the keys; the dramatic shape of the set (calm openings; stormy preludes in the middle; the dramatic D-minor close) suggests an overall design; Robert Schumann's 1839 review treated the set as a single work; many 19th- and 20th-century pianists have performed the set complete.

The arguments for the collection reading: individual preludes were composed at different times and may have had independent existence before being assembled; the lengths vary so widely (45 seconds to 5 minutes) that the set lacks the formal coherence of a single work; many 19th-century pianists performed selected preludes rather than the complete set; the title "Preludes" suggests works that were originally intended to precede something else (a fugue, a suite, a longer work) — but here precede nothing.

The honest reading: both are partly true. The set can be performed complete (as Argerich does in the Primary Recording, as Pollini does in his 1974 recording, as Sokolov does in his 1990s recordings) and works as a cycle; the individual preludes can also be performed in isolation and work as independent miniatures. The Op. 28 is the rare case of a work that succeeds in both modes.

#### The Bach connection

A short note on the Bach connection. Bach's _Well-Tempered Clavier_ (Book 1 1722; Book 2 1740s) is the precedent for any composer writing twenty-four pieces in all the major and minor keys. Chopin certainly knew Bach's WTC; the Eigeldinger evidence and the Chopin pedagogy testify that Bach was central to his teaching. The Op. 28 Preludes are partly an homage to the WTC tradition.

But the differences are more important than the resemblances. Bach's WTC pairs each prelude with a fugue and presents the pairs as compositions of comparable substance; Chopin's Op. 28 is preludes only. Bach's WTC orders the pieces chromatically; Chopin's orders them by the circle of fifths with major-minor pairing. Bach's WTC preludes range from minute-and-a-half pieces to large four-minute structures; Chopin's preludes range from 45 seconds to five minutes. Bach's preludes are functional (each precedes a fugue and provides a contrasting opener); Chopin's preludes precede nothing.

The deeper difference: Bach's WTC is a pedagogical-encyclopedic work demonstrating the well-tempered tuning system's musical possibilities across all keys. Chopin's Op. 28 is an emotional-encyclopedic work surveying the Chopin expressive register through twenty-four miniature character pieces. The Bach is a tonal-system encyclopedia; the Chopin is an emotional-style encyclopedia.

#### The harmonic miniatures

Each Op. 28 prelude is a miniature in form — typically through-composed (no clear ABA structure) or in a simple two-or-three-section shape. The miniature form puts pressure on the harmonic content; each prelude has to make its musical point in a short span, which means the harmonic motion has to be efficient. The Op. 28 preludes are among the most harmonically concentrated works in the Chopin catalogue.

Some examples: No. 1 in C major is about 30 seconds long; it consists of a single rising figure repeated and varied, with harmonic shifts every couple of bars. No. 2 in A minor is about a minute and a half; the left hand plays a static repeating bass while the right hand explores chromatic descents — a strange, harmonically restless piece. No. 4 in E minor is about two minutes; the descending bass and the static-chord right hand carry the piece without melodic development. No. 7 in A major is about 45 seconds; a single short lyric phrase, repeated with slight variation. No. 20 in C minor is about a minute and a half; chordal, march-like, slow.

The miniature form is what makes the cycle a survey rather than a single argument. Each prelude is its own moment; the cycle is the accumulation of twenty-four moments.

#### The "Raindrop" question

A short note on the No. 15 nickname. The "Raindrop" nickname for the D♭-major Prelude is attributed in some 19th-century sources to George Sand — the popular story has Sand returning from a rainy walk in Mallorca to find Chopin at the piano with the prelude in progress, and either Chopin or Sand connecting the music's pulsing inner voice to the sound of raindrops. The nickname appears in Niecks 1888 and in subsequent biographies.

The documentation is not strong. Chopin did not use the nickname; Sand's own writings (_Un hiver à Majorque_ , _Histoire de ma vie_) do not directly claim it. The "Raindrop" association is reception-history, plausibly dating to the late 1840s or 1850s. Chopin himself reportedly rejected pictorial readings of his music (Chopin's discouragement of programmatic interpretations is mentioned in the pupil-recollection literature gathered by Eigeldinger), and the "Raindrop" reading is exactly the kind of pictorial association he discouraged.

As you listen: the pulsing inner voice is a real musical feature of the piece, and it does sound something like a steady rhythmic background — like rain, or like a clock, or like a heartbeat. The "Raindrop" label is convenient but not definitive. The piece works without it.

#### The No. 20 funeral-music role

The C-minor No. 20 has become one of the standard pieces for funeral and memorial occasions across the 19th, 20th, and 21st centuries. Its chordal, slow, dignified character suits the form. The contemporary evidence indicates it was used at Chopin's own funeral at the Madeleine on October 30, 1849, alongside Mozart's Requiem and other works.

The No. 20 has also been adapted by other composers — Ferruccio Busoni's _Variations on a Chopin Prelude_ (1922) takes the No. 20 as its theme; Sergei Rachmaninoff's _Variations on a Theme of Chopin Op. 22_ (1903) also uses the No. 20 as the theme. The work's prelude-status (a 90-second miniature) has not prevented it from becoming one of the most-used Chopin works across reception.

#### The closing D-minor cadence

The three fortissimo D-minor low-bass octaves at the end of No. 24 are the most dramatic ending in the Chopin catalogue. They close the cycle with a force that no other Chopin work matches. The choice to close the twenty-four-prelude cycle with this assertive, almost angry ending is one of Chopin's structural choices that argues for the cycle reading (the dramatic ending implies a dramatic arc across the whole set).

Modern performers vary in how they play the three octaves. Argerich plays them with full forte but with a touch of restraint; Pollini plays them with more measured precision; some Polish-school pianists play them with more weight; the live-concert pianists often play them with maximum forte. The dramatic ending is a deliberate part of the cycle.

### 9.4 The Stories

#### What the cycle is and is not

The Op. 28 Preludes are not the most-immediately-loved Chopin set. The Op. 9 Nocturnes are more accessible; the Op. 53 Polonaise is more dramatic; the Ballade No. 1 is more famous. The Op. 28 is the more cerebral set — twenty-four miniatures requiring sustained attention across thirty-five minutes. A first-time listener may find the cycle demanding; a second listen reveals more; a third listen reveals more again.

What the Op. 28 is: a comprehensive survey of the Chopin expressive register in twenty-four concentrated miniatures, arranged in a coherent circle-of-fifths sequence, completed under difficult conditions at Valldemossa. It is one of the central works of 19th-century piano literature.

What the Op. 28 is not: it is not a programmatic work depicting the Mallorca winter (popular reception has tried to read it this way, but the documentation does not support it). It is not a set of preludes designed to precede something else (Chopin's preludes precede nothing). It is not a Bach-style pedagogical-encyclopedic work (the WTC is the pedagogical-encyclopedic work; the Op. 28 is the emotional-encyclopedic Chopin equivalent).

#### The Schumann review

Robert Schumann reviewed the Op. 28 in his _Neue Zeitschrift für Musik_ in late 1839 (the review appeared after the September 1839 publication). Schumann's review is one of the strongest documents of contemporary critical reception. He described the set as "twenty-four sketches" of various moods, characterized the cycle as "wild and various" in its expressive range, and treated the Preludes as significant compositional work.

Schumann's most-quoted phrase from the review: "Their character is unique. Each one is a sketch, the beginning of something larger." This reading frames the preludes as fragmentary — each one suggesting a larger work that the prelude initiates without completing. The reading has some textual support (the prelude form historically does suggest something coming) but Chopin himself did not present them this way (his preludes precede nothing).

The Schumann reading is therefore one possibility. The cycle reading (the twenty-four as a unified work) is another. The collection reading (the twenty-four as independent miniatures) is a third. The set supports all three readings; performance practice and listening practice both accommodate the multiple frames.

#### Recording history briefly

The Op. 28 Preludes have been recorded many times. The major lineages divide as follows.

The Cortot lineage. Alfred Cortot recorded the complete Op. 28 multiple times; the 1933-34 EMI recording is the canonical pre-war document; the 1942 and 1949 recordings continue the tradition. Cortot's reading is the rubato extreme, full of interpretive freedom and occasional wrong notes; the historical document.

The Argerich lineage. Martha Argerich recorded the complete Op. 28 in 1977 for DG (the Primary Recording for this chapter). Her reading is the most-electric major recording — fast tempos, extreme dynamic range, sharp articulation. Some listeners find it overwhelming; others find it the most-revealing reading of the dramatic shape of the cycle.

The Pollini lineage. Maurizio Pollini recorded the complete Op. 28 in 1974 for DG (one of his first major Chopin recordings after the 1972 Etudes). The reading is the modernist-clean standard — extreme precision, minimal rubato, linear articulation. Many consider it the safe-ground reference.

The Sokolov lineage. Grigory Sokolov has recorded several live concert performances of the complete Op. 28 since the 1990s. His readings are slow, weighted, and harmonically deep; they push the cycle toward more profound spaciousness than Argerich's electric reading.

The Polish-school lineage. Several Polish-school pianists have recorded the complete Op. 28. The Zimerman documentation is partial. The Argerich and Pollini DG readings are the safe ground for the contemporary listener.

For the four anchor preludes specifically, the Argerich 1977 is the chapter's Primary; the Pollini 1974 is the alternative; the Sokolov readings (especially live recordings from Berlin in the 1990s) offer a deeper, slower perspective.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The Op. 28 connects to many works.

To the late nocturnes (Chapter 7). The harmonic experimentation of the Op. 28 No. 2 (A minor) and No. 22 (G minor) prefigures the late-style nocturne harmony of Op. 62. The miniature form of the preludes does not allow the late nocturne's larger structural development, but the harmonic vocabulary is consistent.

To the late mazurkas (Chapter 13). The harmonic concentration of the Op. 28 preludes is similar to the harmonic concentration of the late mazurkas; both are miniatures with substantial harmonic content per minute.

To the Op. 35 Sonata (Chapter 12). The Op. 35 Sonata was composed at Nohant in 1839, in the same year as the Op. 28 publication. The two works share certain harmonic moves; the dramatic-funeral-march character of the Op. 35 third movement is anticipated in the Op. 28 No. 20.

To the Op. 49 Fantasy (Chapter 15). The free-form structural ambition of the Op. 49 builds partly on the through-composed shape of some of the longer Op. 28 preludes (No. 15, No. 24).

#### The biographical context: 1838-1839

Anchoring the chapter biographically: the Op. 28 Preludes were completed during the Mallorca winter (November 1838 - February 1839) and the early Nohant spring (1839). The cycle is therefore the work that most directly carries the Mallorca conditions into the catalogue. The publication in September 1839 brought the set into the Paris-and-international market just as Chopin and Sand were settling into the Nohant-summer rhythm that would characterize the next eight years.

The Mallorca winter was the worst period of Chopin's physical health to that date. The Pleyel piano arrived late; the cells were cold; the local conditions were hostile. That the cycle was completed in this setting is one of the established facts about Chopin's working life — the work continued through difficulty.

Chapter 10 covers the Ballades, which were composed across 1835-1842 and include works begun before and during the Sand years. Chapter 11 the Scherzos. Chapter 12 the Sonatas including the Op. 35 (composed at Nohant in 1839, the same year as the Op. 28 publication).

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The Op. 28 Preludes have been read variously as a cycle (one work of 35 minutes), as a collection (24 independent miniatures), and as Schumann's 'sketches' (each prelude the beginning of something larger). Pick one of the three readings and argue for it on the basis of having listened to the complete cycle."_
* _"The 'Raindrop' nickname for the No. 15 in D♭ major is attributed in some sources to George Sand. Chopin himself did not use the nickname and reportedly discouraged pictorial readings of his music. Listening to the prelude, does the pulsing inner voice suggest rain, or could it suggest other things equally well?"_
* _"The Op. 28 closes with the No. 24 in D minor — three fortissimo D-minor low-bass octaves at the end. What does this dramatic close do to your sense of the cycle's overall arc, and would the set work as well if it closed with a quieter piece?"_
* _"The Mallorca winter of 1838-39 was difficult — cold, illness, isolation. Yet most of the Op. 28 was completed there. Does the difficulty of the setting come through in any of the preludes, or are the music and the setting independent?"_
* _"The Op. 28 No. 20 in C minor has become standard funeral music in Western culture across two centuries. What about the piece — its slow tempo, its chordal texture, its C minor — makes it suit the funeral occasion, and is it possible to hear it without the funeral associations?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Argerich 1977 Op. 28 Preludes (or the alternatives). Listen to the four anchor preludes a second time and attend to these moments:

  1. **No. 4 in E minor (about 2 minutes): the descending bass.** Listen _only_ to the left hand if you can; the chromatic descending bass is the piece's structural spine. The right hand's chord-pulsing is the static rhythmic frame above. The interaction of moving bass and static treble is the piece's design.

  2. **No. 15 "Raindrop" (about 5 minutes): the pulsing inner voice.** Listen for the steady pulsing A♭ (or G♯ in the middle section) — once you can hear it, you will hear it through almost the whole piece. The pulsing is the piece's continuous element across the major-to-minor-and-back shift.

  3. **No. 20 in C minor (about 90 seconds): the chordal slow texture.** Listen to the whole piece; notice how brief and how complete it is. Ninety seconds of chordal slow C-minor writing accomplishes what a longer piece might struggle to achieve.

  4. **No. 24 in D minor (about 2:30): the closing three octaves.** Listen for the last few seconds — the three D-minor low-bass octaves are the most dramatic ending in the Chopin catalogue. Notice how they close not just No. 24 but the whole cycle.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair the Op. 28 with the first volume of Bach's _Well-Tempered Clavier_ (1722) — particularly the C-major Prelude (the first piece, ~2 minutes; the Glenn Gould 1962 recording on Columbia or the Sviatoslav Richter 1971 RCA recording is the standard) and one of the minor-key fugues. The Bach C-major Prelude is the textbook model that Chopin had in mind when he conceived the Op. 28; listening to it before listening to Chopin's No. 1 in C major shows what Chopin inherited (the prelude-as-opener idea) and how he transformed it (his C-major prelude is half as long, more harmonically restless, less linear in development). About fifteen minutes total.

**Read** : the chapter on the Op. 28 in Jim Samson, _Chopin_ (Oxford Master Musicians series, 1996). Samson treats the cycle question thoughtfully, discusses the Bach connection, analyzes several of the individual preludes, and surveys the reception. About twenty pages; one of the most useful single English-language analytical treatments of the work.

## Chapter 10: The Ballades (1835-1842)

The four Chopin Ballades — Op. 23 in G minor (1835), Op. 38 in F major (1839), Op. 47 in A♭ major (1841), Op. 52 in F minor (1842) — are one of the great single-movement arcs in 19th-century piano music. Chopin invented the keyboard ballade nearly from scratch; the genre had not existed in his form before him, and the four Ballades together are the genre's creation, development, and peak. By the time Chopin finished the Ballade No. 4 in 1842, the genre was complete; he did not write a fifth.

This chapter anchors on the first and the fourth — the Ballade No. 1 in G minor Op. 23 (re-anchoring from _Close Listening: Classical Music_ Chapter 17) and the Ballade No. 4 in F minor Op. 52 (the late peak). The two together demonstrate the arc: the No. 1 is the genre's invention with all the original-discovery energy that goes with it; the No. 4 is the genre's late master-work with all the seven-years-of-development behind it. The chapter also discusses Nos. 2 and 3 in passing.

### 10.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

The Adam Mickiewicz connection. Adam Mickiewicz (1798-1855) was the major Polish poet of the 19th century. He was twelve years older than Chopin and had already become a Polish cultural reference point during Chopin's Warsaw youth (the _Ballady i romanse_ poetry collection of 1822 had been a foundational text for the Polish Romantic literary movement); he arrived in Paris in 1832 and lived there for most of the 1830s; Chopin and Mickiewicz were friends across the Paris Polish-émigré community.

The Schumann letter. Robert Schumann wrote in a letter dated November 12, 1841 — to a friend in Leipzig — that Chopin had told him at a Leipzig meeting (in September 1836, when Chopin was traveling through Germany) that his ballades had been "inspired by" Mickiewicz poems. Schumann is the source; Chopin himself did not write a similar statement in his own surviving letters. Schumann did not name specific Mickiewicz poems that were said to be the inspiration.

The popular reception has linked specific Chopin Ballades to specific Mickiewicz poems:

* Ballade No. 1 in G minor → _Konrad Wallenrod_ (1828), Mickiewicz's long narrative poem
* Ballade No. 2 in F major → _Świtezianka_ (1822), Mickiewicz's lake-spirit ballad
* Ballade No. 3 in A♭ major → _Świteź_ (1822), another Mickiewicz lake ballad
* Ballade No. 4 in F minor → _The Three Budryses_ (1827), Mickiewicz's ballad about three Lithuanian brothers

These specific pairings are 19th- and early-20th-century reception readings; they appear in Niecks 1888, in various Polish-school biographies, in popular program notes. The documentation for the specific pairings is thin. Chopin did not name them; Schumann did not name them; the pairings developed through subsequent critical commentary.

The honest reading: Chopin's ballades carry a literary-narrative character that the keyboard genre had not had before him. The connection to Mickiewicz is attested in the broad sense (Schumann's 1841 letter testifying to Chopin's stated link) but not in the specific pairings. The ballades are narrative without text; the listener feels a story is being told without the composer naming the story. This is the genre's signature innovation.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

For each of the two anchor ballades:

  1. **Ballade No. 1 in G minor Op. 23 (re-anchoring from _Close Listening: Classical Music_).** Listen for the _opening slow narrative_ : a Largo introduction of about fifteen seconds, then a slow narrative-like main theme in G minor. The piece builds gradually across about ten minutes. Listen for the contrasting middle section in E♭ major (the relative major). Listen for the climactic coda in G minor — a faster tempo, increasing dynamic, rapid figuration, building to a fortissimo close.

  2. **Ballade No. 4 in F minor Op. 52.** Listen for the _opening introduction_ : about thirty seconds of preparatory music in C major before the F-minor main theme arrives. Listen for the structural depth across about twelve minutes — the longest and densest of the four Ballades. Listen for the multiple thematic returns, each transformed by what came before. Listen for the closing coda — also fast, also fortissimo, but with a more harmonically restless build than the No. 1's.

#### One-sentence background

Ballade No. 1 in G minor Op. 23 (composed 1835; published 1836; ~9 minutes). Ballade No. 4 in F minor Op. 52 (composed 1842; published 1843; ~12 minutes). The **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Krystian Zimerman on Deutsche Grammophon (recorded 1987, the complete four Ballades on a single LP), chosen for the Polish-school depth and the canonical contemporary reading. Zimerman won the 1975 Chopin Competition; the 1987 Ballades recording was made at the peak of his early career and is the standard reference.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Chopin Ballades Zimerman 1987" or "Chopin Ballade G minor Op 23" / "Chopin Ballade No 4 F minor Op 52" ZH: "肖邦 叙事曲 齐默尔曼" 或 "肖邦 g小调第一叙事曲" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** :

* Arthur Rubinstein on RCA (recorded 1959 mono and 1959 stereo, the four Ballades) — the canonical mid-20th-century reading; warmer than Zimerman, less dramatically pointed.
* Martha Argerich, various recordings of the Ballades (the 1965 DG recording of Op. 23 alone is famous; later live recordings include Nos. 1, 3, 4) — the electric Argerich-Chopin reading.
* Stefan Askenase on DG (1955 four Ballades) — the Polish-school predecessor to Zimerman; warm, songful, slightly slower.

### 10.2 The Listening Window

Timestamps refer to the Zimerman 1987 Primary Recording.

**Ballade No. 1 in G minor Op. 23** (about 9:30):

* **0:00 Largo introduction.** A slow, weighted introduction in C major (or the related Neapolitan harmonic area; analysts disagree on the exact tonal frame). The piece begins not in G minor (the home key) but in this unrelated key, which is a striking opening choice.
* **About 0:25 The main G-minor theme.** The narrative main theme arrives in G minor (slow, lyric, with a melancholic and almost vocal character). The bel-canto inheritance is clear.
* **About 1:30 Development of the main theme.** The melody is repeated and elaborated.
* **About 3:00 The E♭ major second theme.** A contrasting second theme in E♭ major (the relative major). Brighter, more hopeful, with a contrasting melodic shape.
* **About 4:30 Development episode.** The two themes are developed; the harmonic motion becomes more restless.
* **About 6:00 Return.** The main themes return, varied.
* **About 7:30 The coda begins.** A faster tempo; rapid figuration; the texture intensifies.
* **About 8:30 Climactic build.** The coda's intensity peaks.
* **About 9:00 The closing pages.** Fortissimo octaves cascade.
* **About 9:30 Final cadence.** The work ends with a fortissimo G-minor close.

**Ballade No. 4 in F minor Op. 52** (about 11:30):

* **0:00 Introduction.** Listen for the _waiting_ quality of the opening: a slow chordal figure in C major, played softly, almost questioning — three or four chord-pairs separated by silences, repeated. The piece has not yet begun; this is the threshold. The same introduction will return, transformed, much later.
* **About 0:30 Main theme.** Listen for the moment the F-minor narrative theme arrives — a singing right-hand melodic line over a gentle left-hand pattern. Compared to the Ballade No. 1's main theme (Chapter 10, ~0:25 in Zimerman), this melody is _more harmonically restless_ : the right hand's notes don't all belong to a simple F-minor scale; chromatic motion is present from the first phrase.
* **About 1:30 Development of the main theme.** Listen for the right hand decorating the theme with small ornamental turns on each return. The texture _deepens_ by adding inner voices: a second melodic line emerges in the middle register, between the right hand's singing line and the left hand's pattern. Two voices singing at once, briefly.
* **About 3:30 A contrasting passage.** Listen for the harmony brightening into a related major key (B♭ major area). A new lyric melody enters — calmer, more contemplative, with simpler rhythmic profile than the main theme.
* **About 5:00 Further development.** The main theme returns but in a _new harmonic context_ — the music has moved further from F minor than the No. 1 ever does. Listen for moments of contrapuntal writing: two or three melodic lines moving simultaneously rather than the right-hand-sings-left-hand-accompanies texture of the opening.
* **About 7:00 The reprise.** Listen for the moment the _introduction_ (from 0:00) returns — but now you can hear it as foundational rather than preparatory. The slow chord-pairs in C major lead, this time, into a transformed restatement of the main theme.
* **About 8:30 Building toward the coda.** The texture intensifies; the harmonic motion accelerates; listen for the right hand reaching the highest register of the keyboard while the left hand sustains a low pedal-point note.
* **About 10:00 The coda.** Listen for the _cascading octaves_ — both hands playing rapid figures, the texture full from one extreme of the keyboard to the other. The harmony is restless: more chromatic motion than in the No. 1's coda, with the F-minor destination not entirely secure until the very last bars.
* **About 11:30 Final cadence.** The work ends with fortissimo F-minor chords. Listen for the _resolution_ — after nearly a minute of harmonic uncertainty in the coda, the closing chords land with the weight of arrival.

### 10.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The genre invention

The keyboard ballade as Chopin invented it has several recurring characteristics:

* **Compound meter** (6/4 or 6/8 typically — three duple beats per bar). The compound meter gives the music a lilting, narrative quality, like a long-breathed narrative poem.
* **A slow, narrative-like opening** in the home key, with a main theme that has a lyric singing character.
* **A contrasting second theme** in a related key, with a brighter or more lyric character.
* **A central development** in which the two themes interact.
* **A return of the main thematic material** in transformed form.
* **A coda in a faster tempo** with increasing intensity, building to a climactic close (often fortissimo octaves).

The form is loosely related to sonata form (the two contrasting themes, the development, the return), but it is not strict sonata form (the development is freer, the recapitulation is more transformed, the coda is structurally more important). Some analysts call the ballade form "modified sonata"; others call it "narrative form" or simply "Chopin ballade form."

The genre's narrative posture is the genre-defining innovation. Before Chopin, the keyboard genres available for serious art-music had been the sonata (multi-movement, structural-formal), the variation set (theme-and-variations), the etude (technical-problem-based), the nocturne (intimate-salon), the prelude (short, opener-like), the mazurka and polonaise and waltz (dance-based). None of these had the long-breathed narrative shape Chopin invented for the ballade. The ballade is the genre Chopin built specifically to carry literary-narrative character at piano-music scale.

#### The Mickiewicz connection (revisited)

Modern scholarship treats the Mickiewicz connection cautiously. Schumann's 1841 letter is the principal documentary source; the connection is real in the broad sense. The specific pairings (No. 1 with _Konrad Wallenrod_ , etc.) are reception readings.

What Chopin and Mickiewicz shared: a Polish-émigré-in-Paris cultural milieu through the 1830s; a commitment to Polish national identity through art; a Romantic-period aesthetic that valued narrative, mood, expressive sustained development. What they did not necessarily share: a specific work-for-work mapping between Mickiewicz poems and Chopin pieces.

For the listener: the four Ballades carry a literary-narrative posture without naming literary sources. You can hear them as untexted narratives — stories being told without words. You can hear them as music alone, without the narrative frame. Both readings are legitimate.

#### The Ballade No. 1's structural design

The Ballade No. 1 has been analyzed exhaustively in the academic literature. The opening Largo introduction in C major (or related) before the G-minor main theme arrives is one of the most-discussed structural choices in 19th-century piano music. Some analysts treat the introduction as foundational — establishing a key-relation that the rest of the piece will resolve. Others treat it as a frame — outside the main narrative, like a prologue before the story begins.

The two main themes (the G-minor first theme and the E♭-major second theme) are clearly contrasted in mood, key, and texture. The development engages both. The return brings them back transformed. The coda in a faster tempo closes the work with sustained intensity.

The closing G-minor fortissimo cadence is the work's destination. The piece does not resolve into G major (the picardy-third convention); it ends in G minor with full dynamic force. The choice is part of the genre's character — the ballade does not soften its endings.

#### The Ballade No. 4 as the late peak

The Ballade No. 4 in F minor is the late-style peak of the genre. Composed at Nohant in 1842, seven years after the No. 1, it is the most structurally sophisticated of the four. The work is longer (about 12 minutes versus 9-10 for the others), more harmonically restless, more contrapuntally dense, more transformed in its returns.

What distinguishes the No. 4:

* The opening introduction (about 30 seconds in C major) is more substantial and more structurally consequential than the No. 1's. The introduction returns later in the work in transformed form.
* The main F-minor theme is more harmonically restless than the No. 1's G-minor theme. The melody moves more freely; the harmonic frame is less secure.
* The development sections are more contrapuntal — the two themes are interwoven, the textures are more complex.
* The returns are more transformed. The main F-minor theme returns several times, each time changed by what has come before.
* The coda is harmonically more restless than the No. 1's. The fortissimo close in F minor is harder-won, with the harmonic destination less certain until the final cadence.

The No. 4 is therefore the genre's late master-work. Where the No. 1 was the brilliant first attempt at a new form, the No. 4 is the mature realization. The seven years between them include the Op. 28 Preludes, the Op. 35 Sonata, the Op. 47 Ballade, the early-Sand-period nocturnes, and most of the etude follow-ups; the composer who wrote the No. 4 had developed substantially.

#### The arc of the four

A short note on the arc across the four Ballades.

**Ballade No. 1 in G minor Op. 23** (1835). The genre's invention. Brilliant first attempt. Most-frequently-performed and most-loved.

**Ballade No. 2 in F major Op. 38** (1839). The strangest of the four. Begins in F major with a calm pastoral theme; explodes into A minor with violent storms; the alternation of pastoral and storm continues; the work ends in A minor (not F major) — one of the few Chopin works to end in a different key from where it begins. Dedicated to Robert Schumann (a friendly gesture toward the German critic who had championed Chopin since 1831).

**Ballade No. 3 in A♭ major Op. 47** (1841). The brightest of the four. Stays in A♭ major and related keys for most of its length; the main theme is dance-like (with mazurka rhythmic inflections); the coda is fast and brilliant but less dramatically intense than the Nos. 1 and 4. The least-troubled of the four.

**Ballade No. 4 in F minor Op. 52** (1842). The late peak. Discussed above.

The arc moves from the dramatic invention of No. 1 through the strange experiment of No. 2 through the brighter exploration of No. 3 to the late master-work of No. 4. Together the four form one of the great single-movement arcs in piano music — comparable to the four Schubert _Impromptus_ D 899 (1827) or the four Brahms _Ballades_ Op. 10 (1854), but more sustained and more individually developed than either.

### 10.4 The Stories

#### What this anchor is and is not

The Ballade No. 1 is among the most-famous Chopin works in the larger culture. It has been used in films (the 2002 Polański _The Pianist_ anchored on it; many other 20th- and 21st-century films have used it); it has been recorded by every major Chopin pianist; it is one of the standard recital pieces in the international piano-competition repertoire.

What the No. 1 is: a brilliant, sustained, narrative ballade of about ten minutes that demonstrates Chopin's genre invention at its most immediate. The work's popularity is partly because it is genuinely effective music and partly because it is structurally readable — the form's lyric opening, contrasting middle, developed return, and dramatic coda are all clearly heard, and the dramatic close lands with full force.

What the No. 1 is not: it is not the deepest of the four. The No. 4 is more harmonically and structurally sophisticated. The No. 1's popularity has occasionally led to its over-performance and to its becoming the "easy" Ballade to play and hear; the No. 4 demands more of pianist and listener and rewards both.

What the No. 4 is: the late peak of the genre. The most-discussed of the four in the academic literature. Often cited as the single greatest Chopin Ballade.

What the No. 4 is not: it is not the most-immediately-loved of the four. A first-time listener may find the No. 1 more accessible; the No. 4 requires sustained attention across twelve minutes and rewards repeated listenings.

#### The Polański film

A short note on Roman Polański's 2002 film _The Pianist_ , which dramatizes the survival of the Polish-Jewish pianist Władysław Szpilman through the Warsaw Ghetto and the German occupation of Warsaw 1939-1945. The film uses several Chopin works prominently, with the Ballade No. 1 as the central work — the Szpilman performance of the Ballade No. 1 for a German officer in the ruined Warsaw of 1944 is one of the film's central scenes (the historical Szpilman did encounter a German officer who helped him survive; the account in Szpilman's memoir confirms the broad outline).

The film's effect on Chopin reception in the 21st century has been significant. The Ballade No. 1 — which had been a famous-Chopin-piece since the 19th century — became, after 2002, the Chopin piece carrying the Polish-Jewish wartime story specifically. Polański (who himself survived the Kraków Ghetto as a child) chose the work deliberately; the choice has shaped subsequent listening.

For the present chapter the connection is reception context, not interpretive frame. The Ballade No. 1 was composed in 1835 with no relation to the 1944 Warsaw; the reception's overlay of the wartime story onto the music is reception, not composition. The piece works without the film association; the association is one reading among many.

#### Recording history briefly

The Ballades have many recordings. Major lineages:

The Rubinstein lineage. Arthur Rubinstein recorded the four Ballades multiple times; the 1959 RCA stereo recording is the canonical mid-20th-century document. Warm, songful, slightly less dramatically pointed than Zimerman's later reading.

The Zimerman lineage. Krystian Zimerman recorded the four Ballades for DG in 1987 (the Primary Recording for this chapter). The Polish-school depth, the harmonic-and-structural awareness, the dramatic shape across the arc. Often cited as the modern reference.

The Argerich lineage. Martha Argerich recorded the Ballade No. 1 in 1965 for DG as a separate single-work recording; the recording is famous for its dramatic intensity. Her later live recordings include selections from the four Ballades; the documentation is scattered but powerful where it exists.

The Askenase lineage. Stefan Askenase (1896-1985), Polish-school predecessor to Rubinstein and Zimerman, recorded the Ballades in 1955 for DG. The reading is more lyric, slower, more songful than Zimerman's; the Polish-school inheritance in its mid-century form.

The Pollini Ballades. Maurizio Pollini did not record a complete Ballades set with DG; his selected-Ballade recordings are scattered across various releases. Where they exist (Op. 52 on one DG release) the readings are modernist-clean — the technical precision and the harmonic-clarity that defines his Chopin approach.

For Ballade No. 1 the safe ground is Zimerman 1987, Rubinstein 1959, Argerich 1965. For Ballade No. 4 the safe ground is Zimerman 1987, Rubinstein 1959, and any Pollini recording where the work appears.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The Ballades connect to many works.

To the Op. 28 Preludes (Chapter 9). The harmonic concentration of the preludes informs the harmonic motion of the Ballades, particularly in the development episodes.

To the Sonatas (Chapter 12). The sonata-form Sonata movements share some of the ballade's structural posture (contrasting themes, development, return) but in multi-movement form. The Ballade is closer to a single sonata movement expanded to fifteen minutes.

To the Polonaise-Fantaisie Op. 61 (Chapter 14). The late-period Polonaise-Fantaisie is the work most-similar in structural ambition to the Ballade No. 4. Both are single-movement late-style works of about 12-13 minutes, both with multiple thematic transformations, both with restless harmonic motion.

To the Op. 49 Fantasy (Chapter 15). The free-form single-movement Op. 49 shares some of the Ballades' through-composed posture but with more episodic structure.

#### The biographical context: 1835-1842

The four Ballades in Chopin's life: the Ballade No. 1 was composed in 1835 in the established-Paris period (post-Schumann-reviews, Pleyel-relationship-active, before the Sand partnership). The Ballade No. 2 was completed in 1839 — partly at Mallorca, partly at Nohant in the first summer. The Ballade No. 3 was composed in 1841 at Nohant in the third Sand-period summer. The Ballade No. 4 was composed in 1842 at Nohant in the fourth Sand-period summer.

The seven-year arc therefore spans the early-Paris establishment through the Sand-period middle phase. The Ballade No. 4 was composed in the same Nohant summer as the Polonaise Op. 53 "Heroic" (Chapter 14) — 1842 was a major Sand-summer year for Chopin.

Chapter 11 covers the four Scherzos, which Chopin also composed across the same broad period (1831-1842). Chapter 12 covers the Sonatas and the Cello Sonata. Chapter 13 covers the late Mazurkas and the Waltzes.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The Ballade No. 1 is among the most-famous Chopin works; the Ballade No. 4 is the late peak. Without having heard them side by side, what would you predict the No. 4 adds or changes compared to the No. 1, and which would you guess rewards deeper listening more?"_
* _"The Mickiewicz connection is attested (Schumann's 1841 letter) in the broad sense but uncertain in the specific work-for-work pairings. Listen to the Ballade No. 1 — can you hear a narrative? If so, is it a Polish-historical narrative, an emotional narrative, or something else?"_
* _"All four Ballades end with a faster-tempo coda building to fortissimo. What does this consistent ending choice tell you about Chopin's understanding of the genre, and what would the genre lose if a Ballade ended quietly?"_
* _"The Ballade No. 2 in F major begins in F major but ends in A minor — one of the few Chopin works to end in a different key from where it begins. What does this structural choice do to the listener's sense of the work, and what does it suggest about the genre's narrative posture?"_
* _"The Polański film _The Pianist_ (2002) anchored on the Ballade No. 1 and has shaped subsequent listening to the work. Is it possible to hear the Ballade No. 1 today without the film's wartime associations, and if so, what would the un-overlaid hearing recover?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Zimerman 1987 Ballades (or one of the alternatives). Listen to the two anchor Ballades a second time and attend to these moments:

  1. **Ballade No. 1: the opening introduction (0:00-0:25).** Listen to the Largo introduction before the main G-minor theme arrives. Notice that the piece begins not in G minor but in a different key area; the structural choice is unusual.

  2. **Ballade No. 1: the coda (about 7:30 onward).** Listen to the coda's faster tempo, rapid figuration, and climactic build to the fortissimo G-minor close. The coda is about two minutes; it carries the work's dramatic resolution.

  3. **Ballade No. 4: the opening introduction (0:00-0:30).** Compare the No. 4 introduction to the No. 1 introduction. The No. 4 is more substantial, more structurally consequential (it returns later in the work).

  4. **Ballade No. 4: a thematic return (around 7:00).** Listen for the main F-minor theme returning in transformed form. Notice that the return is not literal — the theme is changed by what has come before.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair the Ballade No. 1 with Mickiewicz's _Konrad Wallenrod_ (1828) — the long narrative poem that some 19th-century reception linked to the work. English translations of _Konrad Wallenrod_ are available (the 1882 Polish-English Society translation; the more recent 1925 Watson Kirkconnell translation; the 1989 Maguire translation). Reading the first few sections of the poem and then listening to the Ballade No. 1 gives you a sense of the Polish-Romantic narrative milieu that Chopin and Mickiewicz both occupied. The exercise takes about forty minutes total — twenty minutes for the poem, ten for the music, ten for reflection.

**Read** : the chapter on the Ballades in Charles Rosen, _The Romantic Generation_ (Harvard, 1995). Rosen's analysis of the four Ballades as a single genre-arc is the standard contemporary scholarly reading. He covers the genre's invention, the within-set evolution from No. 1 to No. 4, the Mickiewicz question, and the structural innovations. About thirty pages.

## Chapter 11: The Scherzos (1831-1842)

The four Chopin Scherzos — Op. 20 in B minor (1831-32), Op. 31 in B♭ minor (1837), Op. 39 in C♯ minor (1839), Op. 54 in E major (1842) — transformed the inherited scherzo into a standalone large-scale virtuoso work. Beethoven and Haydn had used the scherzo as a third-movement inner movement of a multi-movement work (sonata, symphony, string quartet) — usually a brief, fast, light-or-fierce intermezzo between the slow movement and the finale. Chopin took the scherzo out of the multi-movement context and made it a stand-alone single-movement piano work of about ten to fifteen minutes' duration with serious dramatic argument.

The chapter anchors on the Scherzo No. 2 in B♭ minor Op. 31 — the most-performed of the four, and the one most-often introduced first to listeners new to the Chopin Scherzos. The chapter also discusses the other three in passing, with particular attention to the Scherzo No. 1's Polish-Christmas-carol quotation in the trio.

### 11.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

The Schumann question. Robert Schumann, reviewing the Scherzo No. 1 in B minor Op. 20 in the _Neue Zeitschrift für Musik_ in 1835, asked: "How is gravity to clothe itself if jest goes about in dark veils?" The Italian word _scherzo_ means "joke" or "jest"; Beethoven's and Haydn's scherzos had often been jokes in the literal sense — playful inner movements with rhythmic surprise and tonal humor. Chopin's Scherzo No. 1 was not a joke. It is a dramatic, dark B-minor work with a Polish-Christmas-carol quotation in the trio (the "Lulajże, Jezuniu" carol, a familiar Polish lullaby for the Christ child); the outer sections are intense and the middle section is tender.

Schumann's question pointed at the genre transformation. If "scherzo" means "joke," what do you call a piece called a scherzo that is fundamentally serious? Chopin had used the genre name without obeying the genre's traditional posture; the result was a structural innovation that Schumann recognized immediately as new.

The answer to Schumann's question came in the four Scherzos taken together. Chopin retained the inherited scherzo elements — the compound triple meter (3/4 or 3/8 with strong upbeat-and-downbeat patterns), the ABA structure with a slower trio middle section, the energetic outer-section character — but transformed the genre into a vehicle for sustained dramatic argument rather than playful intermezzo. The Op. 20 is the demonstration of the transformation; the Op. 31, Op. 39, and Op. 54 are the elaborations of the new genre's possibilities.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

For the Scherzo No. 2:

  1. **The opening sotto voce question and the forte response.** The Op. 31 opens with a quiet B♭-minor figure played sotto voce (under the breath), followed by a forte response that interrupts it. The pattern repeats. The interaction of question-and-response is unusual for a scherzo opening and is part of the work's signature.

  2. **The lyric A♭ major main theme (after about 30 seconds).** Following the opening sotto-voce/forte interplay, a sustained lyric theme arrives in A♭ major (the relative major of D♭ — which is enharmonic-equivalent of the home tonic area but not the home tonic itself; the Op. 31 has a much-discussed harmonic ambiguity at its opening). The melody is one of the most-singable Chopin themes.

  3. **The slower trio middle section in A major.** The middle section provides the contrasting slow lyric material — chorale-like writing, more sustained, more inward. The trio's mood is calm; it is the still center of the work.

#### One-sentence background

Scherzo No. 2 in B♭ minor Op. 31 (composed 1837; published 1837; ~10 minutes). The **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Maurizio Pollini on Deutsche Grammophon (recorded 1990, the complete four Scherzos), chosen for the modernist-clean reading and Pollini's recognized authority in the Chopin repertoire. The companion piece — the Scherzo No. 1 in B minor Op. 20 (composed 1831-32; published 1835; ~10 minutes) — is also discussed in this chapter; the same Primary Recording.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Chopin Scherzo No 2 B flat minor Op 31 Pollini" or "Chopin Scherzos Pollini 1990" ZH: "肖邦 第二谐谑曲 降b小调 作品31 波利尼" 或 "肖邦 谐谑曲全集" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** :

* Arthur Rubinstein on RCA (recorded 1959 stereo, the four Scherzos): the canonical warmer-singing reading.
* Martha Argerich on DG (recorded 1960s-70s; the Scherzo No. 3 1965 in particular is famous): the electric Argerich-Chopin reading.
* Krystian Zimerman on DG (1985 four Scherzos, complementing his 1987 Ballades): the Polish-school depth.

### 11.2 The Listening Window

Timestamps refer to the Pollini 1990 Primary Recording.

**Scherzo No. 2 in B♭ minor Op. 31** (about 10:00):

* **0:00 Sotto voce opening.** Quiet B♭-minor figure, played softly.
* **About 0:05 Forte response.** A loud interruption (chord and arpeggio in forte).
* **0:00-0:30 Repetition of the pattern.** The sotto voce / forte interplay repeats twice or three times.
* **About 0:30 The lyric A♭ major theme.** The singing main theme arrives in A♭ major; the texture is full and lyric.
* **About 2:00 Development of the lyric theme.** The melody returns elaborated; the harmonic motion is wider.
* **About 3:00 Transitional material.** The texture changes; the harmony begins to move toward the middle section.
* **About 3:30 The trio (middle section) in A major.** A new texture: chorale-like writing in A major, sustained and inward. The mood is calm; the music is the still center.
* **About 5:30 The middle section's continuation.** The chorale-writing continues with more variation.
* **About 7:30 Return.** The opening sotto-voce/forte interplay returns; then the lyric A♭ major theme.
* **About 9:00 Coda.** A faster tempo; rapid figuration; the texture intensifies.
* **About 10:00 Final cadence.** The work ends with a fortissimo B♭-major close (the parallel major of the home B♭ minor, the picardy-third resolution at the end).

### 11.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The genre transformation

The inherited scherzo of Beethoven and Haydn had three standard characteristics:

  1. **Brief duration.** Typically 3-5 minutes in a multi-movement context.
  2. **Functional role.** The scherzo was the third movement of a four-movement sonata, symphony, or string quartet, providing rhythmic energy and contrast between the slow movement and the finale.
  3. **Light or fierce character.** The scherzo could be playful (Haydn) or dramatic (Beethoven), but it was always intense in some direction.

Chopin's transformation kept the rhythmic-energy character but changed everything else:

  1. **Extended duration.** 9-15 minutes for each of the four.
  2. **Standalone form.** Not part of a multi-movement work but a single complete composition.
  3. **Sustained dramatic argument.** Not playful or fierce intermezzo but a developed musical argument with a trio middle section providing contrast.

The result is a genre that resembles the inherited scherzo in rhythmic profile but functions like a single-movement-of-a-symphony in scale and ambition. The Chopin scherzos are, in this sense, the closest he came to writing symphonic-scale piano music; the Polonaise Op. 53 "Heroic" and the Polonaise-Fantaisie Op. 61 are the other works in this register.

#### The Op. 31 specifically

The Scherzo No. 2 in B♭ minor is the most-performed of the four Chopin Scherzos. The reasons noted in performance and critical literature:

* **The opening is dramatic and immediately attention-grabbing.** The sotto voce / forte interplay catches the listener within the first ten seconds.
* **The lyric A♭ major main theme is one of the most-singable Chopin melodies.** It carries the work's emotional center.
* **The trio middle section in A major is a clear, restful contrast** — the listener can rest in the trio before the outer-section material returns.
* **The structural shape is clearly heard.** The ABA + coda design lands without analytical effort; the listener follows the structure intuitively.
* **The closing fortissimo B♭ major cadence is the most dramatic ending of any Chopin scherzo.** The picardy-third resolution from B♭ minor to B♭ major gives the work its final affirmation.

The Op. 31 is therefore the "easy" Chopin scherzo to enjoy. The other three are more demanding in different directions — the No. 1 is darker and more concentrated; the No. 3 is more virtuosic and harmonically restless; the No. 4 is calmer and structurally most ambiguous.

#### The other three Scherzos

A brief survey of the other three.

**Scherzo No. 1 in B minor Op. 20** (1831-32). The first of the four. The most-concentrated and the darkest. The outer sections are agitated and stormy in B minor; the trio middle section is the "Lulajże, Jezuniu" Polish-Christmas-carol quotation — a familiar Polish lullaby for the infant Christ, here transformed into a tender slow trio in the parallel B major. The juxtaposition of stormy outer sections with the lullaby trio is one of the work's signature features and one of the most-discussed passages in the literature on Chopin's Polish-folk-music quotations. The work ends in B minor (no picardy-third resolution); the genre's transformation is here proposed in its most concentrated form.

**Scherzo No. 3 in C♯ minor Op. 39** (1839). Composed partly at Mallorca. The most virtuosic of the four — fast outer sections with cascading right-hand and left-hand figuration; the trio middle section alternates a chorale-like passage (in D♭ major, the enharmonic-equivalent parallel-major) with rapid right-hand cascades. The cascade-and-chorale alternation in the trio is unusual; the work is structurally the most experimental of the four.

**Scherzo No. 4 in E major Op. 54** (1842). The calmest of the four. The outer sections are still energetic but less dramatically pointed than the others; the trio middle section is lyric and chorale-like. The work is the closest to the inherited scherzo's lighter character — though still about ten minutes long and structurally complete. The Op. 54 closes the Scherzo arc on a more relaxed note than the dramatic Op. 31.

The arc moves from dramatic invention (No. 1, 1831-32) through the popular mainstream (No. 2, 1837) through virtuosic experimentation (No. 3, 1839) to the calmer maturity (No. 4, 1842). The arc parallels the Ballade arc (Chapter 10) in time but moves in a different direction emotionally.

#### The trio sections

A note on the trio middle sections across the four scherzos. The traditional scherzo has a trio middle section — a slower, contrasting central passage that provides relief from the energetic outer sections. The inherited Beethoven-Haydn scherzo had used the trio as a brief contrasting episode (often in a related major key, with simpler texture and slower rhythm).

Chopin's trios are more developed than the inherited model. Each of the four Scherzos has a substantial trio middle section that does substantial musical work:

* **No. 1's trio** : the "Lulajże, Jezuniu" Polish lullaby quotation in B major. The most-discussed of the four trios.
* **No. 2's trio** : the chorale-like A-major passage. Sustained, inward, the work's still center.
* **No. 3's trio** : the chorale-and-cascade alternation. Structurally the most experimental.
* **No. 4's trio** : the lyric chorale-like passage. Closest to the inherited model.

The trios are the lyric heart of each work; the listener's experience of the scherzo's outer-section drama depends on the trio's contrast. Without the trio the scherzo would lose its structural shape.

#### The "Lulajże" quotation in No. 1

A brief note on the Polish-Christmas-carol quotation in the Scherzo No. 1's trio. "Lulajże, Jezuniu" is a traditional Polish lullaby for the infant Christ, dating from the 17th or 18th century, sung at Christmas in Polish Catholic tradition. The melody is simple, lullaby-like, in a major key.

Chopin's quotation of the carol in the trio of the Scherzo No. 1 is a Polish-cultural-memory citation — the kind of quotation that Polish listeners would have recognized instantly and that non-Polish listeners would not have caught without the context. The quotation appears in the trio's slower passages; Chopin treated it gently, with full Polish-folk-music respect, and integrated it into the work's structural design.

The quotation is one of several Polish-folk-music citations in the Chopin catalogue. The mazurkas use Polish-folk modes (Chapter 4 and Chapter 13); the polonaises use Polish-court-dance rhythms (Chapter 14). The "Lulajże" quotation in the Scherzo No. 1 is the most-specific Polish-folk-music citation in the non-mazurka/non-polonaise repertoire.

For Thread B of the volume — Chopin's compositional signatures, collected in Chapter 17 — register the "Lulajże" quotation as one of the Polish-folk-modal-coloring examples, alongside the mazurka raised fourths and the polonaise rhythms.

### 11.4 The Stories

#### Placing the Op. 31 in the four Scherzos

The Scherzo No. 2 in B♭ minor Op. 31 is among the most-popular Chopin works in the larger culture. It has been used in films, recorded by every major Chopin pianist, performed in countless recitals. The work's popularity is partly that it is genuinely effective music and partly that its structural shape is so readable.

What the No. 2 is: a brilliant standalone scherzo of about ten minutes that demonstrates Chopin's genre transformation at its most accessible. The work's three-section ABA + coda design is heard clearly; the lyric main theme is one of the most-singable Chopin melodies; the closing picardy-third resolution gives the work its full-circle affirmation.

What the No. 2 is not: it is not the deepest of the four. The No. 1 is more concentrated; the No. 3 is more virtuosic; the No. 4 is more mature. The No. 2's popularity has sometimes led to its over-performance, with the Scherzos as a complete arc receiving less attention than they deserve.

#### Recording history briefly

The four Scherzos have many recordings. Major lineages:

The Rubinstein lineage. Arthur Rubinstein recorded the four Scherzos multiple times; the 1959 RCA stereo set is the canonical mid-20th-century reading. Warm, songful, slightly less dramatically pointed than later Polish-school readings.

The Pollini lineage. Maurizio Pollini recorded the four Scherzos for DG in 1990 (Primary Recording for this chapter). The modernist-clean reading — technical precision, structural clarity, minimal rubato.

The Zimerman lineage. Krystian Zimerman recorded the four Scherzos for DG in 1985 (the companion volume to his 1987 Ballades). The Polish-school depth; harmonically deep; structurally clear; particularly fine in the No. 4.

The Argerich lineage. Martha Argerich's recordings of individual Scherzos are scattered across her career. Her 1965 DG recording of the Scherzo No. 3 in C♯ minor is one of the most-electric Chopin recordings in the discography.

The Cortot lineage. Alfred Cortot recorded the four Scherzos in the 1930s for EMI. The reading is the rubato extreme with occasional wrong notes; the historical document.

For Op. 31 specifically the safe ground is Pollini 1990, Rubinstein 1959, Zimerman 1985. For Op. 20 the safe ground is the same recordings; the Zimerman 1985 is particularly worth hearing for the Polish-school treatment of the "Lulajże" trio.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The Scherzos connect to other works.

To the Ballades (Chapter 10). The Scherzos and Ballades share the standalone-single-movement scale and the ABA-plus-coda structural shape. The Ballade No. 1 (1835) and the Scherzo No. 2 (1837) were composed two years apart; they share certain structural and harmonic moves.

To the Sonatas (Chapter 12). The third-movement scherzos of the Op. 35 Sonata (1839) and Op. 58 Sonata (1844) are condensed versions of the standalone scherzo posture — shorter, more compressed, but with the same compound-meter energetic character.

To the Polonaise-Fantaisie Op. 61 (Chapter 14). The late-period polonaise-fantasy uses some of the scherzo's structural posture in its central episode.

To the Etudes (Chapter 6). The fast outer sections of the Scherzos use etude-derived figuration; the Op. 31's lyric theme connects to the etude's bel-canto singing-line vocabulary.

#### The biographical context: 1831-1842

Where the four Scherzos sit in the life: the Scherzo No. 1 was composed 1831-32, in the early-Paris establishment period (the work was composed across the period of the Schumann reviews and the Paris-arrival establishment). The Scherzo No. 2 was composed in 1837, in the established-Paris middle period (before the Sand partnership began). The Scherzo No. 3 was composed in 1839, partly at Mallorca during the Sand-period winter. The Scherzo No. 4 was composed in 1842 at Nohant, the same Sand-summer year that produced the Ballade No. 4 and the Polonaise Op. 53 "Heroic."

The eleven-year arc spans the same broad period as the Ballades (1835-1842). The two arcs together — four Ballades, four Scherzos — comprise eight major standalone single-movement works composed between 1831 and 1842, representing Chopin's most-sustained mid-period work in the standalone large-scale format.

Chapter 12 covers the Sonatas (1839, 1844) and the Cello Sonata Op. 65 (1845-47). Chapter 13 covers the late Mazurkas and the Waltzes.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"Schumann's question about the Scherzo No. 1 — 'how is gravity to clothe itself if jest goes about in dark veils?' — points at Chopin's genre transformation. What does Chopin gain by using the 'scherzo' name for a serious work, and what does the genre name conceal or reveal?"_
* _"The four Scherzos move from the dramatic No. 1 through the popular No. 2 through the virtuosic No. 3 to the calmer No. 4. Listening to the No. 2 (this chapter's anchor), where would you predict the No. 1 sits emotionally — more concentrated, darker, or simply different in some other way?"_
* _"The 'Lulajże, Jezuniu' Polish Christmas-carol quotation in the trio of the Scherzo No. 1 is the most-specific Polish-folk-music citation in the non-mazurka/non-polonaise Chopin catalogue. What does the quotation do to the work's overall posture — does the lullaby tenderness fit with the stormy outer sections, or does it create a productive tension?"_
* _"The Op. 31 closes with a picardy-third resolution from B♭ minor to B♭ major. What does the major-key ending do to your sense of the work, and would the scherzo be more or less effective if it ended in B♭ minor like the Mazurka Op. 17 No. 4?"_
* _"The Scherzos and Ballades together comprise eight major standalone single-movement works composed by Chopin between 1831 and 1842. What is it about the standalone single-movement format that suited Chopin's middle-period ambitions, and why did he not write multi-movement sonatas more often?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Pollini 1990 four Scherzos (or one of the alternatives). Listen to the Op. 31 a second time and attend to these four moments:

  1. **The sotto-voce/forte opening (0:00-0:30).** Listen for the alternation of soft and loud, question and interruption. The interplay is the work's structural opening signature.

  2. **The lyric A♭ major main theme (about 0:30 onward).** Listen for the singing quality of the main theme. Notice that the melody is genuinely singable — a major reason for the work's popular reception.

  3. **The trio middle section in A major (about 3:30-7:30).** Listen for the chorale-like writing and the slower, sustained mood. The trio is the work's still center.

  4. **The closing fortissimo cadence (about 9:30-10:00).** Listen for the picardy-third resolution from B♭ minor to B♭ major. The major-key ending gives the work its final affirmation.

If you have time also listen to the Scherzo No. 1 in B minor Op. 20, particularly the trio middle section (the "Lulajże, Jezuniu" Polish-Christmas-carol quotation). The contrast between the stormy outer sections and the tender lullaby trio is the most-discussed passage in the Chopin Scherzos literature.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair the Scherzo No. 2 with the third-movement scherzo of Beethoven's Symphony No. 9 in D minor (premiered Vienna 1824). Beethoven's symphonic scherzo is about thirteen minutes (with the repeat) and demonstrates the inherited scherzo at its largest scale. Listening to it before listening to the Chopin Op. 31 shows what Chopin retained from the inherited model (the compound-meter energetic outer sections, the ABA + trio shape) and what he transformed (the standalone form, the lyric melodic content, the bel-canto-piano texture). Recordings of the Beethoven 9 — Karl Böhm with the Vienna Philharmonic (DG, 1970), Wilhelm Furtwängler (multiple historical recordings; the 1951 Bayreuth recording is canonical), or any contemporary cycle — provide the comparison. About 25 minutes total.

**Read** : the chapter on the Scherzos in Jim Samson, _Chopin_ (Oxford Master Musicians series, 1996). Samson covers the genre transformation, the within-set evolution from No. 1 to No. 4, the trio sections, the "Lulajże" quotation, and the structural innovations. About fifteen pages.
