## Chapter 12: The Sonatas (1839, 1844) + the Cello Sonata Op. 65 (1845-47)

Chopin wrote three piano sonatas across his life. The first, in C minor Op. 4, was composed in Warsaw around 1828 and published posthumously in 1851; it is an early student work and is rarely performed. The second, in B♭ minor Op. 35 ("Funeral March"), was composed in 1839 at Nohant and published in 1840; it is one of the central works of his middle period. The third, in B minor Op. 58, was composed in 1844 at Nohant and published in 1845; it is the mature piano-sonata statement. The Cello Sonata in G minor Op. 65 was composed 1845-46 at Nohant and Paris, published in 1847, premiered February 16, 1848 at Chopin's last Paris public concert with cellist Auguste Franchomme; it is the major chamber work that closes the Chopin catalogue.

This chapter anchors on the Op. 35 third-movement Funeral March (the most-universally-cited Chopin slow movement after the Op. 21 Larghetto) and discusses the Op. 35 as a four-movement work, the Op. 58 as the mature piano-sonata statement, and the Op. 65 Cello Sonata's late chamber-music register.

### 12.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

The 1841 Schumann review of Op. 35. Robert Schumann reviewed the Op. 35 Sonata in his _Neue Zeitschrift für Musik_ in 1841. The review contains one of the most-famous critical phrases in the 19th-century Chopin reception: Schumann called the four movements "four of his maddest children, brought together under the same roof." The phrase has been quoted in nearly every subsequent biography and analytical commentary on the work.

Schumann's reading focused on what he heard as the work's lack of cyclic unity. The four movements — a turbulent sonata-form first movement in B♭ minor; a fast minor-key scherzo with a contrasting lyric trio; the slow Funeral March third movement (composed separately in 1837 and incorporated into the 1839 sonata); a strange very-short last movement that Schumann famously characterized as "wind whistling over the gravestones" — do not, in Schumann's reading, form an integrated four-movement design. They are four strong individual movements that happen to be published together.

Schumann's reading was contested even in 1841 — and remains contested today. Some analysts (Samson, Rosen) argue that the four movements do form a coherent design, with the Funeral March as the work's emotional center and the other movements as its surrounding context. Other analysts treat Schumann's reading as essentially correct — the work is a collection rather than a unified sonata. The Op. 35 sits in an analytical controversy that has not been resolved across nearly two centuries.

In practice: the Op. 35 works as a four-movement piano sonata of about twenty-four minutes' duration, even if the analytical question of cyclic unity remains debated. The Funeral March is the emotional center; the other three movements provide context. The work's reception across two centuries — as a complete sonata, as a vehicle for the standalone Funeral March, as a critical-controversy piece — is part of what makes it interesting.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

For the Op. 35 third movement Funeral March:

  1. **The opening slow march in B♭ minor.** A funeral procession: slow, chordal, deeply weighted. The left hand plays sustained low octaves; the right hand plays the march melody above. The texture is heavy; the rhythm is processional; the mood is funereal. The opening four bars establish the entire movement's character.

  2. **The middle section in D♭ major (the trio of the funeral march).** After about three minutes of the procession, the music shifts to D♭ major (the relative major). A contrasting lyric section follows — bel-canto-piano texture, singing right-hand melody, the still center of the funeral march. The Sand-period middle-style writing at its most singing.

  3. **The return of the funeral march.** After the lyric middle, the funeral march returns in B♭ minor. The return is literal — the same procession, same chordal texture, same heaviness. The work ends with the procession descending toward silence.

If you have time also listen to the rest of the Op. 35 — the turbulent first movement, the fast scherzo, and the strange last movement. The four together give the Schumann-controversy work in its complete form.

#### One-sentence background

Sonata No. 2 in B♭ minor Op. 35 "Funeral March" (composed 1839 at Nohant, the funeral march itself composed separately in 1837 before being incorporated; published 1840; four movements; ~24 minutes total). The Funeral March third movement is ~9 minutes. The **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Maurizio Pollini on Deutsche Grammophon (recorded 1984, the Op. 35 + Op. 58 sonatas on a single LP), chosen for the modernist-clean approach to the sonata structure and the technical precision in the demanding outer movements.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Chopin Sonata No 2 B flat minor Op 35 Funeral March Pollini" or "Chopin Sonata Op 35 Funeral March" ZH: "肖邦 第二钢琴奏鸣曲 降b小调 作品35 葬礼进行曲 波利尼" 或 "肖邦 葬礼奏鸣曲" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** :

* Arthur Rubinstein on RCA (recorded 1961 mono and 1961 stereo, the Op. 35 + Op. 58) — the canonical warmer-singing reading.
* Martha Argerich on DG (recorded 1974, the Op. 35) — the electric reading.
* Krystian Zimerman on DG (recorded 2002, the Op. 35 + Op. 58) — the Polish-school depth.

### 12.2 The Listening Window

Timestamps refer to the Pollini 1984 Primary Recording.

**Op. 35 third-movement Funeral March** (about 9:00):

* **0:00 Opening procession.** Listen for the left hand alone for the first half-second: a low B♭-minor octave that _thuds_ rather than rings. The right hand enters with the march melody above — a short rising figure followed by a longer descent, repeated, dotted-rhythmic, like a slow drum cadence under a sung lament. The texture is heavy precisely because the left hand uses both hands' worth of bass weight (octaves, not single notes) while the right hand keeps to chordal writing rather than singing line.
* **About 0:30 The march continues.** The same rising-and-descending figure returns. Listen for the _lack_ of modulation — the harmony stays within a narrow B♭-minor area for nearly a full minute. The processional gravity comes partly from this harmonic restriction; the music does not wander, it endures.
* **About 1:30 Development of the march material.** The right hand begins adding small ornamental turns to the descending phrase, while the left-hand pattern remains the same. The harmonic motion expands slightly: a brief modulation to the relative major (D♭) and back, used as a moment of color rather than as contrast.
* **About 2:30 Building toward the climax.** Listen for two changes happening at once: the right-hand melody starts climbing higher than before (reaching toward the upper register of the keyboard), and the left-hand octaves grow louder. The texture _thickens_ — more notes sounding simultaneously, more weight per beat. The procession is approaching a peak.
* **About 3:00 Transition to the middle section.** The funeral march pauses on a held chord. Listen for the silence in the left hand — the bass octaves stop, and there are three or four seconds in which the texture is suspended. Then the harmony arrives at D♭ major (the relative major).
* **About 3:30 The middle section (D♭ major trio).** Listen for the moment the bel-canto-piano singing texture you know from the Op. 9 No. 2 (Chapter 2) returns: the right hand floats a long-breathed lyric melody, the left hand provides the bass-note-and-three-chords pattern of the nocturne. The contrast with the opening procession is total — same composer, same work, but the heavy octaves give way to a singer's line over a gentle accompaniment.
* **About 5:30 The lyric section continues.** The singing melody is repeated and elaborated. Listen for the moments where the right hand's ornamentation grows more elaborate on the repeat — bel-canto practice transferred into the funeral march's middle.
* **About 7:00 Return of the funeral march.** Listen for the moment the left-hand octaves come back. The return is literal: same texture, same B♭-minor heaviness as the opening. The lyric middle has not transformed the procession; the procession resumes unchanged.
* **About 8:30 Closing pages.** The march continues with diminishing dynamic — the left-hand octaves are still there but quieter, the right-hand melody more restrained.
* **About 9:00 Final cadence.** Listen for the _fading_ rather than the resolution. The work ends on a quiet B♭-minor chord; there is no picardy-third resolution into major, no closing flourish. The procession has walked into silence.

### 12.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The Funeral March

The Op. 35 third movement is the universally-cited funeral-music piece in Western culture. It has been used at innumerable state funerals across the 19th and 20th centuries, at private memorial services, in films and television depicting funeral occasions, in popular culture as the shorthand for "funeral." The orchestration of the movement (the original is solo piano; orchestral arrangements by various composers include Henry Wood's 1933 arrangement) extends the reach into orchestral contexts.

The funeral march's compositional history is separate from the rest of the sonata. The third movement alone was composed in 1837, two years before the sonata's other three movements were composed. It existed as a standalone Funeral March before being incorporated into the 1839 sonata. The standalone composition history partly explains its independent life in 19th- and 20th-century reception; the piece was a funeral march before it was the third movement of a sonata.

The march's structural design is straightforward — a chordal slow ABA with a lyric middle section. What gives it its weight is not structural complexity but the chordal texture's solemn density and the slow tempo's processional gravity. The middle section in D♭ major provides the bel-canto-piano lyric contrast that the Sand-period middle style produces consistently.

For the listener: the Funeral March can be heard on its own (about nine minutes) or as the third movement of the four-movement Op. 35 sonata (within the twenty-four-minute work). Both modes of listening are legitimate; performance practice supports both.

#### The Op. 35 as a four-movement work

The other three movements of the Op. 35:

**Movement 1 (Grave - Doppio movimento) in B♭ minor** : a turbulent sonata-form first movement, about eight minutes. The Grave introduction (a few bars) gives way to the Doppio movimento main material — agitated, restless, with a contrasting D♭-major second theme that provides brief lyric relief. The movement ends with the same B♭-minor turbulence with which it began.

**Movement 2 (Scherzo) in E♭ minor** : a fast scherzo with a contrasting trio in G♭ major. About six minutes. The outer sections are agitated and quick; the trio middle section is more lyric. The scherzo is conventional in form but harmonically restless in detail.

**Movement 4 (Presto) in B♭ minor** : the famous "wind whistling over the gravestones" finale. Very short — about 75 seconds. Two voices in continuous rapid unison; no melody, no harmony in the conventional sense; the movement is texture rather than tune. The closing chord is a fortissimo B♭-minor cadence. The movement has divided critical opinion since 1840 — some hear it as a perfect closure for the funeral-march-centered work; others hear it as a strange and incomplete coda. Schumann's "wind whistling" reading is the most-quoted.

The four movements together: the first movement establishes the turbulent B♭-minor world; the scherzo continues the energy in a different register; the Funeral March is the emotional center, the work's gravity; the finale closes with the strange windy texture that descends into silence. Whether this is a unified design (Samson and Rosen partially argue) or a collection of four strong individual movements (Schumann and many modern analysts partially argue) remains contested.

#### The Op. 58 as the mature sonata statement

The Sonata No. 3 in B minor Op. 58, composed at Nohant in 1844, is the mature piano-sonata statement that the Op. 35 was reaching toward. It is in four movements:

**Movement 1 (Allegro maestoso) in B minor** : sonata-form first movement, about eight minutes. The most-sustained sonata-form Chopin wrote; the development is more extended than in the Op. 35.

**Movement 2 (Scherzo - Molto vivace) in E♭ major** : a fast scherzo in a brighter key. About three minutes. The trio middle section provides lyric contrast.

**Movement 3 (Largo) in B major** : the slow movement, about nine minutes. A long lyric movement in B major (the parallel major of B minor); bel-canto-piano texture throughout; the slow movement of the mature Chopin in the four-movement-sonata frame. Often compared to the Op. 35 Funeral March as the work's emotional center — but the Op. 58 Largo is lyric and sustained where the Op. 35 March is processional and weighted.

**Movement 4 (Finale - Presto non tanto) in B minor** : a sonata-rondo finale, about five minutes. Fast, virtuosic, with multiple thematic returns. Ends in B major.

The Op. 58 is structurally more integrated than the Op. 35; the analytical literature generally accepts the Op. 58 as a cyclic four-movement sonata in a way that the Op. 35 is not. The Op. 58 is also less frequently anchored in popular reception — the Op. 35 has its Funeral March; the Op. 58 has no equivalent shorthand piece. The Op. 58 is consequently the lesser-known of the two sonatas, despite being analytically more sophisticated.

#### The Cello Sonata Op. 65

The Cello Sonata in G minor Op. 65 was composed 1845-46 at Nohant and Paris, published 1847, and premiered February 16, 1848 at the Salle Pleyel — Chopin's last Paris public concert — with the cellist Auguste Franchomme (1808-1884), Chopin's friend of fifteen years and the dedicatee. The work occupies an unusual position in the catalogue. It is Chopin's only mature chamber composition (the early Piano Trio Op. 8 of 1828-29 is the only other chamber work, and is rarely performed); it is his only mature work written for an instrument other than the piano; and it was the last of his works to be premiered in his lifetime. Twenty months after the premiere he was dead.

The Op. 65 is in four movements:

**Movement 1 (Allegro moderato) in G minor** : a sonata-form first movement, about ten minutes — the longest of the four and the most structurally ambitious. The piano opens alone with the main theme in G minor; the cello enters at the second statement; the two then _exchange_ the theme back and forth across the development. Listen for moments where the cello carries a melodic line and the piano provides the accompaniment, then the roles reverse — this is genuine chamber-music dialogue, not the piano-dominant texture of the Chopin concertos. The development section explores the main theme through several remote keys; the recapitulation brings it back transformed; the closing pages settle into G minor without dramatic flourish.

**Movement 2 (Scherzo - Allegro con brio) in D minor** : a fast scherzo with a trio middle section in D major, about five minutes. The outer sections have a characteristic Chopin scherzo energy — compound meter, rapid figuration, an off-balance rhythmic profile — but the texture is conversational: the piano and cello alternate the fast figuration rather than the piano carrying it alone. The trio middle section provides lyric contrast; here the cello carries the singing melodic line while the piano provides a gentle accompaniment, in something close to the bel-canto-piano texture of the slow nocturnes (Chapter 7) but with the cello rather than the right hand as the singer.

**Movement 3 (Largo) in B♭ major** : the slow movement, about three minutes. A brief lyric movement; bel-canto texture, with the cello carrying the long-breathed melodic line and the piano providing the bass-note-and-broken-chord accompaniment familiar from the nocturnes. Listen for the cello's singing line: this is the _most direct_ transfer of the bel-canto piano texture from solo piano to chamber music in the Chopin catalogue. If you have heard the Op. 9 No. 2 (Chapter 2) and want to hear the same singing inheritance with a real string instrument carrying the line rather than the right hand of the piano, the Op. 65 Largo is the work.

**Movement 4 (Finale - Allegro) in G minor** : a fast finale in G minor with a closing G-major coda, about six minutes. The most dance-derived of the four movements; some of the rhythmic figures recall the polonaise rhythm without being polonaise. Listen for the closing pages: the music modulates from G minor into G major (the picardy-third resolution that the Funeral March refused), and the final cadence has the lift of a work concluded rather than a procession descending.

The piano-and-cello dialogue across the four movements is genuinely conversational. In the Chopin piano concertos the orchestra accompanies and the piano carries the musical interest; in the Op. 65 the cello and piano are equal partners. Franchomme had been performing with Chopin in private settings since the early 1830s and knew the composer's voice intimately; the Op. 65 was written for that specific cellist and that specific decade-long collaboration. Modern cellists describe the work as one of the most rewarding mid-19th-century cello sonatas in the repertoire — alongside the Mendelssohn Op. 58 (1843) and the Brahms Op. 38 (1865).

The premiere on February 16, 1848 was the public event. Chopin played the last three movements; the first movement was omitted from the program (some accounts say at Chopin's request because of his declining stamina; others say for the program's length). Franchomme played the cello part throughout. The concert drew the established Paris salon community; surviving accounts describe it as a significant musical event of the season. Eight days later, on February 24, the Paris Revolution began; within two months Chopin had left for Britain. The Op. 65 is therefore not just Chopin's last completed major work — it is also the last work that the Paris salon culture, in its pre-Revolution form, would hear from him.

For the listener: the Op. 65 is not a piece for first acquaintance with Chopin. It rewards listeners who have absorbed the late-Chopin harmonic vocabulary (the late nocturnes of Chapter 7, the late mazurkas of Chapter 13) and the late-period structural innovation (Chapter 15). Heard at that point, it is one of the deepest works in the catalogue — a Chopin chamber work that opens a register the rest of the catalogue does not.

#### What the three sonatas attempt

The three works in this chapter — Op. 35, Op. 58, Op. 65 — represent Chopin's engagement with multi-movement formal architecture. The other major Chopin works are single-movement (the Ballades, the Scherzos, the Polonaise-Fantaisie, the Fantasy, the Berceuse, the Barcarolle, the nocturnes, the etudes, the preludes, the mazurkas, the polonaises, the waltzes). The sonatas are the works in which Chopin took on the four-movement structural challenge of his Beethoven-and-Schubert predecessors.

The three are differently successful. The Op. 35 is dramatically the most-effective but analytically contested. The Op. 58 is analytically the most-integrated but less popular. The Op. 65 is the chamber-music outlier — the Cello Sonata that doesn't quite fit either the solo-piano repertoire or the standard cello-chamber repertoire.

Together the three represent Chopin's late-period structural ambition. The Op. 58 in 1844 and the Op. 65 in 1845-47 are late-style works; the Op. 35 in 1839 is middle-period. The three span Chopin's mature multi-movement compositional life from middle through late.

### 12.4 The Stories

#### The Funeral March's place in the catalogue

The Op. 35 Funeral March is the universally-cited funeral-music piece. It has been heard at countless state funerals; in countless films depicting death; in countless private memorial occasions. The piece's reach across cultures is wider than that of any other single Chopin work.

What the Funeral March is: a slow chordal B♭-minor procession of about nine minutes with a contrasting lyric D♭-major middle. It is one of Chopin's universally-recognizable melodies.

What the Funeral March is not: it is not specifically Chopin's response to grief, to mortality, to the Sand relationship, or to any specific event. The piece was composed in 1837, before the Sand partnership, before any major loss in Chopin's adult life beyond his sister Emilia's 1827 death (which by 1837 was a decade past). The popular reception's tendency to associate the Funeral March with specific biographical griefs is generally not supported by the record; the piece works as funeral music for the universal reason of its musical character, not for any specific biographical motivation.

#### The Chopin funeral

A documentary note. Chopin's own funeral was held at the Madeleine in Paris on October 30, 1849, thirteen days after his death on October 17. The order of service included Mozart's Requiem (Chopin had requested it) and Chopin's own Op. 28 Preludes No. 4 and No. 20 (per the contemporary accounts; the specific Preludes used at the funeral are reported in the press of October-November 1849). The Op. 35 Funeral March was not, per the contemporary record, played at Chopin's own funeral. The work has been used at countless later funerals, but the contemporary record does not establish its use at Chopin's own.

This is a detail worth knowing — the funeral music for Chopin's funeral was Mozart and his own Preludes, not the famous Funeral March. The popular assumption that Chopin's funeral used his own Funeral March is reception, not established fact.

#### Recording history briefly

The Op. 35 has many recordings. Major lineages:

The Rubinstein lineage. Arthur Rubinstein recorded the Op. 35 multiple times; the 1961 RCA stereo recording (paired with the Op. 58) is the canonical mid-20th-century document. Warm, songful, particularly fine in the Funeral March's middle section.

The Pollini lineage. Maurizio Pollini recorded the Op. 35 + Op. 58 in 1984 for DG (the Primary Recording for this chapter). Modernist-clean reading; technical precision; structural clarity.

The Argerich lineage. Martha Argerich recorded the Op. 35 in 1974 for DG. The electric reading; particularly intense in the first movement and the strange finale.

The Zimerman lineage. Krystian Zimerman recorded the Op. 35 + Op. 58 in 2002 for DG. The Polish-school depth, weighted but not slow.

For the Op. 58 the same recordings apply. For the Op. 65 Cello Sonata the safe-ground recordings include Jacqueline du Pré with Daniel Barenboim on EMI (1971), Mstislav Rostropovich with Martha Argerich on DG (1980), and Pierre Fournier with Vladimir Horowitz on RCA (1959-60).

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The Op. 35 connects to many works.

To the Op. 28 No. 20 in C minor (Chapter 9). The Op. 28 No. 20 — chordal, slow, march-like — is the miniature companion to the Op. 35 Funeral March. Both works treat the funeral-music idiom but at different scales.

To the Polonaise-Fantaisie Op. 61 (Chapter 14). The late-period Polonaise-Fantaisie shares some of the Op. 35 first movement's structural ambition — multi-thematic development in a single-movement context.

To the late Mazurkas (Chapter 13). The harmonic concentration of the Op. 35's middle movements parallels the harmonic concentration of the late mazurkas; both are middle-period works.

To the Berceuse Op. 57 (Chapter 15). The Berceuse's serenity is the affective opposite of the Funeral March's gravity; both are middle-period works of about five-to-nine minutes, but the emotional registers are opposite.

#### The biographical context: 1837-1847

Locating the three sonatas in the life: the Op. 35 Funeral March was composed in 1837 in the established-Paris middle period (before the Sand partnership). The Op. 35 sonata was completed in 1839 at Nohant, in the first Nohant summer. The Op. 58 was composed in 1844 at Nohant in the sixth Nohant summer. The Op. 65 Cello Sonata was composed 1845-46 across Nohant and Paris, in the late Sand period.

The Op. 65 premiere on February 16, 1848 was Chopin's last Paris public concert before the April-November 1848 British trip. The work therefore stands at the threshold of Chopin's final year.

Chapter 13 covers the late Mazurkas and the Waltzes. Chapter 14 the late Polonaises and the Polonaise-Fantaisie. Chapter 15 the late single-movement masterworks (Fantasy, Berceuse, Barcarolle). Chapter 16 the 1846-1849 final years and the death.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"Schumann's 1841 phrase about the Op. 35 — 'four of his maddest children, brought together under the same roof' — has been quoted in nearly every subsequent commentary. Does the work hold together as a four-movement sonata, or does it work better as a vehicle for the standalone Funeral March?"_
* _"The Op. 35 third-movement Funeral March is the universally-cited funeral-music piece. What makes a piece of music suitable for the funeral occasion, and what would the Funeral March lose if it were heard only as 'the third movement of the Op. 35 sonata'?"_
* _"The Op. 58 in B minor (1844) is analytically more integrated than the Op. 35 but is less popularly known. What does this gap between analytical importance and popular reception tell you about how musical works become 'famous' — is it analysis or accessibility that drives reception?"_
* _"The Cello Sonata Op. 65 (1845-47) is the major chamber work of the Chopin catalogue. Why did Chopin not write more chamber music, given his evident command of the piano-and-cello dialogue in the Op. 65, and what would more chamber Chopin have added to his legacy?"_
* _"Chopin's own funeral on October 30, 1849 used Mozart's Requiem and the Op. 28 Preludes (per the contemporary record), not the Op. 35 Funeral March. What does the gap between the contemporary record (Mozart + Preludes) and the popular assumption (the Funeral March) tell you about how reception shapes biographical memory?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Pollini 1984 Op. 35 + Op. 58 (or one of the alternatives). Listen to the Op. 35 third-movement Funeral March a second time and attend to these moments:

  1. **The opening procession (0:00-0:30).** Listen for the chordal heavy texture, the slow processional rhythm, the low-octave left hand. The opening four bars establish the entire movement's character.

  2. **The transition to the middle section (about 3:00).** Listen for the moment the funeral march pauses and the music shifts to D♭ major. The transition is one of the most-discussed moments in the work; the gear-change between processional and lyric is structurally precise.

  3. **The middle section in D♭ major (about 3:30-6:00).** Listen for the singing right-hand melody. Notice the bel-canto-piano texture — the same texture you know from the nocturnes, now embedded in the funeral march's emotional architecture.

  4. **The return of the funeral march (about 7:00).** Listen for the literal return of the opening B♭-minor procession. The return is unchanged from the opening; the lyric middle does not transform the procession on its return.

If you have time, listen to the rest of the Op. 35 — the turbulent first movement (about 8 minutes), the fast scherzo (about 6 minutes), the strange last movement (about 75 seconds). The four together give you the complete sonata.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair the Op. 35 with Beethoven's Piano Sonata No. 12 in A♭ major Op. 26 (composed 1801) — the Beethoven sonata that also includes a slow funeral march in the third position (the "Funeral March on the Death of a Hero" third movement). Comparing the two funeral marches — Beethoven's 1801 and Chopin's 1837 — shows what each composer made of the funeral-march genre as a sonata movement. The Beethoven is more orchestral in conception (often arranged for orchestra; played in the orchestral version at Beethoven's own funeral in 1827); the Chopin is more pianistic and bel-canto in texture. Recordings of the Beethoven Op. 26 are widely available (Wilhelm Backhaus, Alfred Brendel, Daniel Barenboim, Maurizio Pollini). About thirty minutes total.

**Read** : the chapter on the Sonatas in Jim Samson, _Chopin_ (Oxford Master Musicians series, 1996). Samson treats the Schumann-controversy question, the analytical case for the Op. 35 as a unified work, and the structural sophistication of the Op. 58. About twenty pages.

* * *

## Chapter 13: The Late Mazurkas + the Waltzes

This chapter completes the mazurka arc that Chapter 4 launched. The late mazurkas — Op. 50 (1842), Op. 56 (1844), Op. 59 (1845), Op. 63 (1847), and the posthumously published Op. 67 and Op. 68 — represent the great within-genre evolution from the early Op. 6 (1830-31) through the late 1840s. The chapter also covers the published-in-life waltzes — Op. 18 (1834), Op. 34 (1838), Op. 42 (1840), Op. 64 (1847) — as a separate genre arc.

The chapter anchors on the Mazurka in C minor Op. 56 No. 3 (1844, ~7 minutes) as the late-mazurka anchor, with brief touchpoints on the Op. 59 and Op. 63 sets. For the waltzes, the chapter anchors on the Waltz in C♯ minor Op. 64 No. 2 (1847, ~4 minutes) as the mature-waltz touchstone.

### 13.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Nohant, summer 1842. Eugène Delacroix has been Sand's guest at the country house for several weeks. He paints in a converted attic studio; he writes long letters back to Paris friends; and across this and subsequent Nohant visits he records, in letters and (in later years) in the journal he kept from 1847 onward, his observations of Chopin at work. Delacroix is in the room next to Chopin's; the sound of the composer at the piano — going through passages and reworkings rather than finished pieces — is one of the recurring elements of the Nohant atmosphere.

Delacroix's recurring observation across his Nohant visits is that Chopin works in fragments and revisits them obsessively. The same handful of bars are tried, set aside, returned to days later, tried again. By the time a mazurka or a ballade reaches publication, every phrase has been weighed many times. The 1842 summer that Delacroix watched produced the Ballade No. 4, the Polonaise Op. 53, and the Mazurkas Op. 50 — the same compositional intensity that, two years later (1844), would produce the Mazurkas Op. 56 anchored in this chapter.

What the Delacroix observation lets us see: the late mazurkas were not improvised salon pieces dashed off in the country quiet. They were the result of months of work — fragments tried and refined, harmonic experiments tested and accepted or rejected, structural designs sketched and rebuilt. The Op. 56 No. 3 in C minor — about seven minutes, substantially longer than the early Op. 17 No. 4 anchored in Chapter 4 — is among the works in which that intensity becomes audible.

The mazurka by the mid-1840s has become for Chopin a genre that can carry substantial musical argument. The early Op. 6 mazurkas (1830-31) were salon-length dance miniatures; the late Op. 56 No. 3 is a concert-scale mazurka in which the genre's rhythmic profile and Polish-folk-modal coloring are preserved while the harmonic ambition and the structural development have reached the late-Chopin scale. The arc from Op. 6 to Op. 56 is the great within-genre evolution; the Op. 56 No. 3 is its peak.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

For the Mazurka in C minor Op. 56 No. 3:

  1. **The opening section.** Mazurka rhythm in C minor; the texture is fuller than in the early mazurkas; the harmonic motion is wider; the melody is more elaborated. Listen for the immediate sense of mature-Chopin density compared to the Op. 17 No. 4 of Chapter 4.

  2. **The middle section in A♭ major.** A brighter, more lyric section in the relative major. The contrast is similar to that in the early mazurkas but the harmonic content is richer and the melodic development more sustained.

  3. **The closing pages and the return.** The work returns to C minor through a passage that is harmonically more restless than any early mazurka. The harmonic vocabulary includes extreme chromaticism — moments where the music seems to leave conventional C-minor functional harmony and reach into more distant territory.

For the Waltz in C♯ minor Op. 64 No. 2:

  1. **The opening tempo-rubato waltz.** The waltz rhythm in C♯ minor; the right hand sings a lyric melody; the left hand provides the bass-and-chord waltz pattern. The tempo is moderate; the rubato is present.

  2. **The middle section in D♭ major (the enharmonic-equivalent parallel major).** A faster, more virtuosic central episode. The right-hand figuration is brilliant; the harmonic motion moves freely.

  3. **The return.** The lyric C♯-minor waltz returns. The piece ends in C♯ minor.

#### One-sentence background

Mazurka in C minor Op. 56 No. 3 (composed 1844 at Nohant; published 1845; ~7 minutes). Waltz in C♯ minor Op. 64 No. 2 (composed 1846-47; published 1847; ~4 minutes). The **Primary Recording** for the Op. 56 No. 3 is Arthur Rubinstein on RCA Victor's 1965-66 complete Mazurkas (the same set anchored in Chapter 4). For the Op. 64 No. 2 the Primary Recording is Arthur Rubinstein on RCA Victor's 1963 complete Waltzes.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Chopin Mazurka C minor Op 56 No 3 Rubinstein" / "Chopin Waltz C sharp minor Op 64 No 2 Rubinstein" ZH: "肖邦 c小调玛祖卡 作品56之3" / "肖邦 升c小调圆舞曲 作品64之2" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** :

* Vladimir Ashkenazy on Decca (complete Mazurkas, 1976-85; complete Waltzes, 1976-77) — the Russian-school readings.
* Dinu Lipatti on EMI (1950 selected Waltzes, the canonical pre-war Waltzes recording before his death from leukemia at 33) — for the Waltzes.
* Idil Biret on Naxos (complete Mazurkas, complete Waltzes, 1990s) — the budget-tier readings.

### 13.2 The Listening Window

Timestamps refer to the Rubinstein recordings.

**Op. 56 No. 3 in C minor** (about 7:00):

* **0:00 Opening.** Mazurka rhythm in C minor; right-hand melodic line; left-hand bass-and-chord pattern.
* **About 0:30 Development of the opening material.** The melody is repeated and elaborated; the harmonic motion broadens.
* **About 1:30 A modulation into a related key.** The music moves through related minor and major key areas.
* **About 2:30 The middle section in A♭ major.** A brighter, more lyric section. The mazurka rhythm continues; the mood lifts.
* **About 4:00 A more harmonically restless passage.** The music reaches into more distant harmonic territory; chromaticism and modal mixture become prominent.
* **About 5:00 Return.** The C-minor mazurka returns in elaborated form.
* **About 6:00 Closing pages.** The harmonic motion narrows; the melody settles toward the cadence.
* **About 7:00 Final cadence.** The mazurka ends quietly in C minor.

**Op. 64 No. 2 in C♯ minor** (about 4:00):

* **0:00 Opening.** Lyric waltz in C♯ minor; tempo rubato; right hand sings; left hand keeps the waltz time.
* **About 0:30 Development of the main material.** The melody is repeated with slight variation.
* **About 1:00 Middle section in D♭ major.** A faster, more virtuosic section; the right-hand figuration is brilliant.
* **About 2:00 Continued middle section.** The figuration continues; the harmonic motion moves freely.
* **About 2:30 Return.** The C♯-minor waltz returns.
* **About 3:30 Closing pages.** The waltz continues; the harmonic motion narrows.
* **About 4:00 Final cadence.** The waltz ends quietly in C♯ minor.

### 13.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The late-mazurka harmonic vocabulary

The Op. 56, Op. 59, and Op. 63 mazurkas, together with the late-1840s sketches published posthumously as Op. 67 and Op. 68, document the late-Chopin harmonic vocabulary at its clearest. The features:

* **Extreme chromaticism** — passages where the music moves freely through key areas that share few common tones with the home key.
* **Modal mixture** — using both major and minor versions of the same chord in close proximity.
* **Polish-folk-modal coloring extended** — the Lydian raised fourth and the augmented-second interval used more extensively and more boldly than in the early mazurkas.
* **Distant modulations** — moves to key areas that the early mazurkas would not have approached.
* **A near-Wagnerian harmonic ambition in places** — modulating across chromatically-rising or chromatically-descending chord sequences that anticipate the harmonic vocabulary that Wagner's _Tristan und Isolde_ (1859) would explore at length.

The late mazurkas are not Wagner — they remain in 3/4 time, they retain the mazurka rhythmic profile, they preserve the Polish-folk-music connection. But the harmonic vocabulary has reached into territory that the early mazurkas did not approach, and that the contemporaneous (1840s) wider European harmonic ambition was reaching as well.

Patience pays off here. The late mazurkas reward repeated listening: on a first listen the rhythmic and melodic profile is heard clearly; on a second listen the harmonic detail begins to register; on a third listen the harmonic restlessness is its own subject.

#### The late-mazurka arc

A brief survey of the late mazurkas (1842-1849).

**Op. 50** (1842; three mazurkas in G major, A♭ major, C♯ minor): the first of the late-style mazurka cycles. The Op. 50 No. 3 in C♯ minor is the most-discussed of the three, with notably complex contrapuntal writing in the trio middle section.

**Op. 56** (1844; three mazurkas in B major, C major, C minor): the cycle of this chapter's anchor work (Op. 56 No. 3). The harmonic ambition is extended; the Op. 56 No. 3 is among the most-developed Chopin mazurkas.

**Op. 59** (1845; three mazurkas in A minor, A♭ major, F♯ minor): one of the most-celebrated late cycles. The Op. 59 No. 1 in A minor returns to the melancholic posture of the Op. 17 No. 4 (anchored in Chapter 4) at full late-style harmonic depth. The Op. 59 No. 3 in F♯ minor is structurally complex and harmonically restless.

**Op. 63** (1847; three mazurkas in B major, F minor, C♯ minor): the last cycle published in Chopin's lifetime. The Op. 63 No. 3 in C♯ minor is the late-mazurka peak — a structurally-elaborate late-style mazurka with the genre's full harmonic and structural ambition at its most-realized.

**Op. 67 and Op. 68 (posthumous publications)** : Julian Fontana, Chopin's friend and copyist, published the unpublished mazurkas in 1855 as Op. 67 (four mazurkas) and Op. 68 (four mazurkas). The posthumous publications raise complications: Chopin in his lifetime had explicitly told Fontana not to publish these works (the "do not publish these" wishes appear in his letters); Fontana published them anyway, partly for financial reasons after Chopin's death and partly because the music was strong. Modern editions of the mazurkas often distinguish the in-life-published works from the posthumous; the listener should know that the Op. 67 and Op. 68 were published against Chopin's stated wishes.

The Mazurka in F minor Op. 68 No. 4 is traditionally identified as Chopin's last composed mazurka (or among the last; the exact ordering of his final compositions is itself a scholarly question; newer Polish editions have revised Fontana's order). The work is brief, melancholic, and harmonically inward — the late-Chopin in concentrated final form.

#### The waltzes

A separate brief survey. The published-in-life waltzes:

**Op. 18 in E♭ major** (1834; "Grande Valse Brillante"): the first of the published waltzes. Bright, virtuosic, ballroom-style. ~5 minutes.

**Op. 34** (1838; "Trois Valses Brillantes"; A♭ major, A minor, F major): three contrasting waltzes. The Op. 34 No. 2 in A minor is the most-melancholic and most-loved of the three; ~6 minutes.

**Op. 42 in A♭ major** (1840; "Grande Valse"): a longer, more developed waltz; ~4 minutes. Often cited as Chopin's most-sophisticated waltz.

**Op. 64** (1847; "Trois Valses"; D♭ major, C♯ minor, A♭ major): the three Op. 64 waltzes published in Chopin's last year of full health. The Op. 64 No. 1 in D♭ major is the famous "Minute Waltz" (the popular nickname is not Chopin's; the "minute" refers to "small" or "petite" in French rather than to the time duration, which is closer to 90 seconds); the Op. 64 No. 2 in C♯ minor is the anchor work of this chapter; the Op. 64 No. 3 in A♭ major is the closing waltz.

The posthumous waltzes (Op. 69 in two waltzes, Op. 70 in three waltzes; published by Fontana in 1855) are early or middle-period works that Chopin had not published in his lifetime. The "do not publish these" complication applies.

#### The Op. 64 No. 2 specifically

The Waltz in C♯ minor Op. 64 No. 2 is Chopin's most-mature single waltz. The piece is shorter than some of the earlier waltzes (about 4 minutes versus the Op. 42's 4 minutes and Op. 18's 5 minutes) but more concentrated. The lyric C♯-minor opening is restrained; the D♭-major middle section is virtuosic without being showy; the return is reflective rather than ballroom-grand.

The Op. 64 No. 2 is the waltz that the C♯ minor — Chopin's favorite minor key after G minor — was reaching toward across the catalogue. The mood is more inward than the "Grande Valse Brillante" of Op. 18; the texture is more restrained than the Op. 34 No. 2 in A minor; the harmonic motion is more secure than the Op. 42's restlessness. The Op. 64 No. 2 represents the mature-Chopin waltz, just as the Op. 56 No. 3 represents the mature-Chopin mazurka.

#### The Fontana publishing question

A brief note on Julian Fontana (1810-1869) and the posthumous publishing question. Fontana was Chopin's friend from Warsaw, his copyist, and his executor. After Chopin's October 17, 1849 death, Fontana inherited (with Chopin's sister Ludwika's agreement) the responsibility for managing the unpublished manuscripts.

Chopin had instructed Fontana, in a surviving letter from his final months, to burn the unpublished sketches and not to publish anything that Chopin had not himself sent to the publisher. The instruction was clear; the documentation of Chopin's wishes is secure.

Fontana did not burn the sketches. He kept them, organized them, and over the following years (especially 1855) published them in editions assigned as opus numbers Op. 66 through Op. 74 — including the late mazurkas Op. 67 and Op. 68, the waltzes Op. 69 and Op. 70, the songs Op. 74, and other works. The publications were against Chopin's stated wishes; they have circulated for 170 years as part of the Chopin catalogue; modern editions often note their posthumous nature without removing them from the standard repertoire.

The complication: the Mazurka in F minor Op. 68 No. 4 — traditionally identified among Chopin's last compositions — exists in the catalogue only because Fontana published it against Chopin's wishes. The work is heard, taught, recorded, performed; its publication-against-wishes is sometimes noted, sometimes not. The reader should know that the late mazurkas Op. 67 and Op. 68 carry this complication; the in-life-published mazurkas Op. 50, Op. 56, Op. 59, and Op. 63 do not.

### 13.4 The Stories

#### How the Op. 56 No. 3 sits among the mazurkas

The Mazurka Op. 56 No. 3 is not the most-known Chopin mazurka. The Op. 7 No. 1 in B♭ major is more frequently played in popular contexts; the Op. 17 No. 4 in A minor (Chapter 4 anchor) is the melancholy-mazurka archetype most-known in the larger culture; the Op. 33 No. 4 in B minor is one of the perennial popular favorites.

What the Op. 56 No. 3 is: a sophisticated late-style mazurka of about seven minutes that demonstrates Chopin's within-genre evolution at its mature peak. The harmonic complexity rewards repeated listening; the structural development is substantial; the late-Chopin harmonic vocabulary is heard in concentrated form.

What the Op. 56 No. 3 is not: it is not an introductory mazurka. A first-time listener to the Chopin mazurkas should start with the Op. 7 No. 1 (bright dance-style), the Op. 17 No. 4 (melancholy archetype), or the Op. 33 No. 4 (popular favorite) before approaching the Op. 56 No. 3. The work rewards listeners who have already absorbed the genre's basic vocabulary.

The Waltz Op. 64 No. 2 is more accessible than the Op. 56 No. 3. The waltz genre's familiarity from the larger ballroom culture provides immediate listening orientation; the Op. 64 No. 2's relatively short duration (about 4 minutes) suits a first acquaintance. The work has become one of the standard Chopin waltzes — frequently recorded, frequently performed, frequently used as a recital encore.

#### The within-mazurka-genre evolution

A short summary of the mazurka arc across Chopin's life, as the reader can now trace it across Chapter 4 and Chapter 13.

**Early mazurkas (Op. 6, 7, 17, 24; 1830-35):** dance-derived, salon-length, with the Polish-folk-modal coloring present but the harmonic ambition modest. The Op. 17 No. 4 in A minor anchors the early-mazurka melancholy.

**Mid-period mazurkas (Op. 30, 33, 41, 50; 1837-42):** the dance-derived posture continues; the harmonic ambition increases; the lengths begin to extend.

**Late mazurkas (Op. 56, 59, 63; 1844-47):** the late-style mazurka peak. Harmonic experimentation at full; structural development substantial; lengths up to seven minutes. The Op. 56 No. 3 in C minor anchors this chapter.

**Posthumous mazurkas (Op. 67, 68; published 1855):** sketches and unpublished mazurkas, including the Op. 68 No. 4 traditionally identified as Chopin's last composed mazurka. Documented against Chopin's stated wishes; circulating for 170 years.

The arc from Op. 6 No. 1 in F♯ minor (1830-31) to Op. 68 No. 4 in F minor (1849) is one of the great within-genre evolutions in Western music. The early Op. 6 No. 1 is a confident salon mazurka; the late Op. 68 No. 4 is a brief, melancholic, harmonically inward late-Chopin essence. Same composer, same genre, same Polish-folk-music roots — but eighteen years of musical development have produced fundamentally different mazurka substances.

Chapter 17 §17.6 collects the within-genre evolution into the synthesis discussion.

#### Recording history briefly

The complete Mazurkas have many recordings. The Rubinstein 1965-66, the Ashkenazy 1976-85, the Sztompka 1960s, the Biret 1990s, the Ohlsson 2008-09 — these were discussed in Chapter 4. For the Op. 56 No. 3 specifically, the Rubinstein 1965-66 is the safe ground; the Sztompka 1960s is the Polish-school predecessor.

The complete Waltzes have many recordings. The Rubinstein 1963 RCA stereo set is the canonical mid-20th-century document. Earlier Rubinstein Waltzes recordings on RCA mono (1953) are the predecessors. The Dinu Lipatti 1950 EMI selected Waltzes is the canonical pre-war document — Lipatti was the great Romanian Chopinist of his generation, dying at 33 in 1950 of leukemia; the recording was made in his final months and is one of the most-revered Chopin documents in any genre. The Ashkenazy 1976-77 complete Waltzes on Decca is a strong modern alternative.

For the Op. 64 No. 2 the safe ground is Rubinstein 1963, Lipatti 1950, Ashkenazy 1976. Each has a different approach: Rubinstein warm and singing; Lipatti restrained and inward; Ashkenazy lyric and Russian-school-articulate.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The late mazurkas connect to many works.

To the late nocturnes (Chapter 7). The Op. 62 No. 1 in B major (1846) and the Op. 56 No. 3 in C minor (1844) share the late-style harmonic vocabulary at concentrated scale. The two works together demonstrate Chopin's late-style at maximum density.

To the Polonaise-Fantaisie Op. 61 (Chapter 14). The Polonaise-Fantaisie shares the late-style harmonic vocabulary at larger scale. The Op. 56 No. 3 is a 7-minute concentrated example; the Op. 61 is a 13-minute extended example.

To the Op. 49 Fantasy and Op. 60 Barcarolle (Chapter 15). The late single-movement masterworks share some of the harmonic and structural ambition of the late mazurkas.

To the Op. 65 Cello Sonata (Chapter 12). The late-period chamber work shares the late-style harmonic vocabulary in the four-movement context.

The waltzes connect more loosely to the rest of the catalogue. The Op. 64 No. 2 shares some harmonic moves with the Op. 28 No. 15 ("Raindrop") of Op. 28 in the related D♭ major / C♯ minor parallel-major-minor pair.

#### The biographical context: 1842-1849

The late mazurkas and the late waltzes in the life: the Op. 50 mazurkas were composed in 1842 at Nohant in the fourth Sand-period summer (the same summer as the Ballade No. 4 and the Polonaise Op. 53 "Heroic"). The Op. 56 mazurkas were composed in 1844 at Nohant in the sixth Sand-period summer. The Op. 59 mazurkas were composed in 1845 at Nohant in the seventh summer. The Op. 63 mazurkas were composed in 1847 — partly at Nohant (the seventh-eighth summer) and partly in Paris after the 1847 Sand-Chopin break; the Op. 63 publication of 1847 brought the late-style mazurka cycle to closure just as the Sand partnership was ending.

The Op. 64 waltzes were composed in 1846-47 across the Nohant summers and the break period. The Op. 64 No. 2 in C♯ minor is the mature-waltz touchstone of Chopin's final productive period.

The mazurka in F minor Op. 68 No. 4, traditionally identified as among Chopin's last compositions, was composed in 1849 in Paris in Chopin's final months. The exact composition date is uncertain; the work was published posthumously by Fontana in 1855. The piece carries the final-Chopin mazurka essence in concentrated form.

Chapter 14 covers the Polonaises and the Polonaise-Fantaisie. Chapter 15 the late single-movement masterworks. Chapter 16 the final years and the death.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The arc from the Mazurka Op. 6 No. 1 (1830-31) to the Op. 68 No. 4 (1849) is one of the great within-genre evolutions in Western music. Pick one feature you can hear that changes across the arc — harmonic vocabulary, melodic character, structural development, mood — and describe its trajectory from early to late."_
* _"The late mazurkas (Op. 56, 59, 63) demonstrate Chopin's late-style harmonic vocabulary. The Op. 56 No. 3 (this chapter's anchor) is harmonically restless in a way the Op. 17 No. 4 (Chapter 4) is not. What does the late-style harmonic ambition do to the genre — does it strengthen the mazurka or strain its limits?"_
* _"The Op. 67 and Op. 68 mazurkas were published by Fontana in 1855 against Chopin's stated wishes ('do not publish these'). The works have circulated for 170 years as part of the standard repertoire. How should the reader weigh the composer's wishes against the music's value, and is the listener complicit in the publishing violation when she listens to the Op. 68 No. 4?"_
* _"The Op. 64 No. 2 in C♯ minor is Chopin's most-mature single waltz. What does maturity in a waltz mean — what does the Op. 64 No. 2 do that the early Op. 18 'Grande Valse Brillante' does not?"_
* _"The 'Minute Waltz' (Op. 64 No. 1) is one of the most-known Chopin waltzes; the popular nickname refers to 'petite' not to a time duration of one minute. What does the nickname's persistence tell you about how works acquire reception-shapes that diverge from the music itself?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Rubinstein 1965-66 Mazurkas and 1963 Waltzes (or the alternatives). Listen to the two anchor works a second time and attend to these moments:

  1. **Mazurka Op. 56 No. 3: the opening section (0:00-1:00).** Confirm the mazurka rhythm in C minor; notice the immediate sense of mature-Chopin harmonic density compared to the Op. 17 No. 4 you heard in Chapter 4.

  2. **Mazurka Op. 56 No. 3: the harmonically restless passage (around 4:00).** Listen for the moment the music reaches into distant harmonic territory. Chromatic motion is dense; modal mixture is present. This is the late-style harmonic vocabulary in concentrated form.

  3. **Waltz Op. 64 No. 2: the opening lyric (0:00-1:00).** Listen for the singing C♯-minor lyric; notice the tempo rubato — the left hand keeps time, the right hand floats around it.

  4. **Waltz Op. 64 No. 2: the middle section (around 1:00-2:00).** Listen for the brighter D♭-major virtuosic central episode. The contrast with the lyric opening is the work's emotional architecture.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair the Op. 56 No. 3 with the Op. 17 No. 4 (from Chapter 4). Listen to the Op. 17 No. 4 first (about 4.5 minutes), then the Op. 56 No. 3 (about 7 minutes). The same composer, the same genre, the same melancholic posture, but separated by twelve years. The differences are wide — harmonic vocabulary, structural development, length, density. The exercise demonstrates the within-genre evolution in direct comparison. About fifteen minutes total.

**Read** : the chapter on the Mazurkas in Jim Samson, _Chopin_ (Oxford Master Musicians series, 1996). Samson's coverage of the late-mazurka harmonic vocabulary, the within-genre evolution, the political dimension, and the Fontana posthumous-publication question is the standard English-language analytical treatment. About thirty pages; useful as the companion to both Chapter 4 and the present chapter.
