## Chapter 14: The Polonaises — From Childhood Through Op. 53 "Heroic" (1842) and the Polonaise-Fantaisie Op. 61 (1846)

The polonaise was Chopin's other Polish-folk-rooted genre. Where the mazurka was the village dance from Mazovia, the polonaise was the court-and-ceremonial dance with national-historical associations across Poland's centuries. Chopin composed in the polonaise across his whole life: the first published polonaise was composed in 1817 at age seven (the Polonaise in G minor); the last polonaise — the Polonaise-Fantaisie Op. 61 — was composed in 1846 at age thirty-six. The arc is wider in years than any other Chopin genre, though the published-in-life polonaise count is small (about ten).

This chapter anchors on the Polonaise in A♭ major Op. 53 (1842, the most-performed Chopin polonaise, known popularly as the "Heroic" — the nickname is 19th-century reception, not Chopin's) and the Polonaise-Fantaisie in A♭ major Op. 61 (1846, the late-period structural-innovation hybrid). The chapter also covers the early childhood polonaises briefly and the mature middle-period polonaises Op. 26 (1834-35), Op. 40 (1838-40), and Op. 44 (1841).

### 14.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

The polonaise was already, when Chopin began composing in the genre, a fully-formed Polish national symbol. It had two-and-a-half centuries of history: court polonaises from the 17th century had marked Polish royal ceremonies; Bach had used the polonaise as a movement in some of his suites; Mozart had written polonaises; the polonaise had circulated through European salons as a recognizably Polish dance. By the 1810s and 1820s the polonaise carried explicit Polish-national associations, particularly in the partitioned-Poland context where Polish music was one of the few ways national identity could be asserted publicly.

Chopin's father Mikołaj — French-born, Polish-adopted — taught polonaise dancing among his other employments. The young Fryderyk grew up with the polonaise in the household and in Warsaw's public life. The first published Chopin work was a polonaise — the Polonaise in G minor of 1817, published in Warsaw when Chopin was seven (the work is preserved and is sometimes recorded).

Chopin's adult polonaises continued the genre's national-symbolic role. The early Paris-period Op. 26 polonaises (1834-35) were composed in the early-émigré years; the Op. 40 polonaises (1838-40) were composed during the Mallorca winter and the early Sand period; the Op. 44 polonaise (1841) and Op. 53 polonaise (1842) were the great Nohant-summer polonaises; the Op. 61 Polonaise-Fantaisie was the late-period genre-hybrid composed at Nohant in 1846. The arc moves from the inherited dance-form polonaise through the elevated-concert polonaise to the late-period polonaise-fantasy hybrid.

The Op. 53 "Heroic" is the most-celebrated Chopin polonaise. It has been used at countless Polish national-historical moments — the 1848 Spring of Nations protests; the 1863 January Uprising commemorations; the 1918 Polish independence celebrations; the 1944 Warsaw Uprising commemorations; the 1989 post-communist independence celebrations. The work has become one of the recognized Polish national pieces, alongside the Chopin mazurkas, the Mickiewicz poetry, and the national anthem _Mazurek Dąbrowskiego_. The reception's reach is wider than for any other Chopin polonaise.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

For the Polonaise in A♭ major Op. 53 "Heroic":

  1. **The opening cascade and the polonaise theme.** The work opens with cascading right-hand-and-left-hand figuration in fortissimo — about thirty seconds of preparatory virtuoso writing — before the polonaise theme arrives. The theme is announced in A♭ major with the polonaise rhythmic pattern (3/4 with the characteristic quarter-eighth-eighth accent profile). The melody is broad, dignified, almost royal.

  2. **The middle section in E major (the trio).** Following development of the main material, the music shifts to E major (a distant modulation from A♭ major). The middle section features the "ostinato bass" — a repeated low-bass figure that pulses across many bars while the right hand develops melodic and harmonic motion above. The ostinato is one of the most-discussed passages in the polonaise literature.

  3. **The return and the closing pages.** The polonaise theme returns; the work builds to a fortissimo close. The energy and dynamic range are larger than in any of the earlier polonaises.

For the Polonaise-Fantaisie Op. 61:

  1. **The opening introduction.** The work begins with about ninety seconds of preparatory writing — slow, exploratory, harmonically restless — before the polonaise rhythm proper arrives. The introduction is more substantial than the Op. 53's opening cascade.

  2. **The polonaise theme in A♭ major.** When the polonaise rhythm arrives, the theme is more harmonically restless than the Op. 53 main material. The piece does not stay in A♭ major for long; the modulations are wider.

  3. **The recitative passages.** The Op. 61 includes recitative-like passages — declamatory passages where the right hand plays speech-like rhythms over sparse left-hand support. These recitative passages are a recurring Chopin signature (cf. the Op. 21 Larghetto recitative in Chapter 3); the Op. 61 deploys them at a larger scale.

#### One-sentence background

Polonaise in A♭ major Op. 53 (composed 1842 at Nohant; published 1843; ~7 minutes). Polonaise-Fantaisie in A♭ major Op. 61 (composed 1845-46 at Nohant; published 1846; ~13 minutes). The **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Maurizio Pollini on Deutsche Grammophon (recorded 1975, the complete polonaises including Op. 53 and Op. 61 on a single LP), chosen for the modernist-clean approach and the technical precision in the demanding Op. 53.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Chopin Polonaise A flat major Op 53 Heroic Pollini" / "Chopin Polonaise-Fantaisie Op 61 Pollini" ZH: "肖邦 降A大调英雄波兰舞曲 作品53 波利尼" / "肖邦 波兰幻想曲 作品61" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** :

* Arthur Rubinstein on RCA (recorded 1964 stereo, the complete polonaises): the canonical warm-and-singing reading.
* Vladimir Horowitz, various live recordings (the Carnegie Hall concerts include the Op. 53 multiple times across his career): the Russian-virtuoso reading.
* Krystian Zimerman on DG (recorded 1981, complete polonaises): the Polish-school depth.

### 14.2 The Listening Window

Timestamps refer to the Pollini 1975 Primary Recording.

**Op. 53 "Heroic" in A♭ major** (about 7:00):

* **0:00 Opening cascade.** Listen for the _full keyboard_ in use from the first second: the left hand sweeps up from low A♭, the right hand cascades down from high A♭, the two crossing in the middle register. No melody yet — just preparatory virtuosity at fortissimo, like a herald's fanfare before the procession enters. About thirty seconds of this.
* **About 0:30 The polonaise theme.** The main theme arrives in A♭ major. Listen for the _quarter-eighth-eighth_ rhythmic profile (a long-short-short pulse on beat one) that identifies the polonaise — the same rhythm a 17th-century Polish court procession used. The melody is broad and dignified; the right hand carries it in chords (not a single singing line), the left hand keeps the dance pulse.
* **About 1:30 Development of the polonaise material.** The theme is restated with more elaborate right-hand decoration. Listen for the harmony briefly leaving A♭ major and returning — about a minute of variations on the same broad theme.
* **About 2:30 Transition to the middle section.** Listen for the moment the polonaise rhythm pauses and the harmony shifts away from A♭ major. The music _steps sideways_ — A♭ is far from E major (the destination), and the modulation is one of the most striking in the polonaise repertoire.
* **About 3:00 The middle section in E major (the trio).** Listen for the _ostinato_ : a single low-bass figure (a rapid E-D♯-E-D♯-E figure played by the left hand thumb) that pulses across many bars. Above it, the right hand develops a melodic line with brass-fanfare character. The ostinato is unmistakable once you hear it; the steady pulsing has often been heard as evoking a galloping or processional approach.
* **About 4:30 The ostinato continues.** The repeated bass figure pulses on; the right hand reaches a series of climactic chords. Listen for the moment the figure stays _exactly the same_ while everything above it changes — this is the late-Chopin pedal-and-bass innovation in concentrated form.
* **About 5:00 Return to A♭ major.** The polonaise theme returns. Listen for the texture growing fuller than at 0:30 — more notes per beat, fuller chords, the left hand now sometimes doubling the right hand's chords at the octave below.
* **About 6:00 Building toward the close.** The dynamic builds. Listen for the appearance of _octave-doubled_ writing: the right hand often plays the same melodic line in two octaves simultaneously, creating a near-orchestral texture on a single instrument.
* **About 6:45 The closing pages.** The polonaise theme is restated at full force. The texture is at maximum density.
* **About 7:00 Final cadence.** Listen for the _three_ concluding A♭-major chords — fortissimo, evenly spaced, with the left hand and right hand striking together. The work ends not with a flourish but with a triple affirmation.

**Op. 61 Polonaise-Fantaisie in A♭ major** (about 13:00):

* **0:00 Introduction.** Listen for the _slow_ opening — single chords spaced out, with silences between them, in no clear key area. Compare with the Op. 53 opening cascade (above): there everything happens at once at fortissimo; here the texture is sparse and the harmony itself is uncertain for nearly ninety seconds. The work is _not yet_ a polonaise.
* **About 1:30 Polonaise theme arrives.** Listen for the polonaise rhythm finally appearing — the same _quarter-eighth-eighth_ profile as the Op. 53. But the theme it carries is harmonically restless: where the Op. 53's main theme stays securely in A♭ major for its whole opening statement, the Op. 61's polonaise theme begins drifting away from the home key within four bars.
* **About 3:00 First development.** The polonaise material is elaborated. Listen for the harmonic motion reaching keys A♭ would not normally visit — wide modulations that signal "this is a _Fantaisie_ , not a conventional polonaise."
* **About 4:30 A recitative passage.** Listen for the moment the polonaise rhythm _stops_ and the right hand plays an irregularly-rhythmed line — speech-like, with pauses, more like a singer's recitative between operatic arias than like a dance. Compare with the recitative passage in the Op. 21 Larghetto (Chapter 3, around 5:00); the Op. 61 transplants the same device into a much larger work.
* **About 6:00 A more lyric central episode.** A contrasting section in a related major-key area; bel-canto-piano singing texture; the polonaise rhythm is suspended.
* **About 8:00 Second recitative.** Another declamatory passage. The structural design relies on these interruptions: the polonaise rhythm gives way, the recitative argues, the music returns to the rhythm transformed.
* **About 9:30 Building toward the climax.** The polonaise theme returns transformed. Listen for the texture growing fuller — more notes per beat than at 1:30, with the right hand sometimes in octaves and the left hand sometimes doubling chords.
* **About 11:00 The climactic passages.** Dense texture, full dynamic range. The polonaise rhythm is unmistakable here; the _Fantaisie_ element gives way to the _Polonaise_ element for the work's peak.
* **About 12:00 Closing pages.** The music settles. Listen for the harmonic motion _narrowing_ — fewer modulations, the home key reasserting itself.
* **About 13:00 Final cadence.** The work ends in A♭ major. Listen for the close: where the Op. 53 ended with three fortissimo affirmations, the Op. 61 ends with a more harmonically resolved cadence — the _Fantaisie_ element accepts the _Polonaise_ destination without the dramatic finality of the earlier work.

### 14.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The polonaise rhythm

The polonaise is in 3/4 time, with a characteristic rhythmic pattern: quarter-note on beat one, eighth-eighth on the rest of beat one, then quarter-note on beat two, with accents typically falling on beats one and three (not on beat two, as in the mazurka). The rhythm gives the dance its stately, processional character — a slow march in three. Polish court polonaises from the 17th century forward used the rhythm for ceremonial processions; Polish national-historical commemorations often used the rhythm; the polonaise was one of the standard dances at Polish-aristocratic balls.

Chopin's polonaises preserve the rhythm faithfully. The Op. 53 main theme is in textbook polonaise rhythm; the Op. 61 Polonaise-Fantaisie's polonaise sections preserve the rhythm even as the work moves beyond conventional polonaise structure into the genre-hybrid territory the "Fantaisie" element names.

#### The polonaise vs. the mazurka

A short comparison. Both polonaise and mazurka are Polish-folk-music genres in 3/4; both became Chopin signatures.

**Polonaise** : 3/4, stately, processional, with the quarter-eighth-eighth rhythmic profile and accents on beats one and three; courtly and national-historical associations; Chopin composed about 21 polonaises across his life (about ten published in life).

**Mazurka** : 3/4 or 3/8, faster (typically), with the offbeat accent often on beat two or three; village-folk-dance origins; Chopin composed about 59 mazurkas (about thirty-two published in life).

The mazurka is the more numerous of the two; the polonaise is the more public-ceremonial of the two. Together they comprise the Polish-folk-rooted core of the Chopin catalogue.

#### The Op. 53 specifically

The Polonaise in A♭ major Op. 53 is the most-performed Chopin polonaise. The reasons:

* **The work is structurally readable.** The opening cascade, the polonaise theme, the middle section with ostinato, the return, the dramatic close — the structural shape is heard clearly within seven minutes.
* **The main theme is broad and singable.** The A♭-major polonaise melody is one of the most-immediately-loved Chopin themes.
* **The ostinato middle section is musically and pianistically striking.** The repeated low-bass figure (with eight repetitions of the E-D♯-E pattern, all within the same bar) creates a distinctive textural moment.
* **The dramatic close is fortissimo and final.** The work's ending has the authority of a national-historical statement.

The "Heroic" nickname is 19th-century reception, not Chopin's. The nickname's appropriateness has been debated — some readings hear the work as heroically national-historical (a celebration of Polish identity); others hear it as more inward-virtuosic (the heroism being pianistic rather than national-historical). Both readings are legitimate; the music supports both.

#### The Op. 53 ostinato

A note on the Op. 53 middle section's ostinato. The middle section in E major (a distant modulation from A♭ major) features a repeated low-bass figure that pulses through many bars. The bass pattern repeats while the right hand develops melodic and harmonic motion above. The ostinato is one of the most-discussed passages in the Chopin polonaise literature.

The technical demand of the passage is recognized as one of the most-difficult left-hand passages in the standard Chopin repertoire. The left hand must execute the rapid repeated figure with precision and stamina across many bars; many pianists slow the tempo slightly through the ostinato to make the figure manageable. Pollini's 1975 recording maintains tempo through the ostinato with characteristic technical precision; Rubinstein's 1964 reading allows slight tempo flexibility; some recordings stretch the ostinato more substantially.

For the listener: the ostinato is the work's textural climax — a sustained passage of low-bass repetition with melodic development above. The contrast with the Op. 53's full-texture outer sections gives the work its central architecture.

#### The Op. 61 as late-style genre-hybrid

The Polonaise-Fantaisie Op. 61 (1846) is one of Chopin's late-period structural innovations. The work is not a conventional polonaise — it is twice as long (13 minutes vs. 7 for Op. 53), more structurally complex, more harmonically restless, and less polonaise-rhythm-centered. The "Fantaisie" element in the title is Chopin's own description of the genre-hybrid posture.

What the Op. 61 is, structurally:

* A substantial introduction (about 90 seconds) before the polonaise rhythm arrives.
* A polonaise theme that is harmonically restless and structurally less stable than the Op. 53 main theme.
* Multiple recitative-like passages where the polonaise rhythm gives way to declamatory writing.
* Lyric central episodes in related keys.
* A return-and-climax that is harmonically more reflective than the Op. 53's dramatic close.

The Op. 61 is therefore the polonaise opened up into something larger — a genre-hybrid that uses the polonaise rhythm as one element among others rather than as the structural spine. Charles Rosen analyzes the work as one of Chopin's late-style structural inventions, alongside the Ballade No. 4, the Fantasy Op. 49, and the Barcarolle Op. 60.

First-listen guidance: the Op. 61 rewards repeated hearing. On a first listen the polonaise rhythm is identifiable but the structural development feels rambling; on a second listen the structural design — introduction, polonaise theme, recitatives, lyric central episodes, return — becomes clearer; on a third listen the work's coherence as a single fifteen-minute argument emerges.

#### The other major polonaises

A brief survey of the polonaises this chapter covers but does not anchor.

**Op. 26 (1834-35)** : two polonaises in C♯ minor and E♭ minor. The C♯ minor Op. 26 No. 1 is the more-dramatic of the two; the E♭ minor Op. 26 No. 2 is the more-restrained. The Op. 26 set was Chopin's first published-in-Paris polonaises; the works document the early-Paris polonaise voice.

**Op. 40 (1838-40)** : two polonaises in A major and C minor. The A major Op. 40 No. 1 — the "Military" Polonaise (the nickname is 19th-century reception, not Chopin's) — is one of Chopin's most-celebratory polonaises; it has been used at military and national-celebratory occasions. The C minor Op. 40 No. 2 is darker and more inward.

**Op. 44 in F♯ minor (1841)** : a single polonaise of substantial scale (about 11 minutes). One of Chopin's longer mid-period polonaises; structurally adventurous; one of the works that prefigures the Op. 53 and Op. 61 ambitions.

The arc moves from the dance-derived early polonaises (Op. 26) through the elevated middle polonaises (Op. 40, Op. 44) to the dramatic Op. 53 and the late genre-hybrid Op. 61. By 1846 Chopin had taken the polonaise as far as he would; he composed no further polonaises in his last three years.

### 14.4 The Stories

#### Where the Op. 53 sits among the polonaises

The Op. 53 "Heroic" is among the most-famous Chopin works in the larger culture. It has been used at innumerable Polish-national occasions across two centuries; it is one of the standard recital pieces in the Chopin Competition repertoire; it has been recorded by every major Chopin pianist. The work's popularity is partly that it is genuinely effective music and partly that its structural shape and national-symbolic role are clearly recognizable.

What the Op. 53 is: a brilliant seven-minute polonaise that demonstrates Chopin's elevated-polonaise voice at its most-immediate. The work's structural shape, its broad main theme, its striking ostinato middle section, and its dramatic close make it the most-readable of the great Chopin polonaises.

What the Op. 53 is not: it is not the deepest of the Chopin polonaises. The Op. 61 Polonaise-Fantaisie is structurally more ambitious; the Op. 44 in F♯ minor is more sustained in its mid-period development. The Op. 53's popularity has sometimes led to its over-performance and to its becoming the "easy" Chopin polonaise to play and hear; the Op. 61 demands more of pianist and listener and rewards both.

#### Recording history briefly

The complete polonaises have many recordings. The major lineages:

The Rubinstein lineage. Arthur Rubinstein recorded the polonaises multiple times; the 1964 RCA stereo recording is the canonical mid-20th-century document. Warm, songful, with the Op. 53 played at moderate-not-extreme tempo.

The Pollini lineage. Maurizio Pollini recorded the complete polonaises in 1975 for DG (Primary Recording). Modernist-clean reading; technical precision; particularly fine in the Op. 53 ostinato.

The Zimerman lineage. Krystian Zimerman recorded the complete polonaises in 1981 for DG. The Polish-school depth; weighted and structurally clear; particularly fine in the Op. 61.

The Horowitz lineage. Vladimir Horowitz did not record the complete polonaises but performed the Op. 53 frequently in concert across his career. Multiple live recordings exist (Carnegie Hall 1965, 1968, 1978; Moscow 1986); the Horowitz Op. 53 is the Russian-virtuoso reading at its most-electric.

For the Op. 53 specifically the safe ground is Pollini 1975, Rubinstein 1964, Zimerman 1981, Horowitz (any of the live recordings). For the Op. 61 the safe ground is the same recordings.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The polonaises connect to many works.

To the late mazurkas (Chapter 13). The Polish-folk-rooted core of the catalogue spans the mazurka and the polonaise; the listener who has heard both genres has heard the Polish-national-identity assertion in Chopin's music.

To the Op. 28 Preludes (Chapter 9). The brief polonaise rhythmic moments in the Op. 28 (No. 18 in F minor briefly evokes polonaise rhythm) connect to the genre.

To the Op. 35 first movement (Chapter 12). The dramatic B♭-minor first movement of the Op. 35 sonata shares some structural posture with the Op. 53 polonaise.

To the late single-movement masterworks (Chapter 15). The Op. 61 Polonaise-Fantaisie shares structural ambition with the Op. 49 Fantasy and the Op. 60 Barcarolle; the three together comprise the late-period single-movement structural-innovation cluster.

#### The biographical context: 1842-1846

The polonaise arc in the 39-year frame. The Op. 53 was composed in 1842 at Nohant in the fourth Sand-period summer — the same year as the Ballade No. 4 and the Op. 50 mazurkas. The Op. 61 was composed in 1845-46 at Nohant in the seventh and eighth Sand-period summers — the same period as the Op. 56 and Op. 59 mazurkas, the Berceuse Op. 57, and the Barcarolle Op. 60.

The 1846 publication of Op. 61 was followed by the late-1846 worsening of the Sand-Chopin relationship and by the 1847 break. The Op. 61 is therefore the last major Chopin work composed in the Sand-partnership context; the works that followed (the Op. 64 waltzes published 1847; the Op. 63 mazurkas published 1847; the Cello Sonata Op. 65 published 1847) span the break and the post-break period.

Chapter 15 covers the three late single-movement masterworks (Fantasy Op. 49, Berceuse Op. 57, Barcarolle Op. 60). Chapter 16 covers the 1846-1849 final years and the death.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The Op. 53 'Heroic' nickname is 19th-century reception, not Chopin's. Listening to the polonaise, is the 'heroic' character national-historical (heroism of Polish identity), pianistic (heroism of virtuoso piano writing), or something else entirely? Would a different nickname have shaped the reception differently?"_
* _"The Op. 53 middle section's ostinato low-bass figure is one of the most-difficult left-hand passages in the standard Chopin repertoire. What does the technical demand do to the music — is the ostinato 'difficult music' or 'music about difficulty,' or both?"_
* _"The Op. 61 Polonaise-Fantaisie is the polonaise opened up into a thirteen-minute genre-hybrid. What does Chopin gain by keeping the polonaise name even when the genre is no longer the structural spine, and what would the work lose if it were called simply 'Fantaisie'?"_
* _"The Op. 53 has been used at countless Polish national-historical occasions across two centuries. What does it mean for a piece of music to become a national symbol, and how does the national-symbolic role affect the listener's reception?"_
* _"The polonaise and the mazurka are Chopin's two Polish-folk-rooted genres. The mazurka was the village dance; the polonaise was the court dance. What does the difference between village and court tell you about the kinds of Polish identity each genre carried, and which feels more central to Chopin's voice?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Pollini 1975 polonaises (or one of the alternatives). Listen to the Op. 53 a second time and attend to these moments:

  1. **The opening cascade (0:00-0:30).** Listen for the preparatory fortissimo writing before the polonaise theme arrives. The work's full dynamic range is established immediately.

  2. **The polonaise theme (about 0:30 onward).** Listen for the polonaise rhythm — 3/4 with the quarter-eighth-eighth profile. Notice the broad, dignified melodic line.

  3. **The ostinato middle section (about 3:00-5:00).** Listen for the repeated low-bass figure. The bass pulses across many bars; the right hand develops melodic motion above.

  4. **The closing pages (about 6:00-7:00).** Listen for the build to the fortissimo close. The work's dramatic close gives it the authority of a national-historical statement.

If you have time also listen to the Op. 61 Polonaise-Fantaisie. The work is twice as long (about 13 minutes) and substantially more demanding; the structural design — introduction, polonaise theme, recitatives, lyric central episodes, return — rewards repeated listening.

### Going Deeper

**Optional listening** (a brief contextual pairing — the Op. 53 stands on its own without this): pair the Op. 53 with the Polish national anthem _Mazurek Dąbrowskiego_ (composed by Józef Wybicki in 1797; in mazurka rhythm). The anthem is freely available in many recordings (orchestral and choral arrangements). Listening to the anthem first and then to the Op. 53 demonstrates the Polish-folk-music context that surrounds both works; both are in triple meter, both are recognizably Polish, both have been used at national-historical occasions. The exercise takes about ten minutes total.

**Read** : the chapter on the polonaises in Jim Samson, _Chopin_ (Oxford Master Musicians series, 1996). Samson covers the genre's history, the within-arc evolution from Op. 26 to Op. 61, the Op. 53's structural design, the Op. 61's late-style innovation, and the polonaises' national-symbolic role. About twenty pages.

## Chapter 15: Late Single-Movement Masterworks — Fantasy Op. 49, Berceuse Op. 57, Barcarolle Op. 60

This chapter covers three late single-movement piano works that do not fit the genre categories the volume has been tracing. The Fantasy in F minor Op. 49 (1841) is a free-form single-movement work that resists conventional form-labeling. The Berceuse in D♭ major Op. 57 (1844) is a lullaby that builds elaborate variations over an unchanging left-hand pattern. The Barcarolle in F♯ major Op. 60 (1846) is a Venetian-gondolier-song genre piece with a "duet of two singers" texture. Charles Rosen analyzes these three as Chopin structural inventions independent of the genre-by-genre evolution covered in Chapters 4-14.

The three together comprise the late-period structural-innovation cluster: works in which Chopin invented new structural designs to carry musical content the inherited genres did not accommodate. The chapter is genre-survey in posture; it does not anchor on one work but visits each of the three.

### 15.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Nohant, the summer of 1841. The household has settled into the third Sand-period summer. Sand writes upstairs in the morning, eats with the household around one in the afternoon, walks the estate in the evenings. Maurice (eighteen, studying painting with Delacroix in Paris through the year) paints in the converted attic. Solange (twelve, turning thirteen in September) studies with her tutors. The household guests this summer have included Marie de Rozières (Solange's piano teacher) and intermittent visits from Paris friends.

Chopin works in a downstairs room he uses as a study, with a Pleyel piano (Pleyels were Chopin's preferred instrument for composing across the Nohant years). The work in progress this summer: a piano composition that does not fit any of the established genres. The form has resisted easy structural labeling across the weeks of attempts. The work is too long for a prelude, too short for a sonata, structurally too discontinuous for a ballade. Chopin decides to call it, simply, _Fantaisie_ — a genre-name that historically meant a piece in free form, not bound by the structural conventions of sonata, rondo, or variation.

The Fantasy in F minor Op. 49 was finished by the end of that 1841 summer and published the following year. What Chopin had done was license his own structural innovation by selecting a genre-name that did not demand structural orthodoxy. The work has subsequently been one of the most-analyzed Chopin pieces in the academic literature; Samson and Rosen both treat it as a major structural achievement; the work has divided opinion on whether the formal design is coherent (a "free" form that achieves its own logic) or rambling (a sequence of episodes that do not cohere). Both readings have defenders.

What the Fantasy is, formally: a multi-episode work without a clear sonata-form, rondo, or theme-and-variations design. The opening march (in F minor) gives way to a lyric central episode in A♭ major; the lyric episode is interrupted by a chorale-like passage in B major; the music returns through related keys; the closing pages bring back the march material in transformed form before settling into A♭ major (the relative major) for the final cadence. The structural design is genuinely free; it does not conform to any of the inherited single-movement forms.

The three works of this chapter together (Fantasy 1841, Berceuse 1844, Barcarolle 1846) are the late-period structural inventions. None is a polonaise, mazurka, ballade, scherzo, etude, sonata, or nocturne. Each is a new structural design that does not fit the inherited genres. The cluster represents Chopin's engagement with form as something he was inventing rather than inheriting — that August afternoon of 1841, when _Fantaisie_ became the title that licensed the rest of the cluster.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

For each of the three works:

  1. **Fantasy in F minor Op. 49.** Listen for the opening march in F minor (slow, processional); the contrasting lyric central episode in A♭ major; the chorale-like passage in B major; the multiple thematic returns; the closing A♭-major cadence. The work is about 13 minutes.

  2. **Berceuse in D♭ major Op. 57.** Listen for the left-hand pattern: a slow rocking figure (the bass-and-chord pattern of a lullaby) that repeats almost unchanged across the entire piece. The right hand plays elaborate variations above the unchanging bass. The work is about 5 minutes; the harmonic stasis is one of the structural innovations.

  3. **Barcarolle in F♯ major Op. 60.** Listen for the "duet of two singers" texture: the right hand often plays two simultaneous voices, one above the other, like two operatic singers in duet (similar to the Op. 27 No. 2 nocturne but in the barcarolle context). The meter is compound (12/8, twelve eighth-notes per bar grouped as four beats of three eighths each), giving the music a swaying, gondolier-like character. The work is about 9 minutes.

#### One-sentence background

Fantasy in F minor Op. 49 (composed 1841 at Nohant; published 1842; ~13 minutes). Berceuse in D♭ major Op. 57 (composed 1844 at Nohant; published 1845; ~5 minutes). Barcarolle in F♯ major Op. 60 (composed 1845-46 at Nohant; published 1846; ~9 minutes). The **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Krystian Zimerman on Deutsche Grammophon (recorded 1999, the "Chopin: Four Ballades + Fantasy + Berceuse + Barcarolle" disc), chosen for the Polish-school depth across the three late single-movement works on a single recording.

**Alternative recordings** :

* Arthur Rubinstein on RCA (various recordings of the three works across his career): the canonical warm-and-singing readings.
* Maurizio Pollini on DG (various recordings of the three works): modernist-clean readings.
* Maria João Pires on DG (selected recordings of the Berceuse particularly): the inward Berceuse.

### 15.2 The Listening Window

Timestamps refer to the Zimerman 1999 Primary Recording.

**Fantasy in F minor Op. 49** (about 13:00):

* **0:00 Opening march in F minor.** Slow, processional. The texture is chordal; the rhythm is dotted.
* **About 1:30 Build toward the central episode.** The march material develops; the texture intensifies.
* **About 3:30 Lyric central episode in A♭ major.** A contrasting lyric passage; bel-canto-piano texture; the singing right hand.
* **About 5:30 Transition to the chorale.** The harmony shifts; the music moves toward B major.
* **About 7:00 The chorale-like passage in B major.** Four-voice chordal writing in B major; sustained and inward.
* **About 8:30 Return to F minor.** The march material returns; the texture intensifies.
* **About 10:30 Build toward the closing pages.** The dynamic builds.
* **About 12:00 The closing pages in A♭ major.** The harmony settles into A♭ major (the relative major).
* **About 13:00 Final cadence.** The work ends quietly in A♭ major.

**Berceuse in D♭ major Op. 57** (about 5:00):

* **0:00 Opening pattern.** The left-hand rocking bass-and-chord pattern in D♭ major. A simple right-hand melody begins above.
* **About 0:30 First variation.** The right-hand melody is elaborated; the left-hand pattern continues unchanged.
* **About 1:00 Second variation.** More elaborate right-hand figuration; the left-hand pattern continues.
* **About 1:30 Third variation.** The right-hand figuration becomes denser.
* **About 2:30 Middle variations.** The right-hand figuration explores fast scales, chromatic passages, ornamental flourishes; the left-hand pattern continues unchanged.
* **About 3:30 Quieter variations.** The right hand settles back into more sustained writing.
* **About 4:30 Final pages.** The right-hand variations settle toward the cadence.
* **About 5:00 Final cadence.** The work ends quietly in D♭ major. The left-hand pattern has played essentially the same six-bar progression repeated about fourteen times across the entire piece.

**Barcarolle in F♯ major Op. 60** (about 9:00):

* **0:00 Opening.** Listen for the _rocking_ left-hand pattern in 12/8 — a long-short-long-short pulse that mimics the rhythm of a gondola pushed by an oar. The right hand enters with the singing main theme. Compare to a waltz: a waltz has a single strong downbeat per bar; the barcarolle has _two_ swaying downbeats, longer than waltz beats. The whole piece sits on this rocking pulse.
* **About 1:00 The two-voice duet texture.** Listen specifically for the right hand splitting into _two_ singing lines — a higher voice and a lower voice, both with their own melodic shapes, both running simultaneously. This is the operatic duet (two sopranos, or soprano-and-mezzo, sustaining contrasting lines at once) transferred to the keyboard. The duet texture is the work's signature; Ravel called the result "one of the most perfect pieces of music ever written."
* **About 2:30 Development of the main theme.** Listen for the melody being elaborated: the higher voice climbs higher, the lower voice descends lower, the interval between them widens — two singers in increasing emotional engagement. The left-hand rocking pattern continues unchanged.
* **About 4:00 Central episode.** Listen for the harmony shifting away from F♯ major into a related key area. The duet texture briefly thins to a single melodic line; the music takes a breath.
* **About 6:00 Return.** The duet texture comes back. Listen for the moment the _two_ singing lines re-establish themselves — same shapes as the opening, but now with more ornamental decoration in both voices.
* **About 7:30 Closing pages.** The melody settles; the duet voices come closer together; the harmony narrows toward the cadence.
* **About 9:00 Final cadence.** The work ends quietly in F♯ major. Listen for the rocking pattern _stopping_ — not fading, but arriving at rest. The two singers have finished their duet.

### 15.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The Fantasy as free form

The Fantasy Op. 49 has been the subject of substantial analytical literature. The structural design does not conform to any of the inherited single-movement forms (sonata, rondo, theme-and-variations); the work is multi-episode without a clear governing scheme.

What the Fantasy includes:

* An opening march in F minor (about three minutes).
* A lyric central episode in A♭ major (about two minutes).
* A chorale-like passage in B major (about two minutes).
* A return of the march material with development (about three minutes).
* A closing section that settles into A♭ major (about two-and-a-half minutes).

The episodes are heard as distinct musical statements; the transitions between them are sometimes abrupt and sometimes gradual; the work as a whole is a sequence of contrasting episodes rather than a developed single argument.

Samson's analysis treats the Fantasy as a successful free-form work: the episodes cohere through harmonic logic (the keys are related; the chorale's B major is the dominant of E major, which is the dominant of A major, which provides the connection to the closing A♭ major) and through Chopin's signature compositional moves (the recitative-like passages, the chorale-into-march juxtaposition, the harmonic surprises). Rosen's analysis treats the Fantasy as one of the great Chopin structural inventions: a work where Chopin demonstrated that free form could carry sustained musical content without conventional structural support.

The opposing reading: the Fantasy is rambling. The episodes do not cohere; the work lacks the structural tightness of the Ballades or the Sonatas; the multi-episode design dilutes the emotional impact. Some 19th-century criticism took this view; some 20th-century criticism has continued it. The work has divided opinion across two centuries.

Repeated listening pays off here. On a first listen the episodes may feel disconnected; on a second listen the harmonic logic and the thematic returns become clearer; on a third listen the work's coherence as a single thirteen-minute argument emerges (or, depending on the reader's experience, does not).

#### The Berceuse as harmonic-stasis variations

The Berceuse Op. 57 is Chopin's most-extreme example of harmonic stasis. The left-hand pattern (a six-bar progression repeated essentially unchanged across the entire piece) establishes the most-restricted harmonic frame in the Chopin catalogue. The right hand plays elaborate variations above the unchanging bass.

What the left-hand pattern is: a six-bar progression in D♭ major built around the I (tonic), V (dominant), and IV (subdominant) chords, with the bass-note-and-chord rocking pattern of a lullaby. The pattern repeats about fourteen times across the work's five minutes. The harmonic content of the piece is therefore the same six bars repeated fourteen times in the left hand.

What the right hand does: variations of increasing elaborateness. The opening variation is a simple lyric melody; subsequent variations elaborate with ornamental figuration, fast scales, chromatic passages, double-note writing, hand-crossings, and other pianistic devices. By the middle of the piece the right hand is in continuous fast figuration; by the closing pages the writing settles back to more sustained material.

The structural design is therefore a set of variations over an ostinato (a repeated bass pattern), but with the variations focused on the figurative-pianistic dimension rather than on melodic or harmonic transformation. Each variation explores a different right-hand texture above the unchanging bass.

Charles Rosen analyzes the Berceuse as a Chopin invention: a late-period structural innovation that takes the variation form into a new register where the harmonic motion is restricted and the variation work is pianistic-figurative. The result is a piece that produces an effect of musical stillness with elaborate surface activity; the underlying harmony is rocked but the surface is in continuous motion.

For the listener: the Berceuse rewards attention to the right-hand figuration. Each variation is a different pianistic problem; the work is a small encyclopedia of right-hand textures. The unchanging left-hand pattern is the work's structural anchor; the right-hand variations are the work's expressive content.

#### The Barcarolle as duet texture

The Barcarolle Op. 60 takes the Venetian-gondolier-song genre (an Italian folk-music form characterized by 12/8 or 6/8 meter, a swaying rhythmic pattern, and lyric melodic writing) and elevates it to art-music. The work is in 12/8; the left-hand barcarolle pattern provides the swaying rhythmic frame; the right hand carries the singing material.

What distinguishes the Barcarolle: the "duet of two singers" texture. The right hand often plays two simultaneous melodic lines, one in a higher register, one in a lower, both singing simultaneously. Charles Rosen identifies the duet texture as one of Chopin's structural inventions (similar to the Op. 27 No. 2 nocturne's duet texture, but here at larger scale and more sustained).

Rosen's reading: the Barcarolle is the apotheosis of the bel-canto-piano texture. The duet of two singers (operatic device) is transferred to the keyboard at sustained scale; the swaying barcarolle rhythm (Venetian-folk device) provides the lyric frame; the late-Chopin harmonic vocabulary provides the harmonic motion. The work is mature Chopin in concentrated form: bel-canto, folk-music, harmonic-experimentation, structural-innovation all in one nine-minute piece.

The Barcarolle has been one of the most-loved late Chopin works in subsequent reception. Maurice Ravel famously called it "one of the most perfect pieces of music ever written"; Debussy admired it; modern pianists treat it as one of the canonical late-Chopin pieces.

#### The three together

The three works of this chapter (Fantasy 1841, Berceuse 1844, Barcarolle 1846) are linked by the late-period structural-innovation posture. Each invents a new structural design that does not fit the inherited genres:

* The Fantasy invents the multi-episode free form for sustained piano composition.
* The Berceuse invents the harmonic-stasis variations with figurative-pianistic variation work.
* The Barcarolle invents the sustained duet-texture barcarolle as art-music genre.

The three represent Chopin's late-period engagement with form-as-invention rather than form-as-inheritance. They sit alongside the Ballade No. 4 (the most-structurally-sophisticated of the Ballades), the Polonaise-Fantaisie Op. 61 (the polonaise-fantasy hybrid), and some of the late nocturnes (Op. 62 in particular) as the late-Chopin structural-innovation cluster. The cluster represents Chopin's compositional development in his final productive years: Chopin engaging with form as something he was creating, not as something he was filling.

#### A brief detour: the four Impromptus

The volume has not anchored on the Impromptus, but the genre belongs in this chapter's neighborhood and the reader who is engaging with the late single-movement works should know where they sit.

Chopin published three Impromptus during his lifetime — the Op. 29 in A♭ major (1837), the Op. 36 in F♯ major (1839), and the Op. 51 in G♭ major (1842) — and a fourth was published posthumously by Fontana in 1855 as the Op. 66 _Fantaisie-Impromptu_ in C♯ minor (composed about 1834, withheld by Chopin from publication during his life; the work is the famous one and is, in popular reception, one of the two or three most-known Chopin pieces alongside the Op. 9 No. 2 and the Op. 53 "Heroic").

The Impromptus do not constitute a single arc the way the Mazurkas, Nocturnes, Ballades, Scherzos, and Polonaises do. They are individual works in a loose genre — the "Impromptu" name suggests improvisatory character but Chopin's are carefully composed. Each has an ABA-like structure: a fast outer-section figuration in the right hand over a regular left-hand accompaniment; a contrasting lyric middle section; a return of the outer-section figuration.

* **Op. 29 in A♭ major** (~4 minutes) is the most-played of the in-life-published three. The outer sections feature rapid right-hand figuration; the middle section provides a singing lyric contrast.
* **Op. 36 in F♯ major** (~6 minutes) is the most structurally adventurous; the middle section pushes harmonically toward more remote keys than the genre conventions allow.
* **Op. 51 in G♭ major** (~5 minutes) is the most inward of the three; the middle section is reflective rather than dramatically contrasting.
* **Op. 66 _Fantaisie-Impromptu_ in C♯ minor** (~5 minutes) is the famous one. The outer sections combine rapid right-hand figuration with cross-rhythm patterns (the right hand plays sixteenth notes against the left hand's triplets — a polyrhythm that is technically demanding); the lyric middle section in D♭ major is the source of the popular "I'm Always Chasing Rainbows" 20th-century song adaptation. Chopin withheld the work from publication; Fontana published it against the composer's stated wishes (cf. the Op. 67/68 mazurkas, Chapter 13). The publication-against-wishes complication applies here just as it does there.

The Fontana publication of the _Fantaisie-Impromptu_ in 1855 is the act that brought the work into the standard repertoire. Without Fontana's posthumous publication, the most-popular Chopin Impromptu would not exist as a heard work. The reader who has been following the Fontana question across the volume should add the _Fantaisie-Impromptu_ to the list of works that exist in the catalogue against the composer's documented wishes.

For the listener: the four Impromptus together comprise about twenty minutes of music. They are worth hearing once as a group, particularly to place the famous _Fantaisie-Impromptu_ alongside the three Chopin published himself — the comparison shows what Chopin considered ready for publication versus what he held back. Standard recordings include the Rubinstein 1962 RCA set (the canonical mid-century reading) and the Cyprien Katsaris 1986 Sony set.

### 15.4 The Stories

#### How these three works sit in the late period

The three works of this chapter are not the most-immediately-loved Chopin pieces. The Op. 9 No. 2 Nocturne is more-loved than the Op. 49 Fantasy; the Op. 53 Polonaise is more-loved than the Op. 60 Barcarolle; the Op. 25 No. 11 "Winter Wind" Etude is more-loved than the Op. 57 Berceuse. The three are demanding pieces that reward repeated listening; they are not entry-point pieces.

What the three are: late-period structural inventions. Each demonstrates Chopin's compositional development at its most-ambitious in his final productive years. Each rewards sustained engagement with the music; each yields more on second and third listenings than on first.

What the three are not: they are not biographical or programmatic works. The Fantasy is not "Chopin's response to anything"; the Berceuse is not "Chopin's portrait of an infant" (the lullaby idea is the genre's, not a specific biographical scene); the Barcarolle is not "Chopin's Venetian travel piece" (Chopin never visited Venice). The pieces work as art-music; biographical or programmatic readings are not required.

#### Recording history briefly

The three works have many recordings. Major lineages:

The Rubinstein lineage. Arthur Rubinstein recorded the Fantasy, Berceuse, and Barcarolle multiple times across his career. The 1960s RCA stereo recordings are the canonical mid-20th-century documents. Warm, songful, with particularly fine readings of the Berceuse and Barcarolle.

The Pollini lineage. Maurizio Pollini recorded various Chopin selections including the Fantasy and Barcarolle. Modernist-clean readings; the structural clarity that defines his Chopin approach.

The Zimerman lineage. Krystian Zimerman recorded the Fantasy, Berceuse, and Barcarolle in 1999 for DG (the Primary Recording for this chapter). The Polish-school depth at full late-style scale; particularly fine in the Fantasy.

The Argerich lineage. Martha Argerich's recordings of the three works are scattered. Her live recordings of the Berceuse and Barcarolle are among the most-electric in the discography.

The Pires lineage. Maria João Pires's recordings of the Berceuse (particularly the 1996 DG selected works disc) are among the most-inward readings; the restrained Pires approach particularly suits the Berceuse's intimate scale.

For the Fantasy specifically the safe ground is Zimerman 1999, Pollini, Rubinstein. For the Berceuse the safe ground is Rubinstein 1960s and Pires 1996. For the Barcarolle the safe ground is Zimerman 1999, Rubinstein, and (where the live document exists) Argerich.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The three works connect to many others.

To the Op. 28 Preludes (Chapter 9). The free-form posture of the Fantasy builds partly on the through-composed posture of some longer Op. 28 preludes (No. 15, No. 24).

To the late mazurkas and nocturnes (Chapter 13, Chapter 7). The harmonic vocabulary of the three is the late-Chopin vocabulary: the same vocabulary the late mazurkas (Op. 56, Op. 59) and the late nocturnes (Op. 62) deploy.

To the Polonaise-Fantaisie Op. 61 (Chapter 14). The genre-hybrid posture of the Op. 61 shares some of the Op. 49 Fantasy's structural ambition; both are multi-episode single-movement works of substantial scale.

To the Ballade No. 4 Op. 52 (Chapter 10). The structural sophistication of the Op. 52 is part of the late-Chopin structural-invention cluster that includes the three works of this chapter.

#### The biographical context: 1841-1846

Biographical anchoring: the Fantasy Op. 49 was composed in 1841 at Nohant in the third Sand-period summer. The Berceuse Op. 57 was composed in 1844 at Nohant in the sixth summer. The Barcarolle Op. 60 was composed in 1845-46 across the seventh and eighth Nohant summers.

The five-year span (1841-1846) is the late-middle Sand period: Chopin in his maximum productive form, the partnership at its stable middle. The three works were composed in the same broad context as the late mazurkas (Op. 56, Op. 59), the Sonata Op. 58, and the Polonaise-Fantaisie Op. 61. The cluster represents Chopin's mid-1840s compositional achievement.

Chapter 16 covers the 1846-1849 final years and the death (the period after the Op. 60 Barcarolle and Op. 61 Polonaise-Fantaisie), during which Chopin's compositional output diminished as his health declined.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The Fantasy Op. 49 has divided opinion across two centuries: some hear it as a successful free-form work, others hear it as rambling. Listening to it, which reading is closer to your experience, and what does the multi-episode design ask of the listener that a conventionally-formed work does not?"_
* _"The Berceuse Op. 57 repeats essentially the same six-bar left-hand pattern about fourteen times across five minutes. What does the harmonic stasis do to the listener's experience, and what does the unchanging bass let the right-hand variations accomplish that they could not accomplish over a changing harmonic frame?"_
* _"The Barcarolle Op. 60 has been called 'one of the most perfect pieces of music ever written' (Ravel). The piece combines Venetian-gondolier rhythm, bel-canto duet texture, and late-Chopin harmony. What does the perfection lie in: the integration of the elements, the technical execution, or something else?"_
* _"The three works of this chapter (Fantasy, Berceuse, Barcarolle) are Chopin's late-period structural inventions. What does it mean for a composer to invent form rather than inherit it, and how does the listener recognize when a structural design is new?"_
* _"The three works do not fit the genre categories the volume has been tracing (mazurka, polonaise, nocturne, etude, ballade, scherzo, sonata, prelude, waltz). Why did Chopin in his late period write so many works outside the inherited genres, and what does this tell you about how he was thinking about composition?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Zimerman 1999 Fantasy + Ballades + Berceuse + Barcarolle disc (or the alternatives). Listen to the three anchor works a second time and attend to these moments:

  1. **Fantasy Op. 49: the chorale-like passage (around 7:00).** Listen for the moment the music shifts into the B-major chorale section. The four-voice chordal writing is one of the work's most-distinctive textures; the contrast with the surrounding march and lyric material is structurally precise.

  2. **Berceuse Op. 57: the left-hand pattern (entire piece).** Listen _only_ to the left hand if you can. Notice that the six-bar pattern repeats essentially unchanged. The right-hand variations move; the bass stays.

  3. **Barcarolle Op. 60: the two-voice duet texture (about 1:00 onward).** Listen for the moments when the right hand projects two simultaneous singing lines. The duet of two singers is the work's signature.

  4. **All three: the late-Chopin harmonic vocabulary.** Across the three works, listen for moments of harmonic surprise: modulations into distant keys, chromatic motion, modal mixture. The late-Chopin harmonic vocabulary is consistent across the three; the structural designs differ but the harmonic language is one.

### Going Deeper

**Optional listening** (the comparison is illuminating but secondary to the Rosen reading): pair the Berceuse with Brahms's Lullaby Op. 49 No. 4 ("Wiegenlied," composed 1868, the universally-known German lullaby). The two lullabies (Chopin 1844, Brahms 1868) represent the lullaby genre at two different compositional registers: Chopin's elaborated variation-set, Brahms's simple strophic song. Listening to both demonstrates the genre's range. Recordings of the Brahms lullaby are widely available; the song is brief (about three minutes). About fifteen minutes total.

**Read** : the chapter on the Fantasy, Berceuse, and Barcarolle in Charles Rosen, _The Romantic Generation_ (Harvard, 1995). Rosen treats the three as late-Chopin structural inventions; his analyses of the Berceuse and Barcarolle are particularly important in the late-Chopin literature. About thirty pages.

## Chapter 16: The 1846-1849 Final Years + the November 1848 British Tour + the October 17, 1849 Death

This chapter is biographical-and-musical. It traces the final three years of Chopin's life: the late-1846 worsening of the Sand-Chopin relationship; the 1847 break; the April-November 1848 trip to Britain; the January 1849 return to Paris; the October 17, 1849 death at age thirty-nine; the October 30, 1849 funeral at the Madeleine; the Père Lachaise burial. The compositional output of the final years was small, and the focus here is on biographical narrative.

The chapter anchors on the Mazurka in F minor Op. 68 No. 4 (traditionally identified as among Chopin's final 1849 mazurka sketches; published posthumously by Fontana in 1855; the exact ordering of Chopin's final compositions is itself a scholarly question and newer Polish editions have revised Fontana's order). The work is brief (about four minutes) and is heard as a final-Chopin essence in concentrated form.

### 16.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

October 30, 1849. Three o'clock in the afternoon. The Madeleine church in Paris is filled — perhaps three thousand mourners, including most of the Paris musical community and the Polish émigré community. Chopin died thirteen days earlier on October 17 at 12 Place Vendôme; the body has been embalmed and is in the church for the funeral. The order of service, per the contemporary press accounts and the correspondence of Chopin's sister Ludwika and Chopin's friend Auguste Franchomme:

* Mozart's Requiem (KV 626) — performed in full per Chopin's wish. The performance involves orchestra, chorus, and four soloists: Madame Castellan (soprano), Pauline Viardot (contralto), Alexis Dupont (tenor), Luigi Lablache (bass).
* The Op. 28 Preludes No. 4 in E minor and No. 6 in B minor (per some accounts; other accounts give No. 20 in C minor) — performed by Lefébure-Wély on the Madeleine's organ.
* The Marche funèbre from Beethoven's Op. 26 sonata (in the orchestral arrangement) — per some accounts, played by the orchestra.

The Funeral March from Chopin's own Op. 35 was not played at the funeral, per the contemporary record. The piece had become standard funeral music in 19th-century Paris but Chopin's own funeral used the Mozart Requiem and a selection of his Preludes; the work that bore his name as the funeral-march piece in popular reception did not feature at his own funeral.

The funeral procession then moved to the Père Lachaise cemetery in the 20th arrondissement, where Chopin was buried. The grave at Père Lachaise is in section 11; Polish soil (which Chopin's friends had brought from Polish lands) was sprinkled over the grave per Chopin's wish. The heart had been removed before embalming, per Chopin's wish; the heart was preserved in alcohol in an urn and given to his sister Ludwika, who took it back to Warsaw in 1850. The heart was eventually interred at Holy Cross Church (Kościół Świętego Krzyża) on Krakowskie Przedmieście in Warsaw, where it remains today as a Polish national-cultural relic.

The funeral and burial of Chopin on October 30, 1849 closed his physical presence. The reception, the recordings, the catalogue, the Polish-national association continued.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

For the Mazurka in F minor Op. 68 No. 4:

  1. **The opening melancholy.** The work opens in F minor with the mazurka rhythm. The texture is sparse, the melodic line is descending, the mood is melancholic. The opening is restrained — no dramatic outburst, no virtuosic display.

  2. **The middle section.** A brief contrasting section in a related key area. The harmonic motion is sparse and gentle.

  3. **The closing pages.** The work returns to F minor and settles toward a quiet cadence. The piece ends in F minor without a picardy-third resolution. The duration is about 4 minutes.

The piece is a final-Chopin concentration of the late-mazurka essence — the harmonic vocabulary you know from Chapter 13's Op. 56 No. 3 in compressed form. The brief duration, the restraint, the melancholic posture, and the unresolved F-minor close are characteristic.

#### One-sentence background

Mazurka in F minor Op. 68 No. 4 (composed 1849 — exact date uncertain, traditionally placed in Chopin's final months; published 1855 posthumously by Julian Fontana; ~4 minutes). The **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Arthur Rubinstein on RCA Victor's 1965-66 complete Mazurkas (the same set anchored in Chapter 4 and Chapter 13), chosen for the Polish-school inheritance brought to the work.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Chopin Mazurka F minor Op 68 No 4 Rubinstein" or "Chopin Mazurka Op 68 No 4 last mazurka" ZH: "肖邦 f小调玛祖卡 作品68之4 鲁宾斯坦" 或 "肖邦 最后的玛祖卡" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** : as in Chapter 13's complete-mazurka recommendations — Ashkenazy, Sztompka, Biret, Ohlsson.

### 16.2 The Listening Window

Timestamps refer to the Rubinstein 1965-66 Primary Recording.

**Op. 68 No. 4 in F minor** (about 4:00):

* **0:00 Opening.** F-minor mazurka rhythm; descending melodic line; sparse texture.
* **About 0:30 Development of the opening.** The melody is repeated with slight variation.
* **About 1:30 Middle section.** A contrasting passage in a related key area; the texture remains sparse.
* **About 2:30 Return to F minor.** The opening material returns.
* **About 3:30 Closing pages.** The melody settles toward the cadence.
* **About 4:00 Final cadence.** The work ends quietly in F minor.

### 16.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The final-Chopin essence

The Op. 68 No. 4 is traditionally identified as one of Chopin's final compositions. The exact date and circumstances of composition are uncertain; the work exists as a manuscript sketch among the papers Fontana inherited and published in 1855. The sparse texture, the brief duration, the restraint, and the unresolved F-minor close are characteristic of the late-Chopin essence in concentrated form.

What the work documents: Chopin's late-style in its final form. The mazurka genre that he had developed across thirty years (from the Op. 6 of 1830-31 through the Op. 63 of 1847 in life-publication; through the Op. 68 of 1849 in sketch form) has reached its final compressed register — brief, melancholic, harmonically restrained, structurally minimal. The Op. 68 No. 4 is the mazurka stripped to its essence; what remains is the rhythmic profile, the Polish-folk-modal coloring (subdued), and the melancholic posture.

For the listener: the Op. 68 No. 4 does not have the harmonic ambition of the Op. 56 No. 3 (Chapter 13) or the structural development of the Op. 17 No. 4 (Chapter 4). It is a brief late-Chopin essence — the mazurka in its final concentrated form. The work rewards quiet attention; it is not a piece for first acquaintance with the genre.

#### The Fontana publishing question (revisited)

A brief return to the publishing complication. The Op. 67 and Op. 68 mazurkas were published in 1855 by Julian Fontana against Chopin's stated wishes. Chopin had instructed Fontana to burn the unpublished sketches; Fontana did not. The works have circulated for 170 years as part of the standard Chopin repertoire; the "do not publish these" wishes are sometimes noted in modern editions and sometimes not.

For the Op. 68 No. 4 specifically: the work exists in the catalogue because Fontana published it. Without Fontana's posthumous publication, the work would have been lost (the manuscript sketch would presumably have been burned or destroyed). The reader should know the complication: the work the chapter anchors on is a Fontana posthumous publication against the composer's stated wishes. The music's value and the publishing violation coexist in the standard repertoire.

Modern Polish editions of the mazurkas (the Polish National Edition under Jan Ekier 1959-onward; the more recent editions) have revisited the ordering of Chopin's late sketches and have proposed different orderings. The "last mazurka" identification of Op. 68 No. 4 is traditional but not securely established; some Polish-edition scholarship has placed other works in the "last mazurka" position. The reader should hold the traditional identification gently — as the conventional but not certain identification.

### 16.4 The Stories

#### Late 1846 to mid 1847: the worsening

The worsening of the Sand-Chopin relationship through late 1846 has been covered in Chapter 8. The proximate triggers: Sand's 1846 novel _Lucrezia Floriani_ (which may or may not have been a Chopin portrait); the tensions over Maurice and Solange; the accumulating health concerns; the decline of the partnership's emotional ease.

By mid-1847 the relationship was over in the decisive way. The Solange-Clésinger marriage of May 1847, the household conflict at Nohant in July 1847, and Sand's July 28, 1847 letter declaring the partnership finished — these closed the eight-year partnership.

Chopin in late 1847 was alone in Paris. He was thirty-seven years old, in deteriorating health, recently separated from his partner of nine years. The Cello Sonata Op. 65 was being completed; the Op. 63 mazurkas had been published; the Op. 64 waltzes were nearing publication. The compositional output continued but at reduced pace.

#### The November 1847 to April 1848 winter in Paris

The 1847-48 Paris winter was the difficult winter. Chopin's health was declining; the income from teaching was sustaining him but his lessons were fewer; the social life had narrowed; the 1848 February Revolution in Paris (the overthrow of Louis-Philippe and the establishment of the Second Republic; the social upheaval in the city) added a public context to the personal difficulty.

The February 16, 1848 concert at the Salle Pleyel — Chopin's last Paris public concert — was the capstone of the Paris career. The program included the last three movements of the Cello Sonata Op. 65 (with Franchomme); the Berceuse Op. 57; selected mazurkas, nocturnes, waltzes; the Barcarolle Op. 60. The concert was a major Paris musical event; the audience was full; the financial result was substantial. Eight days later, on February 24, the Paris Revolution began.

The February Revolution made Chopin's Paris untenable. The salon culture that had sustained him through seventeen years collapsed; the aristocratic patrons who had been his audience and income source were dispersed; the bourgeois economy was disrupted. By April 1848 the decision had been made to travel to Britain.

#### The April-November 1848 British tour

The British tour of 1848 was a near-heroic effort by any biographical reading. Chopin was visibly ill — the tuberculosis was now visibly advanced; he coughed blood regularly; he required assistance to climb stairs. Yet he traveled from Paris to London (arriving April 21, 1848), then to several British cities, then back to Paris in late November.

The London engagements (April-August 1848): private concerts in aristocratic London houses; a public concert at the home of the pianist Sterndale Bennett (April 27); a Queen Victoria's-presence concert at the home of Lord Falmouth (May 15); a concert at the Hanover Square Rooms (June 23); a concert at Lord Falmouth's home (July 7). The London Polish émigré community provided support; Jane Stirling (1804-1859), a Scottish pianist who had been Chopin's pupil since the late 1830s and who organized much of the tour, arranged invitations and travel.

The Scottish portion of the tour (August-October 1848): travels through Manchester (Manchester concert August 28, at the Gentlemen's Concert Hall — a large public venue; surviving reviews praised the delicacy of the playing while noting that the larger hall did not suit Chopin's quieter dynamic range), then to Scotland — Calder House (Lord Torphichen's estate near Edinburgh, where Chopin stayed with the Stirling family for several weeks; Jane Stirling and her sister Mrs Erskine handled correspondence and travel arrangements on Chopin's behalf, with Chopin reserved for limited playing and social appearances), Glasgow (concert September 27, at the Merchants' Hall), Edinburgh (concert October 4, at the Hopetoun Rooms — this was Chopin's last full Scottish concert), Keir (the Stirling family estate). The Scottish visits were partly social-recovery, partly concert-engagements; Chopin's health deteriorated through the period, and by the end of the Scottish tour he required help with the basic exertions of travel.

The London return (October-November 1848): one final London concert at the Guildhall on November 16, 1848 — a benefit appearance for Polish refugees. This was a brief appearance rather than a full recital; Chopin played a few pieces in deteriorating health, attended by a small audience. The Guildhall appearance was Chopin's last public performance in his life.

The return to Paris was completed by late November 1848. Chopin was severely weakened; the journey had drained his physical reserves; he would not perform publicly again.

#### November 1848 to October 1849: the final year in Paris

The final year was the steady decline. Chopin's residence at 12 Place Vendôme (his last Paris apartment) was provided by Jane Stirling, who had become the principal financial support; her generosity sustained him through the final months. His sister Ludwika arrived from Warsaw in August 1849 with her husband and young daughter; she was with him through his final weeks.

The compositional output through 1849 was minimal. The Op. 68 No. 4 mazurka sketches and a few other late mazurka sketches (traditionally identified as Op. 67 No. 2, Op. 67 No. 4, Op. 68 No. 4 — the exact ordering uncertain) were the principal late-life works. The Mazurka in F minor Op. 68 No. 4 — this chapter's anchor — was composed in 1849, possibly in the final months. The work's brevity and restraint reflect the diminished physical capacity.

The final days: by early October 1849 Chopin was bedridden. Friends visited regularly — Delfina Potocka (the singer-friend whose presence at the deathbed is recorded in multiple letters and recollections, though with subsequent embroidery); Franchomme; the Czartoryski circle members; the Polish-émigré community. Solange visited (the Sand-side daughter who had remained friendly with Chopin through the break). Chopin's wishes for the funeral — Mozart's Requiem, the Op. 28 Preludes, the heart returned to Poland — were stated to Ludwika and Franchomme in the final weeks.

Chopin died on October 17, 1849, at 2 a.m. in his Place Vendôme apartment. The cause of death was tuberculosis (the post-mortem confirmed the disease; some 20th-century scholarship raised cystic fibrosis as a hypothesis, but the post-mortem findings support the tuberculosis diagnosis and the modern consensus remains tuberculosis). He was thirty-nine years old.

#### The October 30, 1849 funeral and burial

The funeral at the Madeleine on October 30, 1849 included Mozart's Requiem, selected Op. 28 Preludes (per the contemporary order of service), and a substantial mourning attendance from the Paris musical and Polish-émigré communities. The procession to Père Lachaise followed; the burial was completed with Polish-soil ceremony. The heart was preserved separately and later returned to Warsaw with Ludwika in 1850.

The grave at Père Lachaise is in section 11, with a marble monument by Auguste Clésinger (Solange's husband, ironically the figure whose presence had triggered the 1847 Sand-Chopin break). The monument has been one of the most-visited Père Lachaise sites for 170 years; flowers and Polish-cultural artifacts continue to be left at the grave.

The heart at Holy Cross Church in Warsaw was interred behind a marble pillar in the church's nave in 1879 (after several decades of Ludwika family preservation). The pillar carries an inscription. The heart-and-pillar at Holy Cross has been one of the Polish national-cultural sites — a relic-of-Chopin in the Polish capital, where his physical body could not return.

### 16.5 What the Final Works Know

A short summary. The compositions of Chopin's final years — the 1846 Polonaise-Fantaisie Op. 61, the 1846 Barcarolle Op. 60, the 1845-46 Berceuse Op. 57, the 1845-47 Cello Sonata Op. 65, the late mazurkas Op. 63 of 1847, the late waltzes Op. 64 of 1847, and the unpublished sketches of 1848-49 including the Op. 68 No. 4 — represent the late-Chopin in its full development.

What the final works show:

* **Late-style harmonic vocabulary** at maximum density (the late mazurkas, the late nocturnes, the Polonaise-Fantaisie).
* **Late-period structural innovation** (the Fantasy, Berceuse, Barcarolle as inventions).
* **Late-period structural integration** (the Op. 58 Sonata, the Op. 65 Cello Sonata as mature multi-movement statements).
* **Late-period concentration** (the Op. 68 No. 4 mazurka as the final essence in brief form).

The arc closes at the Op. 68 No. 4. Chopin in 1849 composed in concentrated brevity what he had composed in expansive development across the previous twenty years. The final mazurkas are short, restrained, harmonically inward — the same vocabulary as the late masterworks but in compressed form.

Chapter 17 collects the compositional signatures across the catalogue. Chapter 18 surveys the recording history. Chapter 19 the Pleyel-vs-Erard question. Chapter 20 the Chopin reception across 175 years. Chapter 21 closes with the listening life from here.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"Chopin's own funeral on October 30, 1849 used Mozart's Requiem and selected Op. 28 Preludes — not the Op. 35 Funeral March that the larger culture has assumed was his funeral music. What does the gap between the contemporary record and the popular assumption tell you about how reception shapes biographical memory?"_
* _"The April-November 1848 British tour was undertaken in deteriorating health and was a near-heroic effort. Why did Chopin go — what was he getting out of the tour, and what did the tour cost him in his final months?"_
* _"The Op. 68 No. 4 mazurka exists in the catalogue because Julian Fontana published it in 1855 against Chopin's stated wishes ('do not publish these'). The work has circulated for 170 years as part of the standard repertoire. Should the listener treat the work as canonical, or as documentation of Fontana's violation? Is there a third option?"_
* _"Chopin's heart was removed before embalming, preserved in alcohol, and returned to Warsaw where it remains interred at Holy Cross Church. What does this gesture — heart returned to the country that no longer existed as a state — tell you about Chopin's understanding of his own Polish identity?"_
* _"The Op. 68 No. 4 is traditionally identified as among Chopin's final compositions — brief, restrained, melancholic, harmonically inward. Listening to it after having heard the larger Chopin catalogue, what does the late-Chopin essence in concentrated form suggest about the trajectory of a composer's life in his final months?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Rubinstein 1965-66 Mazurkas (or the alternatives). Listen to the Op. 68 No. 4 a second time and attend to these moments:

  1. **The opening melancholy (0:00-0:30).** Listen for the sparse F-minor texture and the descending melodic line. The brief introduction establishes the mood.

  2. **The middle section (around 1:30).** Listen for the brief contrasting passage. Notice that the middle section is short — unlike the Op. 17 No. 4 or the Op. 56 No. 3 (Chapter 4 and Chapter 13), the Op. 68 No. 4's middle section is brief, almost suggested rather than developed.

  3. **The return and the closing (about 2:30-4:00).** Listen for the return to F minor and the quiet close. The work ends in F minor without a picardy-third resolution; the melancholy holds to the end.

  4. **The full piece in comparison.** If you have time, listen to the Op. 6 No. 1 in F♯ minor (the first published mazurka, 1830-31), then the Op. 17 No. 4 in A minor (Chapter 4 anchor, 1832-33), then the Op. 56 No. 3 in C minor (Chapter 13 anchor, 1844), then the Op. 68 No. 4 in F minor (this chapter's anchor, 1849). The four together trace the great within-genre evolution across nineteen years.

### Going Deeper

**Optional listening** (Schubert is a different composer and a future _Close Listening_ volume; skip if you prefer to stay within Chopin for now): pair the Op. 68 No. 4 with Schubert's _Im Dorfe_ (the seventeenth song of the _Winterreise_ cycle, composed 1827; ~3 minutes). Schubert composed _Winterreise_ in 1827 in failing health and died in 1828 at age thirty-one; the cycle represents another major composer's late-life concentrated essence. Recordings of _Winterreise_ by Dietrich Fischer-Dieskau (DG, multiple recordings 1955-90), Hans Hotter (EMI 1954), or Matthias Goerne (Decca 2014) provide the comparison. Listening to one _Winterreise_ song and then to the Op. 68 No. 4 demonstrates the late-style compression that 19th-century composers sometimes reached in their final years. About fifteen minutes total.

**Read** : the final chapters of Alan Walker, _Fryderyk Chopin: A Life and Times_ (FSG, 2018), covering the 1847 break through the October 17, 1849 death. Walker's documentary rigor on the British tour, the final year, the funeral, and the post-death disposition of the body and the catalogue is the standard contemporary biographical treatment. About 100 pages across the closing chapters.

* * *
