## Chapter 17: Chopin's Compositional Signatures — Spot Them Across the Catalogue

This chapter is the Thread B convergence. Across Chapters 2 through 16 the volume has visited about fifty Chopin works and has noted along the way the recurring compositional features that characterize Chopin's voice. This chapter collects those features into a single synthesis. A reader who has worked through the previous chapters has already heard each of these features in context; the present chapter pulls them together as a listening framework.

The seven signatures we will examine:

  1. The bel-canto vocal lineage (the "right hand sings" approach to piano writing)
  2. The harmonic surprise and the chromatic experimentation
  3. Tempo rubato as a Chopin signature
  4. The Polish-folk-modal coloring
  5. The pedal innovation
  6. The within-genre evolution (early-to-late)
  7. The late-period structural innovation

Each section cites the chapters and anchor works where the feature was first introduced, and points the listener toward the works where the feature is most-clearly heard. The chapter is a synthesis tool; it does not introduce new works.

The bridge section at the end (§17.8) prepares for Chapter 18's recording-history discussion and Chapter 20's reception history. The bridge re-organizes material the chapter has already established; it does not introduce new factual claims (per series rule §10.5).

### 17.1 The bel-canto vocal lineage

The single most-pervasive Chopin signature is the right-hand-sings-left-hand-accompanies texture, with the right-hand melodic writing modeled directly on Italian operatic bel-canto vocal practice — particularly the style associated with Vincenzo Bellini (1801-1835), whose operas Chopin attended repeatedly in Paris in the early 1830s and whom Chopin treated as one of his compositional models.

What bel-canto piano texture involves:

* A singing right-hand melodic line, with phrasing, breathing pauses, and ornamentation modeled on operatic vocal practice.
* A left-hand accompaniment pattern that holds the harmony and the rhythmic frame (often a broken-chord pattern, an Alberti bass, or a rocking bass-and-chord figure).
* Phrase shapes that mimic the singing voice — arcs of melody with breath-points, repeated phrases with elaborated ornamentation, climactic high notes prepared by stepwise approach.
* Lengths of melodic phrases compatible with a singer's breath — typically 4-8 bars before a breath-pause.

The bel-canto piano texture is heard most clearly in:

* **Op. 9 No. 2 in E♭ major** (Chapter 2, the chapter's anchor; textbook example).
* **Op. 21 Larghetto** (Chapter 3): the larger-scale slow movement with orchestral support.
* **Op. 27 No. 2 in D♭ major** (Chapter 7): the duet-texture extension.
* **Op. 10 No. 3 in E major** (Chapter 6): the etude that takes the bel-canto into the technical-problem context.
* **The slow movement of the Op. 35 sonata's middle section** (Chapter 12): the Funeral March's lyric center.
* **The Berceuse Op. 57** (Chapter 15): the variation work that elaborates over the bel-canto pattern.
* **The Barcarolle Op. 60** (Chapter 15): the sustained duet of two singers.
* **The Op. 62 No. 1 in B major** (Chapter 7): the late-style elaboration of the texture.

Chopin's pupil Mikuli (per Eigeldinger 1986 EN) recorded that Chopin urged his students to attend Italian opera as part of their piano study — to listen for how singers breathed, how they ornamented, how they sustained the long line. The bel-canto piano texture is the compositional consequence of that aesthetic.

In your listening: whenever you hear a Chopin work with a singing right-hand line over a regular left-hand accompaniment, the bel-canto vocal lineage is in operation. The signature is consistent across the catalogue from Op. 2 (1827) through Op. 65 (1845-47).

### 17.2 The harmonic surprise and chromatic experimentation

Chopin's harmonic vocabulary became more adventurous across his career. The early works stay close to standard major-minor functional harmony with occasional brief excursions; the middle works deploy wider modulations and more chromatic motion; the late works reach into harmonic territory that anticipates the chromatic ambitions of the second half of the 19th century (Wagner, Liszt's later works, Brahms's late piano music).

Features of the harmonic experimentation:

* **Distant modulations** shift to keys that share few common tones with the home key: the Polonaise Op. 53's E-major middle section in an A♭-major work; the Op. 28 No. 15's D♭-major / C♯-minor enharmonic shift; the Op. 61 Polonaise-Fantaisie's wide modulations.
* **Chromatic motion** moves the music stepwise through chromatic notes rather than diatonically: the late mazurkas; the Op. 28 No. 2 in A minor; the late nocturnes.
* **Modal mixture** places both major and minor versions of the same chord in close proximity: the late mazurkas; the late nocturnes.
* **Ostinato-and-pedal effects** sustain low-bass figures with harmonic motion above: the Op. 53 ostinato; the Berceuse left-hand pattern.
* **Cadenza-like harmonic exploration** produces rapid harmonic motion within ornamental figuration: the Op. 9 No. 2 cadenza; the Op. 27 No. 2 ornamental passages.

The harmonic experimentation is heard most clearly in:

* **The Op. 9 No. 2 cadenza** (Chapter 2): the early demonstration.
* **The Op. 28 No. 2 in A minor and No. 15 in D♭ major** (Chapter 9): the prelude's harmonic concentration.
* **The Op. 48 No. 1 in C minor** (Chapter 7): the dramatic harmonic build.
* **The Op. 52 Ballade No. 4** (Chapter 10): the late-style ballade's harmonic restlessness.
* **The late mazurkas Op. 56, 59, 63** (Chapter 13): the genre at maximum harmonic ambition.
* **The Op. 62 No. 1 in B major** (Chapter 7): the late nocturne's late-style harmony.
* **The Op. 61 Polonaise-Fantaisie** (Chapter 14): the late genre-hybrid's harmonic motion.

A listening practice. Chopin's harmonic surprises are sometimes subtle — a single chromatic chord, a modulation through a related but unexpected key. Listening for them rewards attention; once you can hear them, you will hear them in many of the late works.

### 17.3 Tempo rubato as Chopin signature

The Chopin tempo rubato — "the left hand keeps strict time; the right hand pushes ahead or holds back, but always returns to alignment by the bar's end" — is one of the most-attested Chopin performance signatures (Eigeldinger 1986 EN, gathering the pupil recollections of Mikuli, Tellefsen, Mathias, and others).

What the rubato involves:

* The left hand maintains strict metronomic time. The bass note on beat one of each bar lands at evenly-spaced intervals.
* The right hand floats around the left-hand grid. Notes that nominally align with the bar's downbeats may arrive a tenth-of-a-second early or late.
* The right hand returns to alignment by the bar's end. The push-and-pull is contained within each bar; it does not accumulate across longer spans.
* The effect is one of vocal breathing — the singer dwells on emotionally significant notes; the singer returns to time.

The rubato is heard most clearly in:

* **The Op. 9 No. 2** (Chapter 2): the textbook nocturne for rubato.
* **The Op. 17 No. 4 mazurka** (Chapter 4): the rubato in the mazurka context.
* **The Op. 64 No. 2 waltz** (Chapter 13): the rubato in the waltz context.
* **The Berceuse Op. 57** (Chapter 15): the variation rubato.
* **The Barcarolle Op. 60** (Chapter 15): the sustained rubato within the compound meter.

The rubato is a performance signature rather than a compositional signature — it lives in how pianists play Chopin, not in the score's notation. Cortot's rubato extreme, Rubinstein's warm rubato, Pollini's restrained rubato, and the various Polish-school approaches represent the range of interpretations within the rubato tradition.

Try this: compare two recordings of the same Chopin work and listen for the differences in rubato. The Rubinstein 1965 Op. 9 No. 2 and the Pollini 2005 Op. 9 No. 2 represent different rubato traditions in the same piece; the differences are the interpretive choices the tradition allows.

### 17.4 The Polish-folk-modal coloring

The Polish-folk-music coloring is heard most clearly in the mazurkas (Chapter 4 and Chapter 13) but extends elsewhere. The features:

* **The Lydian raised fourth** raises the fourth scale degree to create a slightly distant, Lydian color. Heard in many mazurkas; also in the trio of the Scherzo No. 1 (Chapter 11).
* **The augmented-second interval** is a wide melodic leap that sounds Eastern-European in character. Heard in some mazurkas; also in moments of the polonaises (Chapter 14).
* **The bagpipe-drone left hand** sustains a low note in the left hand while upper voices move above. Heard most clearly in the Op. 6 No. 1, Op. 7 No. 1, and other early mazurkas (Chapter 4); also in moments of the Scherzo No. 1 trio (Chapter 11).
* **The polonaise rhythm** : 3/4 with the characteristic quarter-eighth-eighth profile. Heard in all the polonaises (Chapter 14); also as a brief evocation in the Op. 28 No. 18 prelude.
* **The mazurka rhythm** : 3/4 with offbeat accents. Heard in all the mazurkas (Chapter 4 and Chapter 13); also as inflection in the Op. 47 Ballade No. 3 main theme (Chapter 10) and in some moments of the Op. 65 Cello Sonata.
* **The "Lulajże, Jezuniu" Polish-Christmas-carol quotation** appears as direct quotation in the Scherzo No. 1's trio (Chapter 11).

The Polish-folk-modal coloring is heard most clearly in:

* **The mazurkas across the catalogue** (Chapter 4 and Chapter 13).
* **The polonaises across the catalogue** (Chapter 14).
* **The Scherzo No. 1 trio** (Chapter 11) for the carol quotation.
* **The Ballade No. 3 main theme** (Chapter 10) for the mazurka-inflection.

A practical observation: the Polish-folk-modal coloring is one of the most-identifiable Chopin signatures and among the most distinctively Polish parts of the catalogue. The mazurkas and polonaises together comprise the core of this material.

### 17.5 The pedal innovation

Chopin was the first composer to specify pedal markings systematically in his published scores (Eigeldinger 1986 EN). The earlier composers (Beethoven, Schubert) had used pedal markings occasionally but not as a regular notation element; Chopin made the pedal a compositional element rather than a performer's choice.

What the pedal innovation involves:

* **Specific pedal markings in the score.** Chopin's editions show pedal-on and pedal-off marks throughout, indicating where the sustaining pedal should be engaged.
* **Sustained-resonance effects.** The pedal blurs the harmonic motion, creating wash-like textures particularly in the late works.
* **Pedal-induced harmonic ambiguity.** The harmonic motion underneath the pedal sometimes blurs into next chord before the next chord arrives, creating a distinctive Chopin texture innovation.

The pedal innovation is heard most clearly in:

* **The late nocturnes Op. 48 No. 1 and Op. 62 No. 1** (Chapter 7).
* **The Berceuse Op. 57** (Chapter 15): the sustained-pedal background.
* **The late mazurkas** (Chapter 13): the pedal-induced harmonic blur.
* **The Barcarolle Op. 60** (Chapter 15): the pedal at sustained scale.
* **Various Op. 28 preludes** (Chapter 9): particularly No. 15 ("Raindrop").

On record: the pedal innovation is most-audible in recordings made on instruments with rich sustaining-pedal effects. Modern Steinways sustain longer than period Pleyels; the pedal-induced harmonic blur is therefore more-prominent in modern-piano recordings. The Pleyel-vs-Erard discussion in Chapter 19 picks up the implications.

### 17.6 The within-genre evolution

Across Chapters 4 through 14 the volume has traced the within-genre evolution of each of the major Chopin genres. The arcs:

* **Mazurka arc** (Chapter 4 → Chapter 13): Op. 6 (1830-31) → Op. 7, 17, 24 (1832-35) → Op. 30, 33, 41 (1837-39) → Op. 50, 56, 59, 63 (1842-47) → Op. 67, 68 (posthumous, 1855 publication). The great within-genre evolution.

* **Nocturne arc** (Chapter 2 → Chapter 7): Op. 9 (1832) → Op. 27 (1836) → Op. 32, 37, 48 (1837-41) → Op. 55 (1843) → Op. 62 (1846). The bel-canto-piano genre's evolution from Field-inheritor to late-style structural ambition.

* **Etude arc** (Chapter 6): Op. 10 (1833) → Op. 25 (1837) → Trois nouvelles études (1839). The genre's evolution from extroverted Op. 10 to inward Op. 25.

* **Ballade arc** (Chapter 10): Op. 23 (1835) → Op. 38 (1839) → Op. 47 (1841) → Op. 52 (1842). The genre-invention to late peak.

* **Scherzo arc** (Chapter 11): Op. 20 (1831-32) → Op. 31 (1837) → Op. 39 (1839) → Op. 54 (1842). The genre-transformation across eleven years.

* **Polonaise arc** (Chapter 14): early childhood (1817) → Op. 26 (1834-35) → Op. 40 (1838-40) → Op. 44 (1841) → Op. 53 (1842) → Op. 61 (1846). The genre's elevation from dance to concert.

* **Prelude arc** (Chapter 9): Op. 28 (1839) as the single set. The genre exists primarily in the Op. 28 cycle.

* **Sonata arc** (Chapter 12): Op. 4 early (1828, published 1851) → Op. 35 (1839) → Op. 58 (1844). The three piano sonatas across nineteen years.

The within-genre evolutions are one of Chopin's most-distinctive compositional patterns. Few composers' catalogues offer such clear within-genre arcs; Chopin's evolution across each genre is a real compositional achievement.

The framework's payoff: the within-genre evolution lets you place any Chopin work in its arc. A nocturne is not just "a Chopin nocturne" — it is a nocturne at a specific point in the arc, with the Op. 9 / Op. 27 / Op. 48 / Op. 62 anchor points letting you locate any other nocturne by ear.

### 17.7 The late-period structural innovation

The late-period structural innovation cluster comprises:

* **The Ballade No. 4 in F minor Op. 52** (Chapter 10): the genre's late peak.
* **The Op. 49 Fantasy in F minor** (Chapter 15): the free-form invention.
* **The Op. 57 Berceuse in D♭ major** (Chapter 15): the harmonic-stasis variations.
* **The Op. 60 Barcarolle in F♯ major** (Chapter 15): the duet-texture barcarolle.
* **The Op. 61 Polonaise-Fantaisie in A♭ major** (Chapter 14): the polonaise-fantasy hybrid.
* **The Op. 62 No. 1 in B major** (Chapter 7): the late nocturne's late-style structural openness.
* **The Op. 58 Sonata No. 3** (Chapter 12): the late multi-movement integration.
* **The Op. 65 Cello Sonata** (Chapter 12): the late chamber-music statement.

The cluster represents Chopin's final compositional development. Across the years 1841-1847 Chopin invented new structural designs (the Fantasy, the Berceuse, the Barcarolle, the Polonaise-Fantaisie) and developed inherited genres to their structural peaks (the Ballade No. 4, the Op. 58 Sonata, the Op. 65 Cello Sonata, the Op. 62 No. 1 nocturne). The late period is the most-structurally-ambitious of Chopin's career.

Repeated listening pays off here. Each work pushes the inherited structural conventions; the listener who has absorbed the earlier-period works will hear the late-period innovations against the background of the conventions they extend.

### 17.8 What Chopin's catalogue does _not_ include

The seven signatures collected above tell you what Chopin's compositional voice _is_. But Chopin's voice is also shaped — perhaps as much — by what he chose not to do. Across thirty-nine years and about 240 published works, Chopin did not compose:

* **A symphony.** Beethoven's nine, Schubert's nine, Brahms's four, Berlioz's _Symphonie fantastique_ (1830, composed in the same Paris where Chopin would arrive a year later) — Chopin wrote none. The orchestra was not his instrument.
* **An opera.** Bellini's ten operas across a decade; Donizetti's seventy-odd across nearly thirty years; Wagner's ten major operas across four decades; Verdi's two dozen across half a century. Chopin attended Italian opera in Paris devotedly, drew his right-hand melodic style directly from Bellini, was a friend of Pauline Viardot and other singers — and never wrote an opera himself. The closest he came was the collection of 17 Polish songs (Op. 74, composed across his life and published posthumously by Fontana in 1859) — individual song settings, not a song cycle, and never assembled by Chopin himself as a published group.
* **A string quartet.** The genre that Haydn elevated, Mozart developed, Beethoven transformed, Schubert mastered, and that mid-19th-century composers continued to use as the testing ground for serious compositional ambition — Chopin did not write one.
* **A choral work of substantial scale.** No mass, no oratorio, no cantata, no requiem. (Chopin asked for Mozart's Requiem at his own funeral; he had not written his own.)
* **Significant solo writing for any instrument other than the piano.** No violin sonata, no cello concerto, no song cycle for voice and instrument that became part of the standard repertoire. The Cello Sonata Op. 65 (1845-47, Chapter 12) is the major chamber work; the early Piano Trio Op. 8 (1828-29) is the only other significant chamber work, and it is rarely performed.
* **A piano concerto after age 20.** The Op. 11 and Op. 21 concertos are both Warsaw works, both composed before he was twenty. He did not return to the piano-with-orchestra genre across the next nineteen years. The Op. 2 Variations and a few other early piano-with-orchestra showpieces complete the orchestral catalogue; the rest of Chopin's mature life is for solo piano.
* **Sacred music in any standard form.** Chopin was Catholic and was buried with full Catholic rites at the Madeleine; his religious sensibility was real. But unlike Bach (cantatas, Passions, the Mass in B minor), unlike Mozart (the Mass settings, the Requiem), unlike Beethoven (the Missa solemnis, the Christus am Ölberge), unlike Schubert (six masses), Chopin wrote no sacred music in any standard form. The closest sacred-music gesture is the chorale-like middle of the Op. 48 No. 1 nocturne, and even that is a chorale _texture_ in a secular work rather than a sacred-music composition.
* **Music for the church organ.** The organ — Bach's instrument, the church-music instrument par excellence — does not appear in the Chopin catalogue at all.
* **Significant pedagogical exercises beyond the etudes.** Czerny wrote thousands of pedagogical exercises; Hanon's _Le Pianiste virtuose_ (1873, two decades after Chopin's death) became the universal piano-warmup. Chopin's Op. 10 and Op. 25 are simultaneously art-music and pedagogy, but he did not write pure pedagogical material outside the etudes; his teaching was through individual lessons rather than through published method books.

What the absences tell us. The piano is not just Chopin's instrument; the piano is _most of Chopin's instruments_. Across thirty-nine years he composed predominantly for solo piano. The exceptions: the two early Warsaw piano concertos (Op. 11 and Op. 21); a handful of early piano-with-orchestra showpieces (Op. 2, Op. 13, Op. 14, Op. 22); the early Piano Trio Op. 8 (1828-29); the late Cello Sonata Op. 65 (1845-47); and the 17 Polish songs Op. 74. Outside these — fewer than ten works total — the entire catalogue is solo piano. He did not extend himself across genres the way Schumann did (piano + lieder + chamber + symphonic + opera-incomplete + sacred); he did not extend across instrumentations the way Mendelssohn did; he did not, even within the piano, write a piano sonata until 1839 (the Op. 35, eight years after his Paris arrival) and only two more after that. The catalogue is concentrated to a degree few other major composers' catalogues are concentrated.

This concentration is part of the voice. A Chopin Mazurka and a Chopin Polonaise and a Chopin Nocturne are all the same instrument, written by the same compositional intelligence, in the same physical key range, with the same player's hands in mind, in the same Paris and Nohant rooms. The genre-by-genre evolution this volume has been tracing happens almost entirely _within_ the single-instrument constraint. Whether Chopin would have been a greater composer if he had written symphonies and operas is a non-question; he would have been a different composer. The Chopin we have is the composer who chose the piano and stayed there.

### 17.9 Bridge: from compositional signatures to reception

The seven signatures collected in this chapter are Chopin's compositional voice across the catalogue. Chapter 18 takes up the question of how this voice has been transmitted across pianist generations through the recording history. Chapter 19 takes up the related question of how the instrument on which the music is played — the period Pleyel of the 1830s and 40s vs. the modern Steinway of the 20th and 21st centuries — affects the pedal innovation, the bel-canto piano texture, and the tempo rubato. Chapter 20 takes up the larger question of how the Chopin reception has shaped subsequent listening across 175 years.

The signatures are established; their reception is contested. The Cortot-tradition reading of the rubato differs from the Pollini-tradition reading; the period-Pleyel reading of the pedal differs from the modern-Steinway reading; the 19th-century-sentimentalization reading of the bel-canto differs from the modern-scholarship reading. Each subsequent chapter addresses one of these contested receptions; together they document the complexity of Chopin's place in contemporary musical understanding.

The signatures-and-reception arc therefore moves: from the catalogue (Chapters 2-16, anchored on specific works) → through the signatures synthesis (this chapter, Chapter 17) → through the recording history (Chapter 18) → through the period-instrument question (Chapter 19) → through the 175-year reception (Chapter 20) → to the listening life from here (Chapter 21).

The reader who has worked through the volume to this point has heard the compositional voice. The remaining four chapters take up the question of how that voice continues to be heard in 2026 and beyond. The composer is the same; the reception is multiple; the listener at any given moment chooses among the receptions even when she does not realize the choice has been made.

In the chapters that follow, we will name the figures (Cortot, Rubinstein, Pollini, Argerich, Zimerman, Pires, the Polish-school lineage including Sokolov, Trifonov, Cho, Liu) and the arguments (the rubato range, the pedal-and-instrument question, the gender-and-genre reading from Kallberg, the Walker biographical synthesis). The reader will be equipped to enter any contemporary Chopin discussion with the historical-and-analytical background that the previous fifteen chapters have established.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"Of the seven compositional signatures collected in this chapter — bel-canto vocal lineage, harmonic surprise, tempo rubato, Polish-folk-modal coloring, pedal innovation, within-genre evolution, late-period structural innovation — which feels most-essential to Chopin's voice? If you had to pick one as the signature without which Chopin would not be Chopin, which would it be?"_
* _"The within-genre evolution from early to late runs across each of Chopin's major genres. Which arc — mazurka, nocturne, etude, ballade, scherzo, polonaise, sonata — has the strongest evolution, and which arc rewards the listener with the most-revealing comparison from early to late?"_
* _"Tempo rubato is a performance signature rather than a compositional signature — it lives in how pianists play, not in the score. What does this say about the relationship between composition and performance, and is rubato 'in the music' or 'added to the music'?"_
* _"The late-period structural innovation cluster (Ballade No. 4, Fantasy, Berceuse, Barcarolle, Polonaise-Fantaisie, Op. 62 nocturne, Op. 58 Sonata, Op. 65 Cello Sonata) was composed across 1841-1847. Why this concentration in the late period — what changed about Chopin's compositional thinking in his final productive years that produced this cluster?"_
* _"The bel-canto vocal lineage is a Chopin transplant from Italian opera (Bellini particularly). Without the bel-canto inheritance from opera, what would Chopin's piano writing have sounded like? Is there a Chopin signature that is independent of operatic vocal practice, or is the bel-canto fundamental?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

For this synthesis chapter, the listening is across multiple works rather than focused on a single anchor. The exercise:

  1. **Listen to one work that demonstrates each of the seven signatures** , in order. The works named in §17.1-17.7 are the textbook examples; pick one from each.

  2. **Listen across two recordings of the Op. 9 No. 2** — Rubinstein 1965 and Pollini 2005. The differences are the rubato tradition's range. Notice specifically how the left hand keeps time in both readings, and how the right hand floats differently.

  3. **Listen across the mazurka arc** — Op. 6 No. 1, Op. 17 No. 4, Op. 56 No. 3, Op. 68 No. 4 in chronological order. The within-genre evolution from 1830 to 1849 is heard most-clearly in the mazurkas.

  4. **Listen to the Op. 57 Berceuse** with attention only to the left-hand pattern. The unchanging six-bar progression repeated across the work is the harmonic-stasis signature in its most-extreme form.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair selected Chopin examples with their compositional sources — a Bellini aria for the bel-canto (cf. Chapter 2 _Going Deeper_); a John Field nocturne for the nocturne tradition (cf. Chapter 7 _Going Deeper_); a Polish folk mazurka for the mazurka rhythm (cf. Chapter 4 _Going Deeper_); a Mickiewicz poem for the ballade narrative (cf. Chapter 10 _Going Deeper_); Bach's _Well-Tempered Clavier_ for the Op. 28 Preludes (cf. Chapter 9 _Going Deeper_). The five comparisons together comprise the compositional inputs to Chopin's catalogue. About two hours total if you do all five.

**Read** : Charles Rosen, _The Romantic Generation_ (Harvard, 1995), the chapters on Chopin (the volume contains substantial Chopin chapters within the broader Romantic-generation framework). Rosen's analysis of the compositional signatures — particularly the bel-canto piano texture, the harmonic surprises, and the late-period structural innovations — is the standard contemporary analytical reading. The full Chopin material across the book runs about 100 pages.

## Chapter 18: Recording History — the Chopin Performance Tradition 1900-2026

The recorded history of Chopin performance is the continuous tradition by which his music has been transmitted from the late 19th century into the present. This chapter surveys the major performance lineages — the major Chopinists across the long 20th and early 21st centuries — and shows how the same Chopin work becomes different works in different pianists' hands.

The chapter does not anchor on a specific Chopin composition; it anchors on the recordings themselves. The exercise the chapter teaches is comparative — listening to two or three recordings of the same Chopin piece and hearing the differences in approach.

### 18.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Munich, April 1972. Deutsche Grammophon's recording team is in session with Maurizio Pollini for the project that will define the late-20th-century Chopin Etudes. Pollini is thirty years old, twelve years past his 1960 first-prize victory at the Warsaw Chopin Competition. In those twelve years he has performed extensively, taken extended sabbaticals from concert life to study with Arturo Benedetti Michelangeli, and recorded selectively (the 1960 EMI Concerto with Kletzki, made just after the Warsaw victory, is the principal earlier Chopin document). The project DG is recording now is the Op. 10 — the first twelve of Chopin's twenty-four mature etudes.

The Etudes are technically among the most demanding works in the standard piano repertoire. The Op. 10 No. 1 is C major arpeggios spanning wide intervals played by the right hand at speed; the Op. 10 No. 2 is right-hand chromatic scales played by the weakest fingers; the Op. 10 No. 12 ("Revolutionary") is left-hand cascade-with-right-hand-melody at sustained intensity. To record the set with the technical precision Pollini brings to it — minimal rubato, no edit-stitching of difficult passages, no wrong notes — is the work of intensive sessions.

The Op. 25 set — the other twelve Etudes — would not be recorded for another five years; Pollini returned to the studio in January 1977 to complete the cycle. The two halves were eventually issued together as a 2-LP set and have remained together in the catalog since. The combined recording became, almost immediately on first release, the contemporary reference for the Chopin Etudes. Pollini's reading is the modernist-clean Chopin in its most-influential form: extreme technical precision, linear melodic articulation, structural clarity, restrained rubato. Younger pianists across the next forty years would record their own Etudes against this Pollini standard; reviewers would compare; teachers would reference; the recording would shape what "competent Chopin" meant in the late 20th century.

The Pollini Etudes project is, in retrospect, one of the inflection points in the Chopin recording history. It marks the moment when the Cortot-Rubinstein tradition of warm-and-flexible Chopin gave ground to a different aesthetic — disciplined, precise, structurally transparent. Most of what we will discuss in this chapter sits on one side or the other of that shift.

A short note on the institution behind much of this. The International Chopin Piano Competition was founded in Warsaw in 1927 on Chopin's centenary by the Polish pianist and pedagogue Jerzy Żurawlew, with the twin purposes of honoring Chopin and consolidating the Polish-school Chopin tradition. The first edition's first prize went to Lev Oborin (Soviet-Russian, 1907-1974). The competition has been held every five years since (postponed in 1942 for the war; in 2020-to-2021 for the pandemic), with the 2025 edition being the most-recent. Pollini won the sixth edition in 1960; Argerich the seventh in 1965; Zimerman the ninth in 1975. The competition has been the discovery mechanism for most of the major Chopin pianists of the past century — including the four whose recordings define this chapter's lineages.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

The exercise this chapter proposes:

  1. **Choose one Chopin work you have listened to multiple times across the previous chapters** — preferably the Op. 9 No. 2 Nocturne, the Ballade No. 1 in G minor, the Op. 53 "Heroic" Polonaise, or the Op. 28 No. 4 Prelude.

  2. **Find two or three recordings of that work** by different pianists across the recording-history lineages. The safe-ground recommendations are listed in §18.2 below.

  3. **Listen across the recordings comparatively.** Same notes, same Chopin score, different musical experiences. The exercise teaches the range of legitimate Chopin interpretation.

#### One-sentence background

The Chopin recording history begins around 1900 (the first Chopin recordings on phonograph cylinders) and continues into 2026 (the present). The major commercial recording technologies — acoustic recording (1900-1925), electric monaural (1925-55), stereophonic (1955-present), digital (1980s-present), and high-resolution audio (2000s-present) — have all been used to document Chopin performance. The catalogue of major Chopin recordings runs into thousands; this chapter surveys the landmark lineages.

### 18.2 The Major Recording Lineages

#### The Cortot lineage

Alfred Cortot (1877-1962) was the leading French-school Chopinist of the early 20th century. He recorded the Chopin Etudes, Nocturnes, Preludes, Ballades, Scherzos, and many shorter works across his recording career (1920s-1950s). The 1933-34 EMI recordings of the Etudes and Preludes are the canonical pre-war documents. Cortot's reading represents the rubato extreme — interpretive freedom across every phrase, with occasional wrong notes and memory slips that the recordings preserve (Cortot did not retouch his recordings; the wrong notes are part of the document).

The Cortot tradition holds that Chopin is fundamentally a Franco-Polish composer whose interpretive freedom is the highest virtue. Pupils and pupils-of-pupils of Cortot — Yvonne Lefébure, Vlado Perlemuter, Samson François, others — continued the tradition through the 20th century. The Cortot pedagogy was central to the École Normale de Musique de Paris (which Cortot co-founded in 1919); generations of European pianists studied Chopin in the Cortot tradition.

For the contemporary listener, the Cortot recordings are historical documents worth hearing. The sound is mid-1930s acoustic-electric and the reading is the most-idiosyncratic of the major Chopinists; the Cortot is recommended as a comparison alongside more-conventional contemporary readings, not as a sole reference.

#### The Rubinstein lineage

Arthur Rubinstein (1887-1982) was the leading warm-singing American-and-international Chopinist of the mid-20th century. Polish-born (Łódź, 1887), trained partly in the Polish-school tradition, Rubinstein held Chopin as central to his career across seven decades. The principal Chopin recording sets:

* **Chopin Nocturnes** (RCA 1965 stereo, Rome recording): the Primary Recording for Chapter 2 and Chapter 7.
* **Chopin Mazurkas** (RCA 1965-66 stereo, Rome recording): the Primary Recording for Chapter 4 and Chapter 13.
* **Chopin Waltzes** (RCA 1963 stereo): the canonical complete Waltzes.
* **Chopin Polonaises** (RCA 1964 stereo): alternative for Chapter 14.
* **Chopin Ballades + Scherzos + Impromptus** (RCA 1959 stereo).
* **Chopin Piano Concertos** (RCA 1968 with Skrowaczewski).

The Rubinstein tradition holds that Chopin is warm, songful, generous, and that interpretive moderation is the appropriate posture. The reading is the safe starting point for any listener building a Chopin recording library. Rubinstein's three recordings of the Nocturnes (1930s HMV, 1949-51 RCA mono, 1965 RCA stereo) demonstrate a single pianist's relationship to the music changing across forty-five years.

#### The Horowitz lineage

Vladimir Horowitz (1903-1989) was the leading Russian-virtuoso pianist of the mid-20th century. His Chopin recordings are episodic (he never recorded a complete Nocturnes or complete Preludes set) but his individual Chopin readings on RCA and Sony across his career are remarkable for technical command and for an interpretive freedom different from Cortot's. The Horowitz Chopin sometimes pushes the music toward an emotional extreme that the Rubinstein tradition would consider mannered, and that is part of the interest. Notable Horowitz Chopin documents include the Polonaise Op. 53 in multiple recordings; the Ballade No. 1 in various live recordings; the Scherzo No. 4; selected Mazurkas; the Sonata No. 2 in B♭ minor Op. 35.

The Horowitz tradition has continued through pupils and admirers — Murray Perahia (in some ways the heir to the Horowitz Chopin), Daniil Trifonov, Yefim Bronfman. The Russian-virtuoso Chopin tradition holds that the music's drama is the central element and that interpretive freedom in the service of dramatic projection is the central skill.

#### The Pollini lineage

Maurizio Pollini (1942-2024) won the 1960 Warsaw Chopin Competition (the youngest first-prize winner to that date, at age eighteen) and recorded a Chopin discography over six decades with Deutsche Grammophon. The principal Chopin recordings:

* **Chopin Etudes Op. 10 + Op. 25** (DG 1972): the Primary Recording for Chapter 6.
* **Chopin Preludes Op. 28** (DG 1974): alternative for Chapter 9.
* **Chopin Polonaises** (DG 1975): the Primary Recording for Chapter 14.
* **Chopin Sonatas Op. 35 + Op. 58** (DG 1984): the Primary Recording for Chapter 12.
* **Chopin Scherzos** (DG 1990): the Primary Recording for Chapter 11.
* **Chopin Nocturnes** (DG 2005, selection rather than complete).

The Pollini tradition holds that Chopin's music is more disciplined than the 19th-century interpretive tradition has acknowledged; the rubato should be small, the bel-canto inheritance should be more strict than free, the structural shape should be clearly heard. Pollini's reading is the modernist-clean standard for the post-1960 Chopin pianism. His 1972 Etudes recording defined the second half of the 20th century's Etudes interpretation; his 1984 Sonatas recording remains a reference.

#### The Pires lineage

Maria João Pires (born 1944, Portuguese) recorded a selective Chopin discography for Deutsche Grammophon and Erato. The principal Chopin recording:

* **Chopin Nocturnes complete** (DG 1996, Salzburg recording).

Pires's reading is the quietest major Chopin Nocturnes recording — softer dynamics throughout, slower tempos, more inwardness. Pires said in interviews that she approached the Nocturnes as private music rather than concert music; the recording reflects this. The Pires tradition holds that Chopin's nocturnes are particularly susceptible to over-projection in the concert hall and benefit from a more chamber-music approach.

#### The Argerich lineage

Martha Argerich (born 1941, Argentine) won the 1965 Warsaw Chopin Competition (at age 24) and has been one of the major Chopin pianists for sixty years. Her Chopin recordings are scattered across her career — there is no complete-Nocturnes or complete-Etudes Argerich set — but her individual recordings are remarkable for dramatic intensity and interpretive freedom. Notable recordings:

* **Chopin Op. 28 Preludes complete** (DG 1977): the Primary Recording for Chapter 9.
* **Chopin Op. 35 Sonata** (DG 1974).
* **Chopin Concertos** (various recordings; the Concertos with Charles Dutoit and the Montreal Symphony, EMI 1968; later live recordings with Mikhail Pletnev).
* **Chopin selected Ballades** (1965 DG Ballade No. 1; later live recordings).

The Argerich tradition holds that the music's drama is the central element and that fast tempos, extreme dynamic range, and sharp articulation are the appropriate posture. Her Chopin is electric where Pollini's is clean and Rubinstein's is warm.

#### The Zimerman lineage

Krystian Zimerman (born 1956, Polish) won the 1975 Warsaw Chopin Competition (at age 18). His Chopin recordings on DG across forty years comprise the contemporary Polish-school Chopin standard. The principal Chopin recordings:

* **Chopin Ballades complete** (DG 1987): the Primary Recording for Chapter 10.
* **Chopin Scherzos complete** (DG 1985): alternative for Chapter 11.
* **Chopin Sonatas Op. 35 + Op. 58** (DG 2002): alternative for Chapter 12.
* **Chopin Polonaises complete** (DG 1981): alternative for Chapter 14.
* **Chopin Concertos** (DG 1999, with the Polish Festival Orchestra; Zimerman self-conducting): the Primary Recording for Chapter 3.
* **Chopin Ballades + Barcarolle + Fantasy + Berceuse** (DG 1999): the Primary Recording for Chapter 15.

The Zimerman tradition holds that Chopin's music has harmonic, structural, and emotional depth that requires a substantial weighted interpretive approach. His tempos are sometimes slower than Pollini's; his rubato is moderate; his orchestral and recording-balance work (for the 1999 Concertos especially) demonstrates the Polish-school depth at full development.

#### The Polish-school lineage broadly

Beyond Zimerman, the Polish-school Chopin lineage extends across four generations from the late 19th century into the present. The cleanest way to think about it is generationally, naming a representative figure or two per generation rather than the full roster.

**The founding generation (late 19th to early 20th century).** Ignacy Jan Paderewski (1860-1941) is the touchstone. Polish pianist and political figure (briefly Prime Minister in 1919), Paderewski toured Chopin internationally across forty years and became the public face of Polish-school pianism for much of his era. His recordings (acoustic, 1911-12; later electric) are the earliest documentary anchor; his interpretive freedom and singing tone established the tradition's broad parameters.

**The inter-war and immediate post-war generation (1920s-1950s).** The Warsaw Chopin Competition (founded 1927) drew this generation together. Stefan Askenase (1896-1985, recordings on DG from the 1950s) and Witold Małcużyński (1914-1977, Chopin Competition 1937 prize-winner) are the typical representatives — pianists who had studied with the previous generation and carried the singing-and-warm Polish-school Chopin into the modern recording era. Halina Czerny-Stefańska (1922-2001, Chopin Competition 1949 first prize, shared) and Adam Harasiewicz (born 1932, 1955 first prize) consolidated the tradition in the immediate post-war decades.

**The Zimerman generation (1960s-1980s).** Krystian Zimerman (born 1956, 1975 first prize) is the central figure. The generation includes Janusz Olejniczak (born 1952, 1970 prize-winner, later prominent in the period-instrument tradition — cf. Chapter 19) and several other Chopin Competition laureates. The Zimerman generation made the Polish-school approach into the contemporary international Chopin standard, with technical precision matching Pollini's and harmonic depth matching the German-tradition pianists, but with the Polish-folk-music inflection in the mazurkas and polonaises that the non-Polish pianists generally do not project.

**The contemporary generation (2000-2026).** Rafał Blechacz (born 1985, 2005 first prize) is the recent Polish first-prize winner; the 2010, 2015, and 2021 first prizes went to non-Polish pianists (Russian Avdeeva 2010, South Korean Cho 2015, Canadian-Chinese Liu 2021), reflecting the contemporary international reach of the Chopin Competition. The Polish-school tradition continues through the Fryderyk Chopin Institute's pedagogical and recording infrastructure rather than being uniquely defined by Polish-born pianists.

The Polish-school lineage is, taken across four generations, the continuous tradition that connects Chopin's own pupils through the late-19th-century Polish pianists through the inter-war Chopin Competition founding through the Zimerman contemporary standard to the present multi-national Chopin Competition winners. The 19th-century chain runs Chopin → Mikuli → late-19th-century Polish pianists → Paderewski's generation → into the Competition tradition; the 20th-century chain runs Competition → recordings → contemporary international winners.

#### Contemporary Chopin Competition laureates

The 21st-century Chopin Competition first-prize winners and major laureates have become the contemporary Chopin scene:

* **Yundi Li** (born 1982, China) — 2000 first prize. The first Chinese first-prize winner of the Chopin Competition.
* **Rafał Blechacz** (born 1985, Poland) — 2005 first prize.
* **Yulianna Avdeeva** (born 1985, Russia) — 2010 first prize. The first woman first-prize since Argerich (1965). DG recordings of selected Chopin from 2014 onward.
* **Seong-Jin Cho** (born 1994, South Korea) — 2015 first prize. DG recordings from 2016 onward including the Op. 28 Preludes (2021) and other Chopin.
* **Bruce Liu** (born 1997, Canada) — 2021 first prize. DG recordings from 2023 onward.
* **Daniil Trifonov** (born 1991, Russia) — 2010 third prize. DG and Decca recordings from 2010 onward including selected Chopin within his broader Russian-Romantic discography.

The contemporary Chopin Competition laureates' recordings provide the up-to-date readings. The 2025 edition (the most-recent at the time of this writing) has produced its own set of laureates whose recordings will appear over the next several years.

### 18.3 What the Comparative Exercise Teaches

A short summary. The exercise of listening across two or three recordings of the same Chopin work teaches:

* **The range of legitimate interpretation.** The Cortot rubato is different from the Pollini precision; both are legitimate Chopin. The Rubinstein warmth is different from the Argerich electricity; both are legitimate. The same notes accommodate the range.
* **The performer's role in the music.** The composer wrote the notes; the performer interprets them. The interpretation is not optional — it cannot be removed from the music. Every recorded Chopin is one performer's choices about every detail of the score.
* **The reader's own preferences.** The exercise reveals what the listener herself prefers — the warmer rubato or the cleaner precision, the dramatic intensity or the inward restraint. The preferences are real and can be developed over time.
* **The community of performance.** The major Chopin recordings are in conversation with each other. Pollini in 1972 is in conversation with Cortot in 1933; Zimerman in 1987 is in conversation with Rubinstein in 1959; Cho in 2021 is in conversation with both. The recordings are a continuing tradition; each new recording engages with the predecessors.

For the listener: a Chopin recording library does not need to be exhaustive. Three to five recordings of the major Chopin sets (Nocturnes, Ballades, Etudes, Preludes, Polonaises, Sonatas, Scherzos, Mazurkas, Waltzes) cover the range. The major lineages — Cortot, Rubinstein, Pollini, Argerich, Zimerman, Pires — can be sampled selectively without requiring complete sets from each.

### 18.4 The Warsaw Chopin Competition as Institution

The Warsaw Chopin Competition is the defining institution of Chopin performance. Held every five years since 1927 (with the wartime and pandemic postponements noted earlier), the competition has:

* **Discovered major Chopin pianists.** Most of the major Chopinists of the past century have been Chopin Competition prize-winners or laureates.
* **Maintained the Polish-school tradition.** The competition is held in Warsaw, with Polish jury members predominant; the Polish-school approach has been continuously transmitted across each five-year cycle.
* **Updated the Chopin reception.** Each generation's Chopin Competition laureates bring new interpretive approaches; the evolution from the Oborin 1927 to the Liu 2021 represents a century of Chopin reception.
* **Provided the contemporary listening event.** The competition is broadcast and recorded; recordings are commercially available; the contemporary listener has unprecedented access to the current state of Chopin pianism.

For the reader: the 2025 Chopin Competition has just concluded at the time of this writing; the 2030 edition is the next major Chopin discovery event. The recordings of the 2025 laureates will be available on the Fryderyk Chopin Institute's YouTube channel and through commercial CD releases over the next several years.

### 18.5 The Evolution of Chopin Performance Across the Century

A short summary of how the Chopin reading has changed across the recording history:

**1900-1930 (early acoustic and early electric eras):** The Cortot tradition dominates. Rubato is extreme; tempos are flexible; interpretive freedom is the highest virtue. The early recordings preserve a late-19th-century performance tradition that had received Chopin from his own pupils (Mikuli was Cortot's teaching ancestor; the chain runs Chopin → Mikuli → various later pianists → Cortot).

**1930-1960 (electric monaural and early stereo):** The Rubinstein tradition consolidates. The warm-singing reading becomes the mid-20th-century mainstream. Cortot continues but represents one lineage among multiple. The Polish-school pianists (Sztompka, Askenase, Małcużyński) provide the Polish-school continuity.

**1960-1990 (stereophonic):** The Pollini revolution. The 1960 Pollini Chopin Competition first prize and the 1972 DG Etudes recording mark the shift toward modernist precision. The Argerich electric reading provides the dramatic-intensity counter-current. The Zimerman 1975 first prize and his subsequent DG recordings establish the contemporary Polish-school standard.

**1990-2010 (early digital):** The recording landscape diversifies. The Pollini-Argerich-Zimerman lineages continue; the Pires inward-Chopin tradition develops; the period-instrument tradition (covered in Chapter 19) opens. Multiple legitimate approaches coexist.

**2010-2026 (high-resolution digital and streaming):** The Cho-Avdeeva-Liu generation. The Chopin Competition continues every five years; the Polish-school standard is now globally distributed; the contemporary Chopin is being made by a multi-national generation including East Asian (Yundi Li, Cho, Liu), Russian (Avdeeva, Trifonov), Polish (Blechacz), and other pianists.

The evolution is real and continuous. Each generation receives the Chopin from the predecessors, processes it through its own interpretive sensibility, and passes it on to the next. The Chopin we hear in 2026 is the inheritance of a hundred-year recording history plus the contemporary contributions of the current generation.

### 18.6 The Future of Chopin Recording

A brief forward-look. The Chopin recording history will continue. The 2030 Chopin Competition will produce a new generation of laureates. New technologies (high-resolution audio, immersive audio, virtual concert technologies, AI-generated transcripts) will provide new recording formats. New interpretive approaches will develop.

For the listener entering the Chopin world in 2026, the recommendation:

* **Build a small core library.** Three to five recordings of the major Chopin sets is enough.
* **Sample across the major lineages.** Cortot for the historical document; Rubinstein for the safe ground; Pollini for the modernist precision; Argerich for the dramatic electricity; Zimerman for the contemporary Polish-school depth; Pires for the inward restraint; one or two contemporary Chopin Competition laureates for the current generation.
* **Listen comparatively.** Two or three recordings of the same work across the lineages teaches more than one recording of multiple works.
* **Attend the Chopin Competition recordings.** Every five years a new round of Chopin recordings documents the contemporary state.
* **Develop your own preferences.** The exercise teaches what the listener herself values; preferences develop over time and are part of the relationship between listener and music.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The chapter argues that the same Chopin work becomes different works in different pianists' hands. Pick a Chopin work you have heard in multiple recordings (Op. 9 No. 2, Ballade No. 1, Op. 53 Polonaise) and describe specifically what differs between two recordings you know. Is the same work being played differently, or are two different works being played?"_
* _"The Warsaw Chopin Competition is the defining institution of Chopin performance. Should a single composer's work justify a single-composer international competition held every five years for a century? What does this institutional concentration tell you about Chopin's place in Western music?"_
* _"The Cortot 1933 EMI recordings preserve wrong notes and memory slips. Modern recording practice would retouch these. Is the imperfection a feature or a flaw — does it preserve something genuine about pre-war Chopin pianism, or does it obscure the music with the performer's lapses?"_
* _"The Pollini-Argerich-Zimerman generation of the 1960s-80s established the contemporary Chopin standard. Three decades on, the Cho-Avdeeva-Liu generation is establishing the next standard. What has evolved in the post-2000 Chopin reading compared to the post-1960 standard?"_
* _"The chapter recommends 'three to five recordings of the major Chopin sets' as sufficient for a building library. Is this an under-estimate (you need ten or twenty for a serious library) or an over-estimate (one or two is enough), and what does the listener's own goals dictate?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

For this chapter, the listening exercise is comparative rather than focused on a single work. The recommended exercise:

  1. **Choose the Op. 9 No. 2 Nocturne.** Listen to it in three recordings: Rubinstein 1965, Pollini 2005, and Cortot 1949 (or another available combination from the lineages). Notice specifically: the tempo, the rubato, the dynamic range, the singing quality of the right hand. Same notes, three different musical experiences.

  2. **Choose the Ballade No. 1.** Listen to it in two recordings: Zimerman 1987 and Rubinstein 1959. Notice: the dramatic shape, the build to the coda, the closing fortissimo. Two contemporary masters of the work; two distinctly different approaches.

  3. **Choose the Op. 53 Polonaise.** Listen to it in two recordings: Pollini 1975 and Horowitz (any of the live recordings). Notice: the opening cascade, the polonaise theme, the ostinato middle section. The modernist-clean Pollini and the Russian-virtuoso Horowitz represent two different traditions on the same work.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : explore the 2015 Chopin Competition recordings on YouTube. The Fryderyk Chopin Institute's official channel hosts the complete competition rounds; Seong-Jin Cho's prize-winning performances (the Stage 3 round including the Op. 35 Sonata, the Finals with the Op. 11 E-minor Concerto) are among the essential recordings. Listening to one or two competition performances gives the contemporary Chopin reading from a recent generation. About 30-60 minutes.

**Read** : any of the Chopin Competition books or documentaries. The official Fryderyk Chopin Institute publications cover the competition's history; the documentary film _Pianoforte_ (2023, directed by Jakub Piątek) follows several contestants through the 2021 edition and provides contemporary footage of the competition experience. About 2 hours for the documentary.

## Chapter 19: The Pleyel-vs-Erard Question — Period Instruments and Modern Steinways

This chapter takes up one of the most-discussed contemporary Chopin questions: should Chopin be played on the period Pleyel piano of the 1830s and 40s (the instrument Chopin himself preferred and composed for), or on the modern Steinway of the 20th and 21st centuries (the instrument on which most subsequent Chopin pianism has been built)? The question is observational; the chapter does not adjudicate.

The chapter does not anchor on a specific Chopin composition; it anchors on the instrument and what the instrument does to the music. The exercise the chapter teaches is comparative — listening to recordings of the same Chopin work on a period Pleyel and on a modern Steinway, and hearing the differences.

### 19.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Chopin's preference for Pleyel. The most-frequently-quoted documentary source is Carl Mikuli's recollection, gathered in Eigeldinger (_Chopin: Pianist and Teacher as Seen by His Pupils_ , Cambridge 1986 English-language edition). Mikuli reports that Chopin said something along the lines of: _Quand je me sens mal disposé, je joue sur un piano Erard; je trouve un son tout fait. Mais quand je me sens dispos et assez fort pour trouver mon propre son, il me faut un piano Pleyel._ ("When I feel out of sorts I play on an Erard piano; I find a ready-made sound. But when I feel well and strong enough to find my own sound, I need a Pleyel piano.")

This preference is the foundation of the Pleyel-vs-Erard question. Chopin's quote distinguishes between two of the leading Paris piano makers of the 1830s-40s: Pleyel, run by Camille Pleyel (1788-1855), Chopin's friend and the dedicatee of the Op. 9 Nocturnes; and Erard, run by Sébastien Erard (1752-1831) and his successors. The two firms made significantly different instruments.

What distinguished Pleyel from Erard:

* **Pleyel** : lighter touch (lighter hammer action; less mechanical effort required to play loudly); more intimate sound; more "singing" quality; better suited to the salon environment; smaller hammers and less sustaining power than Erard.
* **Erard** : heavier touch (heavier hammer action; more mechanical effort but more dynamic projection); more orchestral sound; more "powerful" quality; better suited to the concert-hall environment; larger hammers and more sustaining power.

Chopin's preference for Pleyel was not unconditional. The Mikuli quote suggests the Pleyel was the more-demanding instrument requiring the player's "own sound" (the player's interpretive control) rather than the ready-made Erard tone. When Chopin was unwell — the tuberculosis through the 1840s left him often less than fully physically capable — he sometimes used Erards because they required less physical exertion to project. When he was well, he preferred Pleyels because they allowed his interpretive nuance.

Liszt, by contrast, favored Erards. Liszt's Erard preference suited the more dramatic instrument to his theatrical performance style; the Erard could project across larger halls; the Erard's heavier touch suited Liszt's more virtuosic-display approach. The Pleyel-vs-Erard distinction has historically been read as the Chopin-vs-Liszt distinction at the level of instrument.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

The exercise this chapter proposes:

  1. **Find a recording of a Chopin work on a period Pleyel piano.** Period-Pleyel recordings include those by Tomasz Ritter (the 2018 International Chopin Competition on Period Instruments first prize winner), Janusz Olejniczak (in some of his Chopin recordings on the Polish state label Muza and on the soundtrack of the 2002 Polański film _The Pianist_), Wojciech Świtała, and Krzysztof Książek.

  2. **Find the same Chopin work on a modern Steinway.** The Rubinstein, Pollini, Argerich, Zimerman, or Pires recordings discussed in Chapter 18 are the safe ground; all use modern Steinways or comparable modern concert grand pianos.

  3. **Listen comparatively.** Same notes, same Chopin score, different instruments. The exercise teaches what the instrument contributes to the music.

#### One-sentence background

The Pleyel-vs-Erard question runs through the Chopin scholarship. The most-detailed contemporary source is Jean-Jacques Eigeldinger, _Chopin: Pianist and Teacher as Seen by His Pupils_ (Cambridge 1986 English-language edition), which gathers the pupil recollections including the Mikuli quote on the Pleyel preference. The contemporary period-instrument tradition was launched by Christopher Hogwood, Frans Brüggen, John Eliot Gardiner, and other period-instrument conductors in the 1970s-80s; the period-instrument piano specifically developed in the 1990s and 2000s with pianists including Andreas Staier, Bart van Oort, Edoardo Torbianelli, Tomasz Ritter, and Krzysztof Książek. The International Chopin Competition on Period Instruments, held in Warsaw, was launched in 2018 as an institutional commitment to the period-instrument approach.

### 19.2 The Listening Window

The chapter does not propose a specific timestamped listening window; the exercise is comparative across recordings. The recommendations:

**For period Pleyel listening** :

* Tomasz Ritter, _Chopin on Period Instruments_ (NIFC, 2019; the 2018 International Chopin Competition on Period Instruments first prize winner's recording).
* Janusz Olejniczak, period-piano recordings (the soundtrack of the 2002 Polański film _The Pianist_ uses Olejniczak on period instruments for some pieces; selected NIFC recordings).
* Krzysztof Książek, _Chopin Recital_ (NIFC, 2019).

**For modern Steinway listening** : any of the Chapter 18 recommended recordings (Rubinstein, Pollini, Argerich, Zimerman, Pires).

The recommended exercise: listen to the Nocturne Op. 9 No. 2 on a period Pleyel (Ritter or Książek) and on a modern Steinway (Rubinstein 1965 or Pires 1996). The Op. 9 No. 2 is the most-recorded Chopin work; period-instrument recordings of it are widely available.

### 19.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### What the period Pleyel sounds like

The period Pleyel piano of the 1830s-40s has several distinctive sound characteristics:

* **Less sustaining power.** The piano's hammers strike the strings; the resulting tone decays more quickly than on a modern Steinway. The result is that long sustained notes do not carry as far on a Pleyel; the singing line cannot rely on the piano sustaining for as long as on a Steinway.
* **Lighter touch.** The hammer action is lighter; the player's finger does less mechanical work. Rapid figuration is therefore easier; Chopin's etude figuration is more-fluently playable on a Pleyel than on a heavier-action Steinway.
* **More intimate dynamic range.** The Pleyel's full-volume forte is less loud than a modern Steinway's forte; the pianissimo is also softer. The dynamic range is compressed compared to the modern Steinway.
* **Different overtone profile.** The Pleyel's tone has a different overtone structure — slightly less rich in the lower partials, with more presence in the upper partials. The result is a slightly more "transparent" or "luminous" sound compared to the Steinway's "full" or "rich" sound.
* **Smaller scale.** The period Pleyel of the 1830s-40s was a smaller instrument than the modern Steinway — typically about 5'8" to 6'0" in length, compared to the modern concert grand's 9'0". The smaller scale produces a more intimate sound.

The period Pleyel was the instrument Chopin composed for. Chopin's scores — particularly the pedal markings — were calibrated to the Pleyel's sound characteristics. The slower decay of the Pleyel's tone made the pedal innovation produce a different effect than on the modern Steinway; the period Pleyel's lighter touch made the etude figuration easier to project; the period Pleyel's intimate dynamic range made salon-environment performance practice viable.

#### What the modern Steinway sounds like

The modern Steinway (and comparable Bösendorfer, Yamaha, Steinway-D and other modern concert grands) has several distinct sound characteristics:

* **More sustaining power.** The modern piano's tone decays slower than the Pleyel's; long sustained notes carry farther.
* **Heavier touch.** The hammer action is heavier; rapid figuration requires more mechanical work from the player.
* **Wider dynamic range.** The modern Steinway's forte is louder than the Pleyel's; the pianissimo can be softer; the range is wider.
* **Richer overtone profile.** The modern Steinway's tone is richer in the lower partials; the result is a "fuller" or "richer" sound.
* **Larger scale.** The modern concert grand is 9'0" in length; the sound projects into large concert halls.

The modern Steinway was developed after Chopin's death (the contemporary Steinway design dates from the late 19th and early 20th centuries). Chopin's scores were not calibrated to the modern Steinway; the modern instrument plays Chopin's music differently from how Chopin composed for it. The differences are real and audible.

#### The Sound Differences

What the listener can hear when comparing a period-Pleyel recording with a modern-Steinway recording of the same Chopin work:

* **The pedal-induced harmonic blur differs.** On the period Pleyel the pedal innovation produces a slightly different harmonic-blur effect than on the modern Steinway. The Steinway's longer sustain means more harmonic blur; the Pleyel's shorter sustain means less.
* **The rapid figuration differs.** The period Pleyel's lighter touch makes the etude figuration easier to project; the modern Steinway's heavier touch requires more mechanical work but allows wider dynamic range.
* **The dynamic peaks differ.** The Op. 53 Polonaise's fortissimo close on a period Pleyel is less overwhelming than on a modern Steinway; the dynamic range that the Steinway accommodates is wider.
* **The intimate passages differ.** The Op. 9 No. 2 Nocturne's bel-canto-piano texture on a period Pleyel is more intimate; the modern Steinway's fuller tone projects the singing line further.
* **The overall character differs.** The Chopin on a period Pleyel is more "of its time" — closer to the 1830s-40s salon environment; the Chopin on a modern Steinway is more "modern" — projected into the contemporary concert-hall environment.

The differences are real but not absolute. The Chopin music works on both instruments. The Chopin tradition has been built primarily on the modern Steinway since the late 19th century; the period-instrument approach is a modern reconstruction of how the music sounded during Chopin's lifetime.

#### Five specific moments to compare

Generalizations about "different sound" remain abstract until you can locate the difference in a specific passage. Below are five 30-second windows where the Pleyel-vs-Steinway difference is most likely to be audible to attentive listening. Use Tomasz Ritter or Krzysztof Książek (NIFC) for the period-Pleyel side and the Chapter 18 Rubinstein/Pollini/Zimerman recommendations for the Steinway side. The descriptions below are predictions drawn from the instruments' known acoustic differences; your own ear is the final arbiter of what you actually hear.

  1. **Op. 9 No. 2 Nocturne, the cadenza (about 3:50-4:15 in Rubinstein 1965).** Notice the rapid right-hand ornamental run. On the Pleyel, you may hear each note speaking more separately — the run can sound _transparent_ , with individual pitches distinguishable. On the Steinway, the longer sustain tends to blur adjacent notes into a wash; the run sounds _fluid_ but can feel less articulated. The Pleyel preserves what Chopin notated; the Steinway smooths it.

  2. **Op. 28 No. 15 "Raindrop", the C♯-minor middle section's climax (about 3:00 in Argerich 1977).** Notice the _pulsing inner voice_ against the thickening chords above and below. On the Pleyel, the inner voice tends to remain audible through the climax — the Pleyel's overtone profile leaves room in the middle register. On the Steinway, the inner voice can be swallowed by the richer chordal writing; many Steinway pianists work to keep it audible. The Pleyel tends to make the pulsing easier to hear; the Steinway makes the climax louder.

  3. **Op. 10 No. 3 "Tristesse", the opening melody (0:00-0:45 in Pollini 1972).** Notice the singing right-hand legato. On the Pleyel, the lighter touch lets the pianist sustain the singing line with less effort; the legato can sound _natural_ , like a singer's breath. On the Steinway, the heavier touch means the pianist works harder to keep the line connected; the legato is more visibly _crafted_. Both can produce beautiful results; the work involved differs.

  4. **Op. 53 "Heroic", the closing fortissimo (about 6:45-7:00 in Pollini 1975).** Notice the dynamic peak. On the Pleyel, the fortissimo is _full_ but the upper dynamic range is capped — the period instrument cannot match a modern Steinway's volume. On the Steinway, the closing chords are overwhelming, near-orchestral, the room shaken by a single piano. The Steinway gives the _Heroic_ its 20th-century scale; the Pleyel gives it closer to its 1842 scale.

  5. **Op. 56 No. 3 mazurka, a chromatic passage (around 4:00 in Rubinstein 1965-66).** Listen for moments where the harmony reaches into distant chromatic territory. On the Pleyel, chromatic chords may retain _individual color_ — each chord's unique combination of pitches can read clearly because the overtone profile is more transparent. On the Steinway, chromatic chords tend to blend with each other slightly more, and the ear may work harder to pick out the harmonic detail. The Pleyel can make Chopin's harmonic writing more _legible_ ; the Steinway makes it more _immersive_.

These five micro-comparisons together suggest what the instrument contributes. They are not arguments for one instrument over the other; they are starting points for the listener's own A/B comparison. What you actually hear depends on the recording engineering, the performer's choices, the playback equipment, and your own ear's calibration.

#### The contemporary period-instrument tradition

The period-instrument tradition for Chopin developed in the 1990s and 2000s. The principal figures:

* **Andreas Staier** (born 1955, German) — period-piano specialist, recordings of Chopin and other 19th-century repertoire on period instruments.
* **Bart van Oort** (born 1959, Dutch) — period-piano specialist, recordings of Chopin and other 19th-century repertoire.
* **Edoardo Torbianelli** (Italian) — period-piano specialist.
* **Janusz Olejniczak** (born 1952, Polish) — Chopin Competition prize-winner 1970; one of the major contemporary Polish-school pianists, with recordings on both period instruments and modern Steinways.
* **Tomasz Ritter** (born 1995, Polish) — 2018 International Chopin Competition on Period Instruments first prize winner; the contemporary leader of the Polish period-piano Chopin tradition.
* **Krzysztof Książek** (born 1992, Polish) — Chopin Competition on Period Instruments laureate; period-piano recordings.

The International Chopin Competition on Period Instruments (held in Warsaw 2018; the next edition 2023; the next 2028) is the institutional commitment to the period-instrument approach. Modeled on the regular Chopin Competition, the period-instrument competition requires performances on period Pleyel and Erard instruments (and occasionally other period pianos including Graf and Buchholtz instruments).

Period-instrument recordings are widely available through the Polish Fryderyk Chopin Institute's recording label (NIFC, the Narodowy Instytut Fryderyka Chopina label) and through other contemporary labels.

### 19.4 The Stories

#### What the period-instrument approach claims

A short summary of the arguments for the period-instrument Chopin approach:

* **Historical authenticity.** Playing the music on the instrument it was composed for produces a closer approximation to how Chopin himself would have heard the music. The argument is one of historical reconstruction.
* **Pedal-and-touch consequences.** Chopin's pedal markings and the rapid figuration patterns make more idiomatic sense on a period Pleyel than on a modern Steinway. The technical-and-musical fit is closer.
* **Dynamic-balance consequences.** The salon-environment dynamic range that Chopin composed for is closer to the period Pleyel's actual range. The piece's dynamic logic is preserved more accurately.
* **Sound-aesthetic consequences.** The lighter, more intimate, more transparent sound of the period Pleyel is closer to Chopin's own aesthetic preferences (per Mikuli's "find my own sound" quote).

#### What the modern-Steinway approach claims

A short summary of the arguments for the modern-Steinway Chopin approach:

* **The post-Chopin tradition.** The Chopin tradition has been built on modern instruments since the late 19th century. The contemporary Chopin pianism — the Cortot-Rubinstein-Pollini-Argerich-Zimerman tradition — is the modern-Steinway tradition. The continuity matters.
* **Modern audibility.** The modern concert hall holds 1500-2500 people; the period Pleyel has limited projection across that scale. The contemporary listening environment requires the modern instrument's projection.
* **Modern listener expectations.** The contemporary listener has been formed on the modern-Steinway Chopin reading. Switching to the period instrument requires re-calibrating the listener's expectations; the modern-Steinway reading is the dominant contemporary currency.
* **Compositional intentions argument.** Chopin's intentions may have favored the improvements that the modern piano provides — longer sustain, wider dynamic range, fuller tone — even if those improvements were not available to him during his life. The argument is that Chopin would have preferred the modern instrument if he had had access to it.

#### The honest reading

The honest contemporary reading: both approaches are legitimate. The Chopin music supports both the period Pleyel and the modern Steinway; the differences are real but not determinative.

For the listener, the practical recommendation: hear the music on the modern Steinway primarily (the Cortot-Rubinstein-Pollini-Argerich-Zimerman tradition is the dominant reading), and sample selected works on the period Pleyel as comparison. The exercise teaches what the modern tradition has added to Chopin and what the period instrument restores.

### 19.5 What This Chapter Does Not Resolve

The Pleyel-vs-Erard question is observational. The chapter does not adjudicate. The honest reading is that both approaches are legitimate; the contemporary listener can engage with both; the choice depends on the listener's preferences and the listening occasion.

What this chapter does not resolve:

* Whether Chopin in 2026 would prefer the period Pleyel or the modern Steinway.
* Whether the period-instrument approach should replace the modern-Steinway tradition.
* Whether historical authenticity is more important than modern audibility.
* Whether the contemporary listener's expectations should be honored or challenged.

These are contested questions the Chopin community does not resolve unanimously. The reader who has worked through this volume has the background to enter the contested conversation; she does not need this chapter to resolve it for her.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"Chopin's quote on the Pleyel-vs-Erard distinction (the 'find my own sound' quote from Mikuli) describes the Pleyel as the more-demanding instrument. Listening to a period-Pleyel recording, do you hear the 'own sound' character — the player's interpretive control — more clearly than on the modern Steinway?"_
* _"The modern-Steinway tradition has dominated Chopin performance since the late 19th century. The period-instrument tradition is a relatively recent reconstruction (1990s-onward). Should the contemporary listener prefer continuity (modern Steinway) or historical authenticity (period Pleyel)?"_
* _"The chapter argues that the Chopin music supports both the period Pleyel and the modern Steinway. Are there specific Chopin works (the late mazurkas? the Op. 28 Preludes? the Op. 9 Nocturnes? the Op. 53 Polonaise?) where one instrument is more appropriate than the other?"_
* _"The International Chopin Competition on Period Instruments has been held in Warsaw since 2018. The regular Chopin Competition continues to use modern Steinways. What does the institutional split tell you about the contemporary Chopin community's relationship to the period-instrument question?"_
* _"The contemporary listener has been formed on the modern-Steinway reading. Switching to the period Pleyel requires re-calibrating expectations — slower decay, smaller dynamic range, different overtone profile. After hearing one period-Pleyel recording, what specifically had to be re-calibrated in your listening, and which of those re-calibrations feels worth keeping when you return to a Steinway recording?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

For this chapter, the listening exercise is comparative across instruments:

  1. **Find a Chopin Op. 9 No. 2 Nocturne recording on a period Pleyel** (Ritter or Książek on NIFC) and on a modern Steinway (Rubinstein 1965 or Pires 1996). Listen to both. Notice the differences: the sustaining power, the dynamic range, the intimate texture, the overtone profile.

  2. **Find a Chopin Etude (Op. 10 No. 3 or Op. 25 No. 7) recording on a period Pleyel** and on a modern Steinway (Pollini 1972). The etude figuration's relationship to the instrument's touch is most-audible in the etudes.

  3. **Find a Chopin late mazurka (Op. 56 No. 3 or Op. 59 No. 1) on a period Pleyel** and on a modern Steinway (Rubinstein 1965-66). The late-mazurka harmonic vocabulary's relationship to the instrument's overtone profile is most-audible in the late mazurkas.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : explore the Fryderyk Chopin Institute (NIFC) recording label catalogue. The label has been producing period-instrument Chopin recordings since the 2000s; the catalogue includes major Chopin works performed on Pleyel, Erard, Graf, and Buchholtz period instruments. The recordings are widely available on streaming platforms (Spotify, Apple Music, IDAGIO) and on physical media. About 1-2 hours of sampling will give you the period-instrument range.

**Read** : Jean-Jacques Eigeldinger, _Chopin: Pianist and Teacher as Seen by His Pupils_ (Cambridge 1986 English-language edition), the chapters on Chopin's instrument preferences and on his pupils' recollections of his playing. The book is the primary source for the Mikuli quote and the surrounding pupil-tradition material on the Pleyel preference. About 200 pages total; the relevant chapters are about 40 pages.
